ARDINIER DE UT LE MONDE COMPLETE TRAIT DE TOUTES BRANCHES DE L'HORTICULTURE NÉCESSAIRE AUX JARDINIERS ET AUX AMATEURS DE JARDINACE, CONTENANT ALL LES DÉTAILS RELATIFS AU JARDIN POTAGER, FRUITIER ET FLEURISTE orné de plus de 100 figures - Intercalées dans le texte. 7. PARIS GARNIER FRÈRES, LIBRAIRES-ÉDITEURS 6, RUE DES SAINTS-PÈRES ET PALAIS-ROYAL, 215 The taste for gardening has never been as widespread as it is today; this taste, like all things in this world, has taken on an entirely new form in a century rich in transformations. From the amateur whose only source of satisfaction is the window garden, to the man who has a vast landscape garden with its obligatory accompaniment of cold, hot and temperate greenhouses, there is hardly anyone who is willing to take up gardening without wanting to know at least a little about it. It's not so very long ago that the most enlightened classes of society owned beautiful gardens, but made a pity of not knowing anything about gardening. Today, it's almost shameful to own a garden of any kind and not know how fruit and vegetables, ornamental plants and shrubs grow; in a word, it's no longer acceptable to have a garden and not be a bit of a gardener. As an expression of this new need, the last thirty years have seen the birth, growth and multiplication of horticultural societies, and the spread among the various classes of amateurs of general or special treatises on horticulture as a whole, or on its main branches. These works, many of which have rendered eminent services to gardening in France, have for the most part aged, despite the sometimes successful efforts made to rejuvenate them. This is because, in gardening as in any other division of knowledge applied to the things of life, progress does not stop; it cannot stop. If tomorrow, by means of a new process, we manage to print on the daguerreotype the complete body of human knowledge, just at the precise point at which it stands at the moment; before the book is printed and put on sale, it will be out of date. In France, and in Europe as a whole, the railroad network, which eliminates distances, has made gardening possible and profitable in places where it was previously out of the question. The rapidity and extreme ease of maritime communications put all the world's plants, whether for utility or for pleasure, within the reach of amateurs in every country. The perfection of manufacturing and the low cost of iron and glass have made it possible to multiply greenhouses everywhere, and to extend all types of garden cultivation to all climates. These are just some of the most striking features of the transformation of contemporary horticulture. At a time when, so to speak, everyone is a gardener or aspires to become one, the time seems right to publish Le JARDINIER DE TOUT LE MONDE. This book, inspired by the benevolent advice of the most skilful horticulturists, written almost under the dictation of the most enlightened members of Parisian horticulture, is not aimed exclusively at professional gardeners; The facts and concepts are presented in such a way as to remind those who know, and teach those who don't, all that is important to know, all that can enlighten practice, and enable them to fully enjoy the sum total of elegant and harmless pleasures that the passion for gardening, so sweet and fortunately so widespread in France, can provide. Let us recall, with a touch of national and professional pride, that if one of our ancient provinces, Touraine, has borne the fine title of Jardin de la France, the whole of France is entitled to claim that of Jardin de l'Europe. Exempted by the happy variety of soils and climates of its departments, from the devouring heat of the Midi, the ice of the North, the fogs and icy mists of the far West, France is the country in the world where fruit, vegetables, flowers and all the useful or pleasant products of gardening can be obtained with the greatest perfection. Le Jarbinier de Tout le MOnde embraces horticulture from the most general and advanced point of view; it is naturally divided into five distinct parts, comprising: 1st part, Notions générales; 2nd, Culture maraichère; 3rd, Culture des arbres fruitiers; 4th, Culture des végétaux d'ornement; 5th, Orangeries et serres. If, to do a job of this nature properly, you need to have spent many years in the company of the most skilful practitioners in France and abroad, to have studied in their own languages the writings of the most famous horticulturists in England and Germany, and above all to be yourself animated to the highest degree by the most passionate taste for gardening, the author of JARDInIeR De TOUT LE MONDE, believes he is in the best position to offer friends of horticulture a guide at the height of current knowledge. As for the form, he has endeavored to make it simple enough, precise enough to be effortlessly intelligible to all, correct enough to be accepted by the enlightened and literate part of the horticultural public, with the hope of being not only consulted, but read with profit; and why wouldn't he add, with pleasure. A. YSABEAU. PART ONE GENERAL NOTIONS CHAPTER ONE Gardening Intimate relationships between gardens and those who cultivate them. - The gardens of Semiramis. - Gardens of the New Zealand savages. - Functional relationships between agriculture and horticulture. - Various types of gardens. - Vegetable gardens. - Marshes. - Market gardening. - Fruit garden. -Fruit and landscape garden. - Parterre. - Suitable location - Landscaped garden. - English garden. - Parkland. - Grove. - Symmetrical gardens. - Used for public walks. - Preserved as souvenirs. - Sully's garden. - Choice of garden location. - Expositica. - Fencing. - Fence walls. - Chaperons. - Their usefulness. - Living hedges. - Shrubs they can be formed from. - Hedges at the bottom of a ditch. - Interior distribution. - Dividing walls. - Apple hedge grafted by natural grafting - Interior fence between the kitchen garden and the rest of the garden. If we were to write the history of gardens from century to century, in every country in the known world, from those of Semiramis, one of the seven wonders of the ancient world, to those of the savages of New Zealand, who hide a kitchen garden in the heart of the most impenetrable forests, accessible by paths known only to the owner, the result would be a picture no less instructive than curious. Everywhere and at every time, gardens have shown the most striking links with the customs, ideas and habits of those who cultivate them. Without going beyond France in our time, the dominant character of the inhabitants of each of our agricultural regions is reflected in the gardens; the more or less advanced state of gardening gives a fairly accurate measure of the state of local agriculture. We know by what intimate links agriculture and gardening hold and help each other. The word horticulture, which has come into common usage for all divisions of gardening these days, seems to bring the two branches of the art of using cultivable soil even closer together, placing them on the same level. We will conform to current usage by using the words gardening and horticulture interchangeably throughout this book. From a general point of view, horticulture offers an outlet for human activity that is as beneficial to individuals as it is to society. As a profession, horticulture leads with certainty to that degree of relative ease which gives more real satisfaction than opulence; as a leisure activity, there is no other which is more favorable to the maintenance of health, or which provides a greater sum of elegant and varied pleasures, which each person can enjoy according to the humble or elevated situation in which it has pleased God to place him. Various types of gardens. - The size, shape and distribution of a garden can vary infinitely, but all gardens fall into four divisions: 1° THE POTAGARDEN; 2° THE FRUIT GARDEN; 3° THE PARTERRE; 4° THE LANDSCAPE GARDEN. 1. Vegetable garden. - The vegetable garden is often surrounded by flowerbeds devoted to the cultivation of ornamental plants in the open ground. In this case, it combines the functions of the parterre with those of the vegetable garden proper. When the kitchen garden is cultivated by a professional gardener who uses the produce to supply vegetables to city markets, it is called a marsh, and the person who cultivates it is called a market gardener; market gardening is rightly considered to be the cultivation of vegetable plants brought to their highest perfection. 2. Fruit garden. - This type of garden is distinct from the others only in very large gardens, such as those in châteaux surrounded by spacious parkland; everywhere else, fruit trees are planted either in espaliers along the enclosing walls, or in the beds bordering the vegetable garden compartments. In recent years, a completely new type of fruit garden has been introduced in the vicinity of Paris and the larger departmental towns. The ground is laid out and distributed as for a bosquet; beautiful lawns, with flowerbeds standing out from them, take up most of the space; views are skilfully created; but all ornamental trees are replaced by fruit trees and shrubs of all kinds: this is what we call a landscape fruit garden. 3. Parterre. - Most often, the parterre, also known as the flower garden, is one of the outbuildings of another type of garden, either because, in the vegetable garden, the best-placed beds are decorated with the most beautiful flowers of each season, or because, in a large landscape garden, the compartments closest to the house are devoted to the same crop. In any case, it's the most pleasant division of the garden. 4. Landscape garden. - This type of garden has long been known as the English garden, because it is in fact English in origin; in large dimensions, it is a park, in smaller proportions, a grove. We mention here only for the record the symmetrical gardens, with wide, straight paths and regular, geometrically-shaped compartments, a style now abandoned except for gardens used for public walks; in France, the Tuileries, Luxembourg and Versailles are the finest examples. In some départements, however, gardens in this style are still preserved as reminders of another era. Such is the case, for example, of the Château de Sully, whose hedges, wide paths and terrace have been restored to exactly the same condition as they were when this garden was used as a promenade by Henri IV's friend. Choice of location - It rarely depends on the gardener to choose the location of the garden, even when it's a new creation on land previously given over to other crops. When he has the choice, he should place the kitchen garden, fruit garden and parterre as far as possible on a slope, with southern, western, south-western and south-eastern exposure. North, east and northeast exposures are the least favorable. The vegetable garden should be isolated and placed far enough away from the house so as not to be seen from the apartments; the flowerbed, on the other hand, should be close enough to the house so that the appearance of the flowerbeds continues to make it pleasant. Fences. - The best fence for a garden, large or small, is a good wall, made of stone or brick, 2.5 to 3 meters high, whose most exposed surfaces can be used to grow espalier trees. But the cost of a fence made entirely of walls is sometimes too high relative to the resources available to the owner. In this case, he simply encloses the north or northeast and west or northwest sides with walls, so that the inner surfaces of these walls are in the best condition for growing espalier and counter-spalier fruit trees. The walls are topped with a double, roof-like ledge, projecting 20 or 25 centimetres beyond the plumb line of their surface: Fig. 1. The chaperon. This is called a chapreon (fig. 1). The main advantage of the coping on a garden wall is that it protects espaliered fruit trees from the excessive cold that results from radiation on winter nights when the sky is not covered with clouds; it's on the edges of the coping that we hang the mats or canvases designed to protect flowering trees from the late frosts of spring, and from the hail that too often accompanies the March sleet, which in the Paris climate lasts until the middle of April. The two sides of the garden that you consider more advantageous and economical not to enclose with walls, can be enclosed with hedges. The strongest and tightest are those of hawthorn, because of the ease with which this shrub can be pruned and sheared with scissors, giving it the shape of a thick wall, impossible to cross. After hawthorn, European liciet, dogwood and privet are the most suitable shrubs for garden hedges. In the south of France, well-mown hedges with even surfaces, like those of a hawthorn hedge, are quite frequently formed entirely with Bengal roses. When there is no reason to spare the land, and you wish to preserve the appearance of the exterior landscape inside the garden, you dig a sloping ditch with a narrow base, in the middle of which are planted the shrubs of which the hedge is to be composed. When these shrubs have reached the height of the upper edge of the ditch, the hedge forms a fence that is as complete and more difficult to cross than if it were established according to ordinary practice, without a ditch on the outer boundary of the garden (fig. 2). Fig. 2. hedge in ditch. Interior distribution. - When the kitchen garden is sufficiently extensive, and not accompanied by another garden more specifically devoted to growing fruit trees, walls are built at intervals parallel to the one of the enclosing walls that faces the most favorable exposure, in order to provide the largest possible surface area for growing espaliered fruit trees. This is what gardeners call the dividing wall; it is pierced by false doors to allow free communication between the various compartments of the kitchen garden; its construction is the same as that of the enclosing walls. The kitchen garden is often isolated from the parterre and landscape garden by an inner fence of hedges. These hedges can be made up of apple trees, the branches of which are directed in such a way as to form a trellis with diamond-shaped compartments. At all points of contact, the branches are welded together by natural approach grafting; the hedge thus ends up being in one piece, giving it great solidity, without the trees of which it is composed being any less productive. CHAPTER II Lands and waters suitable for gardening. Lands and waters suitable for gardening. - How gardening is possible everywhere. - Origin of cultivable land. - Their elements - Strong soils. - Clay soils. - Brick soil. - Dose of clay in strong soils. - The need to drain heavy soils - Drainage in gardens. - Principle of drainage. - Light earth - Siliceous sand without cohesion. - Its sterility. - Dose of sand in a good light soil. - Calcareous or chalky soils. - Dose of lime in a good chalky soil. - Clay-limestone soil. - Clay-siliceous earth. - Wood soil. - Heather earth. - Composts. - Orange tree soil - Camellia soil. - Carnation soil - Tulip soil - Water suitable for gardening. - Matter dissolved in them. -Well water. - Spring. - Spring water - Running water - Stagnant water. - The useful effect of water in gardening. The old Flemish proverb applies especially to gardening: "There is no such thing as bad soil; there are only bad farmers. Wherever land and water are available, it's possible to create and maintain a good garden. This is not to say that melons can be grown successfully in the open air in Greenland, or pineapples in Siberia; but by adapting crops to the nature of the soil at hand, success is not impossible anywhere. Before reviewing the horticultural properties of the various types of arable land, let's take a look at the general composition of these soils and how we understand how they came to be. In the part of the earth's surface not covered by water, man's real domain, i.e. that which it is possible for him to convert into cultivated fields and gardens, is not very extensive; once the ice deserts of the north, the sandy deserts of the south, the barren rocks and steep slopes of the high mountains have been removed, he is left with the plains crossed by the valleys of the rivers and their tributaries. The layer of earth that covers these plains and valleys was formed by the mixture of the debris of various rocks, broken, divided, decomposed and violently transported by the waters following the cataclysms that upset the surface of our planet, as geology shows. Without this mixing and shifting, there would be no cultivable soil, and farming and gardening would be impossible. Soil suitable for cultivation is based on clay, lime, sand and some organic matter derived from the decomposition of plants and animals; these substances, taken separately, make up what we call humus or loam. These various elements of cultivable soil can be combined in any proportion; if clay dominates, the soil is strong; if sand dominates, it is light; if lime is present in excess, it is chalky or calcareous; depending on the proportions of its main elements, it can be clayey-limestone or clayey-siliceous. All these types of soil, whose nomenclature can be expanded by a host of names that lack precision, must contain a fairly high proportion of loam in order to be suitable for various garden crops. Heavy soil. This type of soil is also known as fat soil or clay soil. When it contains too much clay, it is unfit for cultivation; as it doesn't allow rainwater to filter through, it forms a pasty mud in winter; in summer, it becomes as hard as brick; its surface then cracks, splits in all directions, heats up to the point of drying out and burning plant roots; all vegetation becomes impossible. Soils in this condition are not common; they contain 85% clay, and are commonly known as brick soils, because they are used to make bricks, tiles and pottery. When, instead of containing 85% clay, a strong soil contains only 45 to 50%, and lime, sand and loam are present in the right proportions, it is of good quality and can become a good garden soil. The modifications it may need for this purpose would not be practicable in large-scale cultivation; on the contrary, they are very practicable in a garden, where only relatively small areas are ever worked. Any soil that is clayey enough to merit the name "terre forte" (strong soil) retains rainwater to a greater or lesser extent, and presents, albeit to a lesser degree, the disadvantages of brick clay. The first thing to do, before converting it into a garden, is to sanitize it with a good drainage system. Drainage in gardens. Since English agriculture has demonstrated the benefits of draining heavy soils, through a series of highly successful applications, this operation has become the greatest and most fruitful agricultural improvement of modern times. It is beyond the scope of this book to give even a brief idea of drainage and the means of applying it; drainage must be studied in special treatises. If the soil is to be ploughed, either with a plow in the field or with a spade in the garden, it must be neither too dry nor too wet. Ploughed too dry, it rises in large blocks that are very-difficult to divide; ploughed too wet, it kneads, clumps and is in worse condition after ploughing than if it had not been touched; it must therefore, in both gardens and fields, be taken at its point. The degree of dryness to which the soil must have reached in order to be properly ploughed in the spring, following heavy rains and snowmelt, is more or less delayed, depending on how difficult it is for the cultivable layer to filter out and evaporate the excess moisture with which it is permeated; this can result in delays that are highly detrimental to the success of gardening operations; for, in horticulture, it is of the utmost importance that everything is not only done well, but also done in its time. When, through the application of a good drainage system, the superfluous water in the subsoil finds a quick and easy way out, the soil, in spring, is at the right point for the re-growth of all crops; the heat it receives from the rise in outside temperature is no longer absorbed by the evaporation of its excess moisture; the vegetation of perennial plants is early and vigorous; seedlings of annual plants are sown in good time; the roots of fruit and ornamental trees are preserved from canker and rot; the strong soil, made healthier by drainage, seems to have suddenly changed its nature and lends itself with perfect docility to all types of garden cultivation. In the presence of such advantages, if you have to create a garden in strong clay soil, don't hesitate to drain. The distance between parallel lines of pipes, the diameter of the pipes and the depth at which they should be buried will vary according to the constitution of the soil and the atmospheric circumstances of the local climate; nothing can be specified in this respect. The homeowner, determined to achieve this fruitful improvement, if he does not have the required knowledge himself, should turn to a competent man, and act under his guidance. It should be noted that drainage in gardens generally needs to be deeper than in open fields, so that the roots of trees and shrubs cannot reach the pipes, enter through the joints and partially obstruct them. Once drained, strong soils need to be modified by various amendments if they are to be in the best possible condition for gardening. (See Amendments, chap. III.) Light loam - When light loam is completely lacking in cohesion and contains up to 70% silica, it is no longer a loam in the strict sense of the term; it is an absolutely sterile siliceous sand, unsuitable for gardening or any other crop. However, when it contains no more than 50 to 55% silica, light soil can easily be made into a very good garden soil. Its main drawback is that it heats up excessively, and doesn't retain water from rainfall and watering for long enough for cultivated plants to take full advantage of it. Among light soils, the best are those that are both light and deep; the thickness of the cultivable layer then compensates to some extent for the disadvantages resulting from its lack of cohesion. Any light soil to be used for gardening needs to be amended with lime and clay (see Amendments, chap. III).-The presence of lime in the soil, even in very high doses, in no way makes it unsuitable for gardening; but when the soil contains 70 to 80 percent lime, it is very difficult to grow garden crops; it is then called chalky earth or white earth, a less-than-fertile soil that covers fairly large areas in France, particularly in the Aube department. (Champagne pouilleuse.) Chalky soil, which contains no more than 40-50% lime, is excellent for all types of gardening; it is easy to plow in all seasons and dries out more slowly than siliceous soil. Clay-limestone soil. In most garden soils, clay and lime are in higher proportion than siliceous sand; these soils are therefore clay-limestone. Where this soil does not exist naturally, the gardener, through the judicious use of various soil improvers, should endeavor to bring the garden soil back to the conditions of a good clay-limestone soil of medium consistency. Sandy-clay soil-This type of soil, in which clay and sand are combined with a small proportion of lime, can be quite good for gardening when it contains enough humus; it is often a little too compact, slow to warm up, and needs to be amended with lime in high doses. Various types of soil adapted to special crops are used in gardening, such as carnation soil, orange tree soil, camellia soil and many others; these soils are mixtures for which horticulture has adopted the English expression "composts". Wood soil and heather soil, which are used in the same way as composts for various specific crops, are nevertheless natural soils that are used without modifying their composition. Both are based on plant debris combined with the light siliceous earth on which woodland and heather generally grow. The compost of decomposed leaves is the useful part of wood soil; the compost of heather, broom and ferns is the active principle of heather soil. Many valuable shrubs and ornamental plants, including Azaleas, Rhododendrums and Fuchsias, grow only in pure heather soil. Composts. Modern gardening has done away with the complicated mixtures once used with a kind of superstitious confidence, and prepared at great expense for a variety of special purposes. Such was the case with the old orange earth, whose recipe, including a host of ingredients, was carefully preserved in the Versailles orangery: it was a kind of theriac. Orange trees only received this compost after it had been stored for three years and stirred several times during this long interval. Today, we know that any kind of fertilizer reduces to compost after a certain length of time. Today, when their soil needs renewing, the orange trees at Versailles receive only the orange-tree compost used elsewhere, and they are no worse off for it. This compost is made up of: two parts free-range garden soil, one part potting soil, one part natural heather soil, all mixed exactly with a spade; it can be used as soon as it has been prepared. The most commonly used Camellia soil is made up of: one part free-range garden soil, one part leaf compost, one part heather soil. To increase fertility, we add to these exactly mixed ingredients undisinfected human fertilizer, diluted in enough water to give it the consistency of a clear brouct. The dose is two to three liters of this brouet per cubic meter of camellia soil; it is incorporated one month before giving the soil to the camellias, which it helps to vegetate with great vigor. Carnation soil is no more than good garden soil mixed with a third of its volume of good potting soil. The soil in which tulip and hyacinth onions are planted is a compost of garden soil and potting compost, in equal parts. Ranunculus soil is made up of garden soil and dried pond sludge, in equal parts. Water for gardening. It takes a lot of water to keep a garden going. The only exception to this rule in France is in the north and in the western maritime departments, where it rains almost every day. Rarely can the gardener choose the water needed to water his plants; he has to make do with what he finds within his reach; more often than not, he has only well water, which is very full of calcareous principles, and therefore of poor quality, and whose defects he can only try to attenuate by leaving it as long as possible in the tanks, in contact with the air, before using it. When he has the means to obtain it in sufficient quantity, rainwater is the best that the gardener can distribute to his plants; then come, in order of relative value, spring water, running water, i.e. water from streams and rivers, and finally, stagnant water, drawn from ponds and puddles of limited extent. From the point of view of its fertilizing action, this last water is undoubtedly the best; but as the organic matter it contains, and which decomposes incessantly in it, gives it a repulsive odor, on the one hand its use is unhealthy for the gardener, and on the other it spoils the flavor of certain garden produce, that of strawberries, for example, which must be watered while the strawberry plant bears flowers, half-formed fruit and ripe fruit at the same time. Water not only quenches the thirst of plants and protects them from the effects of drought; even the purest water, such as that which flows over a bed of pure silica sand or granite, always contains dissolved air, carbonic acid gas, ammonia and various salts in varying proportions, all of which have a significant influence on the vegetation of garden plants. The foregoing shows how important it is for the gardener, before creating a garden, to ascertain the nature of the water he will be able to obtain; for without abundant water of at least fair quality, gardening is impossible. (See Watering, chap. VII.) CHAPTER III Fertilizers and soil improvers. Fertilizers and soil improvers. - Manures. - Sheep manure - Rarely used for gardening. - Horse manure. - Advantages in horticulture. - Suitable soils. - Manure from horned animals. - Its properties. - Pig manure. - Common prejudices. - Potting soil. - Columbine. - Barnyard bird droppings. - Powdered fertilizers. - Guano. - Poudrette. - Refinery black. - Goëmon. - Sea sludge. - Pond slime. - Wool rags. - Crushed bones - Leather shavings. - Bone shavings. - Horn shavings. - Lime. - Sand. - Clay. - Preparation of soil improvers. - Sieved ash. - Soil additives. Garden cultivation, whether for the personal enjoyment of those involved, or because the gardener intends to sell the produce, requires very large quantities of all kinds of fertilizers. Whereas the ploughman does his best to fertilize his land every three or four years, and necessarily takes several harvests from one manuring, the gardener must renew the manuring several times a year. If he tries to avoid this necessity, it is for him the most ruinous of savings; he obtains, in this case, only miserable products which do not even pay for the little fertilizer distributed too sparingly to the garden soil. Manure. Livestock manure, which has reached a more or less advanced stage of decomposition, is the most common type of fertilizer used in gardens. For this part of his duties, as for many others, the gardener does not always have a free choice; he must adapt his crops to the type of manure he can obtain. More often than not, professional gardeners can only obtain manure from the large numbers of luxury or service horses employed in cities. Far from major population centers, it's the manure from horned cattle that's easiest for them to buy. As for sheep manure, although excellent for all kinds of garden cultivation, it is almost never used; farmers who keep flocks of sheep never sell manure from their sheep. Pig manure is considered harmful to many of gardening's most delicate products; it's better than its reputation. Of all manures, its chemical composition brings it closest to human fertilizer, whose fertilizing power it almost equals. It can therefore be used without fear in gardens, either alone or mixed with other fertilizers. Horse manure is invaluable for gardening because of the property it possesses of suspending fermentation when dry, and resuming it as soon as wet; for this reason, it is the only manure that can be used to build warm and hot beds for forced cultivation. Horned cattle manure, which is more watery and colder, cannot provide the same kind of service to the gardener. When garden soil is more or less cold, compact and heavy, only horse manure should be used; this manure, in a relatively early state of decomposition, acts mechanically by lifting and dividing the soil, which it prevents from closing up, and to which it promptly yields its fertilizing principles, due to the activity of its fermentation. If the garden soil is warm and light, it should preferably be given either horned-animal manure or half-decomposed horse manure. Potting soil. Any kind of manure, when left to its own devices, eventually runs out of fermentable matter, leaving a black, crumbly mass that's soft to the touch and much smaller in volume than recent manure. Potting soil is the same and has the same properties, whatever the type of manure it comes from. In gardens, potting soil, which is constantly needed for all crops, comes from layers that are dismantled when they no longer give heat: potting soil from broken layers still possesses some of the properties of the manure from which the layers were built; when it is to be used as potting soil for the cultivation of certain plants that cannot stand contact with manure, it is kept in a heap before use, until it has reached the most complete degree of decomposition. Columbine. - Strictly speaking, colombine is pigeon droppings from the cleaning of dovecotes or pigeon lofts, from which it derives its name. It is one of the most active fertilizers, and is stored dry for use as a powder. It has a very energetic influence on vegetation, even in small doses, especially on melons, cucumbers, gherkins, pumpkins, gourds, coloquintes and all plants in the same family. In the absence of real colombina, the gardener can always obtain a fairly large quantity of barnyard bird droppings by frequent cleaning of the henhouse; this fertilizer has the same properties as colombina and is suitable for the same crops. Powdered fertilizers. - The trade supplies agriculture with a number of powdered fertilizers, some natural, others artificial, some of which can be used to great advantage in horticulture; these are, in particular, Guano from Peru, whose properties are more or less the same as those of colombina; Poudrette, or disinfected human fertilizer, endowed with properties of the same kind, but much weakened; and Animalized Black, or refinery black, less energetic than guano, but less expensive, which can be used in higher doses and applied, especially in light siliceous soils, to a large number of crops. In addition to these fertilizers, which are available almost everywhere in France, the gardener, depending on local circumstances and the nature of the land he cultivates, can also use Goëmon, Tangue or sea sludge, pond mud after it has dried out in the open air, woollen rags, crushed bones, leather shavings, and bone and horn shavings from button factories to stimulate the vegetation of the plants under his care. Amendments. The action of soil improvers differs essentially from that of fertilizers. Fertilizers activate vegetation for a more or less short time, after which there is nothing left in the soil: the crops grown on the manure have absorbed it all, and it must be renewed; the nature of the soil, once the useful effect of the manure has been exhausted, is not appreciably changed. They modify the composition of the cultivable layer and change the proportions of its constituent elements. When a garden is created and the soil needs to be amended, it is amended in much greater proportions than cultivated fields to which the same amendments are given, so that there is no need to come back to it. Let's take a garden established on schistose, clayey soil, which can only become suitable for gardening by receiving a high proportion of lime; in a field only 4 hectares in size, with a 25-centimeter thick topsoil, to add a tenth of lime to this layer, you need a thousand cubic meters, a very considerable quantity, of such a high value when the cost of transport is added to the purchase price, that the operation is impossible to carry out; in a one-hectare garden, the soil can be limed with 250 cubic meters of lime; it's an expense once made, and the certain benefits it should produce will be in proportion1 to the sum spent: there's no need to hesitate. The same applies to silica sand, used to amend clay soils that are too compact, and to clay added as an amendment to garden soil when it has the defect of being too light and lacking in consistency. Lime, sand and clay are, as we've just seen, the most common soil improvers used to improve garden soil. Ashes are no less useful when the soil lacks the fertilizing principles they contain. PREPARING SOIL IMPROVERS. Once it has been recognized that a garden's soil needs such an amendment, lime should not be given to it without preparation; it would be difficult to incorporate it exactly into the cultivable layer; it would be too unevenly distributed. To get the most out of it, it should be layered, at the right rate, in alternating beds with thin turf or dried pond sludge. Piles are formed in autumn, reworked once or twice with a spade during the winter, and dismantled in spring. The lime, intimately mixed with decomposed sod or pond sludge, is in the best possible condition to be distributed on the soil surface and ploughed in by very careful deep ploughing. The advantages of this process for liming soils are so obvious that enlightened growers use no other, whether for field or garden cultivation. SAND, added as an amendment to compact clay soil, which it serves to render less heavy and more easily divisible, needs no prior preparation; all that's required is to spread it on the soil, in the appropriate dose, and mix it well with the soil by autumn or spring ploughing. CLAY incorporated into garden soil that is too light to increase cohesion should be exposed to the open air and thoroughly dried before use. In this state, it is easy to reduce it to a coarse powder, which is spread by hand in the furrow or dip opened by the spade during ploughing. In this way, it mixes very evenly with the ploughed soil, without forming blocks that are difficult to divide, and produces its full useful effect. ASH, especially coal ash, is widely used in the North as a soil improver, due to the high proportion of lime and potash it contains. When mixed with manure, it is more effective than when used on its own, as a soil improver. After having been roughly sifted to separate the slag, the manure pile is first dismantled and built up layer by layer. The manure mixed with ashes is then spread over the garden squares at spring or autumn ploughing time, and buried with a spade, like ordinary manure. The careful gardener takes every opportunity to improve his garden soil, either with the aforementioned amendments, or by adding good soil when available. If, for example, a building in his neighborhood is to be erected on sufficiently fertile ground, he will not fail to take advantage of the opportunity to add, at little cost, a large dose of good soil to that of his garden, which could not receive a more effective amendment es of a more lasting useful effect. CHAPTER IV Gardening tools. Gardening instruments. - Their classification. - Ploughing implements - Common spade. - Curved blade. - Flemish spade. - Pickaxe. -- Pick. - Their use in ripping. - Square-bladed hoe. - Triangular blade. - With two flat teeth. - Hoe or serfouette. - Sharp-toothed rake. - With obtuse tines. - Fork with straight tines. - With curved tines. - Irrigation tools. - Flat-sided watering can. - Long-necked. - Portable pump. - Transport tools. - Common wheelbarrow. - Lined with sheet metal. - Clerestory. - Stretchers. - Arboricultural tools. - Serpette. - Secateurs. - Saw. - Grafting machine. - Pliers. - Crescent. - Cleaver. - Miscellaneous instruments. - Roller. - Rakes. - Shoe planter. - Advantages. - Cleat. - Trowel. - Its use in the greenhouse. It is very important for the gardener to be equipped with an assortment of the best tools for the various tasks involved in his profession; with the wrong tools, he can only do a poor job. Tools and instruments for horticultural use are classified into four main divisions, comprising ploughing, irrigation, transport and arboriculture implements; a fifth division includes implements for various purposes that do not fit into any of the preceding divisions. Ploughing implements. - The main ploughing implements used by gardeners are the spade, pickaxe, hoe, hoe, rake and pitchfork. SPADE. - Perfect ploughing with a spade is one of the most essential conditions for successful gardening. The spade, in terms of weight, diameter and length of handle, must be perfectly suited to the size and strength of the person using it; a good spade is to the gardener what a good rifle is to the soldier. In a garden of a certain size, the soil is never homogeneous; there are veins of hard, strong soil alongside other veins of soft, light soil: hence the need for several different spades, because soil that differs essentially from one another cannot all be ploughed well with the same spade. COMMON SPADE (fig. 3). - For ploughing good ordinary garden soil, neither too strong nor too light, there is no better instrument than the common spade, with its flat iron, slightly narrower at its cutting edge than at its upper edge. When the spade is to be used in slightly hard soil, the two portions of the upper edge, to the right and left of the socket through which the handle passes, must be wide enough for the worker to rest his heavy clogged foot on them. CURVED EDGE SPREADER (fig. 4). - When the subsoil is stony and difficult to cut, the common spade is replaced by another spade which differs from it in only one respect: instead of a straight edge, the cutting edge describes a curve and forms two points at the two corners, making the work easier without increasing the worker's fatigue. Fig. 3: Common spade. Fig. 4. curved-edge spade. Fig. 5. Flemish spade. FLEMISH SPADE (fig. 5). -Gardeners often have to plough soil that is otherwise very good, but so light that when it is a little dry, if you want to work it with one of the two preceding spades, the portion taken up each time by the instrument crumbles, slips on the blade, and falls back without having been properly turned, which makes ploughing more or less imperfect. Soils of this kind are very common in Flanders, so farmers there have adopted a spade with a long, narrow blade which, instead of being flat like the common spade, forms a slight curve towards the middle of its length, which holds the clod of earth in place and gives the worker plenty of time to turn it over as he sees fit. The Flemish spade, widely used throughout northern France, is excellent for all garden soils that are lighter than heavy. PIOCHE (fig. 6). - The pickaxe itself is only useful to the Fig. 6. gardener in breaking operations, where the soil must be turned over and excavated to a depth of om, 60 and sometimes om, 80. (See Gardening operations, chap. V.) When carrying out work of this kind, we frequently come across either large roots or stones which must not be left in the subsoil. These are removed with the common pickaxe; Fig. 7. Pickaxe. When this instrument is insufficient, a special kind of pickaxe called a pick (fig. 7) is used, similar to the instrument of the same name used by pavers; it differs from the pickaxe only in the strength and length of its thick, narrow blade, and in its point, which can, in the hands of a sturdy and skilful worker, penetrate the hardest subsoil, where the ordinary pickaxe would break. HOUE. - The hoe is one of the gardener's most useful implements, used to give the most expeditious superficial treatment to all types of soil. It is the best instrument for ridging potatoes and a host of other common tasks. The most commonly used hoe has a square blade (fig. 8), curved slightly inwards. Fig. 9. triangular-bladed hoe. Fig. 10. Two-tooth hoe. is fitted to a short handle, so that the worker who uses it can only work bent over, moving straight ahead, whereas when plowing with a spade, he can only work backwards. The hoe blade is triangular in shape (fig. 9), while the length and layout of the handle remain the same as for the square-bladed hoe. For digging up potatoes and other root vegetables, which you often risk cutting when digging with a spade, we recommend a two-tine flat hoe (fig. 10), which does the job very quickly and very well. The length and strength of the tines can be varied according to the resistance of the soil and the depth at which the instrument is to penetrate it. BINETTE. - The hoe or serfouette (fig. 11) is the tool par excellence for careful weeding and hoeing between rows of cultivated plants, without risking damaging them; the gardener should have several of different strength and size, but all composed of a blade on one side and two tines on the other, with a short socket through which the handle is passed, long enough for the worker to work without bending. Rare. - This instrument is to the gardener what the harrow is to the ploughman. There should be at least two of them, one with strong, pointed teeth long enough to give ploughed land the equivalent of a harrow, and the other with round teeth, obtuse at the end, used for clearing paths, trimming the surface of flowerbeds and lightly burying fine vegetable seeds. FORK. - The three-pronged fork, either straight or slightly curved inwards, is often used to even out the soil after a vegetable harvest, and to quickly give it a very superficial appearance. This means that the fork can be classed as a ploughing tool, although its most common use is to work with manure, especially to build up the beds, and also to remove weeds from garden weeding. Irrigation tools - Watering cans and portable pumps are the main tools used for watering gardens. WATERING CAN. - The shape of the old watering can, with its very rounded sides, has been more or less abandoned these days; the flat-sided watering can (fig. 12) is rightly preferred, as the worker can carry it without being forced to hold his arms outstretched and tense; this alone very-sensibly reduces the fatigue of watering. 2. The sheaf at the end of the watering can's neck can be removed Fig. 12. at will; the gardener must have several spare sheaves, Fig. 13. pierced with more or less fine holes; delicate plant seedlings cannot withstand watering with a sheaf pierced with large holes, which is the equivalent of a heavy rainstorm. For greenhouse service, we use watering cans with very long necks, which can be used to water pots in the upper rows of shelves, without having to move them or use the footboard. PORTATIVE PUMP (fig. 13). - Horticulture has several good models of portable pump, which can replace the use of watering cans for a large number of crops; they are especially useful for watering the surface of fruit trees in summer, when the foliage needs to be refreshed during long droughts. Transport tools. - Transport in the garden is carried out using two different instruments: the wheelbarrow and the stretcher. BROUETTE. - The common wheelbarrow is too well known to need describing. The gardener needs one lined with sheet metal and fitted with a hinged lid, for transporting liquid fertilizers, which are very useful in the vegetable garden. He also needs a wheelbarrow with long stretchers, no edges and a slatted bottom, for easy transport of doormats and bulky objects, which are more cumbersome than heavy. CIVIERE. - The most common type, with a double stretcher and requiring the help of two workers, has a slatted, rimless bottom and is very practical for transporting manure and removing produce from the vegetable garden, wherever a wheelbarrow cannot easily be used. The gardener also needs a hopper-shaped stretcher for transporting certain objects which, like potting soil, do not fit on the stretcher. Arboricultural tools. - The pruning, management and maintenance of fruit and ornamental trees require the use of a fairly large number of instruments, the main ones being: the pruning shears, secateurs, saws, grafting shears, pliers, crescents and weeders. SERPETTE (fig. 14). - Although the increasing use of pruning shears has considerably reduced the use of the pruning knife, this instrument is nonetheless indispensable to the gardener; many operations can only be carried out properly with the pruning knife; in particular, no other instrument can adequately repair the wounds resulting from the removal of large branches cut with the saw. There should be several different-sized pruning shears; the handles of the strongest ones, designed for pruning branches of a fairly large rig.14. diameter, should be fitted with a projecting edge that holds the instrument firmly in the workman's hand. It is important that the steel used for the crimper is of the highest quality; Rouen's crimper has a well-deserved reputation in this respect. SEWER (fig. 15). - The use of this instrument is so expeditious and convenient that, despite a few drawbacks noted at the outset, it has become universally used in Fig. 15. Secateurs. Fig. 16. Saw. Fig. 17. Grafting machine. gardens. Several sizes are required, the smallest of which are used for pruning roses and delicate shrubs. Some gardeners use secateurs armed with two long handles to prune high tree branches; it is better to use ordinary secateurs, and not to shy away from the need to use a double ladder; if you prune from too far away, you can't see what you're doing, and irreparable mistakes can result. SAWS (fig. 16). - Saws used by gardeners are fitted in such a way that they can always be used between the branches of overgrown trees, when they need 33 to be pruned or cut back on their main branches. Wherever the saw has passed, the pruning knife must pass in turn, so that the surface of the cut is left clean and smooth, as if the pruning had not been done with the saw. GRAFTING. - The various types of grafting require the use of a fairly large number of grafting tools, the most common of which is Fig. 18. Pliers. 2 Fig. 19. Pince. a knife with a rounded blade at the end, and a small ivory spatula on the lower part of the handle for lifting the bark of trees for scape grafting (fig. 17). (See Gardening operations, chap. V.) PINCE. - In recent years, gardeners have made frequent use of a variety of pliers, some suitable for pinching and disbudding fruit trees (fig. 18), others for making annular incisions (fig. 19) in the branches of trees too slow to set fruit. GROWING (fig. 20). - This instrument is used only in groves and large landscape gardens, for pruning large ornamental trees; the very long handle to which it is adapted makes it very convenient for this special purpose. Fig. 20. Crescent. Fig. 21. ladder pruner. Fig. 22. Ratissoire. SHEAR (fig. 21). - This instrument is used with advantage in spring, before the leaves develop, to remove the webs in which caterpillars have encased themselves for the winter. Its long handle gives access to the highest branches, without the need for a ladder. Various instruments. - The most useful tools for gardening, and which do not fit into any of the previous divisions, are the roller, rake, planter, rake and trowel. RATTISOIRE (fig. 22) - The two most common forms of the ratissoire, in constant use for keeping paths clean, allow you to use it either by bringing it to you, or by pushing it forward; it cuts the roots of weeds between two plots of land, preventing them from growing back in the paths. ROLLER (fig. 23). - To firm up the soil in the paths of Fig. 23. The cast-iron roller is a hollow cylinder with a handle that the worker pushes in front of him. It can be made heavier at will by loading the inside of the cylinder with stones. PLANTOIR. - The most practical planter is made of wood, with a crook-shaped handle. It is made more durable by wrapping an iron band around its tip, which is polished by use, so that the planter enters the hole with little effort and exits in the same way, without causing the edges to fall into the hole. The iron planter does not get stuck in wet soil like the non-iron planter, which is therefore much less convenient to use. CLaIE (fig. 24). - Gardeners often need to sift the soil, compost and potting compost they need to grow their crops. For this purpose, he uses a sieve fixed to a wooden frame and made up of rods set more or less close together. The rack is held in an inclined position by a support; the substances to be screened are thrown over it with a shovel; the most divided parts fall on one side, the largest on the other. Racks are more durable and provide better service when wooden rods are replaced by iron rods. Fig. 24. Rack. Fig. 25. Trowel. TRUELLE (fig. 25). - This instrument is used exclusively in the greenhouse to lay out the soil in the pots in which the plants are to live, and to open the holes in the tannage into which the pots are to be sunk (see Greenhouses, chap. VII). CHAPTER V Shelters and layers. Shelters proper. - Stone walls. - Brick. - Plank s. Palisades or windbreaks of Jerusalem artichokes. - Yew. - Thuja. - Italian poplar. - Doormats. - How to make them. - Cloths. - Their use as shelter. - Bells. - Verrines. - Cages. - Counter floors. - Frames. - Panels. - Boxes. - Sheet metal frames and chests. - Wooden floor under hot-water heated chests. - Economical frames. - Hot layers. - Stoves. - How to assemble layers. - Warm layers. - Elements of which they are composed. - Deaf layers. - Their distinctive features. - Pits for establishing them. - Duration of their heat. - Usefulness of muted layers. Most of the produce in our gardens is of foreign origin; we have borrowed it, some in modern times, others since the most remote antiquity, from climates further south than our own; there are some, and the best, which, in the average climate of France, can only vegetate in the open air from the time frosts cease in spring, until they begin again in autumn. As a result, for most of their vegetative life, these plants cannot do without shelter. In horticulture, the term "shelter" is used to refer to anything used to protect plants from the cold; in this sense, greenhouses of all kinds, to which the following chapter is devoted, are all shelters too. Shelters in the strict sense of the term are not only useful against the cold; they also protect certain delicate plants against the heat of the sun's rays and the destructive action of strong winds. The most commonly used are walls, palisades or windbreaks, doormats, canvas, ados or cótières, cloches, verrines, counterfloors, glazed châssts and layers. 3 Walls. - As we saw in the previous chapter, the height and method of construction of enclosing and dividing walls are the best of all shelters for fruit trees that are more or less sensitive to the cold. In countries where stone is in short supply and brick is expensive, board walls are erected for ospaliers, with the same useful effect as masonry walls. Peach trees and vines are trained on plank walls, and their produce reaches perfect ripeness in the islands of Zeeland, where the climate is much more unfavorable than in northern France. These walls, running from north-east to south-west, are, like most garden enclosure walls, 2.50 m high; their tree-lined surface is plain; the other surface is supported from distance to distance by structural buttresses. Although they are built of white wood, the layers of tar that are carefully applied every year make them quite durable. Palisades or windbreaks. - Cold and temperate greenhouse plants that spend the summer in the open air would frequently be knocked over and damaged by the winds, if they were not secured in some way. On the other hand, seedlings planted during the summer would be destroyed by drought without the protection they receive from palisades or windbreaks, a type of shelter that is absolutely essential in a large garden. The simplest of these consist of one or two rows of Jerusalem artichokes. The very rapid growth of this plant and the extent of its abundant foliage make it very suitable for use as a windbreak, while still allowing a weakened ray of sunlight to pass through from distance. Hedges of yew, cedar and Italian poplar are planted permanently for the same purpose; these should not be kept beyond 6 to 7 years. Doormats. - In gardens, excellent diminutive forms of espalier are improvised by planting lines of stakes about a metre high above the ground in an east-west direction, from which doormats facing south are suspended. These temporary shelters, which are removed when no longer needed, hasten the growth of plants sown in trays and transplanted in the open air when outside temperatures permit. Gardeners need straw mats all the time, not only for this purpose, but also to protect seedlings covered with branches from the cold. The straw mats should be placed on these seedlings during the night, when a bit of cold is likely to occur. Greenhouse frames, tarpaulins and windows are also covered with doormats to keep out the cold. This multiplicity of uses for doormats means that the gardener must always have a fairly large quantity at his disposal. It would be difficult for him to buy them for money, which would be very expensive, so he usually makes them himself using the following process. The top of a large table, or a door taken off its hinges, is placed by means of a support under one of its ends, in a slightly inclined position. Three hooked nails, in a line, at equal distances from each other, are fixed to each end of this surface; thick strings are stretched tightly over these nails. Once this has been done, a layer of rye straw is spread as evenly as possible over the table. Care must be taken to alternately take a handful on one side of the cob and one on the opposite side, otherwise the mat will be too thin on one edge and too thick on the other. A shuttle is then loaded with strong but fairly thin twine, and passed along the three lines of stretched twine, each time taking a small quantity of straw of equal thickness, which is secured with a slipknot. The straw mat, made to the length of the board being worked on, is rolled back from the board and continued indefinitely by the same process, until it reaches the desired length. This very light work is easily performed by women and children during the forced leisure of the worst days of the bad season, when outside work is interrupted. Canvas. - In the north of France, when espaliered trees are in bloom, canvases are hung from the coping of the wall. These canvases can be very clear, provided they are strong enough; large canvas is usually used for this purpose, not only to protect peach and apricot blossoms from the cold, but also to prevent these blossoms, the hope of the harvest, from being destroyed by hail, which is quite common in April. Ados. - Ados or "cótières" are simply flowerbeds laid out on slopes facing south. Many very low plants, especially winter lettuces, kale and seedlings of plants to be transplanted in spring, are sufficiently sheltered here from the east and north winds, so that they overwinter very well on ados, without any other protection. Bells (fig. 26). - Glass cloches are one of the most common forms of shelter used in vegetable Fig. 26. cultivation; they are laid flat on the ground when the plants they shelter can do without air. When the temperature is milder, the edge of the bell is raised towards the south to let in a little air. On cold nights, long litter or straw mats are thrown over the cloches to provide extra protection for delicate plants. Glass jars (fig. 27). - The use of glass jars has been virtually abandoned in the garden, due to their high price; they are cloches made of glass compartments joined by lead strips. Their greatest advantage over one-piece glass cloches is their solidity, and consequently their durability. Verrines usually have two compartFig. 27.ments on opposite sides of the top. Verrines. hinged to give air to the plants housed in the verrine. Cages (fig. 28). - Under the name of cages, gardeners use cylinders made of wickerwork, fitted with pointed legs that are driven into the ground Fig. 28. Cages. Fig. 29. Counterfloors. so that the cage cannot be blown over by the wind. This kind of shelter is very useful for many delicate plants during the first period of their growth; as soon as these plants, accustomed to contact with the air, have become sufficiently robust, the cages are removed. Counterfloors (fig. 29). - We mention counter-soils here, even though they are rarely used in most gardens, despite their undeniable usefulness. These are large flower pots cut in half lengthways. They are placed behind certain plants, either on the south side to protect them from the sun, or in the direction of the wind, the violence of which can be harmful to delicate, very watery plants, when the bedded plant is transplanted into the ground. These include cucumbers, gherkins, squash, pumpkins and all cultivated plants of the Cucurbitaceae family; no other type of shelter ensures the recovery of these plants better than counter-soils. Counter-soils are available at very low cost from all horticultural pottery factories. Frames (fig. 30). - Frames are widely used in horticulture Fig. 30. Chassis. in horticulture, consist of two distinct parts: the box and the chassis itself. The trunk, designed to support the frame, is usually made of wood; its length varies at will; nevertheless, it rarely exceeds four meters, a length sufficient for three frames or panels, according to the expression received among Parisian gardeners. The width is only one meter when the box is to contain a warm layer for forced cultivation of early vegetables; it is one meter thirty centimeters for warm or dull layers. The rear part of the frame is always a little higher than the front, so that the frame is not laid horizontally, but has a certain slope to the front. The difference in height varies from eight to ten centimetres, depending on the height of the plants to be grown under this type of shelter. Chests are usually made of oak wood, well tarred on the inside and painted with several coats of oil on the outside, to make them more durable. They are also made of sheet iron, of the dimensions indicated above, for the same purposes. When artificial heat is to be produced inside the chest by a layer of fermenting manure, it rests on the ground, into which even its lower edges enter by one or two centimeters; when this heat is to be produced by the pipes of a thermosiphon, the chest rests on a wooden floor under which the heat pipes pass. (See Thermosiphon, chap. VI.) The frames that are placed over the chests to concentrate heat internally for the benefit of plants raised in forced cultivation, are most often covered with panes of glass laid one on top of the other. It's important that the glass is both transparent and thick enough to withstand the various chances of breakage, which can make maintenance very costly. A rack-and-pinion support allows the frames to be lifted to varying degrees, either to clean the glass panes or to allow free access to the outside air. In addition, each frame is fitted with two iron handles for moving and replacing it as required. Gardening enthusiasts whose means do not allow them to use glazed frames supported by oak or sheet-iron casings, can substitute inexpensive chássis, consisting of white wooden casings surmounted by simple rough wooden frames on which is stretched a canvas coated with linseed oil, or even oiled paper. Forced cultivation of the most delicate plants is undoubtedly not as successful here as under good glass frames, but almost all the most common types of forced cultivation are practicable with advantage and at very little cost, especially since, with a little skill and activity, anyone in the countryside can make economical frames for his garden, whereas ordinary frames can only be made by professional carpenters or sheet-metal workers. The frames, whatever they may be, are made very durable by the twofold precaution of giving them a heavy coat of paint every year in spring, and storing them in the shelter of a shed for as long as they are not needed. Layers. - Layers are made up of fermenting manure, either on its own or combined with dead leaves and moss to produce artificial heat of varying intensity and duration. As the manure and other fermentable elements used to build the layers are not directly used to feed the plants grown in layers, they are covered with a more or less thick bed of potting soil, or a mixture of potting soil and garden soil in various proportions. It is on this bed, more or less heated by the fermentation of the layer, that the forced plants vegetate. Depending on the degree of heat they are intended to provide, layers are warm, lukewarm or muted. Hot layers. - This type of bed can only be built with horse manure at the height of its fermentation. If you have stockpiled horse manure with a view to using it when the layers are to be erected, you must take care to keep it in loosely packed heaps, through which long poles are passed from distance to distance, lifting the mass so that the air can penetrate and the heaps dry out inside. Horse manure possesses, above all others, the precious property of interrupting fermentation when dry, and resuming it when wet. So, when you want to build up the layers, you break up the heaps and wet the manure you're using without delay; fermentation sets in immediately: it's very active, but doesn't last long. For this reason, we take care to make the hot layers higher than they are wide. When their heat begins to wane, reheaters are used to revive it. This is the name given to the belt of recent manure that surrounds a warm layer that has cooled halfway, warming it up for a while. In market gardening, where a large number of layers are used, they are placed close together, so that the new manure stoves occupy the service paths, thus producing all their useful effect without causing any inconvenience. To build up a hot layer properly, you must first mix all the parts of the manure with a fork; if this mixing is not done carefully enough, the fermentation of the different parts of the layer will be very uneven. Many gardeners use almost dry manure to build up the beds, which they wet and trample when the bed is fully erected. But it is much better to water the manure before using it; this ensures that it is more completely and evenly moistened, which contributes to the even fermentation of the bed. With these arrangements in place, the bed is marked out by four stakes, over which cords are stretched to ensure that the edges of the bed are perfectly straight. The layer is then built up, starting at one end, and the gardener works backwards to the opposite end; he takes care to strike the manure with the back of the fork as his work progresses, so that the mass is very evenly packed. Warm layers. - These are usually built up with half manure, half recently fallen leaves, fresh moss or other vegetable substances capable of providing gentle, long-lasting heat. These are mixed well and moistened to form a layer, as described for warm layers. Deaf layers. - To build up a deaf layer, a trench the length and width of the layer is opened in the ground, to a depth of 30 centimeters. Either the manure alone, or a mixture of manure, leaves and moss, is piled into this pit to produce the warmth of the layer. This heat must last a long time; but once exhausted, it cannot be 3. renewed by means of the stoves that this kind of bed does not allow; so we only start to build up deaf beds as spring approaches, to be able to transplant plants that have started to grow on other hot or deaf beds, during the bad season. Instead of potting soil, the surface of the deaf layer is covered with a thick bed of good soil taken from the pit opened to house it. Like the others, it is then covered with a glass frame supported by a box, to concentrate the gentle heat that is maintained in this type of layer for a very long time. CHAPTER VI Orangeries and greenhouses. The origins of greenhouses in Europe. - Orangeries. - What distinguishes it from the cold greenhouse. - Why they are no longer built. - Interior layout. - Shelves. - Stoves. - Cold greenhouse. - Flemish greenhouse. - Dutch greenhouse. - Camellia greenhouse, - Pelargonium greenhouse, - Calceolar greenhouse. - Winter garden. - Cold frame. - Temperate greenhouse. - Heating system. - Thermosiphon. - Its advantages. - Ventilation. - Reservoir. - Living room greenhouse. - Warm greenhouse. - Interior divisions. - Vestibule. - Aquarium. - Forcing greenhouse. - Multiplication greenhouse. - Greenhouse shading. - Mobile greenhouse. - Placed in front of espaliered trees. - Heated portable greenhouse. - Unheated. - Apartment aquarium. The need to give a large number of plants, originating in the hottest regions of the globe, an artificial climate as similar as possible to the natural climate of their native land gave rise to greenhouses, the first of which were built, it is said, in the 15th century, for the botanical garden of the University of Padua. Since then, this type of construction has continued to proliferate, and today there is no country house of any comfort that is not accompanied by the obligatory accessory of a greenhouse. Greenhouses, according to their purpose, are classified in the following order: Orangeries. - Cold greenhouses. - Temperate greenhouses. - Warm greenhouses. - Aquarium. - Forcing greenhouses. - Multiplication greenhouses. To this list should be added miniature greenhouses and aquariums, which can easily be placed on a pedestal table and serve as the most pleasant part of home horticulture. Orangery (fig. 31). - The orangery differs from the greenhouse in one essential respect: the greenhouse, whatever its purpose, is covered by a glass roof; the only materials used in its construction are wood or iron on the one hand, and glass on the other; the back wall against which it is usuallyHoSueao1831 leaned is the only masonry; the plants grown there therefore receive light from all directions. The orangery, on the other hand, is a masonry structure with large windows on the south side, covered with a roof of tiles, slate, zinc or lead, just like a residential building. The plants receive light from one side only. This alone makes the orangery much less useful than the cold greenhouse; so orangeries are hardly ever built any more, even though we still make use of those built in large numbers last century; for the same expense, we prefer a cold greenhouse, where we can grow a greater variety of ornamental plants. When the orangery is intended, as its name indicates, to house mainly orange trees and other trees of the same temperament, such as pomegranates, myrtles and oleanders used to decorate large gardens in summer, it must be fairly large. The back wall, as well as the front, faces south; the door, very high to facilitate the entry and exit of large shrubs grown in boxes, is open on the sunset side. A ceiling, usually as high as the top of the door, covers the entire interior of the orangery, which is thus surmounted by a vast attic. In winter, this ceiling prevents the cold resulting from snow on the roof from penetrating into the interior of the orangery. The health of the plants, trees and shrubs that overwinter in the orangery is not compromised as long as the inside temperature doesn't drop below zero; consequently, there's no need to install a permanent heating appliance, just a stove that's lit only when it's very cold; it makes just enough fire to prevent the orangery from freezing; it's dismantled in summer. The front facade of the orangery should be pierced by high, wide windows; the best orangeries are those where all the windows touch. The interior dimensions of the orangery can be varied at will; as it receives light only through the windows on its front facade, it must not be too deep, otherwise the last row of plants placed along the back wall, being too far from the windows, would suffer from lack of daylight and air, which would cause the plants to lose much of their foliage, and could even compromise their existence. The thicker the walls of the orangery, the harsher the local climate; when only large shrubs are to be wintered in crates, the stove and pipes are all that's needed. If the orangery is also to house a variety of small plants, grown in pots, shelves are placed in front of the windows, on which the pots are arranged so as to be as close as possible to the light. These shelves should never be so high as to intercept too completely the air and light that the large shrubs placed behind them, between the shelves and the back wall, demand their fair share of. Cold greenhouse (fig. 32). - English horticulturists call this type of greenhouse conservatory; this term expresses very well the purpose of the cold greenhouse, where the gardener houses a very wide variety of ornamental plants, with the sole aim of preserving them. Among these plants, some naturally suspend their vegetation in winter, without losing their leaves; others experience a complete vegetal sleep; others, finally, are naturally disposed to vegetate without stopping, winter being unknown in their native climate. This last series of plants is the most difficult to preserve in the cold greenhouse; it can only be achieved by slowing down the movement of their vegetation. In the cold greenhouse, as in the orangery, it's always warm enough when it's not freezing; the stove should only be turned on when absolutely necessary. The cold greenhouse is built with one or two slopes, with a flat or curved surface. Buildings known as Flemish greenhouses (fig. 33), Dutch greenhouses, camellia greenhouses, pelargonium greenhouses and calceolaria greenhouses are nothing more than cold greenhouses, with no distinguishing features. In order to keep the fire as late and as short as possible, when the cold greenhouse is double-sided, the north side is covered with a thick layer of long litter or leaves, held in place by a blanket of thin white wooden boards; the south side, during light frosts, is covered only with straw mats that are Fig. 32. Cold greenhouse. Fig. 33. Flemish greenhouse. a som d'enlever dès que la température adoucit; car les plantes de serre froide souffrent plus en hiver du manque de lumière que des atteintes d'un froid modéré. THE WINTER GARDEN (fig. 34) is yet another variety of the greenhouse Fig. 34. Cold winter garden, very popular today. It's a two-sided greenhouse, spacious enough for pathways and rustic seating. Orangery and cold greenhouse plants, trees 1 and shrubs, especially camellias, thrive here all year round in the open ground; a basin fed by a trickle of running water should be considered an indispensable accessory to the winter garden. THE COLD CHASSIS is the cold greenhouse reduced to its simplest form; it has the shape of an ordinary frame; only, the plank box supporting the glazing is two meters high at the back and one meter sixty centimeters high at the front. A sturdy rack supports the upright panels when the gardener has to work inside the cold frame. Most of the time, as its dimensions remain the same, it is sunk forty to fifty centimetres into the ground. When the cold is severe, leaves, litter and straw mats piled up around the cold frame, and temporarily spread over its windows, prevent frost from penetrating. All orangery and cold greenhouse plants can overwinter under the cold frame. Temperate greenhouse. - The temperate greenhouse is the most useful of all in horticulture. Its construction differs in no way from that of the cold greenhouse, but it allows the cultivation of a far greater variety of plants borrowed from the flora of tropical regions. These plants spend only a short period of the summer in the open air; for the rest of the year, they need artificial heat. The various heating methods previously used in temperate greenhouses have all given way to the thermosiphon, whose superiority cannot be disputed. The apparatus known as the thermosiphon consists of a hermetically sealed boiler, under which fire is lit at will; the boiler and its hearth are usually placed outside the temperate greenhouse, either in an insulated compartment, or in a cellar built underneath for this purpose. Cast-iron or copper pipes (the latter being the best) run from the boiler around the greenhouse one or more times before re-entering the boiler at a point on its lateral surface lower than their starting point; like the boiler, these pipes are filled with water and hermetically sealed. When the fire is lit under the boiler, the portion of water in contact with the heated wall immediately heats up, making it lighter than the rest of the liquid mass; currents of hot water rise and cold water fall, until all the water contained either in the pipes or in the boiler has reached the same temperature. (Thermosiphon, fig. 35). The advantages of heating greenhouses with hot water, using Fig. 35. Thermosiphon. are obvious. If, for example, a temperate greenhouse is heated by ordinary heat pipes, from a fireplace fed by wood or coal combustion, the gardener must, during frosts, stay up day and night to maintain the fire, and he succeeds only with difficulty in maintaining that equality of temperature which is the first condition of success for the cultivation of tropical plants. Whether he falls asleep or lacks vigilance, his plants can be burnt out by too much heat, or destroyed by frost; for, with this heating system, an hour after the fire is extinguished, it freezes in the greenhouse to the same degree as outside. If there's a crack in one of the heat pipes, smoke is introduced into the greenhouse; then, to repair the accident, the mason has to be called in; and smoke and the mason are no less to be feared than frost for greenhouse-grown plants. With the thermosiphon, there are no heatstrokes to worry about; the temperature equals itself; there's never any smoke or masons in the greenhouse: the gardener can go to bed and get a good night's sleep; several hours will pass after the fire is extinguished under the thermosiphon boiler, without the temperature inside the greenhouse dropping in a way that's dangerous for the plants. Air renewal in the temperate greenhouse is achieved by means of hinged windows that can be opened and closed at will, at the top of the roof; these openings are used to let warm air out; outside air is introduced through pipes communicating with the outside; When the air enters the tempered greenhouse, it is sufficiently warmed by contact between the air pipes and the pipes filled with hot water; in this way, the best possible ventilation is obtained, without sudden cooling. Water for temperate greenhouse plants must be brought to greenhouse temperature before use. A stone reservoir, fed by a trickle of water, is built into the temperate greenhouse for this purpose; goldfish are kept there to feed on the microscopic animalcules in the water, preventing it from becoming corrupted. The temperate greenhouse is often built on the same level as the living room on the first floor of a country house, in which case it is called a greenhouse-lounge (fig. 36). Plants are arranged on shelves, which are placed to the right and left on days when the greenhouse is to be used as a living room. A carpet is laid out on the space left free in the middle of the greenhouse; candle-laden chandeliers hang from its top; the plants do not suffer appreciably, provided that the days of reception do not recur at too close intervals; for the influx of visitors, the stale air and the burning of the candles always do them more or less harm. hot greenhouse. - The construction of the hot greenhouse, its heating system, reservoir and ventilation are the same as for the temperate greenhouse. As the temperature is constantly very high, it would be dangerous for the gardener to go from this temperature to the outside temperature without any transition. The gardener hangs up his jacket before entering, and takes it off again before leaving. Not all greenhouse plants require the same degree of dry or humid heat; in order to be able to give each series of these plants the temperature that suits it best, the greenhouse is divided into several compartments by glass partitions; the gardener modifies the interior temperature of each compartment as required. Aquariun (fig. 37).- In recent years, the number of very fine tropical aquatic plants introduced into Europe has increased to such an extent that greenhouses have been purpose-built for this particular type of cultivation.
