Inside these greenhouses is a basin full of water constantly maintained at the desired temperature; all around this type of greenhouse, for which the new term of aquarium has been coined, is a bed in which the most beautiful ornamental plants for hot greenhouses are cultivated, chosen from among those that need an atmosphere that is both very hot and very humid. Forced greenhouses. - The forcing greenhouse is a temperate or warm greenhouse, dedicated entirely 3 Fig. 37. Aquarium. to force either ornamental plants to flower before their natural blooming time, or fruit trees to bear fruit at a time when the same trees grown in the open air have only flowers. The construction and interior layout of the forcing greenhouse do not differ from those of other warm or temperate greenhouses. Multiplication greenhouse. - This is an ordinary cold greenhouse, usually built on northern or sunset exposures, so that greenhouse plants propagated by grafting or cuttings are never exposed to direct contact with the sun's rays, which would be essentially harmful to them. How to shade greenhouses. - All the greenhouses described above, with the exception of the propagation greenhouse where the sun never shines, need to be protected by various means from the scorching heat that could cause irreparable damage in summer. In England, glass with an uneven surface is widely used to break the sun's rays, thus weakening their action. In France, when the aim is to reduce the intensity of the sun's rays without excluding them altogether, a slightly thicker whitewash is smeared on the inside surface of glazing facing south. To shade the inside of greenhouses more completely, cloth or straw mats are laid over them. The latter are not only awkward to handle, but also cast too thick a shadow. In Belgium, greenhouses are frequently shaded with thin slats held together with wire, which can be rolled and unrolled as required, like doormats. These slats leave small gaps between them through which enough light can pass, although sunburn is perfectly prevented. Mobile greenhouses (fig. 38). - These are glazed frames attached to the coping of walls planted with espaliered trees, especially peach trees and vines. These frames are tilted forward so as to leave a free space of 1 meter and 30 centimeters at ground level between them and the wall. The bottom of the frames rests on a plank placed on the field, sunk a few centimetres into the ground, and held downFig. 38. Mobile greenhouse. dement by two rows of stakes, one inside, the other outside. At both

At the ends of the wall temporarily sheltered beneath the mobile greenhouse's frames, two triangular plank partitions intercept all communication with the outside world. A stove with a long pipe, or better still, a thermosiphon, is set up at one end of the mobile greenhouse, heating the interior; this is a safe and inexpensive way of forcing espaliered fruit trees. The mobile greenhouse is set up in February in the Paris climate; it is dismantled once the forced fruit has been harvested. Portable greenhouse (fig. 39). - Horticultural enthusiasts who are kept at home by sedentary occupations in the cities can find great pleasure in multiplying cold and temperate greenhouse ornamental plants by sowing, cutting or grafting in a portable greenhouse. If we limit ourselves to the cultivation of those plants for which the ordinary temperature of an inhabited apartment is sufficient, there is no need to adapt a heating appliance; for those requiring a little more heat, we use a portable greenhouse equipped with a reservoir full of water (fig. 40), under which we place a lighted lamp; the flame of the lamp gently heats the water in the reservoir, which communicates its temperature to the interior of the portable greenhouse. THE GARDENER Fig. 39. Unheated portable greenhouse. O 6 0 Fig. 40. Heated portable greenhouse. Apartment aquarium (fig. 41). - The predilection for ornamental aquatic plants on the part of a large number of enthusiasts has led to the invention of the home aquarium. This charming device can only be set up on the Romonedde p anpenbv 'iy 81" first floor, on a table with four columns, through one of which passes a lead pipe designed to carry the water into the aquarium, itself supported by four copper columns, one of which contains the upper end of the same pipe, while another is pierced by a hole through which the same quantity of water escapes, the aquarium being fed by a continuous stream of running water. In addition to small ornamental aquatic plants, the home aquarium can be used to propagate sticklebacks, small fish with highly unusual habits, and various freshwater shellfish whose development phases are extremely interesting to study. The home aquarium can contain a fairly varied assortment of aquatic plants, without the need for any special heating equipment. The growing popularity of the home aquarium has led many wealthy hobbyists to replace freshwater with seawater, either natural or artificial. This water, because of its salt content, does not corrode, and does not need to be constantly renewed, as is the case with freshwater aquariums. The seawater aquarium takes the form of a square box, with sides made of two-way glass joined by a solid putty. The bottom is lined with pebbles, flat, rounded stones collected from the seashore, to which we add a few rocks and a little sea sand. In this aquarium, in addition to seaweed, kelp, eelgrass and a host of very interesting marine vegetation, you can breed seahorses, various shellfish and a fairly large number of marine animals and small species of sea fish, whose movements make for interesting observations. To facilitate these observations, the aquarium can be placed near a false window, whose frame is replaced by a permanently fixed one-way glass. As the light from outside passes through the aquarium, all parts of the water are constantly illuminated, and nothing that happens there can escape the observer's notice. CHAPTER VII Gardening operations. Gardening operations. - Plowing; - Spading. - Method of execution. - Gauging. - Burying manure. - Ploughing with the square hoe, - With the triangular hoe, - With the two-tooth hoe, - Under trees in the grove. - Ripping. - How they differ from plowing. - Hoeing and weeding. - Watering. - Irrigation. - How it differs from watering. - Watering in the greenhouse. - Age at which plants most need watering. - Mulch. - Its useful effect - Buried barrels. - Roman cement basin. - Plant propagation. - Sowing and planting. - Sowing in the field - Nurseries. - Stratified seeds - Tuber planting. - Transplanting herbaceous plants - Trees and shrubs in the nursery. It is of the utmost importance to the gardener that all operations within the scope of his profession are well executed; acquiring the greatest practical skill in each of them is the study of a lifetime. The most important of these operations are: ploughing, ripping, hoeing and weeding, watering and the various procedures for multiplying plants by sowing, planting, cutting, layering and grafting. Ploughing. - Most garden ploughing is done with a spade, which should be to the gardener what the plough is to the ploughman. Ploughing with a spade always begins with the opening of a pit at one end of the bed to be ploughed; the depth of this pit is usually equal to the length of a spade head; it is adjusted, moreover, according to the greater or lesser thickness of the layer of topsoil. The soil extracted from the pit is carried to the end of the plank, where it remains in reserve to fill the pit when the worker, working backwards, has finished his task. Gardeners give this pit the name of "gauge"; they say, in praise of a worker who spades carefully, without sparing any effort: "We'd bury him in his gauge". The gauge must be the same width and depth throughout the ploughing operation. If the purpose of the operation is to bury manure, the gardener takes care that the manure is distributed very evenly everywhere, and that it is placed between two plots of land, only 5 or 6 centimetres from the surface; if it were buried too deeply, the roots of the cultivated plants would not benefit from it. When the soil is strong rather than light, care should be taken to cut and divide it with the edge of the spade as you move it, so that it is in the most perfect state of loosening possible. There is, however, one exception to this rule: if, at the onset of winter, you plough those parts of the garden that are not occupied by perennial or biennial vegetables, you lift the soil in large blocks and place them upright on the ground, without dividing them, so that, during the winter, the alternating frosts and thaws ripen and crumble them, giving the soil a very good preparation for spring crops. Garden soil is not always ploughed with a spade; in the vegetable garden, the gardener makes frequent use of the square- or triangular-bladed hoe, with which he quickly makes superficial cultivations that are sufficient to prepare the soil when one crop is to follow another; in the tree and shrub beds of the landscape garden, the spade would not work properly because of the roots its cutting edge would encounter in the ground; this part of the garden can only be shaped with the two-toothed hoe, which works between the roots of trees and shrubs without damaging them. Ripping. - Ripping is ploughing to twice the depth of ordinary ploughing. Gardeners only break up the ground when they want to create a garden on a piece of land previously occupied by other crops, or when they need to plant fruit or ornamental trees. The work is carried out in the same way as ploughing, by opening a pit at one end of the plot measuring between om.80 and 1 metre, from which the soil is carried to the other end of the room. When the subsoil is of a poor nature, care is taken not to mix it with the surface layer, which is laid out in strips, so that it can be distributed evenly throughout the area when the trenching is complete. During the operation, stones and roots of perennial weeds are removed, as well as any pieces of dead wood the subsoil may contain; this last point is extremely important, as the underground fungi that grow on rotten wood can spread to the roots of trees and shrubs and cause them to rot. Hoeing and weeding. - These methods, the most superficial of all, are carried out using the hoe or hoeing tool, carefully loosening the soil between cultivated plants. The less water you can give the different divisions of the garden, the more necessary it is to give them frequent hoeing, which prevents the soil from closing up and leaves it in the best conditions for the plants to take full advantage of the night dew. When the garden has been well tended for a long time, weeds only appear if their seeds are accidentally carried by the wind or birds, or if they are mixed with manure without losing their germinative properties. Deep ploughing in autumn and spring, shallow ploughing in summer, and repeated hoeing are enough to keep the garden perfectly clean. If the soil is infested with the roots of bad perennials, they should be pulled out by hand as they grow, and the roots cut off between two plots of land with a fairly long knife; this frequently repeated weeding and the contact of fresh manure will eventually remove all the bad perennials that garden cultivation soon gets rid of. Watering. - Watering is one of the most important gardening 4. operations, except in cantons near the coast and river mouths, where it rains almost every day. In the south of France, evaporation caused by the heat of the climate is so rapid that gardening is impossible wherever irrigation is not possible. Irrigation differs from watering in that it involves running water through gutters onto garden beds levelled for the purpose, and saturating them with water by soaking. This is done on a large scale in the gardens of Cavaillon, Pézénas and around Perpignan. In the climate of Paris and central France, watering is done using a watering can. All garden plants need plenty of water from spring to autumn. Cabbages, cauliflowers and other plants whose foliage doesn't need wetting are watered with the nozzle of the watering can, without the sheaf; all others receive water in the form of rain. In principle, you should water very sparingly, or not at all. When plants, tired by drought and the heat of the day, receive only a small quantity of water, which is quickly dissipated by evaporation, watering does them more harm than good; you should, as the gardeners around Paris say, wet thoroughly, or refrain from watering; but, in this case, you run a great risk of harvesting nothing. In the greenhouse, watering can only be done with a watering can without a spout, with a very long spout; not all plants need the same quantity of water; each one needs to be watered separately, according to its temperament and the more or less advanced state of its vegetation, which would become impossible if we watered all the potted plants arranged on the shelves of a greenhouse in the form of rain. When a plant's foliage needs refreshing, we take the pot outside and hold it in an inclined position, while the spray from the watering can gives the plant a fine shower of rain, without saturating the soil in the pot with excess moisture which is harmful to the plant's roots. In the open ground, it's during the first period of their growth that plants need the most water; some, however, including strawberries and pumpkins, want to be saturated with water while they're bearing flowers and fruit. For most vegetable plants, it's a good idea to cover the beds with a good mulch of long litter, both to slow down the drying-out process by evaporation, and to prevent watering from beating too hard on the surface of the soil, hardening it, or, as the saying goes, plummeting the earth. In gardens that are not blessed with a pond intake, or the proximity of a stream, it is necessary to establish a system of underground pipes, usually made of terracotta, through which water from a well or pump is brought into barrels buried flush with the ground, so that it is available at every point in the garden, within easy reach of the watering can. In recent years, many gardens have replaced barrels with circular basins, deeper than they are wide, made of bricks laid on the ground and impregnated with Roman cement; these basins are frost-resistant, more durable than barrels and more useful in every way. Plant propagation. - This operation, the main aim of all horticulture, involves four distinct processes; cultivated plants can be multiplied by sowing and planting, by cutting, by marcotting and by grafting. Sowing and planting. - Sowing requires special study and care on the part of the gardener; no two plants need to be sown in exactly the same way. Large, hard-barked seeds need to be sown deeply, otherwise drought would reach them and they would not germinate; others need to be covered only with a thin layer of soil or pulverized compost; all very fine seeds are in this case; the smallest ones are only spread on the ground, where they germinate without being buried. If we were to follow nature's way of doing things, we would never cover the seeds, because natural seedlings are never covered; but nature lavishes seeds, most of which are destined to perish without being used to propagate plants: the gardener, on the other hand, only sows in the hope that each seed will produce a plant. Plants that are difficult to transplant, or which can produce the desired results without being transplanted, are sown in situ. In-place sowing can be done in rows or broadcast; sowing in rows, in gullies traced in advance to a depth proportionate to the volume of seeds, makes weeding and hoeing easier, and allows plants to be spaced evenly from one another, which never happens with broadcast sowing. Plants that only produce after one or more transplants are sown in nurseries. Sowing is very important in fruit and ornamental tree nurseries. All large seeds, such as kernels, walnuts, chestnuts, acorns and dwarfs, need to be stratified in order to survive the winter. Stratification consists of placing the seeds in alternate beds, in fresh earth or sand, either in the cellar or at the foot of a south-facing wall, at a depth of 30 to 40 centimetres, so that frost cannot reach them. As soon as the first fine days of late winter arrive, with a few moderate waterings, the seeds germinate; they are then picked up one by one, carefully so as not to damage the radicle, and planted in rows in suitably spaced gutters. Larch seeds, covered with a very hard envelope, need to be kept for a day or two in heaps which are moistened so that they warm up by a fermentation movement which, if pushed too far, would cause the germs to perish, but which, if stopped in time, favors their development; they are then sown either in rows or broadcast. Transplanting is the name given to any transplant that is not definitive. In the vegetable garden, for example, cauliflower seedlings obtained from sowing are transplanted once or twice; the purpose of transplanting is to make the seedling more robust and to hasten the formation of its head; it is only definitively planted when, through transplanting, it has developed sufficiently. Plants which, like the dahlia in the parterre, and the potato in the vegetable garden, do not usually multiply by sowing their seeds, are propagated by dividing their tubers; they are planted, not sown. The depth at which they should be buried varies according to their volume. When transplanting nursery-raised seedlings, care must be taken to ensure that the roots are not folded back on themselves in the hole opened by the planter, which can happen when the worker is not sufficiently attentive to his task. The transplanting of seedling trees and shrubs, while waiting for the moment when they can be grafted, requires great care; the future of young trees depends on it. (See chap. XXIX, 3rd part.) CHAPTER VIII Cuttings and marcottes. Cuttings. - Rapid propagation. - Open air cuttings. - Cutting by planting. - Trees that propagate by planting. - Simple cuttings. - Crossette cuttings. - Bead cuttings. - Incision or ligation to create the bead. - Cutting under a bell or under a frame. - Applicable to coniferous and resinous trees. - Bengal and Chinese roses. - Greenhouse cuttings. - Suitable soils. - Pure sand. - Cutting pots and terrines. - Oleander cuttings in water. - Cuttings from leaves and leaf portions. - Favorable times for cuttings in the greenhouse. - Time required for cuttings to root. - Single cuttings. - By cepée. - By incision. - By amputation. - In hanging pots or cups. Propagating plants by cuttings and marcotte are the two operations that offer the gardener the most resources for propagating useful or ornamental plants, trees and shrubs; they are also those that, as a form of recreation, are the source of the most varied pleasures for the amateur gardener. Cuttings. - To cut a plant is to place a portion of it - a branch, a section of stem or root, a whole leaf or just a fragment of leaf - in such conditions that roots develop and a new plant results. It is one of the most striking advances of modern horticulture to have succeeded in multiplying by cuttings almost all the plants grown in gardens and greenhouses. With the exception of a small number of plants that have hitherto refused to take cuttings, all the others, when placed in the right conditions, can take root, or, as the saying goes, take cuttings again. Recent advances in the art of taking cuttings are, to put it mildly, the most effective way of making foreign plants readily available at low cost soon after their introduction. The greater or lesser ease with which the gardener multiplies a new plant by taking cuttings determines its value; if it can be cut easily, it soon ceases to be rare; its price, very high in principle, falls to the most moderate rate. The many and varied methods of taking cuttings naturally fall into three divisions: cuttings taken in the open air, cuttings taken under a bell or under a frame, and cuttings taken in a greenhouse. CUTTINGS TAKEN IN THE OPEN AIR. - This procedure is mainly used for trees and shrubs that are easy to regrow, and which for this reason are only multiplied by sowing their seeds when new sub-varieties are expected. The hardiest trees, such as poplars and various willow species, can be cuttings in place, preferably in cool, light rather than strong soil, and at the water's edge. For cuttings, we prepare twigs that can be from 1 metre 50 centimetres to 2 metres long; we give this type of cutting the special name of plancon. The lower end of the plank, cut to a point, is placed in the ground in a hole drilled with an iron bar; the lateral shoots at the bottom of the plank are pruned, leaving those at the top; a sturdy stake, to which the plank is attached by one or two ties of wicker or twisted straw, prevents it from being blown over by the wind. The activity of the sap in trees usually propagated by cuttings soon leads to the formation, at the bottom, of a bead from which young roots grow, and the cutting becomes a fully-formed tree. SiMple BoUrureS. - Various softwood shrubs, especially redcurrants, both those cultivated for their fruit and ornamental varieties, can be taken from cuttings without any preparation; a portion of two-year-old twig or from the previous year is stuck into the ground, a few centimetres deep, leaving two or three good eyes above ground; these cuttings can all be taken at the gardener's discretion; they are the simplest of all cuttings. CROSSETTE cuttings. - For other trees and shrubs, see Fig. 42. Crossette cuttings. vine, among others, root with greater certainty when the one-year-old shoot, used as a cutting, is accompanied at the bottom by a portion of two-year-old wood, forming a sort of hook; this is what gardeners and winegrowers call a crossette (fig. 42). Crossette cuttings are not planted straight, or at a slight angle, like ev plançon cuttings and simple cuttings; they are placed horizontally, a few centimetres below ground level, with the top end, which has one or two good eyes, straightened out to the outside. In this case, the shoot born from the top eye of the cutting becomes a stock in the very place where it is to grow and bear fruit. cuttings to be boURRed. - The above procedures for taking cuttings apply exclusively to those plants which, because of the way they vegetate, lend themselves most readily to taking cuttings. Many others are not; they need to be prepared a year in advance to obtain roots. The most common method is to make an annular incision in the bark around midsummer, or to bind the twig slightly tightly with wire, so that it can be used as a cutting in the spring of the following year. At the end of autumn, the branch is detached with a portion of wood below the ligature; it is then buried, either in the open air or in a cold frame, deep enough to cover the ligatured part. A bead is formed immediately above the ligature or incision, from which the roots should emerge; the beaded cutting's winter sojourn in the ground prepares it for rooting when it is put in place in spring, after all the reserved portion below the bead has been cut away. CUTTINGS UNDER A BELL OR UNDER A FRAME. - For a cutting to live and become a young plant, whatever the process used, it must be able to sustain its vegetative life at the expense of its own resources, from the moment it is detached from the plant until it has produced enough roots to continue vegetating at the expense of the soil. In plants that are very slow to root, the incessant evaporation produced by transpiration of the leaves would lead to complete desiccation and death of the cutting long before the roots were formed, if the gardener did not take care to render it null or very weak, by placing cuttings of this kind either under a bell or under a frame. This type of cutting is especially useful for evergreen resinous trees of the conifer family, for which it is difficult to obtain fertile seed to multiply by sowing. This method of propagation is always successful, if the cloche or frame is lifted when needed to provide a little air, and the temperature and humidity levels required by each type of plant are carefully regulated. Many conifers cut under a bell give better results when cut from root sections than when cut from branch sections. Similarly, cuttings from remontant roses in Bengal and China should be taken under a bell or under a frame, as simple cuttings would not be successful in the open air. CUTTINGS IN THE GREENHOUSE. - Delicate plants, which are difficult to root, cannot be cut under a bell or under a frame; the suppression or reduction of evaporation is not enough to induce them to put down roots; moreover, their cuttings must be stimulated by the mild or high temperature they find in a temperate or hot greenhouse. After a time, most of these plants only form excessively delicate fibrous roots, which rot in the slightest excess of humidity. For this reason, cuttings of this kind can only be made in heather soil, either pure or mixed with a small amount of potting soil, or even in 5 silica sand with no soil mix, kept at a suitable level of humidity until the roots have formed, after which the cutting, now a young plant, is transplanted into soil or compost appropriate to its nature. Greenhouse cuttings are taken in small pots; the smaller the pot, the better. Several cuttings can also be successfully placed around a terrine or pot with a wide opening; the closer they are to the inner wall of the pot, the faster they root, which is attributed to the favourable action of the air filtering through the thickness of the pot or terrine. Pelargoniums, Indian chrysanthemums and several fuchsias are all suitable for this purpose. All you need to do is cover them with a low bell, which concentrates evaporation and prevents them from drying out. Oleander and its sub-varieties root easily in water. When the weather begins to warm up in spring, cut a young oleander shoot from the previous year and soak it in a bottle near the window, where it will receive the sun's influence. When white, filamentous roots form at the bottom of the cutting, we remove it from the water and plant it in a pot filled with orange-tree soil, where it continues to vegetate as if it had put down its first roots. In the hot greenhouse, cuttings of leaves or parts of leaves are taken under the influence of a high temperature in small cloches adapted for this purpose; an inverted glass does the job perfectly if necessary. When the leaf has a petiole, it is at the bottom of this that the bead from which the roots grow is formed. In the Begonia genus, for example, ample, fairly thick leaves used as cuttings wither and are gradually destroyed; the petiole alone lives long enough to root. When the beading is well pronounced, the petiole can be split lengthwise into three or four pieces, each of which takes root and becomes a good cutting. Plants of the Gloxinaceae and Gesneriaceae families have an even more pronounced tendency to root by means of cuttings from leaf fragments, under cloche in the warm greenhouse; a single achimene leaf, for example, cut along its main veins, provides a large number of cuttings, not one of which fails to root. In the opinion of gardeners, June and July are the months in which cuttings under glass, in the greenhouse, are most successful; cuttings in the greenhouse can be taken at any time of year, provided that the plant being cut is still active; Others, without completely slumbering, experience a pause in their vegetation; it's when they start growing again with renewed vigour that they should be cut. The gardener must be patient and not despair of success as long as the plant life in the cutting is sustained, even though it remains stationary, neither moving forward nor backward. One fine day, he will see young leaves, then young shoots announcing the resumption of the cutting, which cannot begin to grow until it has put down young roots. Marcottes. - Marcottes are true cuttings which are given a better chance of success by leaving the mother plant to feed them until they are sufficiently rooted to feed themselves; only then are the marcottes weaned, i.e. detached from the mother plant. Layering therefore always consists of placing a portion of a plant in the ground, one end of which is left protruding outwards, while the other adheres to the plant you wish to propagate by this means. Provignage, so widely used in vine-growing countries, is a form of layering. Layering is simple when the operation is limited to covering the layered shoot with soil kept sufficiently moist. Some plants, such as carnations, which multiply mainly by layering, would not take root if the nodes of the buried part were not incised crosswise, to about half their thickness. This incision creates the bead that gives rise to the roots. In nurseries, linden trees and several species of avenue trees are marcotted by "cépée", which is the accepted term. The mother tree is cut back close to the ground, giving rise to a multitude of offshoots. Each of these shoots, once it has reached the right length, is covered with soil and treated like a simple marcotte, which is weeded when it has taken root, and transplanted to the place where it should continue to grow. Quince trees can be rooted in the same way. Some plants can only be rooted by layering when, instead of making a simple incision in the buried nodes, a part of them is cut off; this is called layering by amputation, and is frequently used in the greenhouse for plants that are difficult to root. Annual shoots suitable for marcotting are not always placed low enough on the plants to make it possible to marcott them in the very soil in which the plants live. In this case, the plant is attached to a stake strong enough to support a Fig. 43. Pot with Fig. 44. Cornet small pot split on the side (fig. 43), marcotter. to marcotter. or better a "cup formed from a sheet of lead or zinc rolled into a horn (fig. 44). The shoot to be marcotted is placed in it and surrounded by soil, which is watered often enough to prevent it from drying out; this method of marcotting is practised by simple marcotting, by incision or by amputation, according to the more or less pronounced disposition of each type of plant to emit roots. Grafting 77 Grafting. - Influence of subject on grafting. - Evidence of this influence. - Advantages of grafting for horticulture. - Approach grafting - Saint Fiacre's ointment. - Grafting wax. - Danger of applying it too hot. - Weaning from approach grafting. - Scion grafting. - Slot grafting. - Single scion. - Multiple scions. - Crown grafting. - English grafting. - Means of increasing strength. - Pontoise grafting. - Its applications. - Herbaceous grafting. - Escutcheon grafts. - Common escutcheon. - Oval escutcheon. - T-shaped incision. - Placement of escutcheon. - Ligature. - Flute graft. - Faun graft. - Growing eye budding. - Dormant eye. Grafting is a forced marriage, often a mismatched pairing that does not always produce happy results. It is a plant that is forced to live at the expense of another of a neighboring species, just as it would live by its own roots at the expense of the soil. Physiologists do not agree on the intimate relationship between the graft and the subject: some admit the influence of the subject on the graft, others absolutely deny it. In practice, the gardener is daily confronted with facts of indisputable value, which prove the existence of this influence in a large number of cases. For example, if he grafts an apple tree onto Paradis, the result is a dwarf tree; onto doucin, he obtains a medium-sized tree; onto egrain, i.e. onto a subject born from an apple seed, he has a first-rate tree. Were it only for this fact, and it is certainly not the only one of its kind, the gardener would certainly be justified in maintaining that the subject sometimes influences the graft, and in believing that this influence is very often exerted, albeit to different degrees, according to the greater or lesser analogy that may exist between the one and the other. The benefits to the gardener of grafting applications are many and far-reaching. It is through grafting that he can fix and perpetuate flower and fruit sub-varieties that any other method of propagation would not maintain; it is through grafting that he rejuvenates old, exhausted fruit trees, and that he makes beautiful ornamental trees live on hardy subjects that are not very difficult in terms of soil quality, in soils where they would refuse to vegetate on their own. It's far from certain, moreover, that grafting, although known and practised from the earliest times, has said its last word. Several types of grafting, such as herbaceous grafting, which was widely used three centuries ago but then fell into oblivion, have regained favor today and are producing results of the highest interest. Grass grafting, successfully used on rice in Italy, opens up a whole new series that time can make fruitful. There is an immense amount of experimental work to be done on grafting, which, if it ever comes to fruition, cannot be the work of just one man; we need to ascertain which plants can be grafted onto each other, which types of grafting are best suited to them, and what advantages can be gained in practice; such interesting points are known, at present, only for a fairly limited number of plants. All grafts used in horticulture fall into three natural divisions: approach grafting, scion grafting and escutcheon grafting. APPROACH GRAFTING. - This graft, the most ancient of all, consists in welding a branch of one plant onto another; when this welding is complete, the lower part of the plant to be grafted and the upper part of the subject are removed, resulting in a new tree; it is therefore a sort of marcotte of one tree onto another; a notch made on the two surfaces to be joined makes the union more intimate and the recovery easier. The graft and the subject are firmly attached to each other by ties which, in the case of fully-formed trees, can be coarse provided they are strong; in this case, they consist of wicker or twisted straw, which can be lined with Saint Fiacre's ointment or grafting wax to prevent rain from penetrating. Saint Fiacre's ointment is made from clay and cow dung, mixed together to form a paste of good consistency, in equal parts. The best and easiest to use grafting wax is composed of the following ingredients: Black pitch 500 grams; resin 100 grams; yellow wax 100 grams; axonge 100 grams, plus a small amount of pulverized brick. When a lot of grafting has to be done at the same time, a small portable furnace is carried into the field to soften the grafting wax. Great care must be taken not to apply the grafting wax too hot, as this could kill the subject or the graft. In modern horticulture, approach grafting is now used almost exclusively in the greenhouse, for plants that do not lend themselves easily to other types of grafting. Ligatures are made with woollen thread, avoiding over-tightening, which would cause strangulation. When the state of vegetation indicates that a graft is approaching, it is weaned like a marcotte, not all at once, but little by little, by making a notch which is deepened every day until complete separation; weaning, depending on the diameter of the subject, can last from ten to fifteen days. There is no fixed time for approach grafting; you have to choose the moment when the sap is at its most active, which, for exotic plants more or less displaced in European greenhouses, only occurs at times that vary from one genus to another. Scion grafting. - This grafting method is one of the most widely used, under the various names of split grafting, crown grafting, English grafting, pontoise grafting and herbaceous grafting. It always consists of inserting a scion as a graft onto a neighbouring species. SPLIT GRAFTING. - Although many ornamental trees and shrubs can be successfully split-grafted, split-grafting is mainly applicable to trees with seeded fruit. Slot grafting can only be carried out using a piece of wood from the previous year. The success of the operation depends largely on the condition of the scion used for grafting at the time it is brought into contact with the subject. For split grafting of fruit trees, the most vigorous shoots of the year are reserved for winter pruning; they are placed in bundles, taking care not to confuse the species; the lower part is pricked out in fresh soil, in the open air, in a shady situation. The bundles of scions for grafting are covered, if necessary, with litter or dry leaves during very cold weather, to protect them from frost damage. For a cleft graft to be successful, the movement of sap must be very pronounced in the subject and ready to begin in the graft. The surface of the cut is then slit transversely with the grafting blade; at one end of the slit is inserted a scion with two good eyes, cut at the bottom so as to present the exact thickness of its bark in contact with the bark of the subject (fig. 45). A wool thread ligature Fig. 45. Split graft. Fig. 46. Grafting with 4 scions. holds everything in place until the next cutting; the wound is covered with grafting wax. When the diameter of the cut is large enough, two similar grafts are placed, one at each end of the slit. The same graft with one or two scions can be used not only on the trunk of a tree cut horizontally, but with equal success on the branches of a tree whose species is to be changed to a better one. On very large branches, not two, but four or six grafts can be placed using exactly the same procedure, by making two or three slits in the cut, each of which accepts two scions, one at each end (fig. 46). Slot grafting, although usually carried out in spring, can also be successful in September. In this case, the graft and the subject enter their hiyernal sleep at the same time, and the movement of their vegetation starts again at the same time for both of them, in the spring of the following year: this is what gardeners call "dormant-eye" split grafting. When split grafting is carried out at this time of year, it is usually to advance the work and avoid having too many trees to graft at the same time in the spring, with a growing eye. CROWN GRAFTING. - This differs from the previous method in that the cut of the tree is not slit to admit the scions, which are cut in the shape of a flute mouthpiece, so as to fit between the tree and its bark, which is lifted for this purpose (fig. 47). A branch with a large diameter can receive eight to ten scions, or even more, by crown grafting; this type of grafting is especially used to rejuvenate old Fig. 47. Crown graft. Fig. 48. English grafting. exhausted trees. It is not to be confused with the gresle en 5. fente of large branches or trunks of trees broken by accident, and on which six grafts have been placed at the ends of three slits; the specific character of the crown graft is to exclude any slit on the surface of the cut and to leave this cut perfectly intact. ANGLAISE Graft (fig 48). - This type of graft can only be applied to subjects of exactly the same diameter as the scion. The top of the tree and the bottom of the scion are cut obliquely at the same angle, so that all their parts overlap and the edges of the two barks coincide at all points. Ligature for English-style grafting, also known as copulation grafting, must be very strong, but not too tight; this graft is one of the easiest to detach when it is not well attached to the tree by a good ligature. If the English grafted tree is to be planted in an open position, exposed to violent gales, the solidity of the graft is increased by cutting two notches into the cut of the subject and the graft, which fit into each other. PONTOISE GRAFT. - This is a split graft particularly suited to ornamental trees and shrubs which do not lose their leaves in winter, and which need to be sheltered during the bad season in the orangery or cold greenhouse. When the shrub emerges from its winter sleep and its sap Fig. 49. The bottom of the graft is cut into a wedge shape, so as to fit exactly into the incision; it is held in place by a ligature which is loosened when recovery is assured. As the scion used for Pontoise grafting is loaded with diggings and often even flowers or flower buds, evaporation would destroy it before it could be grafted onto the subject, if we did not take the precaution of placing the shrubs thus grafted under a frame for two weeks, in return for which success is assured. (fig. 49). HERBACEOUS GRAFTING. - This is another split grafting technique usedFig. 50. Herbaceous grafting. on plant parts that have not yet become woody. This graft is mainly applied to the terminal shoot of coniferous resinous trees, during the height of their vegetation. This shoot is cut off by a triangular cut into which the wedge-shaped scion fits. This type of graft should only be tied with great care; their lack of consistency makes them more prone than others to strangulation when too tightly tied (fig. 50). Escutcheon grafts - Escutcheon grafts are characterized by the fact that no part of the wood used for grafting is introduced onto the subject; a single eye (or several eyes), attached to a piece of bark, performs the grafting function, and is applied to a part of the subject from which the bark has been removed over an area the same size as the escutcheon. The most delicate aspect of good budding is to select the eyes with discernment on the middle part of a shoot from the previous year, and to remove it with the bark. Fig. 51. Oval escutcheon and surrounding cortex without emptying the eye, common escutcheon. i.e. without tearing out the inner germ it contains, which is to become a plant or a tree, and to keep for it a piece of the leaf stalk in whose axil any annual eye has formed. The incision to detach the escutcheon is made with the tip of a sharp penknife; the piece of bark surrounding the eye is usually oval or escutcheon-shaped, hence the name of this grafting method (fig. 51). To attach an escutcheon, two straight incisions are made in the bark, in the shape of a straight T or an upside-down y. With the tip of the grafting knife, the escutcheon is removed from the bark. With the tip of the grafting tool, both sides of the T are lifted to the right and left, and the escutcheon is immediately applied to the subject; the two edges of the split bark are then brought over the escutcheon, leaving only the eye exposed; the whole is held in place by a ligature several turns above and below the eye: this is the most ordinary way of setting escutcheons. You can also lay the escutcheon flat on the subject's bark, and use it as a template to cut a piece of bark precisely the same size as the escutcheon. After removing this piece of bark, the subject is placed in its place and secured with a ligature. FLUTE OR RING Grafting (fig. 52). - This graft is based on the same principle as the escutcheon graft; it is Fig. 52. Flute graft. Fig. 53. faun graft. a strip of bark with several eyes, removed at the time of sap movement and applied to the tree stripped of its bark. If the subject's diameter is greater than that of the branch from which the ring has been detached, the ring is trimmed to the appropriate length so that it fits exactly on the subject. FAUNA GREEF (fig. 53). - This is a variety of the flute graft, particularly used for grafting walnut and chestnut from seed. Instead of removing the bark at the point where the bark ring is to be applied, the bark is slit lengthwise by several incisions, dividing it into strips which are then folded over from top to bottom without being detached; the ring used as a graft is then placed in its place as for flute grafting. The bark strips are folded up over the ring, leaving only the eyes exposed, and tied up like a shield graft. The various methods of scape grafting are practised either from May to July, with a growing eye, i.e. the scape eye should immediately come into vegetation, or in August and September, with a dormant eye, which means that the branch we hope to obtain from the scape eye will only develop in the spring of the following year. The foregoing is sufficient to give a good understanding of the principle of grafting and its most important applications; specific details will be found in the chapters devoted to the various crops for which each type of graft is specially used. CHAPTER X Plant diseases Plant diseases. - Diseases caused by excessive vigor. - Etiolation. - Principal diseases. - Potato disease. - Mr. Tombelle-Lomba's method. - Sowing potato seeds. - Results. - Frisole. - First potato disease in Belgium; -Vine disease. - Tuckeri powdery mildew. - Blowing out diseased vines. Glanders, or softening of flowering onions. - White or mulberry. - Plants specially affected. - Ulcers. - How to treat them - Ulcers on tree roots. - Magnifying glasses. - Wounds. - Contusions. - Wounds. - Peeling. - Tears. - Remedies. - Fractures. - Lesions due to various causes. - By heat - By cold - Under what circumstances do plants freeze? - Treatment of greenhouse and orangery plants affected by frost. - Injuries caused by thunder. - By hail. - Stale air. Ever since the Italian physiologist Philippo Ré published an extensive work on the diseases that can affect plants, all those who have tackled the same subject have included in their classification a host of phenomena or particular plant conditions, which in no way constitute disease affections. We therefore refrain from mentioning here, except as a reminder, plants that are ill due to excessive health, producing an abnormal quantity of leaves, flowers or fruit; it is often useful for the gardener to provoke this state of vegetation; it is then the aim of his cultivation care; it is not in any way an ailment to be remedied. The same can be said of the transition of leaves from green to yellow at the time when they are due to fall, a natural transition which we have tried to turn into a plant disease, and of the wilting of leaves on cabbages and head lettuces, a state which must be provoked but never combated. Thus reduced to its proper limits, the framework of real plant diseases now comprises only a fairly limited number of diverse, well-characterized affections, arranged in two divisions, the first of which contains diseases properly so called, and the second injuries to plants, trees and shrubs in the field of horticulture. The main diseases are: potato disease, grapevine disease, glanders or softening of onions, powdery mildew of pansies, plums and some fruit trees, ulcers and burls. These last two diseases are specific to woody plants (trees or shrubs) and do not exist on herbaceous plants. Plant injuries, or at least those which the gardener is most often called upon to combat, are contusions, wounds, decortications, tears, fractures and more or less serious lesions caused by excessive heat or cold, thunder, hail and prolonged contact with air contaminated by unhealthy vapors. Diseases. - Diseases of gardening plants can be cured or prevented if the first symptoms are carefully observed and remedied in good time. potato disease. - Of the countless varieties of potato, only the garden varieties such as marjolin, chaville, schaw and seven-week-old, all in the early series, should be considered for horticultural use, as they are less prone than the others to the disease, which only rages in full force once these varieties have produced their produce. Nevertheless, even the earliest potatoes can be diseased in the garden as well as in the field. What is potato disease? So little is known about it that physiologists haven't even named it. When it first broke out in Europe in 1765, Belgian farmers, whose crops were devastated by it, gave it the name frisole, because it causes the leaves to curl up and turn from green to dark brown. Without going into the theory, which concludes that nothing is certain, let's confine ourselves to indicating the curative procedure of Mr. Tombelle-Lomba, of Namur, the only one which, to our knowledge, has given positively advantageous results. As soon as the first brown spots on the leaves and stems indicate the invasion of the disease, all the outer parts of the plant are cut off at ground level; the soil is then strongly compressed by trampling. At the usual time of uprooting, the potatoes are only slightly below the normal volume for their species, but they are not diseased. This simple, easy-to-apply procedure works just as well in the field as it does in the garden, especially for early varieties. In gardens, these same species, planted in late autumn deep enough to prevent frost from reaching them, produce vigorous plants in spring the following year that are very rarely affected by disease. The gardener who lives in a country where potatoes are often diseased, and where this tuber nevertheless holds a place in the crop rotation, can profit greatly from multiplying good species by sowing their seeds. In the first year, he obtains only small plants which produce tubers of very little volume; in the second year, these same tubers planted in the usual way, in good quality, well-smoked soil, produce masses of tubers such that it seems that at the foot of each plant the earth has literally turned into potatoes. However, seed potatoes are much less prone to disease than other varieties, and it was through seed potatoes that Belgian growers in 1765 successfully combated and eradicated curly disease, which has now returned not only to Belgium, but to the whole of Europe. When the gardener can offer the farmer good-quality tubers from seed, which are unlikely to be affected by 9 - disease, he is sure of getting good prices for them at the time of planting. When he grows early potatoes in large enough fields, using second-year seed tubers for his plantadons is a very effective way of preserving his crops from being invaded by the disease. Vione disease. - The losses caused by the scourge of grapevine disease in all the vine-growing countries of Europe have been almost as severe as those caused by potato disease. The disease first appeared in England, in greenhouses used for forcing vines, and from the outset showed the same characteristics as those we recognize today. It is the rapid multiplication of mycalium and the spores of a microscopic fungus, Oidium Tuckeri, on leaves, shoots, bunches and grapes, which appear to be covered with a whitish dust. When the disease has reached its peak, vegetation stops, leaves crisp up and fall off, grapes crack and dry out; vines are so badly affected that it takes several years to restore them to good health. In greenhouses, as in the open air, the only effective remedy is sulphur flower spread over the entire surface of the diseased vine, moistened by a good basinage in the greenhouse to be forced, dampened by the morning dew when it grows outside. In greenhouses and gardens, sick vines can be sulphured very quickly and very well using a specially designed bellows. With one or two sulphurings, the disease disappears; the value of the crop preserved is always far greater than that of the sulphur and labor spent on this beneficial operation. GLANDERS OR SOFTENING OF FLOWERING ONIONS. - Onions grown on bulbous ornamental plants often suffer from a disease which consists in the decomposition of the tissues of their tunics; this is what gardeners call onion glanders. This disease always leads to the death of the onion affected, but no serious harm is done to the far-sighted gardener, who must have stocked up on carefully cultivated young onions to replace those that die each year from exhaustion or softening. By frequently visiting them during the interval between the removal of the onions after they have flowered, and their installation, either in spring or autumn, the gardener cannot fail to recognize the first signs of the disease which starts with a small round spot and ends up invading the whole onion. If it belongs to a rare species, removing the damaged part will not preserve the onion, but enable it to produce cloves that will replace it later. BLANc or MEunier. - The disease known to gardeners as blanc or meûnier is due to the development of a microscopic fungus, called erysiphe by botanists, which is not very different from the one which, under the name of oidium, causes vine disease. In gardens, this disease particularly attacks plums among fruit trees; roses among ornamental shrubs; and pansies among ornamental annuals. There is no known cure for powdery mildew, but it is known to develop very quickly on plants, trees and shrubs with vigorous vegetation; With the judicious use of solid, powdered or liquid fertilizers, and well-directed cultivation care, cultivated plants can be placed in such conditions that powdery mildew cannot invade them. In fact, powdery mildew is one of those plant diseases that cannot be cured, but can always be easily prevented. ULCERES. - The trunks and main branches of certain trees are quite often attacked by ulcers, from which, depending on their nature, gum or various juices derived from the decomposition of their sap flow. These ulcers gradually eat away at the wood, penetrating right to the heart of the tree and eventually leading to its death. The only remedy is to cut away the damaged part, then cover it with St. Fiacre's ointment, or grafting wax, until 2 3 5 2 I 91 the bark, advancing on both sides, finally masks the scar. Sometimes, the ulceration occurs on one or more roots; this can be seen when the branches corresponding to these roots die back; we must then hasten to uncover them carefully, remove all diseased parts and put the soil back in place; the tree will soon regrow by putting out new roots. WOLVES. - Various trees, notably elms and their ornamental varieties, are often deformed by very voluminous, rounded growths that are veritable plant magnifiers. They are rarely so numerous as to interfere with the tree's growth; they are left to survive for use as marquetry wood, due to the various shades of veins and knots with which their interiors are marbled. Injuries. - Most of the time, it depends on the gardener to prevent most of the wounds that the plants under his care may accidentally receive; when they do receive wounds in spite of his supervision, he must hasten to dress them, just as he does ulcers. CONTUSIONS. - Trees and shrubs whose wood is hard enough can receive heavy bruising without suffering noticeably; their vital energy repairs the damage by itself, without it showing on the outside. In recent times, it has even been suggested that a stick be used to strike the bark of the trunk of fruit trees when they are too slow to set fruit - a savage method that can always be replaced by a more rational one designed to achieve the same goal. When, by violent bruising, the bark and the portion of wood it immediately covers are crushed, the result is a swelling which degenerates into a wound and eventually takes on the characteristics of an ulcer. This can be prevented by removing the wounded part and dressing it like an ulcer, using Saint Fiacre's ointment or grafting wax. WOUNDS. - Plant wounds can be accompanied by decortication, tearing or fracture, produced by external causes. When decortication does not embrace the entire circumference of the trunk, the tree suffers to a greater or lesser extent; but it rarely dies, and the wound eventually heals as the beads on its two edges gradually come together. This process can only take place extremely slowly, without compromising the tree's vigor or slowing its growth. The traces of the decortication they underwent in 1814, by the teeth of Cossack horses bivouacked on the boulevard Bourdon in Paris, can still be seen very clearly on the lime trees; after forty-four years, not all the wounds are completely healed. This result can be achieved more rapidly by applying a sufficiently thick layer of grafting wax to the wounds; in this case, St. Fiacre's ointment would not be sufficiently durable. DECHIRURES. - When, for whatever reason, a branch of a tree, without being broken, has burst at its insertion on the trunk or on a larger branch, and is still attached by a portion of wood, it should not be considered as lost; Without wasting any time, it must be put back in place, secured with a strong wire ligature, to which we add, if necessary, some kind of splint consisting of pieces of wood hollowed out in a gutter, and hold the whole solidly in place by ties attached either to the trunk or to the surrounding branches. The main obstacle to the healing of wounds of this kind is the moisture that can enter through the edges of the tear; but, as long as circulation is not completely interrupted by a complete separation of the torn branch, recovery is certain. Ligatures and splints should only be removed when the branch has returned to its original state of vitality, which sometimes takes several years. Instead of grafting wax, molten black pitch is used to coat the edges of the tear, taking great care not to apply it too hot, otherwise the remedy would be worse than the disease. When the tear has completely detached the branch, or when there is so little adherence left that it cannot be expected to recover, the wound is treated as if it had been made with a saw; it is carefully dressed with a strong serpette with a sharp blade, then covered with grafting wax. FRACTURES. - Any fracture in the branches of a tree should be treated as an amputation; the broken part is sawn low enough below the fracture that no trace of splitting or tearing remains on the amputated surface; then the wound is trimmed and dressed with grafting wax. When an overload of snow, or the violence of the wind during a hurricane, has fractured the trunk of a fruit tree that is still young and full of vigor, instead of sacrificing it and planting another, it is much preferable, after having sawn the fracture and trimmed the wounds, to provide the tree with the means to quickly regrow by crown grafting (See Grafting, chap. IX). LESIONS DUE TO VARIOUS CAUSES. - There are various types of lesions that cannot be confused with either plant diseases or wounds as just described, but which the gardener must be aware of in order to remedy them when necessary. LESIONS CAUSED BY HEAT. - Excessive heat is only to be feared for plants grown in gardens when watering is neglected in proportion to need, and to prevent plants from suffering from both heat and drought. Heat can be particularly harmful to plants grown in pots, when the rays of a hot sun heat up the walls of the pots and burn the delicate roots in contact with them. Frequent watering is not enough to prevent such accidents, which lead to the death of plants; pots must also be arranged in such a way that the sun cannot strike them directly, at least during the hottest hours of the day. LESIONS CAUSED BY COLD. - Every type of cultivated plant goes into dormancy under the influence of a given degree of heat, and ceases to vegetate as soon as the outside temperature drops below that level. With the exception of a small number of plants, which seem to be completely insensitive to the most rigorous cold of our winters, all suffer to a greater or lesser extent from very intense cold; vines sometimes freeze right down to the lower end of the shoots, which then have to be completely folded back onto the stump; the largest trees split, and sores or ulcers are the consequence of these cracks; but, fortunately, this only happens in exceptionally harsh winters. The crucial fact that dominates the whole question of the deleterious action of cold on cultivated plants is the state in which the frost catches them. If their vegetative sleep is complete, unless the cold is so severe that it thwarts all predictions, trees and shrubs do not suffer appreciably; if, on the contrary, late spring frosts seize the same plants after they have awakened from their winter sleep, a few degrees of cold can kill them. Thus, in the parterre, the garden carnation does not freeze when it is kept in healthy, dry soil, which completely interrupts the movement of its sap; as soon as this movement is resumed, whether a frost of only one or two degrees occurs, the same carnation perishes without resource. The knowledge of this fact should guide the gardener in the care he takes to preserve the plants in his domain from the lesions caused by the cold, especially in the climate of Paris, during the months of March and April, when a few days of mild temperature are followed by a resumption of very sharp cold. If, through negligence on the part of the gardener, the cold has penetrated the greenhouse or orangery, and the plants housed there have been hit by frost, they should be sprayed with fresh water at a temperature of four to five degrees, and the same temperature maintained as evenly as possible, day and night. By following this method, only some of the most delicate plants are lost; the others are saved. They would all be lost if, in an attempt to repair the damage, the temperature in the greenhouse or orangery were raised a little higher. INJURIES CAUSED BY THUNDER. - When a vigorous tree is struck by lightning, it is not always completely killed; cutting off the branches that die, either immediately or in the following days, causes new branches to emerge, and the tree can recover. When the trunk is torn to a greater or lesser depth by a lightning strike, the injured parts are removed to the bone, and as the result is usually a wound far too extensive to be treated with the usual St. Fiacre's ointment or grafting wax, the crack is filled with plaster. More often than not, the tree, although severely damaged, can be preserved. LESIONS CAUSED BY HAIL. - Not only does hail cause purely mechanical damage to the herbaceous parts of the plants it affects, it also disorganizes them through the small quantity of nitrous acid it contains; the presence of this acid can be detected after a storm, through the particular odor of hail clusters that do not melt immediately. On the leaves and stems of melons, for example, every spot hit by hail forms a small spot which grows, turns from green to brown and causes the affected part to die. There is no remedy for lesions of this kind; the careful gardener must always have glass frames and straw mats within reach of his melon beds during the storm season, each of which may be accompanied by hail. It's better to cover a precious crop ten times over than to risk losing it in a few minutes, if you haven't taken the trouble to protect it in good time from the fatal damage caused by hail. Hail caused by foul air. - In the vicinity of factories producing various products, habitual contact with air contaminated by a mixture of gases and vapors harmful to plant life causes more or less serious damage to plants cultivated in gardens; gardens should only be established out of contact with these destructive causes. To maintain the health of cultivated plants, the air must not be too pure. Smoke alone, in cantons where tall factory chimneys are concentrated in large numbers in a small area, has an unfortunate effect on certain plants which, without being a real poisoning, nevertheless disturbs the normal course of their vegetation. In England, for example, and in some parts of Belgium, rose collections can only be cultivated at a certain distance from factories, and out of reach of the streams of smoke that their chimneys constantly pour into the atmosphere. Under the influence of the air altered by this smoke, rose bushes produce shoots, leaves and buds every year, but they flower poorly, and even those of the most beautiful varieties do not flower at all. CHAPTER XI Animals harmful to garden produce Animals harmful to gardens. - Mammals. - Moles. - Its galleries, - Time when it works. - Rat. - Rat trap. - Four of a kind. - Dormouse and ferret. - Dormouse trap. - Bait for this trap. - Mollusks. - Slugs. - How to destroy them. - Ducks hunt slugs in gardens. - Snails. - Crush oyster scales to keep them away. - Insects. - Beetles. - Hunting beetles. - Damage caused by their larvae. - Flea beetle. - Plants attacked by flea beetles. - Grapevine flea beetle. - Its various names. - How to destroy it. - RynchiteBacchus, or green vine weevil. - Cantharides. - Scolyte. - Mr. E. Robert's method for destroying the bark beetle. - Wasp. - Finding and destroying bee-eaters. - Ant. - Finding and destroying ant hills. - Golden beetle. - Forficule or earwig. - Mole cricket. - Green aphid. - Black aphid. - Woolly aphid. - Casting to destroy it. - Caterpillars. - General weeding procedures. - Cabbage worm. - Delivery. - Grape vine borer. - Carpocapsa, or fruit moth. - Ways to reduce its ravages. Garden produce is exposed to damage by a large number of animals of various kinds. It is very important for the gardener to be familiar with them and to do his utmost to prevent their ravages. It's easy to understand how unpleasant it is to grow dahlias for snails, carnations for earwigs, peach trees for wasps and ants, and apple trees for woolly adelgids. Animals harmful to garden produce belong to the three classes of mammals, molluscs and insects. Mammals. - The mammals whose presence is most to be feared in gardens are: the mole, the rat, the toir and the lérót: TAUPE. - The soil in any well-kept garden is so frequently shaped in all seasons that the mole cannot easily make it his home. If by chance they do find their way in, their presence is betrayed by the rounded mounds of crumbled earth they form above the center, around which radiate their admirably symmetrical underground galleries. Nothing is easier than spotting it at the hour when it is working between two plots of land to hunt down the insect larvae it feeds on. It is usually in the morning, from 10 a.m. to noon, that its work is most active and you can catch it at work, spade it out of the ground and kill it without giving it time to return to the soil. For gardens, where the presence of the mole can never be more than rare and accidental, this means of destruction is sufficient; it is never necessary to have recourse to the various mole traps, nor to the intervention of professional mole catchers who make it their trade to destroy moles in fields and meadows, where they often multiply in such numbers as to become a veritable scourge. RAT. - When garden walls, or those of nearby buildings, are old and in a state of disrepair, they serve as a home for rats, which multiply at their leisure. It is rare for rats not to pay frequent nocturnal visits to espaliered fruit trees when the fruit ripens. The most commonly used trap for destroying these bold and voracious rodents is known as the "four of figures", because of the way in which the sticks are adjusted to balance a plank loaded with a heavy stone. It must be primed with grilled rancid bacon, the strong smell of which attracts rats from afar. LOIR AND LÉROT. - These two pretty animals, not very different from each other, are fortunately not very common in France, except in a few localities not far from large woods, where they live on the tallest trees, like their close relative the squirrel. The dormouse and the ferret are caught in a trap shaped like a wicker cage; the ammal can enter easily, but cannot get out; with this type of trap, it can be caught alive without injury; if it is young, it is easily tamed and becomes as familiar as a squirrel. Dormice and ferrets only eat fruit; to start the trap, place a nice ripe fruit in the center, but of a different species from those you wish to protect from the teeth of these animals. If, for example, at the foot of a peach tree you place a peach as bait in the dormouse trap, this bait will not attract them; they would prefer to stick to the same fruit available to them on the espalier. Molluscs. - Two kinds of molluscs, slugs and snails, are often the despair of gardeners in spring and autumn; they can cause very serious damage in gardens if measures are not taken to destroy them. SLUGS. - The two small species of slugs, the brown and the gray, multiply so rapidly and are so voracious that, in wet years, they are a scourge in gardens with cold or compact soil; young seedlings are their favorite food. The easiest way to reduce their numbers is to lay cabbage and romaine leaves flat on the ground in the evening. Slugs invade these leaves, both to feed on them and to shelter under them at night. In the morning, before sunrise, they can be found in great numbers under these leaves. Instead of crushing them, they can be fed to farmyard birds, which are very fond of them. The duck does not, like the hen, have the unfortunate habit of scratching the earth, which means it must be kept away from all cultivated land; it can be a useful auxiliary to man by hunting slugs; although this bird does not disdain vegetable herbs, when introduced into a garden on an empty stomach in the morning, as long as there are earthworms and slugs, it will not touch any gardening produce. The only thing to do is to keep an eye on its hunt and remove it from the garden as soon as it has finished. LIMAÇONS OR ESCaRGoTS. - Although they multiply less rapidly than slugs, and are never as numerous as slugs in gardens, snails should be sought out and destroyed in vegetable, fruit and flower gardens. Especially after a heavy shower of rain, during the summer months, it's easy to catch large numbers of them; they then set off, with as much agility as their nature allows, in search of their food; the young shoots of all kinds of vegetable and ornamental plants, ripe fruit and flowering buds of dahlias are especially to their liking. To keep them away from dahlia clumps and fruit trees, a circle of coarsely crushed oyster shells is formed on the ground. These scales divide into sharp blades which the snail cannot touch, forming a barrier that it is impossible for it to cross. Insects. - An entire volume could be written on the insects that cause varying degrees of damage to field and garden produce, and on the more or less effective means used or proposed to destroy them. We shall confine ourselves here to mentioning the most widespread, the most harmful, and those whose ravages it is most necessary to prevent or stop. These insects are, in the coleopteran order: the hanncton, the flea beetle, the vine eumolpe, the Bynchite-Bacchus, the cantharide and the scolyte; in the social hymenopteran order : the wasp and the ant; in the orthopterans, the forcicle or earwig and the mole-cricket; in the hemipterans: aphids, and in the lepidopterans or butterflies: the cabbageworm, the tent caterpillar, the vine moth and the carpocapsa. HANNETON. - The most damaging of all European insects is undoubtedly the chafer. Many other insects are only harmful during their larval stage, and cause no further damage once they have undergone their final transformation into perfect insects. The cockchafer gnaws at plant roots for the three, sometimes four, years of its larval existence; then, having become a perfect insect, it devours the green parts of all kinds of plants, trees and shrubs. The female beetle digs a hole in the ground, usually a decimeter deep, at the bottom of which she deposits her eggs. It is one of the most extraordinary facts to emerge from the study of entomology that the instinct common to all females of all insects, without exception, is to constantly place their eggs where the young larvae, at the moment of their birth, will find within their reach the kind of food necessary for their sustenance, even when this food differs most from that which is suitable for the perfect insect, when the latter needs food; for many perfect insects do not eat tont in their last form. For example, the female beetle doesn't eat roots, yet this insect never lays its eggs in vegetation-free soil; the young generation of beetles, when the eggs hatch, is surrounded by provisions and cannot fail to prosper. Destroying beetles is not difficult, but it can only be effective if all the inhabitants of a township take care of it at the same time and in a consistent manner, working together at the same hours. Every evening, the beetles retire to the tall trees; they cling by their legs to the underside of the leaves and spend the night there in a state of numbness that lasts until quite late in the morning, and from which they only emerge when the sun has evaporated the dew. It has been suggested that resin-coated straw torches should be carried under trees laden with beetles; this procedure is dangerous for the trees and useless for making the beetles fall; the branches have to be shaken strongly; the beetles can neither prevent themselves from falling to the ground, nor fly away to escape. This simple method, generally practised, would result in the complete destruction of the beetles; but for this to happen, it would 6. have to be made compulsory by law, like weeding. It is all the more necessary for gardeners in every canton to reach agreement on this subject, as the chafer larva does not spare shrubs or young trees any more than it does vegetable plants; in areas infested with these larvae, known as mau, turc or white worm, it is impossible to keep roses, dahlias or young fruit trees in the nursery; As soon as you see the leaves of strawberries and lettuces wilting, you can be sure that the roots of these plants are being eaten away by several white grubs, which can be exposed with the stroke of a spade. When a garden plot is infested with grubs, we destroy a considerable number at ploughing time by bringing in turkeys that have been left to fast on purpose. As each grub is brought to the surface, it is devoured. ALTiSe. - This insect is known to gardeners as the flea beetle and the ground flea. The flea beetle mainly attacks the seminal leaves of vegetable plants in the cruciferous family: cabbages, cauliflowers and turnips. In large-scale cultivation, flea beetle control is so difficult that it usually has to be dispensed with; this is not the case in gardens, where the seedlings of plants attacked by flea beetles never occupy large areas. As soon as the seedlings are covered with flea beetles, they can be watered with a strong infusion of tobacco, or well water mixed with chimney soot. But the real remedy against the ravages of the flea beetle is to sow the seeds of the plants it feeds on only in such conditions that they pass through the first phase of their vegetation very quickly. As soon as they have acquired a certain degree of strength, their tissues have enough consistency to have nothing to fear from the mandibles of the flea beetle, which is too weak to penetrate them, so that the insect is reduced to starving to death.- In its multiplication, the flea beetle offers a phenomenon that has long passed for an inexplicable mystery. Sow cabbage, turnip or rape seed in a well-ploughed field, where there is obviously not a single flea beetle, or in any of the neighboring fields; as soon as these plants emerge, they will be invaded by millions of flea beetles, often leaving not a single one behind, so that sowing will have to be repeated. Naturalists have recognized that the flea beetle deposits its eggs in the thickness of the ribs or veins of the leaves of cruciferous plants, whether wild or cultivated; the species maintains itself in this way and remains unnoticed because of the small number of isolated individuals. However, as soon as suitable food is within its reach, it arrives, probably attracted by the scent of crucifers, and takes possession of sown fields; there, as it undergoes its transformations in a very short time, it soon becomes prodigiously numerous, and its ravages are impossible to stop. In gardens, it is always possible, by careful weeding, to leave no plant that could serve as a refuge for flea beetles and their larvae, and thus to be completely free of them. GRAPEVINE FLEA BEETLE. - This insect hardly multiplies in gardens to the point of becoming dangerous there; it is only fearsome in vineyards, where it is known, depending on the locality, under the various names of gribouri, bud cutter and writer. This last name derives from the fact that the marks left by the vine eumolpe on the leaves whose green substance it has gnawed away quite distinctly represent several letters of the alphabet, especially the capital letters I, L, A and V. This singular fact is only natural; the eumolpe, starting from any point on the surface of a leaf, gnaws straight ahead until it encounters one of the main veins in its path, which is too hard for it to penetrate; it then turns away at right angles, following the edge of the vein: hence the characters it seems to trace, and which have earned it the nickname writer. Like the chafer and most coleopteran insects, the vine eumolpe is unable to escape until it awakes from its nocturnal numbness. The vines on which its presence is noticed must be stripped early in the morning and shaken briskly over a basket where the eumolpes fall without being able to escape; this hunt, repeated two or three times, leaves not a single one remaining. RYNCHITE-BACCHUS. - Under the common name of green vine weevil, we know the insect that naturalists call Rynchite-Bacchus; it's a pretty beetle, with beautiful green elytra, whose presence on vine stocks is revealed by the way it lodges itself in the leaves, which it rolls up like a paper cone, in order to shut itself in and undergo its transformations. As the fresh vine leaf is not flexible enough to lend itself to the shape the rynchite wants to give it, its larva, in the form of a whitish worm, begins by gnawing the tail of the leaf, about halfway through its thickness; this is not enough to make the leaf fall, but to wilt it and make it flexible enough for the larva to roll it without too much effort. Multiplication of the rynchite is prevented by removing all the rolled leaves, each of which is home to a larva. CantHarid. - We mention the cantharid here, not as an insect harmful to garden produce, where it does no damage, but solely because various species of ash tree, especially the weeping ash with its drooping branches, used to decorate our gardens, are often frequented in summer by cantharids, which can produce painful blisters when they fall on the necks of people sitting in the shade of these trees. It is therefore advisable to shake the branches of ash trees in pleasure gardens early in the morning, in order to remove the cantharids which, like all coleopteran insects, are immersed in their nocturnal numbness. SCOLYTE. - Several species of bark beetle create galleries between the wood and bark of elm and other coniferous trees, eventually causing the bark to fall off in patches and seriously jeopardizing their existence. Rarely do bark beetles show up in dangerous numbers =- Re- 9 91 in groves where, moreover, the scoIvte's prized trees rarely feature. Various methods have been proposed for destroying the elm bark beetle, which has been blamed for the death of most of the elms on the public promenades in Paris. Perhaps a closer look would have revealed that these very old trees were attacked by the bark beetle because they were sick, and not sick because they were invaded by bark beetles. The most widely-used method, developed by Eugène Robert, involves lifting evenly-spaced strips of bark from the top to the bottom of the tree trunk, in order to disturb the bark beetles in their retreat.
