There's no doubt that this method kills the bark beetles; unfortunately, more often than not, it also kills the trees. WASP. - The wasp rarely wanders far from its home. When a garden's ripe fruit is the prey of wasps, it's best to look nearby: the beehive is not far away. The wasp's stings are extremely painful, so once you've identified the location of their home, pour one or two liters of boiling water over it during the night; surprised in their sleep, the wasps perish without vengeance. If the beehive tree cannot be found, and the fruit has been damaged by a few isolated beehives, small vials full of honeyed water are hung from the branches of the trees; the beehives come to perish in them, victims of their taste for all sweet liquids. FOURMI. - Like the wasp, the little black ant, all too common in gardens, greedily seeks out anything sweet, making this insect the most dangerous enemy of fruits which, like apricots and peaches, cannot be picked until they have reached maturity. A large number can be destroyed by attracting them in vials full of sugar water or honeydew; when the anthill is found, boiling water with a few spoonfuls of oil is poured over it in the evening. Above all, gardens should encourage the multiplication of the golden beetle, an insect known as the gardener's beetle, which wages fierce war not only against the little black ant, but also against a host of other harmful insects. The carabid is all the more useful in this respect as it lives exclusively on the product of its hunt, and does not eat any vegetable substance. FORFICULA OR EARWIG. - This insect is particularly harmful to carnations, gnawing the buds ready for flowering rather than any other food. To prevent this, recently slaughtered sheep's hooves are hung from the stakes supporting the stems of budding carnations in the evening. The earwig spends the whole day withdrawn in holes or under large stones, and only comes out to seek its life at night, when it spreads its folded wings under its horny elytra and flies great distances. If it comes across clumps of carnations, the strong smell of sheep's hooves attached to their stakes attracts it more than that of the carnation, and it retreats to spend the night. Early the next morning, it can be caught there still numb, and destroyed without difficulty. COURTILIÈRE or TAupe-GRillon. - The mole-eater wreaks havoc in the inner layers and heavily smoked flowerbeds of the vegetable garden, by cutting the roots of cultivated plants between two plots of land, not to feed on them, but to create, like the mole, underground galleries where it lives and hunts insects and their larvae, its only food. The various methods used to destroy the cricket are of little use, with the exception of the following procedure. When a garden bed is so infested with crickets that it is impossible to grow anything, which is all too often the case, we pull up any plants that may remain, then soak them in horse or horned beast urine, heated to 40 or 50 degrees. This liquid immediately kills not only crickets and their eggs, but also earthworms and any subterranean insects it can reach. Interrupted crops can then be safely resumed; their main enemy has disappeared. BEETLES. - Gardeners have three species of aphid to contend with: the green aphid, or common aphid, the black aphid, or bean aphid, and the woolly aphid, or apple aphid. Green aphid. - They often appear in prodigious numbers on roses, sucking the young shoots and buds, and on the seedbeds of cruciferous vegetables such as cabbages, cauliflowers, radishes and turnips, sucking the siliques, which very often dry out to the point of sterility. When the aphid invades only a few plants, it is killed by fumigating them with tobacco in the evening or morning, when the air is perfectly still. To give these fumigations to high-stemmed grafted roses attacked by the aphid, a large melon bell is hung over their heads; the smoke produced under this bell would harm the rose if it stayed there too long; as soon as the asphyxiated aphids fall, the bell is removed so that the tobacco smoke dissipates, without giving it time to damage the rose's vegetation. Black aphid. - Naturalists regard this aphid as the same as the previous one; they suppose that its black color is due to the juice of the bean on which it lives and multiplies with the same fecundity as the green aphid. It multiplies so rapidly that plants are usually lost before they can be rescued. If the plantation is not very large, you can remove all the black aphids, which always start by invading the leaves at the top of the plant; these leaves are then removed and placed in a basket to be burned immediately, taking great care not to leave any behind; otherwise, from one evening to the next, you'll find as many black aphids on the beans as you removed, and you'll have to start all over again. Woolly aphid. - This insect is not even remotely related to the previous two; all it has in common with them is its rapid spread and the way it lives at the expense of apple trees, sucking the bark and creating cankers that are often fatal. The woolly aphid rarely attacks apple trees in the fruit garden; it is especially formidable on the large apple trees in orchards whose fruit is used to make cider. In groves, however, it quite often kills the semi-double-flowered apple tree, a charming spring-flowering ornamental. Various caustic liquids are used against the woolly aphid; these liquids have the double disadvantage of not always killing the woolly aphid, preserved by the fleece of white hairs to which it owes its name, and of doing almost as much damage to the bark of the trees to which they are applied as the woolly aphid itself. The only effective remedy is the operation known as coulinage, which has eliminated the woolly adelgid from cider apple orchards in Normandy and Picardy. In winter, coulinage consists of passing a torch made of twisted straw coated with resin over trees infested with woolly aphids. The heat produced by this flame is too short-lived to harm the trees; it scorches the hairs of the woolly aphid, causing it to perish without fail. WILLS. - Plants grown in gardens, as well as vines and fruit trees, are attacked by a multitude of caterpillars; those living in isolation, or dispersing shortly after hatching, are difficult for the gardener to reach; he must rely on insectivorous songbirds to chase them away. To this end, place a hollow stone in a quiet, secluded corner of the grove, always full of water, and a small wooden begging bowl into which a few mealworms are placed once or twice a week. This is enough to attract and fix the warblers and other insect-eating songbirds, who, as a reward for this politeness, seek out the caterpillars that are their main food source when these birds have a brood to raise. At the time of winter pruning, the gardener destroys all the caterpillar nests in the grove and fruit garden, which are easy to distinguish because of the whitish webs they have spun in autumn in order to overwinter under 2 8̀ 2 this shelter; he also makes the more difficult search for the caterpillar egg clusters deposited by the females of various butterflies around the small branches of the trees, and stuck to the bark, by the sticky matter that envelops them. When, despite this care, the trees are invaded by caterpillars whose eggs have escaped his attention, he seizes the moment when, towards evening, the caterpillars gather in clusters at the junction of two large branches, and sprays them with a concentrated soap water that kills them. A strong sprinkling of fresh water on the bark of the tree is necessary after this operation, otherwise the concentrated soap water will do the tree about as much damage as the caterpillars could have done. The above information applies in general to all caterpillars that live on fruit and ornamental trees in horticulture. CABBAGE WORM. - This green caterpillar, mottled black-brown, attacks mainly cultivated plants of the

crucifers, cabbages, turnips and turnips, which it destroys completely. It should be hunted early in the morning, shortly after sunrise, before it has retired to the ground, where it hides to avoid the heat of the day. POULTRY. - The most common caterpillar to attack fruit trees, especially plums, this caterpillar, living in groups and not dispersing to eat, is one of the easiest to find and destroy, even when the trees have taken their foliage. PIRALE. - The pyralid is a very small caterpillar, born from the eggs of a very small butterfly, which caused the vineyards of France to lose many millions, until a winegrower near Beaujeu found a way to destroy it. The method consists of boiling water to scald the vines and stiles into which the moth caterpillar retreats to hibernate. The heat of the boiling water, too short-lived to harm the vine, is enough to kill the moth. When this dangerous caterpillar does appear, the vine must be scalded in winter, while vegetation is completely suspended. The caterpillar of this insect lives inside apples and pears, where it emerges from the egg deposited by the female carpocapsa moth on the head of the newly tied fruit. When fully grown, the caterpillar emerges from the fruit, cutting a cylindrical gallery through its thickness, and retreats to the ground or into cracks in the tree's bark to undergo its final transformations into a butterfly. The number of moths can be significantly reduced by washing the bark of trees with a strong milk of lime during the winter, and by removing some of the soil from the base of the tree and replacing it with new soil before vegetation resumes in spring. CHAPTER XII Gardener's meteorology. Gardener's meteorology. - Phenomena of importance to the gardener. - Winds. - Causes of winds. - Hurricanes. - Wind speed during a storm. - Study of local climate. - Violent winds always harmful to gardens. - Rainfall. - Average amount of rainfall in a given area. - Signs of a wet summer, a dry summer. - Insufficient barometer and hygrometer readings. - Snow. - Produced by the same causes as rain. - Useful for field crops. - Harmful to winter crops in gardens. - Hail. - Hailstorms useless. - Hail insurance disappointment. - Fog. - Dew. - White frost. - Effects of cold. - Heat. - Electrical phenomena. Atmospheric air, at the expense of which cultivated plants subsist for the most part, and which they cannot do without in any case, is the scene of a large number of phenomena whose study constitutes one of the most interesting branches of physics, under the name of meteorology. The gardener undoubtedly has much more to do than devote himself to the in-depth study of meteorology, a study which is sufficient in itself to fill the entire existence of a hard-working man; but there is in this division of physics a set of facts and notions that he cannot dispense with knowing, because he can draw from it at every moment useful indications for the practice of gardening. Winds, rain, snow, hail, fog, dew, white frost, the effects of cold and heat, and electrical phenomena, are the facts of meteorology that the gardener must study in particular. Winds. - Warm air is, for the same volume, significantly lighter than cold air; the colder the air, the heavier it is. In the atmosphere, therefore, there is a continual tendency for the coldest portions to be displaced, as their weight forces them to rush from north to south, to take the place of air masses warmed by their contact with the ground in tropical regions. Another permanent cause of atmospheric mass displacement is the earth's rotational movement. This movement and the differences in temperature at various points on the surface of the globe are the main general causes of winds, whose speed and intensity vary over a wide range, from a light, barely perceptible breeze to a storm whose highest calculated speed is 65 kilometers per hour. The most violent winds periodically prevail in regions close to the equator. When the Spaniards discovered the West Indies, the natives of these islands called the storm's breath hurracan; this word is a veritable onomatopoeia; it depicts the howling of a furious wind and the cracking of the trees it overturns in its path. European nations have adopted this term, modifying its pronunciation to create the French word ouragan. The gardener must make a thorough study of the local climate in his or her area. The direction of the prevailing winds, some of which bring rain, others drought, merits particular attention, so he must frequently consult a weather vane placed on a building visible from inside his garden. Whenever winds exceed the force of even a slightly brisk current of air, they should be considered harmful to gardening, and delicate plants should be protected from their effects by means of strong fences called windbreaks, and various other shelters. It's true that foresters consider high winds to be favorable to tree growth, as they clear them of dead wood and superfluous branches; but in landscape gardening, the gardener must not count on such help; he must prune his trees without waiting for the winds to do this part of his work. When sowing, transplanting or planting, he should consult the state of the atmosphere, and adjust according to what the wind direction allows him to expect in terms of dryness or humidity. Rain. - One of the features of the local climate that the gardener must study most carefully is the average amount of rain that falls each year, and the distribution of rainy days and sunny days between the various months of the year. When, in the first few months, the average is not reached, he can count on a rainy summer and frequent thunderstorms; in the opposite case, he must expect a more prolonged drought than in ordinary years. There isn't a gardener in the world who hasn't made his or her own observations about the signs of approaching rain, snow, drought or severe cold in his or her region, simply by living in the open air. These signs vary according to the proximity or remoteness of the sea or the mountains, which are the main causes of the abundance or scarcity of rain, as well as the severity or mildness of the climate, in relation to latitude. The prognostications formulated in almanacs, and in special treatises on meteorology, have the defect of lacking precision; their application is subject to so many exceptions, that they most often give rise to nothing but errors. When, following a more or less prolonged period of drought, the weather becomes overcast as the full or new moon approaches, this is a fairly certain sign that the weather is about to turn to rain. The indications of the barometer, nor those of the hygrometer representing a moinc taking off or putting back its hood, never announce rain with certainty; a rising or falling barometer simply says that the air is heavier or lighter; the kvgromêtre, which lengthens or shortens as it caps or uncaps the religious person it represents, says that the air is more or less dry, more or less humid; it doesn't necessarily follow that it will soon rain or shine: nothing certain can be deduced from this. Snow. - Snow is frozen rain; when it snows, it rains ice crystallized in the atmosphere, in needles whose confused agglomeration forms flakes. The gardener needs to know by observation which winter winds are most likely to bring snow; he must therefore adjust the direction of his ados in order to avoid snow piling up on his winter salads and those plants, the object of his care, which must overwinter in the open air. For while the farmer is pleased to see a thick blanket of snow covering his winter cereals, to which such a blanket can only do good, the gardener, placed in different conditions, can only see snow as the enemy of his winter crops. Hail. - Hail is the most formidable enemy of all garden crops; in a matter of minutes, it can destroy everything. The gardener has no way of predicting it or preventing its ravages. The paragrels advocated a few years ago consisted of tall poles similar to cocagne masts, topped by a magnetized iron point to which a thick brass wire was attached, guided along the length of the pole and buried in the ground. The usefulness of hail protection was so questionable that it was eventually abandoned. Insurance against hail is no less illusory; self-respecting companies do not insure bells, frames, greenhouses and all the glazed equipment of the gardener, nor the products of his industry; the risks are too great to be covered by even a very high premium. Those who insure against this kind of risk receive premiums as long as there are not too many claims; when there are too many, they go bankrupt. So what can a gardener do against hail? Nothing, as far as his outdoor crops are concerned. For his crops under glass, it's a different story. As long as the thunderstorm season lasts, and any storm can be accompanied by hail, the gardener keeps rolled straw mats within constant reach of his melons and other produce grown under glass and uncovered only in midsummer. With a flick of the wrist, he can spread them over them as a sure-fire protection against hail, unless he moves them after the storm, with the same promptness. Mists. - In spring and autumn, the fog that rises from the earth at dusk and is dissipated by the morning sun is more beneficial than harmful to garden crops; the gardener doesn't have to worry about it. Dew. - Like fog, night dew is beneficial to all garden produce; it refreshes plants and can, to a certain extent, make up for insufficient watering, where it is not possible to water thoroughly. It is due to the condensation of atmospheric humidity on the surface of plant leaves, just as air in an inhabited area condenses on window panes when it's cold outside. White frost. - The phenomenon of white frost often occurs on clear days, when the thermometer suspended in the atmosphere does not drop to the freezing point of water. It frequently occurs in spring on moonlit nights. Hence the bad reputation of the red moon. While it is very true that the moon has nothing to do with late white frosts, which damage or even completely destroy the flowering of fruit trees, the fact is no less real, and gardeners have always been right to oppose the disastrous effects of white frost with all the types of shelter available to them (see Shelter, chap. V). In the chapter on plant diseases, we explained the effects of cold, heat and electrical phenomena, as well as the nature of the lesions that frequently result (see chap. X). CHAPTER XIII Perennial vegetables. Market gardening. - The hortillons of Amiens. - Market gardeners in Paris. - Perpignan. - Nantes. - From Metz. - Cavaillon. - From Pézénas. - Plants for market gardening. - Perennial vegetables. - Vegetables proper. - Root vegetables. - Edible bulb vegetables. - Edible leaf vegetables. - Salads. - Vegetables with edible flowers. - Vegetables with edible fruit. - Asparagus. - Wild asparagus. - Cultivated asparagus. - From Holland. - Ghent. - Ulm. - Vendôme. - Common asparagus. - Nursery culture. - Claws. - Roots. - Root removal. - Planting claws. - Cultivation care. - Sowing. - Harvesting asparagus. - Forced cultivation. The hortillons of Amiens. - Vegetable gardening, generally referred to in France as "maraichère" (market gardening), has been brought to its greatest perfection in the dry marshes of the Somme valley near Amiens, and in those of the Seine valley near Paris. The history of this branch of gardening is of great interest, both in itself, from a horticultural point of view, and in relation to those who practise it, from a moral point of view. The market gardens around Amiens still bear the name of hortillons, obviously derived from the Latin word hortulani (gardeners); this name applies to the land of these gardens and to those who cultivate it; hortillons have had an uninterrupted existence, perfectly proven, since the time of Lorzine domination in Gaul. A few years ago, when a hortillon appeared as a witness in a trial, the presiding judge seized the opportunity to state publicly that, having examined not only the annals of the current courts since they came into existence, but also those of the former parliament of Douai, he had not found the name of a single hortillon involved in a criminal or simply correctional trial. The president's words are a kind of letter of nobility for the hortillons of Amiens. The market gardeners of Paris. - The market gardeners of the Paris region are also remarkable for the high moral standards they maintain in their contact with the halles population, without deviating from the straight and narrow. Family spirit, love of work, deep attachment to their parents and their duties, are also common qualities among Parisian market gardeners. There are families of market gardeners who have been devoted to the same profession for centuries; they have carefully preserved the titles of concessions made in the 21st century by Charles V, known as the Wise, granting marshlands to families who still exist and all of whose members are exclusively gardeners, on condition that they dry them out and sell the produce on the halles floor. Proportionately, the same is true of market-gardening populations in various parts of France, especially around Perpignan (Pyrenées-Orientales) and Nantes (Loire-Inférieure); there, as in the beautiful market gardens of Metz, Cavaillon and Pézénas, gardening families set an example of regular morals, and their names never appear in the annals of justice. In Paris, the fortified enclosure on the one hand, and the encroachment of buildings on the other, have displaced market gardening; it has retreated, but without losing any of its perfection; it is still to the market gardeners of the Paris area that one must come to learn the best that can be done in terms of the most perfect cultivation of vegetable plants. 7. Because of the importance of this crop in feeding the people of the cities, it shares with the farmer the task of bringing the nation's food supplies out of the soil. Not only does the scarcity and high cost of vegetables cause great inconvenience to the worker, but public health is always more or less compromised when the products of market gardening are not abundant and moderately priced in the markets of populous cities. Vegetable classification. - Vegetables are classified into eight sections: 1° perennial vegetables; 2° vegetables whose seeds are eaten; 3° root vegetables; 4° edible bulb vegetables; 5° edible leaf vegetables; 6° salads; 7° edible flower vegetables; 8° edible fruit vegetables. Although the plants in each of these sections do not always belong to the same botanical family, they all require cultivation care that differs little from one to another, which justifies their natural similarity from a gardening point of view. PERENNIAL VEGETABLES. This division includes only two plants, asparagus and artichokes, both of which are widely cultivated, producing an enormous amount of excellent produce for urban consumption. Since all points of our territory have been easily and rapidly connected by rail to all major population centers, large-scale cultivation of perennial vegetables is possible and advantageous where previously it was impracticable and totally unknown, because its products would not have found buyers. ASPARAGUS. - An asparagus patch is indispensable in any vegetable garden, whether grown with the aim of sending produce to market, or just for family consumption. Asparagus is one of the most sought-after and healthiest vegetables a garden can provide. Wild asparagus. - In all our southern departments bordering the Mediterranean, from Antibes to the Spanish border, asparagus grows in the wild, stretching 90 to 100 kilometers from north to south. It is especially abundant in the Var region, where, in spring, we carefully seek out its young shoots, which are the size of a feather pipe, tender green and more flavorful than garden asparagus. In this part of France, asparagus is virtually uncultivated; we make do with what comes naturally along ditches, on the sides of ravines and in hedgerows. Enthusiasts will always miss a large number of them. These grow to heights of two meters and more, and are laden with flowers that give off a strong vanilla scent, a sure sign of their presence. As a result, country folk take Sunday strolls when the wild asparagus is in bloom, and mark the places where it grows, so that they can visit them the following spring. Growing asparagus. - In gardens, asparagus can be grown by sowing or planting; in all cases, it is important to obtain either seeds or seedlings of the best sub-varieties. Asparagus has no distinct species or variety in the strict sense of the term; the most esteemed sub-varieties are Holland, Ghent, Ulm and Vendôme asparagus. Common asparagus is nothing more than Dutch asparagus, reduced to a lesser volume by conditions of soil, climate and cultivation less favorable than those found in its native country. Asparagus seed is usually sown in nurseries, in order to obtain a plant that can be planted after one or two years of preparatory cultivation; it can also be sown in place, in pits prepared in the same way as for planting. The best soil for growing asparagus is very light, but fertile; if you want to grow this plant in strong soil, you must first amend the soil with silica sand and ashes, in high doses. Peaty soil, made suitable for cultivation by the deacidifying action of lime and horse manure, produces excellent asparagus. They are also very good, albeit small, in sandy soils of mediocre fertility. Nursery sowing. - Nursery sowing is done in rows or broadcast, from February 10 to March 20; it can be done much later, but with less chance of success. The seed should be covered with 3 to 4 centimetres of soil; in addition, a centimetre or so of good potting soil from broken layers should be spread over the seedlings, so that if droughts occur and watering is necessary, the soil will not be compacted and hardened on its surface by the water from watering. Sowing in rows is preferable to broadcast sowing, simply because the plant is easier to see when it emerges. The young asparagus plant needs more than any other to be freed from weeds by frequent weeding. When the seed has been broadcast, the plant, which is excessively small and resembles barely visible threads, gets lost under parasitic plants and is partly removed during weeding. This doesn't happen with seedlings in rows; as the place where the seedling should be is known in advance, it's easier to respect it. One-year-old nursery stock is at least as good for planting as two-year-old stock. Nevertheless, opinion is so strongly in favor of the two-year-old nursery stock that, in all countries where asparagus is grown on a large scale, gardeners who raise plants for sale are forced to cultivate them for two years, otherwise they would not be able to sell them. Cultivation care during the second year must be the same as during the first; weeding, hoeing and watering as necessary must not be spared. When the plant is uprooted, great care must be taken to avoid damaging the roots. Asparagus tubers with fleshy divisions called fingers are called claws. These are not asparagus roots in the strict sense of the term; true fibrous roots, rather like those of the violet, extend from between the fingers and plunge into the soil as far as its more or less penetrable nature will allow. It is important to keep these hairy roots as whole as possible when pulling them out. Pulling should only be carried out when everything is ready for planting, so that the claws remain out of the ground for as little time as possible. Planting claws. - Several methods are in use for planting asparagus claws; here is the most commonly used. The ground is divided into plots 1 m 30 cent. wide, separated by intervals of O mêtre 60 cent. If the lay of the land permits, the planks of indeterminate length should be laid from east to west. The surface of the planks is removed to a thickness of 0 m 40 cent; the soil removed is deposited on the intervals. The bottom of the planks is then deeply ploughed and widely smoked with half-consumed manure. If the subsoil is damp and impermeable, a bed of dry broken branches, chopped broom or heather, about a decimeter thick, is placed at the very bottom of the pit, forming a sort of drainage system that prevents the ends of the asparagus' fibrous roots from coming into contact with the stagnant subsoil humidity. On top of the ploughed and smoked bottom soil, a bed of soil from the pit itself is spread, taken up at the intervals; this bed should be one decimeter thick. Once these arrangements have been made, the surface of the pit soil is levelled with a fine, close-toothed rake, and the claws are planted. Two lines are first drawn with a chalk line at o meter 16 cent. from the edge of the pit, on each side. The interval between these two lines is then divided in two by a third line occupying exactly the middle of the pit. On these three lines, we mark the place of the claws, spaced o meter 40 cent. apart and arranged in a chessboard pattern. A good handful of potting soil forming a small mound is placed at each spot where a claw is to be planted. The purpose of this precaution is to prevent a void forming under the claw's tray, where air could enter and expose it to rot. The fingers of the claw and its fibrous roots are spread out in all directions, taking care not to break them; the whole is then covered with a decimetre of good soil, over which a little compost is spread, the fine rake is passed, and the operation is completed. Many authors criticize this method of planting asparagus, even though it is the most common, because it has two serious disadvantages: firstly, the air does not circulate well in asparagus pits, which can harm their vegetation to a greater or lesser extent; and secondly, gardening is more difficult in such pits than on boards set at the level of the surrounding soil. However, in ordinary circumstances, when the soil is of good quality and light rather than strong, these disadvantages are offset by the undeniable advantage of having on hand, for several years, the soil necessary for the annual replenishments required by asparagus cultivation. By not exceeding the depth indicated above, the advantages significantly outweigh the disadvantages. Asparagus can also give very good results when planted flat in rows on well-ploughed, well-smoked soil, with 0.60 m intervals between beds. This should be done when the soil is strong rather than light, and when the subsoil is of poor quality or saturated with stagnant humidity dangerous to asparagus roots. All planting conditions remain the same. For subsequent cultivation, the soil needed for annual replanting is taken from the gaps, which become deeper and deeper, and eventually deep gullies, while the asparagus beds become higher and higher. In autumn of the first year, the pit floor, which must have been kept free of weeds by frequent weeding, is topped with a layer of long manure equivalent to half a manure. The dried-out stalks of young asparagus are cut off at ground level, and everything remains in this state until the following spring. The intervals between the spring and autumn beds have been used for crops that don't grow very high, and don't provide shade, such as bush beans, onions and various types of lettuce. From the end of February, the asparagus beds receive a very superficial treatment, carefully applied with an iron-tined fork, in order to mix into the soil the manure decomposed by the weather during the winter. Immediately afterwards, enough soil is taken from the spaces between the beds to refill their surface by 5 centimetres. The same care is taken during the second and third years, after which the asparagus can be harvested. Sowing in place. - When it is difficult to obtain good one- or two-year-old asparagus from a nursery for planting, sow two or three seeds in each hole, in the pits laid out exactly as described above and spaced at the same intervals. When the plant is well established, we pull out the two weakest plants, and leave in place the one that seems to be the most promising. The rest of the cultivation of asparagus beds created by sowing is very similar to that of beds formed by planting. The only difference is that, when sown, the first harvest is not due until the fourth year at the earliest, and the fifth year in less fertile soils: this is a year later than with claw plantations. Harvesting asparagus. - The way asparagus is harvested varies from country to country. In northern France, like the Dutch and Belgians, consumers only want asparagus that is completely etiolated, entirely white, and can be eaten almost whole. To get them in this state, you have to cut them between two plots of land as soon as the top shows, and before it has had time to color on contact with light. Around Paris and in the rest of France, asparagus is only harvested when it has sprouted a shoot about a decimetre long, lightly coloured green and purple. The knife used to cut the asparagus should not be too long; during harvesting, care must be taken to avoid damaging the central plate of the claws, which could cause numerous voids in the boards; this harvest can only be well done by dexterous and practiced hands. You must not, through misguided greed, prolong the asparagus harvest too far into the season; especially during the first three or four years, you will ruin the plantations. Forced cultivation. - In the vicinity of large cities, it is advantageous to treat asparagus during the winter by forced cultivation; this method of growing asparagus is also easy to practice by the amateur gardener, who grows only for his own consumption. The best asparagus for forced cultivation are those that have been planted for five or six years, so that almost all the soil deposited in the gaps between the beds has been used for annual replanting, and the asparagus pits are completely filled in. At a depth of Om.40, gullies are dug in place of the plank gaps; these are filled with fermenting stable manure, which is lightly moistened and trampled underfoot. The boards are then covered with a thick layer of fresh manure, on top of which are placed chests covered with glass frames. Economy frames, covered with canvas or oiled coarse paper, can be used for the same purpose. The operation can be started in December and continued until February; the asparagus beds are only forced successively, so that forced asparagus can be harvested throughout the winter, when it is most valuable. From time to time, we lift the frames and disturb the manure to watch for the first asparagus shoots. As soon as they appear, the manure cover inside the frames is removed; new manure replaces that in the gutters, whose heat is exhausted; doormats or dry litter protect the asparagus from the cold of the night, and are moved from over the frames during the coldest hours of the day, as long as it doesn't freeze. If care is taken not to force it two years in a row, and to give it a year's rest, asparagus can be forced every two years without ruining it or shortening the duration of the planting. It sometimes happens that an asparagus plantation made in good soil, with good manure, a vigorous plant, and all the necessary care, fails completely. This is usually due to a peculiarity worth mentioning. Asparagus is one of those plants that cannot succeed itself, either because it leaves excretions of a special kind in the soil, or because of some other cause that escapes observation. The land on which an asparagus plantation has done its time is excellent for any other crop; but asparagus can only return and prosper there after an interval of at least ten years; the earth is tired of asparagus, say the gardeners; it must relieve itself with other crops. So, when planning an asparagus plantation, it is essential to ensure that the land has not produced asparagus for at least ten years. CHAPTER XIV Continuation of Perennial Vegetables. Artichoke cultivation. - Provence artichoke. - Gros camus from Tours or Brittany. - Gros vert de Laon. - Artichoke hay. - Multiplication by eyelets. - Seedling propagation. - Planting. - Site preparation. - Spacing. - Ridging. - Winter care. - Annual artichoke cultivation. - Advantageous conditions. - Preservation of artichoke heads in winter. - Duration of planting. ARTICHOW. - The artichoke ranks second only to asparagus as a perennial vegetable, occupying an important place in the vegetable garden for several years running. It is a plant with long, strong roots, closely related to the thistle, native to North Africa and southern Europe; it can only succeed in soil that is both rich and deep. Artichoke species and varieties. - Three distinct varieties of artichoke are grown in France: the petit artichaut de Provence, of which there are two sub-varieties, one purple, the other pale green; the gros camus de Tours, also known as the artichaut de Bretagne, and the gros vert de Laon, the most esteemed and widely grown of the three. The artichoke of Provence is sometimes referred to as artichaut sans foin, to distinguish it from the other species: this expression is inappropriate; there can be no artichoke sans foin in the true sense of the term. Artichokes are eaten with the base of the leaves, which are not true leaves but calicinous scales, and the base or common receptacle that these scales surround. What we call artichoke hay is the reunion of the flowers before their full bloom. Left to the natural course of their vegetation, these flowers would blossom into a silky, violet aigrette, followed by thistle-like seeds. The artichoke head, when picked and delivered for consumption, is a compound flower bud. In this bud, the hay is short and inconspicuous in the small Provence artichoke; it is a little longer in the large Camus artichoke and very long, very hard and very tight in the Laon artichoke, filling the entire interior. The distinctive feature of the artichoke gros camus is the shape of its scales, which are indented at the top and laid one on top of the other. Less sensitive to the cold than the artichoke from Provence, which does not succeed outside the olive and mulberry region, the artichoke gros camus is very well suited to the climate of central France; it thrives particularly in the Loire Valley and is only half successful in the climate of Paris. The artichaut gros vert de Laon is distinguished by the shape of its pointed scales, which diverge in all directions; it is the only variety that can be grown with any chance of success in the climate of the Seine valley and to the north of this valley. Multiplication. - Artichokes are propagated by separating the offshoots or "œilletons", detached from the base of old stocks, each of which can supply 8 to 10 in the spring; these œilletons, used for planting, maintain the varieties in all their purity. Artichokes can also be propagated by sowing their seeds, but the seedlings do not always reproduce the same variety as those from which they were sown. Eyelets. - Around March each year, when the cold weather seems to have passed and the artichokes have nothing to fear from the return of late frosts, the artichoke stalks are removed to detach the eyelets, which are separated by pulling them up and down, so that each one retains a heel, which sometimes has roots. Whether it has roots or not, this plant is usually used as if it did; they are true cuttings that rarely fail to take root. In dry, slightly thin soils, where it may happen that not all the carnations root, which would leave gaps in the planting, they are planted in nurseries, very close to each other; they are only definitively put in place when they have formed good roots, thus ensuring the evenness of the planting. Artichoke seedlings - When, following an extraordinarily harsh winter, most of the artichokes have frozen, and consequently there are not enough eyelets to renew the plantations, we are forced to resort to seedlings. This is a foreseen circumstance, as a result of which far more seeds are sown than would be necessary if all the seedlings could be used for planting. In this way, you can be sure of having enough after cutting back on the one whose stiff, sharply-divided leaves indicate a tendency to turn to thistle, as gardeners say. To avoid being caught unprepared, even though artichoke seed retains its germinative properties for 5 or 6 years, it's a good idea to let a few of the most beautiful heads flower and bear seed every year. The seed not used for sowing can be used in place of rennet; it curdles milk very well without imparting any foreign flavor to the cheese. Planting. - The soil for planting artichokes must be prepared by deep ploughing and heavy manuring. In the South of France, where few cattle are raised, and where manure is therefore always in short supply, artichokes are planted in pits om.50 deep and of the same width; manure is mixed with the earth removed from the pits, which is then filled with the same well-smoked earth; the artichokes are planted 1 meter apart in all directions; the half of the soil where their roots should not extend, receives no manure. In the rest of France, where manure is less scarce, the entire artichoke plot is smoked and planted 1 meter apart in all directions; their roots eventually invade almost the entire plot, and the manure benefits them in full. The plant's recovery cannot be guaranteed unless it is thoroughly wetted at the time of planting, and watered thoroughly at least every two days until vegetation is vigorously growing. Plants treated in this way, either with eyelets or seedlings, planted in April, produce their first harvest in autumn. Wintering. - Winter is the critical period for artichoke cultivation, at least in the climate of Paris; in the Midi, artichoke plantations spend the winter in the open air without any kind of protection. From the Loire Valley to our northern frontiers, artichoke gros camus and gros vert de Laon can only overwinter with continuous care, which doesn't always save them. At the end of autumn, before the first frosts arrive, you need to start buttering the artichokes. Choose dry weather for this work, so that the mounded earth around each clump, whose outer leaves have been cut back beforehand, is as moisture-free as possible. Mounding not only protects artichokes from the cold, it also helps the formation and development of eyelets around the stump. Once the work is done, a generous supply of dry leaves and litter is spread in the spaces between the mounded artichokes, where it remains until the time of a serious frost. The artichokes must then be covered, and carefully uncovered during the coldest hours of the day. As long as it doesn't freeze, the center of the artichoke clump must remain uncovered, otherwise it will wither and rot. Their preservation depends on the gardener's careful covering and uncovering of artichokes throughout the winter. When spring returns, the leaves and litter are removed for good; the mounds are dismantled, the artichoke patch is levelled, the clumps are grommeted and two of the best grommets are left on each for annual production. Plantations treated in this way produce three good harvests, after which they must be renewed. They could be left for another year, but in the fourth year they would only produce tough artichokes of a volume below the normal size for their variety. Annual calture. - In gardens with rich, cool, very deep soil, you can avoid the embarrassment of winter storage by planting artichokes every year, and requiring only one harvest per planting. By sparing neither manure at the time of planting, nor watering throughout the summer, the artichokes all rise, without exception, before the end of autumn; the abundance and beauty of the produce make the harvest very advantageous, after which the clumps are pulled up, and the land is given over to other crops. Only the number of plants needed to produce sufficient carnations the following spring are kept. This is the method used since time immemorial in the market gardens around Amiens; this method, which boils down to treating artichoke cultivation like that of an annual plant, is applicable wherever the same soil and cumat conditions prevail. When, at the start of winter, a patch of artichokes that has reached the end of its third year, or has been treated as an annual crop as we have just seen, retains a certain number of half-formed heads, they can be delivered successively for consumption throughout the winter, by uprooting the clods and transporting them to a cellar, a healthy cellar, or any other frost-free location. If they retain enough soil around the roots, and if they are also buried in fresh sand, they will keep as well as at the natural harvest time. In Paris, artichokes preserved in this way fetch very good prices in winter. When artichokes have yielded their annual crop, the stems should be cut off at ground level, at the root collar. If any are left, the growth of the eyelets will suffer, and the following year's harvest will be more or less compromised. You should not hesitate to sacrifice part of the harvest of small artichokes growing sideways, which are intended to be eaten raw under the name of artichokes à la poivrade, on artichoke plants that still have several years to live. If the artichoke vines are exhausted by overabundant production of low value, by the second year they will not yield half of what could be obtained with care. CHAPTER XV Vegetables proper Vegetables proper - Peas. - Principles of cultivation. - Peas for Saint Catherine's Day. - Candlemas. - Wood, coal and peat ashes for growing peas. - Sowing times. - Cultivation care. - Pea weevil. - Species and varieties. - Early peas. - Late peas. - Snow peas. - Forced cultivation of peas. - Chick peas. - Beans. - Transplant cultivation. - Forced cultivation. - Dwarf beans without oars. - String beans. - Mangetout beans. - Marsh bean. - De Windsor. - Julienne. - Common lentil. - Queen lentil. In the language of botanists, the word vegetable does not have the same meaning as in ordinary language. A vegetable is a pod- or silique-shaped fruit that gives its name to the numerous legume family. Vegetables proper are therefore those whose seeds are eaten in fruits that are true vegetables. Peas, beans, broad beans and lentils are among the leguminous plants grown in the kitchen garden for human consumption. PEAS - Fresh shelled peas, known as petits pois, are one of the most sought-after vegetables, the return of which is eagerly awaited each year by all consumer classes. The cultivation of peas, whatever the species, is subject to a number of simple and easy-to-apply principles, without which it cannot produce good results. The best soil for growing peas is light rather than strong, yet sufficiently fertile; this plant does not tolerate the immediate contact of fresh manure; it does very well in soil that has been smoked the previous year, and which has yielded some kind of crop on manuring. Peas sown in strong, recently-smoked soil produce long stems and abundant foliage; they flower poorly and produce almost no grain. Peas are among the plants that cannot be cultivated for several years in a row in the same place, otherwise their products become bitter and cease to be edible; this is what happened in several communes around Paris, where peas were, a few years ago, grown very extensively to supply the capital's markets; By persisting in constantly sowing peas on the same land, the gardeners in these communes ended up harvesting only small quantities of inferior quality produce; they were forced to give up altogether on a crop that no longer covered their costs. In any well-managed garden, the square where a crop of peas has been harvested should be given over to other crops for at least a year, after which the peas can return without inconvenience. Saint Catherine and Candlemas peas. - Peas are native to warm climates, and can only be grown in the open air between the time of year when it no longer freezes and the time when it doesn't freeze yet. Nevertheless, a few sub-varieties that are a little hardier than the others can be sown at the foot of a wall in the midday sun, towards the end of November: this is what gardeners around Paris call "Sainte-Catherine peas". Under the same conditions, peas can also be sown in the first days of February: these are known as Chandeleur peas. The peas resulting from these sowings always die as a result of frosts, against which their situation only half guarantees them. But this doesn't mean they're lost: their roots don't freeze, unless there's an exceptionally severe cold spell; as soon as the weather is fine, two stems emerge to the right and left of the root collar. These stems flower and produce shelled peas, long before peas of the same species sown in the open air after the last late spring frosts. In any case, St Catherine's Day peas, like Candlemas peas, can only produce produce you can't count on too much, so only a limited amount of space should be devoted to them each year, choosing the best possible exposure. If there is a lack of land at the foot of south-facing walls, this can be made up by planting a row of stakes in an east-west direction, to which straw mats can be attached, forming a sort of temporary espalier in front of which the peas are protected from the cold, as well as they can be. Although strong soils are unsuitable for peas, this vegetable is so necessary for cooking in summer that it should not be neglected in gardens with very strong soils, cultivated to provide a family living in the country with its vegetable supply. In this case, the plot where peas are to be grown is amended with a heavy dose of wood ash. The best for this purpose are the charcoal ashes collected from kitchen stoves, and from those where laundresses heat their irons. If these ashes are not available, coal ashes, properly sifted to separate the slag, and even peat ashes, in cantons where this type of heating is generally used, can be substituted for wood or charcoal ashes to make soils that are naturally too strong, suitable for producing good quality peas. Wherever possible, a small quantity of ashes mixed into the soil at the time of sowing peas is always very beneficial to their rapid growth; it helps to make them productive and gives quality to their produce. Sowing. - Cultivation care. - Most garden peas are sown in March and April. In localities where long droughts are not to be feared, peas can be sown in the open ground from March 15 onwards throughout the summer season, but no later than July 15. The last sowings should be done with peas of the earliest varieties. Peas sown later will grow beautiful stems full of flowers, but these flowers will not produce grain. Fertilization of the pea flower is only possible during the day, under the influence of bright light; flowers that bloom when the days have already become too short are only imperfectly fertilized; the siliques or pods that follow them are empty, or nearly so. A fortnight or so after they have emerged, peas need very careful hoeing, after which, if they are of a dwarf species, they can simply be left to the natural course of their vegetation, until the time comes to harvest their produce. In gardens, when peas are in full bloom, it is always useful to summer the plants, i.e. to remove the top of the stem with the flowers and flower buds it usually bears. Most of these flowers would be sterile; removing them, far from reducing the harvest, tends to increase it by making the pods at the bottom and middle of the stem fuller and fuller. After hoeing, species with more or less long stems need to be supported by oars to which the peas cling by the tendrils of their leaves, which makes them much more productive than if they dragged unsupported on the ground, as in open field cultivation where the use of oars is not possible: too many would be needed. We etè̂te rowed peas like the others and with the same result. Everyone is keen, and rightly so, to have the earliest peas in their garden. Apart from the earliness specific to each species, between which there is plenty to choose, very early peas can be obtained by the following procedure. As the peas flower and bear fruit, instead of removing the full pods, as is done to consume their contents, the first peas are left to ripen, and to accelerate their maturity, not only the top, but the entire upper part of the stalks is removed, leaving only 3 or 4 pods at most. Peas from these pods used for seed the following year gain in earliness over those of their species, for which the same measure has not been taken. As the flowering period of peas is very long, the pods arrive successively at the point where they can be harvested; the gardener would suffer a considerable loss on this product if he did not take great precautions when harvesting peas, a task that can only be entrusted to trained hands. The stem of the pea is tender and fragile, especially at the bottom, when the pods begin to be harvested; this stem is held to the ground only by delicate, short roots, which give way at the slightest effort. If, when the first pods are picked, the plant loaded with half-full pods and flowers ready to open has broken or been torn off, the rest of the harvest is lost. Insect enemy of peas. - The pea's insect enemy is a beetle called the bruchid, whose female bites into the pods to lay one of her eggs in each newly-formed grain. The larva of the bruche lives inside the pea, gnawing its substance, undergoing its transformations and emerging as a perfect insect. Peas whose cotyledons have been pierced by the bruchid larva should not be considered as lost; more often than not, this larva, commonly known as the pea worm, does not attack the germ, so that pierced peas can be used for sowing just as well as others. Ripe peas, stored as seed grains, retain their germinative properties for several years; they should only be shelled when ready for use. Species and varieties. - There are many species and varieties of peas, usually divided into early and late varieties. The best early peas are the early dwarf pea, of which there are three varieties: the early dwarf from around Paris, the dwarf from Holland and the dwarf from Brittany; the green dwarf from Prussia and the wrinkled dwarf are less early than the previous ones; all of them are recommended only for their rapid vegetation, which makes it possible to obtain peas very early, but in small quantities; they are generally not very productive. Other early peas include Michaux, or petit pois de Paris, Michaux de Hollande, Ruelle and Prince-Albert, an English variety of excellent quality. The most esteemed of the late peas are the large sweet dwarf, the Marly pea and the Clamart pea, the best of all the late peas; the latter two need to be supported by large oars. The late pea series also includes Knight's finger pea and wrinkled pea, two very good English varieties, the giant pea and the large Norman green pea. The latter, whose seeds remain green when fully ripe, is mainly grown for consumption as a dry vegetable, and is found more frequently in the field than in the garden. In addition to these two series, there are many other varieties of peas known as goulus, mange-tout or sans parchment. The distinctive feature of these peas is the absence of parchment or tough skin inside the pods, which means they can be eaten without shelling. Their cultivation is not widespread in France, except towards our northern border; in the rest of France, they are preferred to early or late shelling peas, whose flavor is indeed more delicate. Snow peas are grown in the same way as other peas. Forced cultivation. - Since the establishment of the railroads, which bring the various points of the territory closer together and put them in communication with the major cities, the cultivation of early peas on beds and under frames has lost much of its importance; Paris receives early peas from the Midi whose cost price makes it impossible to compete with peas from forced cultivation. Nevertheless, as it is always a pleasure for the amateur gardener to be able to produce early peas for his own consumption, we present here the procedures for their forced cultivation. The most successful are early dwarf peas, selected from the least developed and earliest varieties. Sow in the first days of January, either in the field, 8. on a warm layer covered with 30 centimeters of good light soil mixed with compost, or in a nursery on a similar layer. In the latter case, the peas should be sown very thickly, pulled out as soon as they are 5 to 6 centimetres high, and transplanted in rows onto other layers prepared in advance to receive them. As the aim of this crop is not to harvest as many peas as possible, but only to harvest them as early as possible, we pinch off the top of the stems as soon as they have 3 or 4 flowers. Another operation necessary to the success of forced cultivation is to lay the pea stems down before they begin to flower, which is done by placing a lath over them to keep them flat for a day or two. When the lath is removed, the upper part of the stems straightens out on its own; the lower part remains flat. This curvature of the stems contributes significantly to hastening fruiting. CHICKEN PEAS. - A plant closely related to peas, but of a different genus, known as chickpea, is widely cultivated in the south of France. In the Paris climate, its grain, which has a very pronounced, distinctive flavor, does not ripen; it must be harvested half-ripe. The thick husk makes chickpeas rather difficult to digest, but they make excellent purée. Chickpeas can only be sown in the open air around April 15 in the Paris region; in the Midi, they are sown in autumn; they survive the winter very well in our southern departments, flowering early in spring and producing ripe produce towards the end of summer. The plant is low, stocky and doesn't need oars. BEANS. - Beans are easier to grow than peas, in that they are equally at home in heavy and light soils, provided they are well fertilized, and their yields do not decrease in quantity or quality when they are grown several times in a row on the same site. Beans are much more sensitive to cold than peas; they can only be sown outdoors in the Paris climate between April 15th and 20th. Beans are sown either in clumps or in rows; clump sowing is the most common; 3, 5 or 7 beans are sown per clump, depending on how large the plants need to grow, so that they eventually cover the whole plot. A little wood ash, coal ash or peat moss, mixed into the soil at sowing time, is no less useful for beans than it is for the various types of peas. When part of the pods are harvested before the grain has formed, to obtain what are known as green beans, care must be taken not to give the plants too many shocks; they are more resistant and have better roots than peas. Beans can continue to be sown in the vegetable garden until mid-summer, when the year is not too dry. Late-sown crops are unable to ripen before the first frosts, which destroy them; but their yield of fresh green and shelled beans is as abundant as that of spring-sown beans. When you want to harvest the first beans early, sow a few liters very close together on a warm or hot bed under a frame, or more simply on a pile of manure covered with 20 to 25 centimeters of soil at the top. These seedlings are sown at the end of March, at a time when beans cannot vegetate in the open air. A good covering of straw mats or bedding is spread over the seedlings, which are removed whenever the temperature permits. Beans sprout en masse and remain in a stationary state, as long as care is taken not to over-water them. As soon as the weather is favorable, they are transplanted, two by two, in rows, at the foot of a wall facing south; they gain in earliness all the time they would have taken to emerge and take their first leaves in the open air. Beans are also forced in winter, using the same method as for peas; the only difference is that forced beans don't want to be laid down or topped. The list of bean varieties grown in gardens is very long, and comprises three distinct sections: 1° dwarf beans, without oars; 2° oar beans; 3° snap beans. Species and varieties. - The best dwarf or pole beans are flageolet, Soissons dwarf, sabre dwarf, American white dwarf and Bollande dwarf. The latter is preferred as the earliest of all for forced cultivation. Flageolet is unrivalled as a green bean and as a fresh hulling bean. Among the pole beans grown mainly for dry harvesting, the best is the large pole Soissons bean; then, in order of quality, the saber bean, the Swiss bean and its many sub-varieties, the Prague bean, the runner bean and the Lima bean. The last two, more remarkable for the abundance than the delicacy of their products, are especially productive in the Midi; their roots, annual in the Paris climate, are perennial in that of the Mediterranean shores. These turnip-like roots are pulled out at the onset of winter; they are stored in the cellar and replanted in spring, like the dahlias in our gardens. Among snap beans, the best are the pre-domme or prudomme bean, and the princess bean, which is widely grown in Belgium and northern France. Beans of all kinds, harvested dry and intended as seed grains, are kept in their pods and only shelled at sowing time. If you grow beans in one of our southern departments, you'll need to make room in your garden for the runner bean, which is well known throughout the rest of France as an ornamental plant, thanks to its beautiful clusters of scarlet flowers. This variety is not fixed; if you sow runner beans with dark purple grain, marbled with light purple, you'll always harvest a few siliques filled with all-white beans, but of the same volume as the purple ones. In autumn, when the stems die after yielding their harvest, the root, the shape and size of a long turnip, does not die; it is uprooted in November to be stored in the cellar in fresh sand and replanted in March; it grows, flowers and bears fruit in the same way for an indeterminate number of years. As an edible vegetable, the runner bean is not of the highest quality; but in the Paris climate, as in the Midi, it can be grown in large quantities in the kitchen garden, to convert its grain into cooked flour. To do this, the harvested, ripe grain is boiled in water, without salt, skinned, crushed in a marble mortar and ground to a paste. This paste, dried in the open air, is pulverized with a rolling pin; it is preserved in bottles and used to make excellent and economical soups or purées. BEANS. - Fava beans never take up much space in the vegetable garden, and are sown in rows or clumps from February 15th to April 15th. They are happy in just about any type of soil - a slightly strong, fresh, well-smoked soil is best. When the pods in the middle of the stem are well formed, remove the top with its flowers, which should not produce seeds; this removal hastens the formation of beans in the lower pods. When the beans have been harvested at half their normal volume, so that they can be eaten fresh, the stems can be cut off at ground level; if they are not allowed to suffer from drought, they grow back from the stalk and give a second harvest in autumn, but this harvest is not very abundant. In the vegetable garden, we grow the marsh bean, the long-podded Windsor bean and the little julienne bean, a dwarf species of excellent quality. LENTILS. - Lentils are sown in April, in rows spaced 25 centimeters apart, because the plant remains small; they are not very productive, each pod containing only one or two lentils. This vegetable can only be eaten dry; lentils only retain their germinative properties for two years. Two types of lentils are grown: the common lentil and the smaller, reddish-brown queen lentil, both of which are cultivated in the same way and have the same properties. CHAPTER XVI Root vegetables. Carrots, Turnips, Parsnips. Root vegetables. - Carrot. - Origin. - Sowing. - Choice of seed. - Broadcast sowing. - Sowing in rows. - Spacing. - Théridion; spider mite. - Means of protecting young carrots from attack. - Soot. - Corporal tobacco. - Forced cultivation. - Uprooting. - Preservation. - Species and varieties. - Turnip. - Little-used spring seedlings. - Summer sowing. - Time of uprooting. - Grains. - Species and varieties. - Parsnips. - Cultivation. - Common parsnip. - Metz parsnip. Vegetables whose roots are eaten play an important role in the daily diet of all classes of the population. This section includes carrots, turnips, parsnips, radishes, salsify, scorzonera, scolyme and beet. Potatoes and Jerusalem artichokes are also included in this section, although the edible parts of these plants are tubers rather than roots. CARROT. - M. Vilmorin's fine experiments in 1837 leave no doubt as to the origin of the carrot; seedlings of wild carrot, renewed under the same growing conditions as those of other vegetable plants, have given him in three or four generations all the varieties of cultivated carrots, red, white and yellow. These varieties are obviously the result of the art of gardening, which has been able to maintain and improve them. Carrots are propagated by sowing their seeds; to preserve the purity of these varieties, particular care must be taken when harvesting the seeds. The best roots of each species, selected for this purpose in autumn, are set aside in a frost-free gauge and planted the following spring at a distance of one meter in all directions. As the umbels successively ripen their seeds, they are harvested and gathered by the stems into bundles that are hung in a dry place. The seeds remain in this state until they are sown; only then are they peeled, and the seeds at the edges of each umbel are separated out; these are always the largest and best fertilized; the seeds in the center of the umbels are smaller and imperfectly fertilized; when they are used for sowing, some of these seeds do not germinate; the products obtained from those that do germinate are never of the highest quality. Carrot seed retains its germinative properties for at least three years; unless absolutely necessary, carrot seed harvested the previous year should not be sown. Although cultivated carrots are generally biennial, some seedlings behave like annuals and go to seed in their first summer; this is a loss for the gardener; any roots that do go to seed become tough and woody on the inside. If you were to harvest the seed of these prematurely-risen carrots, it would be the surest way of producing completely degenerate produce. Annual carrots, which flower prematurely, are always numerous in seedlings made from one-year-old seed; two- or three-year-old seed should therefore be preferred. Sowing. - Carrot seed is sown in rows or broadcast; for even sowing, it's a good idea to first rub the seed between the hands, handful by handful, with fine, dry sand, so as to wear away the roughness; it is then easier to distribute evenly over the whole surface of the ground to be sown. Broadcast sowing is preferable when carrots are to be eaten before they reach full size, as a spring vegetable, because the plant, if it has not sprouted at equal distances from one another, will nevertheless end up being suitably spaced out by the pulling up of carrots which successively become fit for consumption. Sowing in rows is preferable for species that are allowed to grow to full size, and which are to be harvested in autumn for use as a winter vegetable. The plants of the latter species are thinned out two or three times; the young carrots from these thinnings are used either for cooking or as rabbit food. The rows are spaced 15 to 20 centimetres apart, depending on how big the carrots need to be. Thinning stops when the carrots are about as far apart as the rows are, i.e. when they are 15 to 20 centimetres apart in all directions; this is the distance they need to grow without getting in each other's way. No matter how it is sown, carrot seed should not be buried deeply; simply mix it into the soil with the tines of a rake; then spread a layer of one or two centimetres of dry, pulverized loam over it. When the soil is very light, it is useful to firm it up after sowing carrot seeds, either by tapping the surface of the planks with the back of a spade, or by walking on them with square planks where the foot fits by means of a rope whose end the gardener holds in each of his hands. This way of treading seeded soil, when it needs to be firmed up by compaction, is widely used in Belgium and throughout northern France; it is quick and gives the soil a very even pressure over its entire surface. Theridion. - Newly emerged carrots are attacked by the black spider mite, whose real name is theridion. The smell of carrots attracts them from afar, and as they multiply rapidly, young carrot beds are soon covered with them. This insect, eager for anything sweet, bites and sucks at the plant's collar, causing it to perish. When the soil is fertile and the temperature favorable, carrots grow so fast that they are soon beyond the reach of the theridion, which is unable to damage them once their tissue has reached a certain consistency. On the other hand, if the soil is not very fertile, especially if the temperature is unfavorable, carrots grow slowly and theridia have plenty of time to take advantage of them. In this case, everything is devoured and it becomes necessary to renew the seedlings. Soot and tobacco infusion are used to prevent the proliferation of theridia. Soot, which is readily available in fairly large quantities at low cost, is diluted in water and sprinkled morning and evening on recently emerged carrots infested with theridia. This is just about the only method applicable to larger seedlings. Although soot does not destroy all theridia, it significantly reduces their numbers, which may be enough to save carrot seedlings. Smoking tobacco infusion can only be used on small seedlings, especially on seedlings on beds, when they are invaded by theridia. The best tobacco for this purpose is the strongest, known as "corporal's tobacco". Put about 60 grams into a 12-liter watering can. Boiling water is poured over the tobacco; it is not used until it has completely cooled. Forced cultivation. - Carrots are best grown in a bed or under a frame; the bed should be filled with 20 to 25 centimeters of good potting soil. Sow several times during the winter, to have young carrots ready for consumption in the first days of spring; this is a highly-prized early vegetable. Forced cultivation of carrots on a bed and under a frame is very easy; sow fairly lightly, thin out the plant if it is too tight, give it air whenever the weather permits, and take the usual precautions against the cold, taking care not to over-water. Carrots for winter supply could, in a pinch, be left unharvested; in the Paris climate, they would only freeze in years of exceptionally severe cold. This crop is removed not so much to preserve it from the cold, which it does not fear, as to be able to give the soil where the carrots have vegetated the necessary preparations for other crops. Harvesting is carried out either with a spade or a flat-tined fork; the latter is the best instrument for harvesting very long carrots, which are difficult to avoid damaging when using a spade. Immediately after harvesting, cut off the leaves and collar, and stack the carrots in a healthy cellar, in alternating beds of fresh sand. If, towards the end of winter, the carrots begin to sprout etiolated leaves, as soon as this is noticed, the piles should be dismantled, what remains of the crown cut off, and the carrots placed back in fresh sand. In this way, the carrots are preserved until the new harvest follows on from the previous one. Species and varieties. - Garden-grown carrots are naturally classified into three divisions: red, yellow and white. The most esteemed of the reds are the short red from Holland, the English red from Altringham, the half-long red, or Meaux carrot, and the common long red. The rouge courte de Hollande, of which gardeners around Paris have obtained a very good sub-variety that they grow in preference to any other, is the best of all for forced cultivation on beds and for the first spring sowings in the open air. Altringham red and Meaux red, both semi-long, are prized for their tangy flavor and beautiful color. Since the fresh dried vegetable industry has expanded greatly, especially to supply armies in the field and ships' crews on long voyages, these two varieties of carrot have been grown on a large scale in the marshes around Paris. Of all the carrots, the common red and the pale red of Flanders keep best from one year to the next. Of the yellow varieties, only the short yellow and the long Achicourt are grown in gardens, both of which are mainly found in the vegetable gardens of northern France. In the white series, the best are the blanche commune, the blanche de Breteuil, shaped like a large spinning top, and the blanche transparente, obtained from seed in Mulhouse in 1849, which is excellent but not very widespread in gardens. The other white species, grown mainly as fodder roots for cattle feed, are only allowed in the kitchen garden by way of exception. Apart from these three series of carrots, the almost black purple Spanish carrot, also known as the Indian black carrot, is grown more as a curiosity than for its real value. TURNIP. - The turnip is the most common and least sought-after of the root vegetables, so it never occupies much space in the vegetable garden. If the soil in your garden is stronger than light, it is essential to amend it with a high dose of fine silica sand, if you want to grow good turnips. Sowing and cultivation. - The turnip is generally treated as a biennial plant, even though, left to its own devices and sown in spring, it is an annual, i.e. ready to flower and bear seed in the year it is sown. For this reason, turnip seeds are usually only sown in gardens from June to August. The root, the only edible part of the plant, has time before the first frosts to acquire all the volume specific to each species, and the plant's vegetation is stopped by the cold before the flower stalks form. However, spring sowing can be done in March and April, using two- or three-year-old seed. In cool or well-watered soil, in a climate not prone to extreme heat or prolonged drought in summer, spring sowings can produce quite good turnips in summer. Turnip seedlings are very vulnerable to flea beetles, a small beetle that eats away at the emerging seminal leaves of turnips that have just emerged. The best way to prevent flea beetles is to sow only in soil that has been recently ploughed and fertilized with well-consumed fertilizer; under these conditions, turnips grow so fast that they soon have nothing to fear from flea beetle attacks. Turnips should be harvested before they suffer the first frosts; although frosts cannot destroy them, they do alter their tissues to a greater or lesser extent, giving them an unpleasant taste and indigestible properties. Turnips should be stored in a healthy cellar or storeroom, in fresh sand, with the same care as for carrots. Turnips that are to be planted in the spring as seedbeds should be kept in good condition over winter. Turnip seeds, which ripen early in summer, are often eaten by eager sparrows, despite the use of scarecrows to frighten the birds away. The seed retains its germinative properties for three or four years. Species and varieties. - For a long time, the turnip was, along with cabbage, the most widely grown vegetable in kitchen gardens. Because of its low price, it was regarded as the food of the poorer classes and the symbol of frugality. Its cultivation in various climates has given rise to a large number of varieties, some flattened and onion-shaped, also known as turneps, others round or more or less elongated. The most sought-after are the yellowish-fleshed Freneuse turnip, oblong in shape and very tasty; the very voluminous Scottish yellow and the much smaller Finnish yellow, both very good for cooking; the Meaux turnip, the long Alsatian turnip and the Claire-Fontaine turnip, the best of the long white-fleshed ones; and the common round turnip, with a pink collar, used above all as an accompaniment to pot-au-feu. PANAIS. - Parsnips are grown in the same way as carrots; the seed is sown in the open air at the same time as carrot seed, but much lighter, as the plant needs more space to develop. In Belgium and on our northern border, parsnip seed is broadcast in March and April, about as tightly as carrot seed. The seedling is thinned out when the young parsnips are the size of a little finger: in this state, it is a very good spring vegetable, which can be eaten seasoned like new carrots harvested at the same stage of development. The parsnip's value is too low and its uses too limited for it to be subjected to forced cultivation. Seed carriers are protected from frost in winter and planted in spring; after a year, the seed loses its germinative properties. In cultivation, parsnips have produced only two varieties, differing only in root volume. The common parsnip is the most deeply rooted of all root vegetables, and can only be grown in very deep soil; the Metz parsnip, short and stubby like the short red Dutch carrot, has all the properties of the common parsnip, and can be grown in gardens where the soil is shallow, and where the common species would not succeed. CHAPTER XVII Root vegetables. - Radish, salsify, scorzonera, beet, potatoes, Jerusalem artichokes. Root vegetables. - Radish. - Spring, summer and winter cultivation. - Species and varieties. - Small radishes. - Black radish. - White and pink Chinese radishes. - Scorsonera. - Cultivation. - Salsify. - Scolyma. - Beet. - Cultivation in the vegetable garden. - Uses. - Garden species. - Cultivation of seed carriers. - Potatoes. - Autumn planting. - Forced cultivation on beds in winter. - Harvesting produce. - Potato seed sowing. - Jerusalem artichoke. - Its place in the vegetable garden. - Its usefulness in forming good shelters. RADISH. - The radish, whose slightly pungent root is eaten raw, is the most common hors d'oeuvre on well-served tables; it is also, because of its low price, much sought-after by workers in big cities; for several months of the year, it forms the usual staple of their morning meal. As a result, year-round cultivation of radishes is equally indispensable in the kitchen garden of a country home, and in the garden whose produce is destined for the market. Radishes are very easy to grow. Radish seed is sown in spring, either alone or mixed with lettuce and onion seed for transplanting. In order to grow radishes early in the open air, these seedlings should be sown on a hillside, with a southern exposure, in soil that has been thoroughly loaded with good loam, and well trodden after sowing, otherwise the radishes would not take on the normal shape of their species. Thin out the plants by harvesting the most advanced in succession to make way for the others. During the summer, radishes should only be sown in a shady position; they should be watered often and generously. Despite this care, most radishes grown during the hot season are hard, stringy and hollow inside.
