With the exception of those whose customers want radishes in summer and pay them accordingly, commercial gardeners can stop sowing radish seed, at least during July and August. In winter, radish seed is sown on a warm bed, filled with firmly packed loam. These sowings can be repeated every two weeks, until the first radishes grown in the open air in spring are harvested; the speed of radish growth makes forced cultivation advantageous in winter, although this product never sells at a very high price. Species and varieties. - The same method is used to grow the common pink radish, the early pink and white radish, the yellow radish and the half-long radish, a variety that is currently more popular than all the others. The same process is also used to obtain the small white, pink and violet rabe varieties, almost abandoned today due to a pure whim of fashion that is making its influence felt even in the kitchen garden; small rabe varieties are as good as the best radish species in every respect. The black radish, more like a turnip than a true radish, is sown in spring and only ready for harvesting in autumn, to be eaten successively throughout the winter; it needs a lot of water throughout the summer; well watered, grown in light, cool soil, it becomes very voluminous; but it always remains hard: it is a food suitable only for the strongest stomachs. The European black radish is rightly preferred to two excellent species, one pink, the other white, now introduced from China and less widely grown than they deserve to be. Their flesh, firm without being hard, is very tasty and easier to digest than that of the black radish. SCORSONERA-SALSIFY-SCOLYMA. - These three roots have the same uses and are obtained by the same method of cultivation; scorsonera is the most widely cultivated of the three. The seeds of this plant are sown either in spring, in February and March, or in summer, in July and August. Spring sowings produce roots that can be harvested in late autumn and throughout the winter. This root doesn't freeze, so it can be left in place and pulled out only as needed. Summer sowings produce roots that are good for harvesting only the following year, before the plant begins to flower and bear seed. Before sowing scoronera seed, it's a good idea to try it out on a cork float, in order to use a greater or lesser quantity of seed, according to the observed proportion of those that don't germinate; because scoronera seed, even when harvested in the best conditions, never lifts in its entirety; it doesn't retain its germinative properties beyond two years. Water until the plant has fully emerged; thin out, if necessary, after fifteen to twenty days, after which it is no longer necessary to care for it. Salsify scolyma. - Salsify, which differs from scorsonera only in its pale yellow outer bark, is treated in exactly the same way as scorsonera. Both roots are equally insensitive to cold and can, in the Paris climate, overwinter in the open air without any protection. The Spanish bark beetle, very common in the wild in our southern departments, can be grown like scorsonera and salsify; it is very prone to climbing, which makes its root tough and of little value; moreover, this root is as good and more vorumine than salsify. It fears the cold and should be placed in gauges with a good cover of dry litter, before heavy frosts. BETTERAVE. - Beet belongs more to field cultivation than to gardening; it is nevertheless a vegetable garden plant, albeit always in small numbers, because the custom of adding slices of baked beet to salad, and eating it fricasseed in a pan with onion, has not been entirely abandoned. Garden beet can only be considered to be the smaller species, of various shades of pink or red; the small, very sweet, very dark red Castelnau dary beet is the most widely grown in gardens. The seed is sown in spring, better in a nursery than in the field; the plant must be transplanted young and watered until it is well established; beets are pulled out around Saint Martin's Day (November 11), to be stored in the cellar like carrots; those to be used as seedbeds are placed in gauges and covered with litter over winter. In countries where sugar beet is widely cultivated, it is usually the gardeners who carefully cultivate the seedbeds of the best beet species, in order to sell the seed to farmers. This seed retains its germinative properties for four or five years. POTATO. - Of the innumerable species and varieties of potato grown in Europe in large quantities under the most varied conditions of soil and climate, only the earliest varieties are suitable for gardening; the later species would take up too much land that could always be put to better use. Tubers for planting. - Tubers are planted either whole, or cut into pieces, each of which should have one or two good wants. Nothing could be less rational than the practice still followed by many gardeners of planting only small tubers which they have not been able to dispose of. Potatoes that are too small have remained small only because they were formed too late in the season, and have not had time to take on their normal volume; their eyes are badly shaped and they cannot give rise to vigorous plants. What's more, the small quantity of early potatoes that can be grown in a garden does not require the use of a large number of tubers for planting; it would be a very ill-judged economy not to use the best preserved potatoes from the previous harvest for planting. When potatoes are too large to be planted whole, they should be cut into pieces and left to dry out in the open air for a few hours, otherwise some of the pieces will rot in the soil and fail to sprout. e. Planting. - Planting should be done either very early in the spring, or at the onset of winter: the latter is the most favorable time. Care should be taken to bury potatoes that are to spend the winter in the ground a little deeper than those planted in February and March, after the severe cold has passed. Autumn planting does not increase the earliness of potatoes; it does not appreciably advance the time when their first products can be harvested; but tubers intended for planting, if they are put in the ground before winter, keep much better there than in a cellar where they have to stay when they are only planted in spring. Vegetation is more vigorous, because the tubers have not exhausted themselves by prematurely emitting etiolated shoots, and the end result is a better harvest at equal cost. The best species for garden cultivation are: marjolin from France, kidney from England and the two Belgian varieties known as nine-week and seven-week; the latter is the earliest of all. Forced calture. - In the vicinity of Paris and other large cities, it can be advantageous to subject potatoes to forced cultivation in winter, on a warm bed under a frame; the tubers obtained are not nearly as good as the well-preserved potatoes from the previous harvest, but they are easily sold at high prices. To force the potato, we start, at the end of November, by putting the tubers we intend to force into the sprouter. The germinator is simply a glazed frame with a box, placed on a flowerbed at the foot of a south-facing wall, and under the shelter of which the potatoes are placed close together; they soon produce young shoots and roots. To prevent this vegetation from withering, the germinator should only be covered with bedding or straw mats during the night; light must be able to penetrate freely, at least during the coldest hours of the day. On the other hand, a warm layer is prepared with a 25-30 cm thick bed of good soil mixed with compost. When the potatoes are well advanced in the germinator, they are planted in rows in the soil of the warm layer, with a very shallow tread. Their underground stems quickly take over all the soil, forming small tubers which are removed several times by carefully removing the clumps, so that they can continue to vegetate and produce new tubers, until the heat of the bed is exhausted. The space left free on the surface of the bed between the rows of forced potatoes can be used to sow radish, lettuce and tomato seed, which produces very early seedlings that can be transplanted once on a warm bed and again in the open air at the end of April. Combined in this way, forced potato cultivation can be very profitable, provided you are sure in advance of the placement of products which, if they are not sought-after and in demand, are of very mediocre value in themselves. Sowing potato seeds - It is always advantageous for the gardener who cultivates a sufficiently extensive vegetable garden to sow a certain quantity of potato seeds every year in spring. These seedlings never produce more than very small tubers in the first year, but these tubers, used for planting the following year, always produce extraordinarily fertile plants. However, experience shows that seed potatoes remain vigorous and productive for several generations, and are much less prone to disease than other varieties. By sowing a little every year, the gardener ensures that his own crops will have a supply of tubers at their most fertile; if he has more than he can use, he will easily be able to dispose of the surplus at planting time. TOPINAMBOUR. - If there's an area in the kitchen garden that's less fertile and less well situated than the rest of the plot, that's the place to grow Jerusalem artichokes, with or even without manure; it's a plant that grows everywhere; it's the providence of the poorest soils. Medium-sized whole tubers are planted early in the spring, and harvested in autumn. Once established, the Jerusalem artichoke plantation supports itself for an unlimited period of time; the smallest fragment of tuber left in the ground at harvest time grows back with astonishing vigor; every year, as many Jerusalem artichokes are found in the same plot as were harvested the previous year. When growing ornamental plants in cold or temperate greenhouses, which must spend the summer in the open air, as these more or less delicate plants dread the violent gales that accompany thunderstorms, we protect them by planting Jerusalem artichokes in rows, tightly packed together, around the space intended for them. The Jerusalem artichoke's ample foliage and sturdy stems provide excellent shelter for greenhouse plants, while allowing the constantly renewed air they need to reach them. Two varieties of Jerusalem artichoke are grown, one white or yellowish, the other purplish-red. Both can be grown in the same way and are equally hardy; they do not need to be uprooted in autumn; the cold of the harshest winters in Paris and even northern France does not damage them in any way; they are only uprooted as and when required for consumption. CHAPTER XVIII Edible bulb vegetables. Edible bulb vegetables. - Onions - Cultivation by sowing in the field. - Quantity of seed for a given area. - Seed testing. - Soil compaction. - Time of sowing. - Twisting of leaf. - Time of uprooting. - Cultivation by transplanting. - Spacing - Nouvellon's method. - Onion species and varieties. - Leeks. - Transplanting. - Cultivation. - Species and varieties. - Garlic. - Chives. - Shallot. - Civet. The series of vegetables whose bulbs are used includes onions, leeks, garlic, chives, shallots and civets. Three of these vegetables, onion, leek and garlic, are of great economic importance in France, because of their many uses in French cuisine; the French climate is particularly suited to them. ONION. - Onions can be grown by sowing or transplanting; the former is the more common method. With the exception of two or three species, some of which produce bulblets and others cloves or small onions for propagation, the onion varieties most widely grown in gardens can only be propagated by sowing their seeds, either in situ or in a nursery. To sow onions in situ, choose soil that is perfectly healthy, soft, light, free of stones and pebbles, and has been smoked for at least a year. No bulbous vegetable plant tolerates contact with fresh manure. If you don't have enough fertilized soil for onion cultivation, you should mix in a high dose of potting soil from broken layers. Manure, even in large doses, can only be useful for growing bulbous plants when it has completely turned into potting soil, because then it no longer ferments and cannot release any principle capable of causing the bulbs to rot. Seed selection and testing. - The average quantity of seed to be used for sowing is one gram per square meter. But as onion seed, even when harvested in the best conditions, is never good in its entirety, and a more or less considerable portion does not raise, the dose for sowing is increased or decreased according to the quality of the seed tested with a float. The

proportion of one gram per meter is only sufficient when the gardener has harvested the seed he sows himself. In the seed he buys, there is almost always a little of the old mixed with the new; two-year-old seed lifts very well; three-year-old seed hardly lifts at all. Once the quality of the seed has been checked on the float, it is most often found that it should be sown at a rate of one and a half grams per square metre. If the seedling rises a little too tightly, once it has taken on the diameter of a feather pipe, it is thinned out, and the one pulled out is transplanted in rows to complete its growth. When circumstances do not allow us to take advantage of the plant pulled out for thinning, we take care to sow a little lighter; then we only start thinning when the plant is strong enough to be delivered for consumption as a spring onion. Around Paris, two-thirds of onion seed and one-third of leek seed are broadcast, mixed together. When the leek, easy to distinguish by the particular shape of its leaves, is strong enough to be uprooted and transplanted, the onion is suitably spaced and does not need to be thinned out. Sowing. - A very important point to remember when sowing onion seed, either alone or in combination with leek seed, is to pack the soil firmly before and after sowing, and never to sow on too recent a ploughing. The ground on which you intend to sow onion seed in spring is ploughed at the start of winter, unless it has already borne a crop of potatoes or scorzonera, in which case it is sufficiently loosened by the removal of these roots, and needs only a superficial tilling either with a hoe or an iron-tined fork. As soon as the first fine days of February arrive, the soil is carefully ploughed, then left to firm up for at least three weeks. When it's time to sow, the surface of the beds is levelled with a rake, then trampled down hard and sown. Immediately afterwards, the surface is raked again, and trampled down a second time. The seed is then in the best possible conditions to germinate properly. The best time to sow onion seed is from around February 20 to March 15, but these dates should not be strictly adhered to, even though they generally represent the most favorable time; it often happens in the Paris climate that prolonged bad weather makes it impossible to plow the vegetable garden beds early; in this case, sowing must be delayed. Cultivation care. - During the first period of growth, onions are very sensitive to drought and weeds. Newly emerged onion beds should be watered as often as the soil appears dry, otherwise young onions will partly perish from the spring droughts known to gardeners as the March sunburn. Weeding must be carried out by hand, without the aid of any instrument, and with great care, so as not to tear off part of the onion plant at the same time as the weed. As they approach maturity, onions whose leaves are still green need a final cultivation treatment to help the bulbs swell. The gardener rakes through the onion beds, pressing the underside of the rake down on the leaf-beds, so that they lie on the ground, where they soon turn yellow. If you've only sown a small quantity, you can hand-twist the onion leaves or tops at their base, to produce more beautiful onions. When the bulbs are fully grown, they can be pulled out in dry weather. After two or three days on the ground, the onions lose some of their water, making them easier to preserve. Transplanting is done either with seedlings obtained from nursery sowing, or with seedlings thinned from broadcast sowing. The seedling can be transplanted at a very young age, 10 cm apart in all directions; the rest of the cultivation is the same as for broadcast sowing. Only one species, the early white onion, is sown in midsummer, in July and August, to be transplanted in autumn; it is covered with litter during severe cold, which, despite this precaution, always destroys part of the plant; it begins to vegetate again in spring of the following year and is harvested half-formed in May and June. In our southern departments, where winter is almost non-existent, this cultivation method can be applied to all varieties of onion, without causing any loss to gardeners; in the Paris climate, it is applied only to early white onions, because onions are the usual seasoning for peas. However, by the time the pea season comes around, supplies of storage onions are exhausted, and onions from spring seedlings are still too small to be used. Finally, a particular method known as the Nouvellon method, named after one of the most successful horticulturists, is widely used, especially in Orléans and in the Varennes or market gardens of the towns on the banks of the Loire. The onion seed is sown in a nursery in August, much thicker than when the plant is to be transplanted; it is watered just as much as is necessary to make the seed stand up, then the plant is left to wither and dry on the ground. In this way, each seed produces a very well-formed onion; the largest are the size of a hazelnut, the smallest the size of a pea. They are removed and stored in a dry place, to be transplanted in rows in spring, at distances proportionate to the presumed volume of the bulbs of each species. The main advantage of Nouvellon's method for growing onions is that it saves on seed, which is often scarce and expensive. Onions to be grown as seedlings should be kept away from humidity, in a cold rather than warm place, where only heavy frosts can reach them. If kept in winter under the influence of too mild a temperature, it would exhaust itself, producing etiolated shoots prematurely; placed in the ground in this condition, it would flower poorly and bear no seed. This natural tendency of onions to overwinter can only be countered by cold; it alone makes it difficult to keep onions of most species from one year to the next. Seed-bearing onions, as their flower stalks lengthen, need to be supported by sturdy stakes, otherwise a strong gust of wind will blow them over, and all will be lost. The ripe seed keeps well in its head-shaped capsules, which are gathered in bunches and hung in a dry place; it is only peeled when you want to sow it. Species and varieties. - The main varieties of onion grown in gardens are dark red, Niort pale red, Vertus yellow, Cambrai yellow, double-stemmed, late white and Spanish yellow. All these onions are good for storing; the last two are better suited to the vegetable gardens of southern France than to those of central and northern France. White onions are grown for summer consumption, and a large proportion of them are used when the bulbs are half their size. The above-mentioned onions are by far the most widespread and the best overall. The following onions are grown only in exceptional circumstances, or for a variety of special purposes. The small, round white onion from Nocera or Florence is grown for pickling in vinegar and eating as an hors-d'oeuvre with gherkins. It is very tasty, but in the Paris climate, it tends to swell and is difficult to keep clean; in the Midi, the pyriform, furiform or ox-horn, Madeira, potato and Egyptian or bulb onions are grown mainly. The stem of the latter provides an ample supply of bulblets that are planted like the small onions obtained by Nouvellon's method; this onion does not multiply by sowing its seeds; the bulblets take their place. It is these bulblets, known as "rocambole", that often give the name to the Egyptian onion. The potato onion can only be multiplied by separating the bulblets it produces in abundance, when it is carefully butterred several times during the summer. LEEK. - Leeks fear fresh manure, especially cattle manure, even more than onions. It never grows well from seed, and must always be transplanted. Leek seed is sown in March and April, either alone or in combination with onion seed; the plant should be pulled up when it is the size of a feather pipe. Before planting, shorten the tops of the leaves and the tips of the roots. Leeks should be transplanted immediately after uprooting; the less time they spend out of the ground, the better they will vegetate. A plant that has been uprooted too long in advance, and has withered before being transplanted, will never produce anything but mediocre results. Plants are planted in rows spaced 15 centimetres apart, with a decimetre between rows. The tray from which the roots grow should be sunk into the ground to a depth of s or 10 centimetres. Fully-formed leeks have little fear of the cold, and can be left in the open air all winter without any problems in the Paris climate. Leeks, an indispensable seasoning for stews, would be unavailable on the market for part of the year if we didn't employ easy-to-use methods to obtain them in all seasons. In spring, when leeks are ready to bolt, we pull them up and put them in the shade in a northern exposure; in this way, they remain in good condition for a month longer than if they had not been pulled up. In November and December, leek seed is sown in a frame on a bed, giving a plant that can be transplanted early in the spring; this plant needs to be carefully protected from the cold, because if the fully formed leek doesn't freeze, the leek plant freezes, even when it's not very cold. This plant, planted in March and April, produces leeks suitable for harvesting when those from the previous harvest begin to run out. Leek seed can also be sown late and very lightly in September, in a sheltered location. These seedlings don't freeze, and are strong enough to withstand the winter before the cold sets in. In the spring of the following year, it comes up much later than leeks grown in the traditional way, when there are no others; it is never very good, but it sells at a good price in the season when this necessary vegetable is scarce on the markets. Three varieties of leek are grown by the same methods: the long or common leek, the gros court du Midi, and the gros court de Rouen, the largest of the three. GARLIC. - The cultivation of garlic is important only in the Midi: southern cuisine accepts garlic in all dishes. In spring, garlic is planted in rows, one decimeter apart in all directions, in healthy soil that has only been smoked for a year or two. In Paris, where garlic consumption is much lower, it can be planted in borders around some of the squares in the vegetable garden, preferably in a south-facing position, in soil that is dry rather than too moist. Garlic should not be planted as a border around beds where Ton grows plants that need frequent and ample watering; it would rot there from excess moisture. In the first week of June, the leaf and flowering stem of garlic are twisted and tied together; this prevents the plant from flowering, and encourages the enlargement of the cal̈eux called garlic cloves, enclosed in a tunic or common envelope. Garlic should only be harvested when the leaves have completely dried out. Garlic can be propagated by sowing its seeds, which can be obtained by letting a few plants rise. But the seedlings produced by sowing these seeds would only be used for the following year's planting, and would be wasting a year without any benefit in return. That's why we're content to multiply garlic by separating the cloves, which are the only ones used for planting. In the Paris climate, only two types of garlic are grown: common garlic and early or "Vertus" garlic. In the south of France, in addition to the common species, which is the most widely cultivated, vegetable gardens grow rocambole garlic, whose stem produces bulblets like those of the Egyptian onion, and ait d'Espagne or red garlic, both of which are suited to the southern climate. CIBOULE - Ciboules can be grown in two ways: as a biennial plant or as a perennial. The usual method is to sow the chive seed in the spring, like leek seed; the plant is transplanted at a very young age and produces chives that are good to pick all summer long. Sow a second time in July to harvest in autumn. Chives, which are never used in very large quantities, can also be planted in borders, like garlic, where they form large clumps that can be doubled twice a year. Three varieties of spring onion are grown: the common spring onion, the early white spring onion, and the perennial spring onion, the most commonly used for border planting. This last species is aptly named, as it is no more perennial than the others, which are all perennial to the same degree. SHALLOT. - Shallots are grown in the same way as garlic; they are planted at the same time in the same soil and require the same care. In addition to the common species, the most widely grown in the kitchen garden, two other varieties are known: the Jersey shallot and the German shallot, both larger than the common species, but with a more delicate flavor and more difficult to preserve. CIVET. - This plant, also known as chives and appéut, is grown like garlic and shallots, in the border. The slender, tapering, green leaves are the only edible part of the plant; clumps should only be removed every three years; it thrives only in slightly dry soils. CHAPTER XIX Edible leaf vegetables. - Cabbage, Kohlrabi. Vegetables whose leaves are eaten. - Cabbage. - Its health-giving properties. - Transplanting. - Winter preservation. - Replanting. - Species and varieties. - Head cabbage. - Round-headed. - White cabbage. - Red cabbage. - With smooth leaves. - Blistered leaves. - Brussels sprouts. - Conical cabbage, - Green cabbage. - Kohlrabi. - Turnip cabbage. - Cultivation. - Preservation. - Species and varieties. - Growing seed cabbage. The vegetables whose leaves are eaten are not all of equal importance; some are simple salad supplies and are therefore used in very-small quantities, although their presence in the vegetable garden is essential. This series of vegetables includes cabbage, sorrel, spinach, celery, cardoon, purslane, parsley, chervil, tarragon, watercress, rhubarb and fennel. CABBAGE. - Cabbage is the most important of all edible leafy vegetables. It has been cultivated since ancient times in France and Italy, and has produced a multitude of sub-varieties, all recommendable in different ways. As a food, cabbage is healthy and easy to digest when properly seasoned. In Rome, for several centuries, cabbage tea was the only medicine used to treat all kinds of illnesses. At least, this system of medication had the rare advantage of doing no harm when it didn't cure. Cabbage cultivation is extremely simple, based on general principles that apply to all species, even though there are many of them and they differ from one another in very pronounced ways. Above all, cabbage requires fresh, substantial soil, rather strong than too light; nevertheless, in light soil, very good cabbages can be obtained, provided they lack neither water nor manure. Sheep manure is the most suitable for growing cabbage. Growing the plant. - Manure should be lavished on the land where the cabbage seed is sown. The seedling is carefully cultivated, weeded and thinned out if necessary, until it is strong enough to be transplanted. Transplant permanently, in staggered rows, at distances ranging from 50 centimetres for the smallest species, to 80 centimetres for the largest. When transplanted in dry weather, a few waterings ensure the plant's recovery. Head cabbages, the only varieties that can be kept from one year to the next, have little fear of the cold. When you don't need to dispose of the land where they've been growing, you can simply uproot the cabbages when they're fully formed, and leave them on the spot, head down, root up; they keep just as well and better than anywhere else. Head cabbages only freeze during the coldest weather, and rot when the thaw comes, if you don't pull them out, because the water from cold rain and melted snow has penetrated the gaps in their outer leaves and become icicles: this is how they perish in winter. As they are uprooted, they have nothing to fear from the cold. However, since most of the time, a plot where cabbages have grown to their full potential needs to be ploughed and smoked in order to receive other crops, the cabbages are pulled up, stripped of their largest outer leaves, and stored in a cellar or a healthy cellar, to be delivered successively for consumption. They can also be placed close together at the foot of a wall with good exposure, and covered with branches over which old mats or long litter are spread. This protects them from contact with snow and cold humidity, which they fear far more than the most intense cold. Counterplanting. - Several species of cabbage can be advantageously grown at the same time as other plants they replace, once the latter have brought their produce to maturity. For example, cabbage plants are transplanted between rows of very-early potatoes. When the potato crop is removed, the gardener only has to give the soil a light tilling to even out the surface; the potato patch becomes a cabbage patch, which soon covers the whole plot. This saves precious time, and is what Parisian market gardeners call "contreplanter". The counter-planting method can be applied with the same advantage to lettuces and various other products. Species and varieties. - Cabbages are divided into two series, one comprising headed cabbages and the other green cabbages, which are not all green, but which do not form apples. Head cabbage-This series, the larger of the two, is divided into two sections: the first comprises round-headed cabbages, and the second, conical-headed cabbages. Round-headed cabbages are divided into two very distinct subdivisions: those with smooth leaves and those with blistered leaves, or curly cabbages. The best smooth-leaf cabbages are the Alsatian white cabbage, also known as quintal cabbage and sauerkraut cabbage; there are many good varieties, the most recommendable of which, grown especially in the western departments, is known there as Nantais cabbage. It is with these cabbages, in preference to all other species, that sauerkraut, so sought-after by the German population, is prepared. Red cabbage, with its particularly mild flavor, belongs to the same series; it is grown only in the gardens of northern France. It is eaten either cooked with apples and sausages, or raw, cut very thinly and dressed as a salad with melted butter and vinegar. This type of salad can only be digested by the strong stomachs of the Flemish people of France and Belgium. Blister-leaved cabbages or kale are no less rich in sub-varieties than smooth-leaved cabbages; they are native to Lombardy and Savoy; in France, they are generally called Milan cabbages; the most esteemed variety is the milan des vertus, very much cultivated around Paris. Spruyt or Brussels sprouts belong to this subdivision. It is grown like all other cabbages, but instead of peeling, it produces a strong, tall stalk, covered with leaves along its entire length: from the axils of these leaves sprout small, tightly packed cabbages, which are the edible part of the plant; they are harvested successively from September onwards; moderate cold does not interrupt their vegetation; it is one of the most delicate fresh vegetables that the kitchen garden can provide in winter. If you want to keep it perfectly true to its species, you need to send the seed from its native land. The most discerning connoisseurs bring in seedlings ready for planting from the Brussels area. Among conical-headed cabbages, the superiority of the English York cabbage cannot be disputed. This cabbage is not widely grown by professional market gardeners, because its heads have little volume, which makes it unattractive for sale, but as for taste, there is none better. The conical Pomeranian cabbage, which is larger and more elongated than the York cabbage, but very prone to degeneration, is more or less on the same level. The most cultivated of the conical cabbages, although not the best, are the pine and oxheart cabbages, with their slightly tightly packed heads and not very delicate flavour, but very early; they arrive on the markets precisely when the previous year's supply of other preserved cabbages is exhausted; the preference given to them has no other cause. Green cabbages, which do not pomace, are generally more suited to field cultivation than to gardening, and are in fact forage plants rather than vegetables. A few species, however, can be found in the kitchen garden, and are eaten chopped and prepared like sorrel and spinach. It's more of a purgative than a food, and should only be used in moderation. The most highly esteemed green cabbages are the dwarf kale, of which there are two sub-varieties, one green, the other red; the variegated kale, and the proliferous kale, both remarkable for the quirkiness of their foliage, and for this reason accepted in the parterre as ornamental plants, as well as in the kitchen garden as vegetables. Another series of cabbages, much less cultivated in France than it deserves to be, but highly esteemed in Germany and England, is known as kohlrabi. Although the leaves are not the edible part, we mention them here, because kohlrabi is a true cabbage, and an excellent vegetable, very easy to grow and very productive. The kohlrabi series comprises two well-characterized divisions: that of kohlrabi proper, whose edible part is not a root, but a bulge at the bottom of the stem, and that of turnips, which form a true root in the ground, similar to that of turnips. All these cabbages are grown in the same way; they are sown in nurseries early in the spring, and may be continued until the end of May, so that the produce does not all arrive at the same time. When the seedling is strong enough to be transplanted, it is planted in fresh, well-smoked soil. The cultivation of kohlrabi differs from that of other cabbages in that good yields can only be expected from it with water; it must be watered continuously and abundantly, otherwise the ball or swelling at the bottom of the stalk does not develop and remains stringy and tasteless. Kohlrabi, like turnip cabbage, doesn't fear the cold; if you don't need to dispose of the land it occupies, you can leave it there; it doesn't freeze in the open air in the Paris climate; kohlrabi are usually pulled up, and after removing the leaves, they are stored in a gauge or in the cellar, like turnips. Kohlrabi, which are more hardy than kohlrabi, are grown like cabbages; in the event of drought, they are watered only when planted, to ensure that the seedlings recover; they are harvested and stored in the same way. Among kohlrabi, white, red and dwarf kohlrabi from Vienna, Austria, and among turnips, yellow-fleshed rutabaga, improperly called Swedish yellow turnip. Some gardeners still follow the old method used in the past to obtain cabbage seed, which consists in keeping the best apples of each species as seed carriers over winter. This method is reprehensible from every point of view: on the one hand, the apples are often spoiled inside, and on the other, even if they are healthy, the shrivelling that gives them their merit as food is detrimental to the vegetation of the flower stalks. It's much simpler to cut off the heads of cabbage for consumption in autumn, keep the cores, transplanted in a sheltered position, and wait for them to emit flowering shoots in spring, whose natural vigour has not been altered by shrivelling. To keep varieties straight, after splitting the horizontally sliced core slightly in four, only one shoot is left at each quarter, and the top is removed when it starts to flower. If the flowering stem produces too many side shoots, cut off some of them. Seed-bearing cabbages treated in this way produce a moderate quantity of very good seed on the middle part of their stems, capable of preserving or even improving the qualities specific to each species. As cabbages are very prone to accidental cross-breeding, seed-stocks of different species should not be planted close together. This method can be applied with equal success to all series of cabbages; in general, the plant only degenerates when each stalk, born from an etiolated apple instead of from a vigorous stalk, is loaded with too many seeds, some of which are imperfectly fertilized and can only produce a weak, degenerating plant. CHAPTER XX Edible leaf vegetables (continued). Edible leafy vegetables other than cabbage. - Spinach. - Procedure for restoring frozen ones. - Species and varieties. - Sorrel. - Winter cultivation. - Species and varieties. - Celery. - Cultivation. - Threshing. - Species and varieties. - Celery root cultivation. - Cardoons. - Cultivation. - Winter storage. - Watercress. - Establishment of a watercress farm. - Cresson alénois or passerage. - Rhubarb. - Cultivation. - Species and varieties. - Parsley. - Its value in winter. - Chervil. - Its uses. - Resemblance to hemlock. - Tarragon. - Purslane. - Fennel. Vegetables whose leaves are eaten, with the exception of cabbages, are of secondary importance; their common characteristic is that they provide very little nourishment; for this reason, they are never given much space. SPINACH. - Spinach has little intrinsic value; its nutritive power is practically nil, so that anyone eating nothing else would soon starve to death. Nonetheless, it is a popular dish with city dwellers, and has its place in the vegetable garden. Spinach seed is sown in spring and autumn. Sowing could be continued throughout the summer, but on the one hand there would be almost no leaves to harvest, the only edible part of the plant; on the other, the vegetable garden provides a profusion of other vegetables in summer, which are preferred to spinach. For the professional gardener, growing spinach is especially advantageous in autumn; the plant's rapid growth makes it eminently suitable for garnishing squares that have been harvested too late in the season to be suitable for further cultivation. Spinach seed, sown very lightly and broadcast, sprouts in 10. just a few days and quickly produces harvestable leaves. If, during winter, spinach leaves appear to be frozen because they have turned a dark green, semi-transparent color due to the action of a bit of bitter cold, you should hurry to pick them and plunge them into a tub of water as cold as possible. Immediately remove them to dry on the floor of a very clean room. As soon as the water adhering to the leaves has evaporated, the spinach has regained its good appearance and is as good as if it had not been frozen; it is mainly in winter that it is best sold. Species and varieties. - Several varieties of spinach are grown in the same way, some with smooth seeds, others with spiky seeds from which their name derives. The most esteemed spinach varieties are common and English spinach, and spinach from Holland, Flanders and Esquermes. In terms of flavor and green hue, qualities sought after in this vegetable, there is no spinach preferable to the common, pungent-seeded species; for this reason, it is the most widely cultivated. Spinach seed is harvested by sowing a bed late in autumn, with the plant flowering the following spring. As soon as the male plants have flowered, they are pulled out to give the female plants more space. The seed, threshed as soon as it is ripe, can be kept for several years. The two-year-old seed is the best for both spring and autumn sowing. OSEIL. - The nourishing properties of sorrel are not far superior to those of spinach; but its habitual use, mainly in soup form, is very beneficial to the health of city dwellers engaged in sedentary occupations; this makes its consumption very widespread, and its cultivation as advantageous as that of any other vegetable, in the vicinity of large cities. Everywhere else, and when the products of the vegetable garden are not to be returned, it is always necessary to have sorrel in every season. Sorrel is propagated by sowing its seeds in rows, in spring or autumn; it succeeds in all soils and at all exposures. To counteract the tendency of most species to bolt in summer, which makes leaf production almost nil, sow a little in a shady spot with a northerly exposure; the formation of flower stems is thus delayed as much as possible. Calture in winter. - Sorrel is not valuable enough to benefit from forced winter cultivation. However, to ensure that the cold does not completely interrupt the vegetation of sorrel, whose young leaves fetch a good price in winter, during the hardest frosts, straw or dry litter can be spread over the sorrel beds and moved around when the weather permits. Sorrel can also be multiplied by dividing clumps in spring or autumn; but to ensure that each species retains all its properties and does not degenerate, it should always be sown from time to time, preferably in autumn. Species and varieties. - In the vegetable garden, we grow common sorrel, the best variety of which is known as Belleville sorrel, obtained from seedlings around Paris; blister-leaved sorrel and virgin sorrel. The latter species is dioecious, meaning it bears male and female flowers on separate plants. By cultivating only male specimens, which can be multiplied by dividing clumps, we avoid the inconvenience of other varieties which spread everywhere and invade more land than the gardener wants to allow them, through the natural sowing of their seeds. CELERY. - Celery grows wild in the meadows of our southern départements, where it is known as "bonne herbe" (a good herb); it deserves this nickname for its stomachic and fortifying properties. Celery can only be grown successfully in naturally cool soil, and requires abundant, continuous watering. Celery is very sensitive to cold; GARDENERS can only start sowing outdoors in April, after the last late frosts, and seedlings only thrive in a southern exposure. Sowing should be very light, so that the seedling can gain considerable strength before being planted. Cultivation. - Ridging. - Celery's aromatic flavor is only really pleasant when softened by blanching; it is only suitable for cooking when it has been blanched. It is in anticipation of the need to butter it to blanch it that the plant must be transplanted in rows separated by sufficient intervals so that the soil required for the buttages can be taken from them, which are given three or four times in succession as the plants lengthen. In the North, we follow the method of Belgian gardeners who, to butter celery more easily, only transplant it to the bottom of a ditch, between two embankments whose soil is later used for ridging. When you want to harvest celery very early, sow the seed on a bed under a frame, starting in January, so as to have good seedlings to transplant at the time when the first sowings are possible in the open air. If you start sowing celery seed in the first days of January, you can supply it for consumption all year round, without interruption. For winter storage, we keep a portion of celeriac in the cellar, protected from the cold that this vegetable fears more than any other. An extra supply is kept in reserve, in a well-sheltered spot, and care is taken not to butter it. As the first part of the supply diminishes, the celery stalks kept green are tied up with one or two straw ties, then partly buried in sand in the cellar, where they soon turn white. This is the best way to avoid running out in winter. Plants used as seedbeds should be transplanted to a southern exposure, where they can easily overwinter with a bedding cover that is moved when the weather permits. In the climate of northern France, the gardener who wants to harvest celery seed is obliged to overwinter the seed-bearing plants under a frame; the following spring, he puts them back in place in the open air; the celery flowers abundantly and sets seed towards the end of summer; this seed retains its germinative properties for no more than two years. In departments north of the Seine valley, gardeners are well advised to bring celery seed from Paris, or even from the banks of the Loire. Species and varieties. - The main celery species allowed in the kitchen garden are early short celery, full white celery, dwarf triseed celery and violet Tours celery. All are cultivated in the same way and produce the same type of product. Hollow celery is also grown, but in smaller quantities. Gardeners call it cutting celery, to distinguish it from the species whose ribs are whitened by shrivelling. Hollow celery cannot be harvested; its leaves are used only as a salad ingredient, and if nothing else, for stews and various stews. Only a few years ago, celery root began to be cultivated in France, a very common crop in northern countries. It differs from its predecessors in shape and uses, but is still quite rare in vegetable gardens, because the very aromatic taste of its edible part is not to everyone's taste. Growing celeriac - Celeriac can only be grown successfully where the soil is naturally cool, and where there is enough water to water it continuously. The root, the only edible part of this species of celery, only develops with water. Seedlings sown at the same time as other celery species are sown outdoors, are transplanted in rows 40 cm apart in all directions. When the lay of the land and the quantity of water available permit, the celeriac beds can be surrounded by a rim of earth that retains the water used for irrigation; in this case, watering is done not with a watering can, but by soaking; this is the method used by German gardeners, who excel in this type of cultivation. The celeriac is then continually soaked in water, which considerably diminishes its aromatic flavor, which is too pronounced and difficult to tolerate for those who are not used to it, when the plant has vegetated in soil that is not dry (it would not grow there) but insufficiently watered. When the root has reached its full size, remove all leaves except those in the center; celeriac can be stored in the cellar like root vegetables; the rootstocks are less sensitive to cold than those of other celery species; they overwinter in the open air, with a light covering of litter during hard frosts. CARDONS. - The various species of cardoon can only be propagated by sowing their seeds; although the cardoon is a true thistle like its close relative the artichoke, it does not tolerate transplanting and must be sown in the very place where it is to grow. Spacing varies according to the volume of each species, from 80 centimetres to 1 mềtre in any direction. Several seeds are sown in each hole, leaving only the most vigorous plant. A pit should be made around the base of each plant, to retain water for watering, which should be frequent and abundant. During the heat of summer, you can't water less than three times a day, very generously each time, if you want the ribs of the leaves, the edible part of the plant, to be tender and fleshy. When the leaves are fully expanded, they are bundled together with two or three ties of wet rye straw, then wrapped in a strong straw jacket to bleach them. The main disadvantage of growing cardoon is that, as soon as the ribs have etiolated to the point required for cooking, they must be eaten immediately, otherwise they will rot in very few days. For this reason, cardoons intended for winter storage should be lifted in clods in late autumn, planted in fresh sand in a cellar or other frost-free location, and blanched as needed for consumption. Every year, a few of the best cardoons of each species should be kept as seedlings. They are protected from the cold by staking them like artichokes; they flower and set seed the following summer. The main cardoon species grown in vegetable gardens are Tours, Spanish, red-ribbed, full Juerne and Puvis cardoons, the largest of all. Although the edges of its leaves are bristling with prickles, which clumsy gardeners are liable to injure themselves on, Tours cardoon is the most widely grown, because of its less bland and more delicate taste than the other varieties, with the exception of Puvis cardoon, which is given a marked preference in the vegetable gardens of southern France. CRESSON. - The cultivation of watercress, which is very advantageous in certain localities, is only possible where there is a trickle of running water that does not dry up in summer; for this reason, it is rarely possible in the vegetable garden, although it essentially belongs to market gardening. To set up a watercress bed, you divide a plot of land into perfectly level plots, the whole of which must have a sufficient slope for the water to run off, circulating slowly but continuously, without becoming stagnant anywhere. Each board is surrounded by a rim with two openings that can be closed at will with a handful of greasy earth; one is for the water to enter, the other for it to leave. Stems of watercress are placed flat in rows on the surface of the boards, 15 to 20 centimetres apart in all directions. The stems are taken from very clean water and harvested just before flowering. These stems, held in place by wooden forks, take root immediately, provided there is no lack of water. All buds ready for flowering are then removed, to allow the side shoots to develop. In a few days, the beds are covered with abundant vegetation, which grows back as it is picked for sale. This is one of the simplest crops, and a very profitable one wherever product placement is assured. A well-maintained watercress bed lasts indefinitely. Watercress enthusiasts who do not have irrigable land in their vegetable garden suitable for this crop, usually limit themselves to filling tubs with soil, in which they plant watercress stems that grow quite well, as long as the soil in the tubs is kept constantly submerged. However, no matter how carefully the water is renewed, it is impossible to prevent it from stagnating in summer, which gives the cress an unpleasant odor and flavor. Because these young shoots have a taste similar to that of watercress, they are improperly referred to as "Alenois cress". Passerage, a species of small thlaspi, is sought-after as a salad ingredient, especially in spring. Cresson alénois only grows in warm, dry soils with a southern exposure. Those who wish to have it all summer long should repeat sowing two or three times a month, as the plant goes to seed very young, and once it has gone to seed it is worthless. RHUBARBUS. - Wherever there are English people, rhubarb pies, a much sought-after national delicacy among Britain's wealthy families, are consumed in large quantities, ensuring a profitable investment for the products of rhubarb cultivation. These products consist of the petioles of the radical leaves of the plant, which are used peeled and cut into sections. What makes rhubarb petioles always rather expensive is that they can only be harvested after the plants, propagated by sowing their seeds or dividing clumps, have been grown for a year in a nursery. Rhubarb seed is sown early, in March, when it reaches full maturity, on plants that have been kept as seedbeds, and which have spent the winter in the open air, as they are in no way sensitive to the cold in the Paris climate, or even that of northern France. Growing care is limited to frequent watering during the first summer, to ensure that plants are well supplied for the following year's harvest. The best types of rhubarb are currant rhubarb, which is not widely grown, although it is considered preferable to all the others, wavy rhubarb and southern or Nepaul rhubarb. In England, many varieties are grown in vegetable gardens, and are readily available in localities frequented by English tourists, the only places where the products of this crop can be sold on a fairly large scale. PARSLEY. - The aromatic flavor of parsley, a little less pronounced but just as pleasant as that of celery, is so widely tasted that parsley is one of the most indispensable seasonings for a large number of dishes in French cuisine. Following long, harsh winters, the absence of parsley from city markets is a real calamity for caterers and cooks; what little parsley is available is sold by weight, at exorbitant prices. As a result, well-managed parsley cultivation can yield significant profits in winter. When they anticipate a hard and prolonged winter, Parisian market gardeners don't hesitate to transplant a certain quantity of parsley in autumn to the foot of a south-facing wall; a glass frame placed on top protects the plants from frost; in this way, they grow more or less during the worst days of winter, and the gardener can profit from the temporary high price of a product that has very little value the rest of the year. Parsley is sown in spring, in a bed well covered with potting soil; the seed usually takes 5 to 6 weeks to germinate. Parsley does not flower or set seed until its second year. Three varieties of parsley are grown in vegetable gardens, often in borders: common parsley, curly parsley and very curly dwarf parsley, all suitable for the same uses. The last two varieties are not con11 stant; sowing them often results in common parsley. PARSLEY. - Chervil is suitable for all soils and exposures, its main fault being that it rises very quickly in summer, producing very few leaves, the edible part of the plant. It can nevertheless be grown in any season, provided the seed is sown in a bed with a northern exposure, and the leaves are cut off as they grow, which delays the formation of the flowering stems. In Paris, chervil is used only as a salad garnish; but in the eastern departments, soup is rarely served without a bunch of chervil, which is cut into the soup as it is eaten. Chervil is also used extensively in medicine, either for baths or as a herb juice, which absorbs fairly large quantities. Whatever you use chervil for, you must peel it very carefully to avoid leaving behind any small hemlock (ethusa cynapium), a plant that resembles chervil, but can be easily distinguished from it by the darker green and sharper divisions of its leaves; small hemlock is a very violent poison, all too often the cause of fatal accidents. This deadly plant can be recognized by the same signs when weeding chervil seedlings, which must be sought out in order to eradicate it with the utmost care. In addition to common chervil, curly chervil and musk chervil are also grown in vegetable gardens. The latter variety has a very strong aniseed flavor, which is not to the taste of most consumers, so it is not widely grown. ESTRAGON. - As the uses of this plant are much more limited than those of chervil, its cultivation is much more restricted; it suffices to have a few tufts whose leaves and young shoots, with a pleasant aromatic flavor, are used as a salad garnish and also to give a spicy taste to the vinegar in which pickles are preserved. Tarragon is not sown, as it is essentially a perennial plant of indefinite duration, and clumps that have become too strong are divided every year. In the Paris climate, tarragon overwinters well in the open air, but north of the Seine valley, it is advisable to cut back the stems on the stump, which is then mounded and covered with dry litter to ensure its preservation during hard frosts. POURPIER. - Purslane leaves are no longer eaten in salads, as they were in Boileau's day; in Paris, this plant has been all but abandoned. In northern France, people follow the Belgian practice of eating purslane stewed, and adding it to lean soups, to which it imparts a less pronounced acidity than sorrel. Purslane seed is sown in the early days of May, in a south-facing position. It should be sown lightly, as the stems spread out in all directions, flat on the ground, which they soon cover. To prolong the production of purslane leaves as much as possible, cut off the tops as they begin to flower. Purslane's very fine and abundant seed remains in the ground for several years, but only lifts when ploughing brings it to the surface. This is why, in a vegetable garden where purslane has been sown once and left to bear seed, it reproduces in perpetuity among other crops, as a weed, without having to be sown again. FENNEL. - The uses of fennel in French cuisine are even more limited than those of purslane. In the Paris area, it is only cultivated by market gardeners whose clientele includes Italian caterers. They blanch the ribs of the fennel leaves in boiling water and use them as a vegetable in a variety of ways. It's a very hot dish, and should only be used with care. Fennel seed is sown in March in the Paris climate; this plant requires dry rather than damp soil, and thrives best in a southern exposure; moreover, it requires no special cultivation care. CHAPTER XXI Salads. Plants whose leaves are eaten as salad. - Lettuce. - Superior to other salads. - Grown in the open air. - Its products. - Forced cultivation. Suffocated cultivation. - Species and varieties. - Spring lettuces. - Summer lettuces. - Winter lettuces. - Cutting lettuces. - Romaine lettuces. - Harvesting the seed. - Scarecrow to keep birds away. - Chicory. - Open air cultivation. - Forced cultivation. - Species and varieties. - Scarole. - Flemish method for blanching. - Wild chicory. - Chicory beard. - Lamb's lettuce. - Rapunzel. The plants whose leaves are eaten in salads form a separate series of edible leafy vegetables, both in terms of the way they are used and the way they are grown. The Italians were the only people in Europe to have retained the custom of eating various vegetable plants raw, seasoned with oil and vinegar, a custom handed down to them by the Greeks and Romans, when, during the French wars in Italy under Charles VIII, this custom was imported into France, from where it spread throughout Europe, along with the cultivation of plants suitable for eating in salads. In France in particular, the taste for salad has become so widespread that it's a dish that all classes of consumers can't do without. For a soldier to be satisfied with his ordinary fare, he is said to require only two dishes: soup and salad. Lettuce, chicory, lamb's lettuce and raiponium are grown as salads. Lettuce is the best and most esteemed. In many départements, when people talk about salad, it's only lettuce they're talking about; in fact, it's the salad par excellence. LAITUE. - Lettuce can be grown in the open air in all types of soil, provided they are not too compact. The best lettuces are obtained from soil that is both fertile and light. Lettuce is sown in March, so that the plant is ready for planting in April. As the saying goes, lettuce only turns, or forms its head, after it has been transplanted. However, if you mix a little lettuce seed with onion and scorzonera seedlings, and leave a few lettuces here and there to grow where their seed has sprouted, they will end up peeling, without ever becoming as beautiful as those that have been transplanted. In big cities, large-scale lettuce cultivation is a very important part of the kitchen garden, so it's important to make the principles clear. The lettuce plant should only be put in place when it has reached a certain stage of development. Transplanting is done in staggered rows, 30 centimetres apart in all directions; one acre of land therefore receives an average of 189 lettuces. The rapid growth of lettuce, favored by abundant manure and watering, means that three lettuce seasons can be planted on the same bed, as gardeners say, i.e., the plantation can be renewed three times, giving 567 lettuces per are, after which there is still time to ask the soil for another harvest of some kind before the bad season. Lettuces are worth 10 centimes a piece in spring, and only 2 and a half centimes in midsummer, i.e. an average of 5 centimes. This represents a gross income of 28 fr. 35 cent for one are. So, despite the low price at which lettuces are sold, a hectare devoted to this crop would yield a gross income of 2,835 fr. and provide yet another harvest. The most important point in growing lettuce is to avoid compressing the root collar by compacting the soil. The milky juice, from which lettuce derives its name, is most abundant at the neck of the plant; if this part is obstructed by soil compaction, the plant suffers; the head has difficulty forming; the lettuce, despite watering, is inclined to climb, that is, to flower and bear seed, instead of turning. So, before transplanting lettuces, the surface of the soil should be covered with a thick mulch, to prevent the water from watering the soil from compacting the surface too much. When transplanting, the plant, whose tissues are extremely delicate, should be pulled out and transplanted only with care, to avoid crushing the central leaves and damaging the root, either by pressing it too hard between the fingers, or by trampling it with the planter. After transplanting, the plant should be thoroughly wetted and, unless heavy rainfall occurs, watered thoroughly. When a planting of lettuces is to follow another on the same plot, after giving the surface of the soil a superficial treatment, the mulch is renewed, then the lettuces are transplanted, as far as possible, into the gaps where the previous crop of lettuces was removed. All you have to do is mark the place of the first lettuce at the beginning of the first row of each bed, measure the distances exactly for the first row, and continue planting with care, placing a lettuce just opposite the middle of the interval between two lettuces in the previous row; in this way, you obtain a very regular staggered row. Forced cultivation. - Forced cultivation of lettuces, practiced on a very large scale around Paris, is a source of considerable profit for the professional gardener. When well managed, the produce can be sold in winter at very moderate prices, so that the bad season does not interrupt the consumption of lettuce salads. In order to have planting material available for when the forced lettuces have to be planted on pre-prepared beds, the seed of the earliest lettuces is sown under a bell on a bed lined with potting soil at the foot of a wall, facing south. The whole process, from sowing the seed to harvesting, should be carried out in a "étouffée" system, giving the plant as little air as possible at all stages of its growth. As soon as seed lettuces have gained some strength, they are not transplanted immediately, but transplanted under other cloches, very close together; this transplanting is necessary to prepare the plant for rapid growth. When transplanting, only the space strictly necessary for the development of early lettuces should be left between the soil of the bed and the inner surface of the glass; the less air there is between the frame and the bed, the more successful the forced cultivation of lettuces. In the large-scale market gardening around Paris, lettuce beds are heated, like all early-growing beds, by the heat pipes of a thermosiphon. From time to time, it's not necessary to provide air - forced lettuce never needs it - but to lift the frames in order to wipe away the moisture condensed on their inner surface. If this cold moisture, which gardeners call mist, were to fall in icy droplets onto the lettuce leaves, it would produce stains that would be detrimental to the sale. SPECIES AND VARIETIES. - Among the many varieties of lettuce produced by cultivation, there are two clear-cut divisions: lettuces proper, or round-headed lettuces, and lettuces with elongated heads, or romaine lettuces; those of the second division are called romaines by abbreviation; they are also called chicons in several central departments. Among the lettuces of the first division, we distinguish spring or early lettuces, the earliest of all; summer lettuces, the most voluminous and the least suiient to assemble; and winter lettuces, which are treated as biennial plants and are planted in autumn to be delivered for consumption in the spring of the following year. Cutting lettuces, which do not head, also fall into this division. Spring lettuces. - These are almost exclusively used for forced cultivation; the two main species are crepe lettuce and gotte or gau lettuce, both of which are of little value in their own right, but have their earliness as a merit. A species with reddish edges, known as cordon rouge, has been cultivated around Paris. It is a variety of gotte lettuce, which can be grown in the open air on a south-facing hillside from the first fine days of spring. Summer lettuces. - There are many varieties of summer lettuce, the best of which are Versailles lettuce, one of the most delicate, lazy blonde, and cabbage lettuce, or Batavia lettuce. Cabbage lettuce, whose heads often reach the size of the largest cabbages, is not very tasty; it is only recommended for its size. It contains the highest proportion of the calming principle known to chemists as thridace. In several départements, Batavia cabbage lettuce is cultivated in large quantities for distribution to pigs; it facilitates their fattening by making them sleepy. Another variety almost as large, Naples cabbage lettuce, has the same properties, but with a slightly better taste. Winter lettuces. - Winter or biennial lettuces include passion lettuce, moraine lettuce and sometimes small crepe lettuce. Seedlings sown in autumn are transplanted to the foot of a south-facing wall as early as October, to give them time to fortify themselves before winter, and to resist the frosts that these types of lettuce withstand fairly well. In reality, they're pretty bad lettuces, with hard green leaves and an unpleasant herbaceous taste. But they do have the merit of being hardy; they pick very early and, as far as consumption is concerned, fill the gap between the harvest of the last forced lettuces and that of the first summer lettuces; this is what makes their cultivation advantageous, even though they are really only of inferior quality. Cutting lettuces. - Cutting lettuces do not refer to specific species, but to very thickly sown crepe and gotte lettuce plants, usually under a frame, and delivered for consumption without being transplanted. This is a very mediocre type of lettuce, sought after only because of its earliness. Cutting lettuce is often eaten in combination with lamb's lettuce and raipons. Romaine lettuces. - Romaine varieties and sub-varieties are even more numerous than those of round lettuces. The most widespread are blonde maraîchère, verte maraîchère, alphange and romaine de la madeleine. Blonde maraîchère is the most commonly grown. All romaines have a tendency to curl up, i.e., to fold their leaves over each other at the top, without needing to be forced by artificial means. However, when they are almost fully developed, gardeners are accustomed to tighten them around the middle of their height with a tie of wet rye straw, which speeds up and completes the process of shrivelling the inner leaves, making them whiter and more tender. Romaines are grown in the same way as round lettuces; they thrive in the same soils and receive the same cultivation care. Green romaine is grown under cloche or under a frame, either for forced cultivation on a bed, or just to be able to transplant it very early in the open air, and have romaine available for consumption from the beginning of spring. Harvesting the seed. - Only the most beautiful lettuces of each variety should be reserved as seedbeds; those of the romaine series that are to be allowed to flower should not be tied. Some of the birds that frequent vegetable gardens, such as goldfinches, are so greedy for lettuce seeds that they prey on the seed-bearers before the seeds are ripe; they would not leave a single one if you neglected to scare them away with scarecrows, the most effective of which are small 11. ten-cent mirrors suspended by a string from a slightly inclined post. Lettuce seed retains its germinative properties for several years; two- or three-year-old seed is preferable for sowing; the plant supplied by this seed is less quick to bolt than that from seed harvested the previous year. CHICORY. - Chicory is, if not the best, at least the healthiest of all lettuces; its mild bitterness in the good varieties makes it both invigorating and refreshing, a property that no other lettuce possesses. In Paris, chicories suitable for salads are consumed almost as widely as round and romaine lettuces; what's more, their ease of preservation makes them invaluable as a winter salad. Many are also used as stuffing, like sorrel, to accompany various meats. All varieties of curly endive are very sensitive to cold, and cannot be grown outdoors before April. It is better not to bury the seed at all, but to sow it on a bed lined with potting soil, and spread a very thin layer of dry potting soil powder over the seed. Only plant when strong enough; as with lettuces, spacing is 30 cm in all directions. The rest of the cultivation of curly endive in the open air is the same as for lettuces, up to the moment when their round leaves meet those of their neighbors on all sides. This is the point in their development when they need to be tied to blanch them. Chicory can be sown in the open air from month to month, until July. When the late season is fine, the chicories obtained from these late plantings are the best for winter supply. They are not very large, but they keep very well in a cellar or storeroom where frost cannot reach them, as they do not tolerate even mild cold. Forced cultivation. - To obtain a plant that can be planted in spring as soon as the outside temperature allows it to be transplanted in the open air, curly endive seed should be sown on a bed under a frame in February and March. Curly endive seed is sown at the beginning of January, on a bed that is as warm as possible, when you want to obtain very early curly endive. In this case, the seedling is transplanted onto an equally warm bed, 20 centimetres apart in all directions; it soon produces small but excellent curly endive plants, which the gardener is sure to find a very good price for. Species and varieties. - The main varieties of curly endive are Italian endive, the best and most widely grown, Meaux endive and Rouen endive, also known as stag's horn. Italian chicory is preferable to the other two for late sowing and forced cultivation on a warm bed. In Paris, known as scarole or escarole, and in northern France as endive, another chicory breed is available in several good sub-varieties. Like curly endive, escarole is grown from point to point in the open air; it is never treated with forced cultivation. When, in late autumn, a patch of escarole is caught by early cold weather before it has been possible to bind the escarole to make it blanch, you can fold the outer leaves all on the same side, lay their joined ends on the ground, and keep them in this position by throwing a shovelful of earth over them. As long as it's cold, don't touch them. As soon as the weather eases, the escarole is pulled off, the outer leaves more or less damaged by frost are cut off, and the blanched heart is delivered for consumption. This method is generally only used in Belgium and on our northern frontier, where escarole, known as endive, is the only salad eaten in winter; it could be used everywhere and applied to late varieties of curly endive. Wild chicory, as it grows in uncultivated areas and along roadsides, or what is known in the North as coffee chicory, because its fleshy root is used in the preparation of indigenous coffee, is also grown in gardens in the least complicated way. Wild chicory seed is sown in the open air in the ground; seedlings should be very thick. The smallest leaves are picked as they grow, and are eaten in salads, often mixed with leaf lettuce and lamb's lettuce. Wild chicory is used to make a type of salad known in Paris as Barbe de Capucin, which is a little tough and bitter, but healthy and very inexpensive. To obtain this salad, in midsummer, sow a few tightly packed beds of wild chicory in loose soil, where the plant's roots grow long like scorzonera, without branching. Towards the end of autumn, everything is pulled out, and the roots are gathered into bunches the size of a bunch of asparagus, taking care to remove all the leaves except those in the heart. These bunches are placed in a perfectly dark cellar, in piles formed by alternating beds of half-consumed manure and bunches of wild chicory roots. The heat of the manure soon causes the roots to emit long, white, etiolated leaves; these are the leaves that make up Capucin's beard chicory. The same result can be achieved more quickly, but with a little more expense, by planting bunches of wild chicory roots close together, upright, in a layer of fermenting manure. This layer should be set up in a room where no light can penetrate. CHEESE. - In France, lamb's lettuce is cultivated only in the vegetable gardens around Paris and a few large cities; everywhere else, we are content to pick what grows wild, particularly in cereal fields; this is why it is known throughout northern France as wheat salad; it is also known as doucette and oreille de lièvre. Three distinct varieties, mâche commune, mâche ronde and mâche d'Italie or mâche Régence, are grown in the kitchen garden; mâche d'Italie is the most highly regarded. The seed is sown towards the end of summer, or even in autumn, on beds that have borne one or more harvests of other vegetable beds. Lamb's lettuce grows very unevenly, so that if you take the ones that are at the right stage of development to be eaten as a salad, you can harvest them all winter long. This salad is most tender and best when the frost has passed over it. Lamb's lettuce seed is rather difficult to harvest, because it ripens successively, and falls off as it ripens. A certain quantity can only be obtained by uprooting the seed-bearing plants before the seeds have fully ripened. These plants, placed in the shade in a healthy but not too dry location, complete the ripening of their seeds, which can then be easily collected, retaining their germinative properties for several years. RAIPONCE. - Riponce seed is sown in midsummer, in light soil heavily amended with potting soil; this plant does not germinate when buried too deeply; it should simply be mixed into the surface soil with the tines of a fine rake. If you neglect to water it abundantly, raiponce does not grow less; but its white, fleshy root, of excellent taste, becomes so hard that it is almost not edible. When sufficiently watered, raiponce is, if not very tender, at least very edible; it usually accompanies lamb's lettuce salads, which it makes much more pleasant. Rapunzel grows wild all over France; the young plants found in February and March along hedges and ditches are as good as, if not better than, those cultivated in the kitchen garden. CHAPTER XXII Vegetables with edible flowers. Vegetables whose flowers are eaten. - Cauliflower. - Edible properties. - Year-round availability. - Suitable soil. - Transplanting, - Watering. - Growing the plant. - Wintering. - Planting. - Spring sowing. - Summer cultivation. - Its disadvantages. - Autumn cultivation. - Advantages. - Product preservation. - Forced cultivation. - Species and varieties. - Cultivation of rootstocks. - Broccoli. - Nasturtium. - Use of flower buds and seeds. - Borage. There aren't many vegetable plants grown for the purpose of eating their flowers: cauliflower, broccoli, nasturtium and borage. The first two are the only vegetable plants in the true sense of the term. Nasturtium is an ornamental plant, and borage is a medicinal plant. CAULIFLOWERS. - There's no vegetable in our gardens that beats cauliflower: it's healthy, light and nourishing, with a delicate flavor that's sought-after by all classes of consumers. It's the best vegetable food you can offer to the sick, to convalescents, and to anyone whose digestive organs are more or less fatigued. It is therefore one of the most important vegetables to be able to supply the kitchen with all year round, without interruption. In the climate of Paris, this is quite easy to achieve, and the high price of cauliflowers at the times of year when they are least abundant amply compensates the gardener for the expense and trouble he must take to ensure that the markets are never short of them. Light, fertile soil is best suited to growing cauliflower. If you want to grow cauliflowers in such soil, you need to amend it generously with potting soil, and if clay dominates, with a little lime and fine silica sand mixed together. Successful cauliflower growing is based on two main conditions: transplanting and watering. Irrespective of when you sow the cauliflower seed, the plant must always be transplanted at least once, and more often twice, before it is finally put into place. Simply uproot the plant and replant it immediately, close to the spot it has just occupied, or in the same spot, it doesn't matter. The aim of transplanting is to make the cauliflower plant stocky, to enlarge the root collar, and to hasten the formation of the head. Transplanting applied to various plants, and especially to different species of cabbage, would produce similar effects; but if it is useful for cabbages, it can be considered indispensable for cauliflowers, although several gardening treatises assert the contrary. Cauliflower is rarely grown entirely in the open air; the plant that finishes its growth in the open air in the vegetable garden is almost always raised under a bell or under a frame, at least during the first period of its growth. To produce cauliflowers that can be harvested from around May 15 to June 15, cauliflower seed is sown in the open air in the first half of September in the Paris climate; this is the most suitable time for the plant to overwinter easily. As the cold approaches, gardeners first transplant the cauliflower plant under a bell or under a frame, at the foot of a south-facing wall; they cover it with litter or straw mats in times of snow or heavy frost, and give it as much air and light as possible whenever the temperature permits. When the severe cold has passed, in the second half of February, the plant is transplanted a second time, under the same cloches or frames, so that it can be transplanted permanently in the first half of March. In small gardens, where a sufficient number of cloches and frames are not always available, you can achieve much the same result by sowing the cauliflower seed at the bottom of a pit, carefully ploughing the soil and mixing in a large quantity of good compost. This pit, no more than 30 centimetres deep, is dug in a flowerbed at the foot of a wall facing south; the soil extracted from it is thrown back to the right and left, where it forms two embankments on which poles of suitable length are placed.
