If it's cold, mats or dry litter are thrown over these poles to protect the cauliflower plant; transplanting is done in another similar pit at the times indicated above, and the plant, if it has been well managed during wintering, i.e. if it has been covered and uncovered at the right time, is ready to be put in place at the same time as the one that has spent the winter under a bell or under a frame. A circular depression is made around each cauliflower plant, when they are finally transplanted, 50 or 60 centimetres in all directions, depending on the volume they are to acquire. From that day on, they should be thoroughly and continuously wetted, except in the event of prolonged heavy rainfall. When the apples start to form, if you want them to be a beautiful white, don't forget to cut a piece of fresh leaf and lay it flat on the apple to protect it completely from contact with air and light. The qualities required of a good cauliflower are an evenly rounded shape, a more or less even surface, free from small leaves and cracks, and a grain that is both fine and tight. Abundant watering with the neck of the watering can without the sheaf, morning and evening, is what contributes most to developing these qualities in cauliflower, which make it so attractive to buyers. Cauliflower seedlings are sown again in March under a sowing frame, but this time on a warm bed, otherwise the plant will not advance sufficiently and cannot be planted in good time. Seedlings sown at this time are transplanted only once, in the first half of April. The heads of these cauliflowers form around Midsummer and are ready for harvesting in July, but even with all the cultivation care they require, you can't always count on a good crop. In years when the hot weather arrives earlier than usual, summer cauliflower, despite assiduous watering, becomes partially "bollarded", as the saying goes; in other words, the head splits, divides and tends to flower prematurely, rendering it worthless. Cauliflower seed cannot be sown in the open air before April 10th or 15th in the Paris climate, or eight or ten days later in northern France. The plant obtained from these seedlings only needs to be pricked out before being transplanted. It is exposed to the hottest summer weather, and therefore very prone to budding. This is the time of year when cauliflower cultivation offers the least chance of success; it is also the time when this crop takes up the least space in the vegetable garden. On the contrary, it's very advantageous, and its success is certain with little expense or hassle, when you sow cauliflower seed around Midsummer's Day, in the last days of June, choosing a position in half-shade, as gardeners say. At the end of July, after transplanting, the plant can be put in place. As it is well watered, it suffers little from the heat of August, which has passed by the time it forms its head, and therefore cannot hinder its development. Depending on temperature and exposure, these cauliflowers can be harvested successively in September and October; the last ones to form can be kept as a winter supply, by stripping them of their large leaves and hanging them upside down in a frost-free place. The later the season, the higher the price of cauliflowers preserved in this way; however, you mustn't be greedy enough to wait until they begin to spoil before disposing of them. When winter is wet, rot quickly invades them, and you risk losing everything by trying to gain too much. When a few frames are available at the onset of winter, cauliflowers with only half-formed heads can be transplanted close together, on a muted layer mainly made up of leaves and well covered with potting soil; they grow slightly and can be delivered successively for consumption throughout the season when fresh vegetables are the rarest and most sought-after: this gives them a fairly high value, even though they remain small. Forced cultivation. - To avoid any interruption in the consumption of cauliflowers, it is necessary to take steps to harvest them as early as April, the time of year when preserved cauliflowers from the previous year and those that have been overwintered on a deaf bed are in short supply on the market. For this purpose, cauliflower seed is sown in early September; the resulting plant spends part of the winter in a cold frame; after being pricked out on an unheated bed, it is planted out on a new warm bed at the foot of a south-facing wall. Depending on the gardener's material resources, a bell is placed over each cauliflower and radish seed is sown around it, or it is covered with glass frames. In this case, the warm layer must be set in a pit deep enough to leave enough space between the glass and the potting soil for the cauliflowers to grow. If you sow the seed of Lenormand cauliflower, an excellent variety introduced to market gardening only a few years ago, in the open ground, choosing the last week of August or the first week of September for sowing, depending on the state of the season, the plant placed at the foot of a wall facing due south will survive the ordinary winters of the Paris climate quite well, provided it is protected as follows: short brushwood is planted at an angle, in front of the planting line and inclined towards the wall; in frosty weather, a little straw or dry litter is thrown over the brushwood, and uncovered when the frost thaws. Unless the winter is exceptionally harsh, Lenormand cauliflower does not freeze; it starts growing very early in the spring of the following year, and bears apples from the end of April to the end of May. Not all of these cauliflowers grow, but those that do are of excellent quality; like other cauliflowers, the plant does not need to be pricked out once or twice before being permanently transplanted to the place where it is to spend the winter. Species and varieties. - The cauliflowers known to gardeners under various names are simple sub-varieties that are often indistinguishable from one another to the untrained eye. In the marshes around Paris, only one variety is grown, although three are known, and are quite distinct in the way they behave in cultivation: these are tender, semi-hard and hard cauliflowers, whose names sufficiently describe their qualities. Demidur is the most widely adopted, either for cultivation in the open air or for forced cultivation. The soft variety, also known as petit Salomon, is best suited to summer cultivation, while the hard variety is fairly successful for late cultivation, and its apples keep well for part of the winter. However, it has the serious drawback of being hopelessly slow to form apples, and sometimes of not forming them at all, which does not help the gardener. For some years now, a fine sub-variety of semi-hard cauliflower has been grown under the name of Lenormand, after the gardener who introduced it, and is remarkable for the extraordinary volume of its apples. As for cauliflowers from Malta, Saint-Gilles (near Brussels), Roscoff (Finistère) and several other localities, they offer no distinguishing qualities; they are all hard or semi-hard cauliflowers, in no way superior to commonly grown varieties. A few of the best cauliflowers from each sub-variety are set aside in the spring as seed-bearing plants. If the seed is to retain all the qualities of the mother plant, the flower stalk should be topped at the start of flowering, leaving only a moderate number of siliques on the side stalks. Cauliflowers grown for seed are quite often invaded by the green aphid; as soon as this is noticed, they should be freed by strong fumigation with corporal tobacco. BROCOLI. - Although broccoli is botanically a very different species of cabbage from cauliflower, the cultivation of one is exactly the same as that of the other; however, broccoli is much more sensitive to cold than cauliflower; the plant is much more prone to melting during the winter, and as, in the final analysis, broccoli is in no way superior to cauliflower as a foodstuff, it is rarely grown in the Paris climate. Their cultivation is easy and advantageous only in the south, from the Loire valley upwards; the seed sown in June produces a plant that is good for setting, after a single transplanting in July and August. The apples, half-formed at the start of winter, can, in the mild climate of this part of France, complete their growth in the open air with a little shelter, which, in the climate of Paris, is only possible with pain and expense for which the gardener is not compensated by the sale of broccoli. The main broccoli varieties are: white, which differs little from cauliflower except for its wavy leaves, green and purple. A beautiful white variety with a very large head, introduced from England as mammoth broccoli around 1850, has not lived up to its promise in terms of hardiness; it is no more resistant to cold than other broccolis and has not spread to France for this peremptory reason. CAPUCINES. - This pretty plant, of which several very fine species are grown as ornamental plants, is sown in March and April and flowers all summer long, along trellises, cradles or any other support required by its climbing stems. The flowers, picked without their support, are not only an ornament to the salad; they also impart a pleasant aromatic odor, a flavor similar to that of watercress, and properties similar to those of the latter plant, which people have rightly dubbed the health of the body. So, for cooking purposes, the kitchen garden must always have a small area devoted to nasturtium. As they form, the seeds are harvested at half their size, and candied in vinegar like capers; these seeds have the pungent taste of mustard. When a large enough number of nasturtium plants are in bloom, part of the bloom can be sacrificed and the pea-sized flower buds picked; the spur is removed, and they are then candied in vinegar, either alone or mixed with the green seeds; they have a milder, more delicate flavor than the seeds, and can replace capers with advantage, especially when candied separately. BOURRACHE. - This plant is listed as a vegetable only for its very pale blue flowers, which, like nasturtium flowers, are used to decorate salads; they are otherwise devoid of odor, flavor and stimulating properties. Borage is very floriferous; if its usefulness were limited to adorning salads, it would suffice to have a few plants here and there in the vegetable garden, by sowing the seed in March or September; moreover, the plant requires no cultivation care. But as its dried leaves are an excellent sudorific, it is always useful in the countryside to place borage in a corner of the vegetable garden as a medicinal plant, whose flowers can also be picked to decorate salads. CHAPTER XXIII Vegetable plants with edible fruit. Strawberries. Importance of vegetables with edible fruit. - Strawberries. - Properties of the strawberry - Multiplication of the strawberry plant - Net or runner planting. - Clump division. - Sowing. - Planting. - Suitable soil for strawberries. - Time of planting. - Spacing. - Cultivation care. - Insect enemies. - White worm. - Moth. - Means of protection. - Forced cultivation. - Shelters to hasten strawberry flowering. - Species and varieties. - Remontant strawberries. - Non-remontant strawberries of American origin. - Strawberry high wood bifere. Plants cultivated for their fruit occupy an important place in the vegetable garden; although these fruits are not among the plant foods we cannot do without, they are the object of very widespread consumption; when they are rare and expensive, this is a very sensitive deprivation for all classes of consumers; their production is one of the most lucrative branches of gardening for the professional gardener, and a source of varied pleasures for the amateur gardener. This series includes strawberries, melons, cucumbers, gherkins, squash, tomatoes, eggplants and chillies. Mushroom cultivation has also been included, although this cryptogam is not a fruit in the true sense of the term. STRAWBERRY. - The fruit of the strawberry tree is the one that ripens in the harshest climate; the plant does not freeze, no matter how cold it is. Towards the northern extremities of our planet, in Iceland, Greenland and Siberia, the strawberry is the fruit found closest to the North Pole; the strawberry plant vegetates there with such activity that it has time to flower and ripen its fruit in the very short interval during which the surface of the ground thaws between two winters of 9 to 10 months. Everyone knows about the strawberry's pleasant taste and refreshing properties; what is not generally known is that strawberries eaten several times a day are the most effective of all known gout remedies. The father of modern botany, Linnaeus, discovered this property of strawberries through his own healing. Multiplication. - All strawberry plants, with the exception of a single species, send nets or runners around them in all directions, whose nodes take root and produce plants which in turn give nets, so that a single strawberry plant left to its own devices covers a considerable area in a short space of time. In woods where a few strawberry plants have survived here and there under the dead leaves, if a cut is made, the air and light develop their vegetative force so well that the following year the ground is literally covered with strawberry plants, as if they had been planted on purpose. The runners developed first after fruiting are the best for planting. Strawberry bushes, which do not produce runners, are propagated by dividing the clumps in spring and autumn. Strawberry plants are only propagated by sowing strawberry seeds, to regenerate good species and keep them unaltered, or to obtain new varieties. The innumerable large-fruited strawberry varieties of American origin, whose invasion of cultivation has revolutionized strawberry production over the last twenty years, owe their origin to sowing. The strawberry seed harvested from the best fruit of each variety, which is left to dry in the shade and then crushed in water to separate the seeds, only grows well in heather soil. Seedlings must be watered assiduously until they have sprouted; if the half-sprouted seed suffers from drought, it will not sprout. The seedling can be transplanted at a very young age, at varying distances according to the volume of the species, to be planted when it has gained sufficient strength. Planting. - Any good garden soil is suitable for strawberry plants, provided it is not too compact; in strong, clay soil, strawberry plants develop a useless luxury of leaves and runners, flower poorly and bear little fruit. In all cases, the soil must be ploughed very carefully and fertilized abundantly with well-consumed manure. When ploughing a garden bed to plant strawberries, be very careful to remove any bindweed roots or other perennials that may be present. Strawberry beds must be mulched thickly, both to keep them cool from watering, and to prevent the contact of bare earth from soiling the fruit. If the perennial weed develops later, it will be difficult to get rid of it; as for the annual weed, the mulch is enough to suffocate it as it emerges; it cannot invade the plantation. Strawberry plants are usually planted in autumn, better in mid-September than in October; in beds planted too late, there are almost always gaps to be filled in the spring of the following year. The wild strawberry plant you intend to grow in your garden should also be uprooted from the woods in the first half of September, leaving as little time as possible between uprooting and planting. Large strawberry plants with very large fruits should be planted at 35 or 40 centimetres in all directions; small and medium varieties should be planted at 25 or 30 centimetres. From the moment it is planted, the strawberry plant needs plenty of water. If planted in autumn, watering should stop when the first white frosts appear; if planted in spring, watering should continue throughout the summer. Apart from the chapter on accidents, a strawberry plantation planted with good seedlings, in the right season, well mulched and properly watered, is in full production in its second and third years, after which its yield declines; it must be renewed. To avoid losing ground, market gardeners plant a row of long-headed cabbage or two rows of leeks between the rows of large strawberry plants in autumn. The produce from this intercrop is removed in good time, so as not to interfere with the growth of the strawberry plants. As for those of the smaller species, which start producing in their first year of planting, they are not spaced far enough apart to admit other crops between their rows. Cultivation care. - Strawberry plants require a lot of water at all times of growth, and nets must be removed once or twice a week, as they exhaust the plant and interfere with fruiting. After the strawberries have been harvested, a part of the plantation is cared for separately, leaving all its nets, so as to have enough planting material to renew the plantations. Observation has shown that rainwater, especially storm water, is harmful to flowering strawberry plants when the soil is more or less hardened by drought. This is why, when a storm approaches, Parisian market gardeners are quick to wet their strawberry plantations thoroughly. Enemies of the strawberry plant. - In the garden, the strawberry plant has only one enemy, whose attacks often bring despair to the gardener. The Turc, man, or white worm, larva of the chafer, attracted by the particular smell of strawberry roots, which it prefers to any other food, often digs very long galleries underground to reach its roots. When a strawberry plantation falls prey to white grubs, there's nothing to be done: it's lost. Accidents of this kind are all the more difficult to avoid as, when ploughing to plant strawberries, the soil can be full of chafer eggs without the gardener noticing. In this case, if the planting is not too extensive, the beds should be dug out to the depth of a spade-shoe, all the soil removed, the bottom of the pit lined with a thick, well-trampled bed of carpenter's shavings or dry chestnut leaves, the soil put back on top, and panted as usual. The mandibles of beetle larvae are not strong enough 12 to pierce this obstacle, especially when dry chestnut leaves have been used, whose tough tissue resists attack.

The leaves of oak, lime or any other tree would not be a sufficient barrier; the white worm would penetrate them to reach the strawberry roots. Strawberries that ripen successively should not be harvested without care; stems bearing ripe fruit, green fruit and flowers at the same time would become sterile, and most of the crop would be lost, if the plants were shaken too much when the strawberries were harvested. The mole cricket also often wreaks havoc on strawberry plantations, especially when the soil has been smoked with fermenting manure, which attracts this destructive insect. When there is reason to fear its presence, the soil should be thoroughly soaked with warm cattle urine, as we have previously advised (Chap. XI), before shaping it for strawberry planting. Forced cultivation. - The vigorous vegetation of the strawberry plant means that strawberries can be harvested without interruption by means of forced cultivation; it is well known that at the English court, as at the Belgian court, it is etiquette to serve a dish of strawberries for dessert at the royal table, every day of the year, without exception. Forced strawberry cultivation offers no serious difficulties. The best plant for forcing is taken from the nets produced in July by young strawberry plants planted in March of the same year; the runner plant from plantations that are already a year or two old would not give nearly as good results. This seedling, detached from the runners as its young roots begin to form, is raised in a shady nursery until the first days of September. It is then lifted and planted at the rate of three strawberry plants in a medium-sized pot filled with the best possible soft, light and very-substantial soil. From December onwards, these strawberry plants are successively forced into portions, either in the pineapple greenhouse, or in frames where the pots are buried in tannin or in the manure of a warm layer. Professional gardeners who force strawberry plants throughout the winter do not heat with manure; they run thermosiphon pipes through a series of chests covered by their glass frames. The forced strawberry plants want to be under the frames as in the pineapple greenhouse, placed as close as possible to the glass, so that they receive as much light as possible. This precaution is especially necessary to give the strawberries good color as they ripen. The strawberry obtained by forced cultivation is always produced in small quantities, for the exclusive use of the most fortunate consumers. At a time when the first strawberries harvested in the open air are eagerly awaited by the public, the gardener can make a handsome profit by simply hastening the ripening of the strawberries by very inexpensive means. The simplest of all consists in planting young strawberry plants in narrow beds, in three rows, running east-west: behind these beds, stakes are erected to which straw mats are attached, forming a sort of temporary espalier facing south. From five to five meters away, the same system is used to establish strawberry mat shelters the same width as the beds; this combination places the strawberry beds in a sort of sheltered area on all sides. They flower very early and produce ripe strawberries eight to ten days before the ripeness of strawberries of the same species grown without shelter; this is enough for the gardener to sell them at relatively very advantageous prices. These strawberry plants can be made even earlier if, in addition to the procedure just described, glass frames are placed over the strawberry beds during very cold weather. Doormats or litter are thrown over these frames, which are uncovered and lifted to provide air when the temperature permits. In this way, without resorting to any artificial heat production, Parisian market gardeners, who do not subject strawberry plants to forced cultivation, are nevertheless able to obtain a fair quantity of very early ripening strawberries. Species and varieties. - From a gardening point of view, strawberry plants, of which there are many collections, form only two distinct series: remontant strawberry plants, which produce several successive harvests in the same year, and non-remontant strawberry plants, which produce only one harvest a year. So far, all efforts to include large-fruited strawberries in the remontant series have been completely unsuccessful. Everbearing strawberries. - The best of the remontant strawberry plants - indeed, the best of all cultivated strawberry plants - is the Alpine strawberry, known as the four-season strawberry. Its strawberries are fragrant, pleasant-tasting and highly productive, giving one of the earliest yields and only ceasing to produce at the onset of winter. It is also one of the most advantageous for forced cultivation, although it is never very voluminous. The Gaillon strawberry bush is an excellent variety of the previous one, obtained from seed. Its fruit is exactly the same; it is just as productive and just as remontant; what distinguishes this strawberry is that it is the only one that does not produce nets or runners and forms large tufts or bushes, which makes it eminently suitable for planting at the front and back of flowerbeds lining vegetable garden paths. It is important to double the clumps every two years to rejuvenate the plantations; otherwise, the bushes become too thick, and the central shoots, blocked in their development, do not flower, which reduces the strawberry harvest. Non-remontant strawberry plants - Most non-remontant strawberry plants, of American origin, were first introduced into England, which is why they are often referred to as English strawberry plants in continental gardens. However, two European species are still grown: the woodland strawberry, with very small, round fruit of excellent flavor, which can be made medium-sized by cultivation in good soil, and the Montreuil strawberry, with very large fruit and the flavor of the woodland strawberry. As for the latter, to obtain it with all the qualities that make it so highly prized, you simply have to plant it in the woods, preferably on the sides of ditches facing south, and treat it like the other garden species. Caprous, with their large, lightly-colored, slightly musky fruit, used to be very popular, but have been more or less abandoned since the introduction of American strawberries. Their main fault is that they are dioecious, i.e. they bear male and female flowers on separate plants; the male plants, which are necessarily sterile, needlessly take up space in the garden beds. Pineapple strawberry plants, remarkable for their swollen stalks, have also been abandoned for strawberry plants of American origin; their somewhat bland and very mussy flavor is not to everyone's taste. American strawberry plants have given rise to countless new varieties every day through hybrid crosses and seedlings, as can be seen from the catalogs of the growers who sell them. A general remark applies to all these varieties: those with rounded fruit are sweeter than acid; those with fruit ending in a point are more acid than sweet; their tip often remains green while the upper part of the fruit is perfectly ripe. The most worthy of cultivation among American strawberry plants are: Virginia Scarlet, easily distinguished by the bluish-green of its leaves and the depth of the fruit cavities in which the seeds are housed. The best sub-variety of the scarlet strawberry is found near Liége in Belgium, where it has long been naturalized. It is the earliest of the non-emerging strawberry species; 12. The Chilean strawberry, with its very large fruit, of which there are several good sub-varieties, is only very productive in warm, south-facing soil; Goliath and superb Wilmolt produce fruit almost as large as that of the Chilean strawberry. Bicton white is the best of the white strawberries. British-Queen is one of the most productive and best of the American round-fruited varieties. Those who have enough space, by allocating a compartment to each of the species we indicate, can harvest non-remontant American strawberries for five to six weeks. Another very remarkable species is called "haut bois", because of the height of its flower stalks. The fruit is very dark in color, with a vinous flavor, and often aborts, a defect that makes the highbush strawberry less common in vegetable gardens. However, this defect can be corrected by cultivation, and is compensated for by a valuable quality. Although it is not precisely a remontant plant, the highbush strawberry is biferous, meaning that in autumn it produces a second bloom followed by a fairly abundant crop of strawberries. In any case, an American large-fruited strawberry as remontant as the four-season Alpine strawberry remains to be conquered. The gardener who obtains it will render a great service to horticulture, and his fortune will be made. = - CHAPTER XXIV Vegetable plants with edible fruit. - Melon. Melon. - The modern revolution in cultivation. - Sowing in beds. - The need to hasten melon growth. - Root ball transplanting. - Precautions to be taken. - Sowing in the open air. - Melon pruning - Natural melon vegetation. - What it becomes when left unpruned - Purpose of pruning. - Results - How to prune - Choosing between meshes. - Cultivation care. - Forced cultivation. - Species and varieties -Cantaloupes. - Embroidered melons. - Smooth-skinned melons. -Seed harvesting. - Accidental crosses. - Watermelons. MELON. - The melon is an entirely artificial product; the good quality of its fruit depends entirely on intelligent cultivation. This is so true that in our Midi departments, whose climate is eminently favorable to melons, they are not eaten as perfectly as in Paris, even though the Parisian climate is not nearly as well suited as that of the Midi to the vegetation of this delicate plant, originally from the hottest regions of Asia. Like many other garden crops, the melon has undergone a complete and very happy revolution since the beginning of this century. Fifty years ago, only the least delicate types of melon were cultivated in the Paris region; nothing was rarer than a good melon, and this state of affairs had lasted for a very long time, for a poet of the XVth century had the right to write the following verses, so often reproduced: Les amis de l'heure présente Sont d'un naturel de melon; Il faut goûter plus de trente Avant que en trouver un bon. Today, the opposite is true; what is rare, wherever market gardening is intelligently practiced as it is in the Paris area, is a bad melon. At several points on the Normandy coast, especially around Honfleur, melon is grown in large quantities for export to England, making it a flourishing and highly profitable branch of gardening. Sowing in beds. - In the Paris climate, melon seeds can only be sown in the open air during the month of May, when there is no longer any fear of cold nights, or only cool nights, which the plant would not tolerate. In years when summer temperatures follow their normal course, and when this season brings its annual quota of heat, the melon has time to blossom and ripen its fruit, while the weather is still warm enough for the sale of melons to be easy, and for this fruit to be sought after by consumers. But if, as often happens, summer is wet and brings with it only a few rare fine days in the Seine valley, the melons sown in the open air, no matter how much care is taken to hasten their growth, flower too late; their fruit, barely fully formed in autumn, ripens badly or not at all, and can only be offered to consumers at a time when the greatest heat has passed, and the melons are no longer sought after. For all these reasons, melons are rarely grown entirely in the open air in Paris. Even those that should grow and bear fruit in the open air come from seedlings sown on a warm bed in a frame. After transplanting, these seedlings are ready to be transplanted at home, at the time of year when, if the gardener had not taken the time to save time, the outside temperature would have allowed them to be sown in situ. - Transplanting. - The melon, like all plants in the Cucurbitaceae family, to which it belongs, is very difficult to transplant; its roots need to be disturbed as little as possible. This is why it has become common practice among gardeners who excel in this type of cultivation, to sow melon seeds only in pots. Two seeds are sown in each small pot, filled with light soil mixed with compost. The pots are immediately placed in a good layer covered with a glass frame. Seedlings of this kind are sown twice, in March and April. Once the seed has set, the least vigorous plant is removed, and as it's still quite cold outside at this time of year, the frames are kept closed, with air only being let in when the weather eases. The inside of the windows are wiped down to prevent the mist from falling in icy drops on the young melon plant, which would then perish. When the plant has its first two well-formed leaves, in addition to the seminal leaves, it's time to transplant it onto a second warm layer, prepared in advance. If the plant is not raised in a pot, much of it will be lost in the transplanting process, but it is easy to remove it from the pot without disturbing the roots. Immediately plant it as a clod in larger pots, filled with soil similar to that in which the seed was sown. The pots are immediately plunged into the new layer; the melons remain there until they can be transplanted definitively to a final layer, established at the foot of a wall, to the south. Sowing in the open air. - Once the seed has been sown and the melon plant transplanted as described above, on two different occasions, one month apart, you can produce, as gardeners say, two seasons' worth of melons, with the seedlings sown indoors. If the weather is favorable, a third season of melons can be grown outdoors. To sow melon seed in the open air, from the 10th to the 20th of May, heaps of half-decomposed manure are prepared on a south-sloping bed, spaced 1.5 meters apart from their centers. When the piles have been on the ground for ten to twelve days, and have more or less settled in on themselves, top them with ten to fifteen centimetres of light garden soil, mixed with half loam, and sow the melon seed; seedlings sown in place do not need to be transplanted. Melon roots tolerate very well contact with the most active fertilizers, which would burn those of any other plant; this property is common to all plants in the same family. As the plants grow, their vegetation is stimulated by spreading a few handfuls of dovecote, or barnyard bird droppings, from the cleaning of the dovecote and henhouse; if dovecote is not available, guano can be used. The water from the sprinklers penetrates these fertilizers right down to the roots of the melons, which benefit greatly from them. Melon pruning. - As soon as the melon plant, whether raised on a bed or sown in the field, has taken its fourth leaf, above the seminal leaves, it's time to think about pruning. To prune a melon successfully, it's essential to understand the principle and to be aware of the melon's natural growth habit. When a melon is left to its own devices and left unpruned, this is how it behaves. At first, it grows a long, straight, simple stem, which crawls along the ground; this stem only branches when it has already moved away from its starting point; it first produces a fairly large number of male flowers, which are perfectly useless, as there are no female flowers yet. It's only when it has grown sufficiently that each branching produces one or two meshes at its tip, the name gardeners give to the female flowers that are to be followed by fruit. These fruits, which are always few in number, never have the normal volume of their species on plants that are not pruned, because while they are forming, the stem continues to lengthen more or less, absorbing sap to their detriment; they lack both flavor and volume, and ripen very late. Observation has shown that the closer a melon is formed to the root collar, the better it ripens; it can only be so far from the root collar on a stem that is not pruned; the unnecessary prolongation of the vegetation of this stem necessarily delays the ripening of the fruit. The above clearly shows the purpose of melon pruning. On the one hand, the aim is to make the fruit grow as close as possible to the root collar; on the other, to hasten ripening by stopping the growth of the stems above the melons as soon as they start to swell. By pruning, the gardener also aims to produce a large number of fruits on each plant, although only one or two, rarely three, should be reserved. The most perfectly shaped fruits, according to their species, are always the best; when each melon plant produces only two or three, whether well or poorly shaped, they must be allowed to grow; when it produces eight or ten, there are always some of good shape; only these are kept, and the gardener removes the others. Now that the need for pruning, its purpose and results are well understood, here's how it's done. First, the central shoot of young plants is pinched off above the fourth leaf. This entrenchment leads to the development of two lateral shoots. When these are eight to ten centimetres long, they are treated in the same way, and after a few days produce two shoots each, making four branches which, pinched back when they have lengthened sufficiently, eventually produce eight good shoots. Pruning stops here for the moment, and the gardener waits for the meshes to form. As each shoot should produce one or two, he can count on a dozen fruits, which he will let grow just enough to judge them with a clear conscience. Once he's made up his mind, he prunes severely, leaving each plant with an average of just two shoots, each bearing a beautiful fruit. Later on, pruning will be aimed at containing the shoots above the fruit, and preventing the development of any branches that might tend to form on parts of the stem whose sap must be concentrated on the fruit. Pruning, as described above, is particularly suitable for melons of the best species, which are grown on a bed under a frame, from the time the seed is sown until the fruit ripens. A little less care should be taken with second-quality melons, whose seed is usually sown in the ground. After pinching once to obtain two shoots, and a second time to obtain four, these four shoots are left to grow away from the collar, producing one or two good meshes each. The greater hardiness of the plants and the less delicate quality of their fruit mean that four to six can be left on each plant; all that remains to be done then is to regulate the subsequent growth of the stems, as above, by pruning, so that the plant's sap is used exclusively for fruit development. Cultivation care. - The best melons, whatever their variety, are known as "bed melons", because the seed is sown on a bed, the plant is transplanted, and the plant goes through all the phases of its vegetation until the fruit is ripe. Once the right season has arrived, bedding melons remain uncovered day and night, and need frequent watering as long as it doesn't rain. The gardener must always be in a position to protect his melons against frost, which, even when it is not very large, causes them to perish without remedy. If, after a storm, you examine the parts of the melon stem that have been hit by hailstones, you can see a small spot; the next day, it's a wound that becomes gangrenous and leads to the death of the plant. As any summer storm can be accompanied by hail, the prudent gardener always keeps glass frames and straw mats within reach of the melon beds. In the twinkling of an eye, when a threatening cloud approaches, this precious crop is sheltered; doormats and frames are removed when the storm has passed. As the fruit nears maturity, a piece of broken tile or pot shard is placed under each melon to prevent the moisture in the layer from rotting the side of the fruit in contact with it. Melons sown in place, and some of those transplanted onto a deaf layer but without a frame, are known as cloche melons. A glass bell raised on one side to give access to the air is placed over each well-formed fruit, and old mats and litter are kept within reach of the bells, which are hastily thrown over the bells when a storm approaches, as melon bells cannot withstand a strong hailstorm when it hits them directly. Forced calture. - Near large cities, it is highly advantageous to force-feed melons, which can produce edible fruit as early as April on a warm bed. To this end, melon seed is sown in pots on a very hot bed from the first days of January. Transplant on an equally warm bed, and place on a third bed as warm as the previous two. Maintain warmth by applying new manure around the bed in the form of stoves, renewed as needed. The pruning and management of forced melons is based on the principles set out above; forced melons are usually left with only one fruit on each plant. These melons, even for professional gardeners who heat them with the thermosiphon, which is less costly than manure when used in large quantities, come at a very high price and are never very good, but as they are sold in proportion to what they cost to produce, this method of cultivation can be very advantageous, provided the sale is assured. Species and varieties. - Among the many varieties of melon, the superiority of the Cantaloupe cannot be disputed. In the climate of Paris, melons from this series can be grown on their own and satisfy all consumer requirements. The best of the Cantaloupes are the black Carmelite, the orange and the fine early, all three of which are not very large, but are fast-growing and, for this reason, eminently suitable either for forced cultivation or for the first season. Prescott, Petit Prescott and Noir de 13 Hollande, all from the Cantaloupe series, are equally suitable for the first and second seasons. For seedlings in the field, we prefer the brodé melon, or market melon, which has gradually been banished from cultivation and replaced by cantaloupes, the elongated Honfleur melon, the Tours melon and the sweet green Angers melon, both excellent, but not very large, and better for amateur gardens than for sale. In the south of France, smooth-skinned melons are preferred, the most highly regarded varieties being the Cavaillon or winter melon, of which considerable quantities are produced for the Lyon and Marseille markets, the Malta melon and the Odessa melon. Harvesting grains. - Great care must be taken to ensure that melon seeds are harvested only from the most beautiful, fully ripened fruits of each species. Pumpkins, squash, cucumbers and generally all cucurbits contain a great abundance of pollen or fertilizing dust, which the bees, in their incessant work, carry from one flower to another, thus effecting frequent accidental crossings. These crosses, when they occur between melons and other plants of neighboring genera, or only between different varieties of melons, completely denature them, causing them to lose the properties that constitute the particular merit of each species. In this respect, there is no danger in keeping varieties that flower at different times close to one another, as this makes accidental cross-breeding impossible. However, species that you wish to keep pure and that flower at the same time as others that are entirely different should only be cultivated at a distance, and in careful isolation. PASTEQUES. - We mention only for the record the watermelon, whose cultivation is only possible on a few points of our extreme southern border; outside the orange region, the watermelon does not ripen its fruit, and the plant does not lend itself to forced cultivation. The watermelon is not a true melon, although it resembles smooth-skinned melons on the outside. In the Var and Eastern Pyrenees regions, watermelons are grown without any precautions, like pumpkins, in open fields, in holes filled with a mixture of good soil and manure. Once the seed has been sown in these holes, no further attention is paid to it. Watermelons, grown only on irrigable land, take their share of general irrigation; they are not pruned or cared for in any way, and nonetheless produce large, healthy and pleasant fruit, thanks to the climate alone. CHAPTER XXV Luminous vegetables with edible fruit. - Cucumbers, Gherkins, Squash, Tomatoes, Aubergines, Peppers. Cucumbers. - Sowing. - Open air cultivation. - Forced cultivation. - Species and varieties. - Gherkins. - Snake cucumber. - Squash. - Pumpkin. Health properties. - Sowing. - Trausplantation. - Protection of the plant by counter-soils. - Watering. - Marcottage of stems - Species and varieties. - Siam ball. - Hungarian or Spanish green. - Giraumont. - Tomato. - Open-air cultivation. - Its disadvantages. - Sowing in layers. - Transplanting. - Stem pruning. - Trellising. - Artificial ripening of tomatoes. - Forced cultivation. - Eggplant or melongene. - Purple fruit. - Blauc fruit or egg plant. - Chili pepper. - Cultivation similar to eggplant. - Species and varieties. - Its properties as a seasoning. - Grows as an ornamental plant. CUCUMBER. - The consumption of cucumbers has little place in French cuisine; in Paris, in particular, the main outlet for this type of product is in the hotels where English travelers, who are great fans of cucumbers, stay. As a result, cucumbers do not occupy much space in the kitchen garden. Cucumbers are grown like melons, either in beds or in the open air. Open-air cultivation is easy and uncomplicated. In May, better after than before the 15th of the month, dig holes 50 cm in diameter and 30 cm deep, spaced 1.5 m apart from the center. The holes are filled with fresh manure, topped with 15 to 20 centimetres of soil mixed with compost. Cucumber seeds sown in these holes soon produce vigorous plants that grow very quickly, provided they are well watered. Cucumber roots, like those of melons, tolerate contact with fermenting manure very well. The free space between the holes is covered with a good mulch on which the long cucumber stems stretch out in all directions. The gardener prunes them to make them branch out; he leaves each plant a number of fruits proportionate to the strength of each species, and he takes care, when the fruit is well formed, to concentrate the sap to his benefit, by arré̂tant the useless prolongation of the stems. The mulch preserves the cleanliness of the fruit, which, depending on the species and the purpose for which it is to be used, must be picked at various stages of development. From each variety, a few of the most beautiful fruits are selected and left not only to ripen, but also to rot on the spot, due to excess ripeness, a necessary condition if we are to be able to count on the complete ripeness of the seed. In some cantons around Paris, the large white Bonneuil cucumber is grown on a very large scale, not for cooking, but for perfumers, who buy considerable quantities to make cucumber pomade. This ointment, excellent as a harmless cosmetic for chapped lips and skin, is the subject of a large export trade from Paris. Cucumbers are sown in rows in a ploughed and smoked bed, just like any other garden crop. As the aim is to obtain as many medium-sized fruits as possible, rather than just a few of the most beautiful, the gardener prunes the stems only once, so that they can grow a few side shoots, which are then allowed to bear all or almost all their fruit, which perfumers buy by weight. Wherever the placement of products for the perfume industry is assured in advance, this simple and economical method of producing a large mass of mediocre cucumbers is highly advantageous. Forced calture. - Gardeners whose clientele includes cucumber lovers wealthy enough to pay for them properly, obtain large quantities of early cucumbers by forced cultivation. Forced cultivation is carried out on a hot bed under a frame, from point to point, like forced melon cultivation. Sow in pots and transplant into slightly larger ones, taking the same precautions as for melon seedlings to avoid disturbing the roots. The cucumber plant, placed on a new layer, is pruned to make it branch out and obtain a good number of meshes, leaving only one or two at each foot. Species and varieties. - In countries such as England, where cucumbers are a speciality, and where special societies are devoted exclusively to perfecting their cultivation, there are a great many varieties. In France, the large white Bonneuil cucumber is grown mainly in the open air, while the early white and early Dutch cucumbers are grown on beds. CURNICHONS. - Gherkins are true cucumbers, picked in the first period of their development; any gherkin left to its natural course of growth becomes a cucumber. The small green cucumber is grown exclusively as a gherkin, and is never subjected to forced cultivation. All other cucumbers are grown in the open air. As it branches naturally and each branch produces a great abundance of fruit, it is not pruned; it needs to be watered often and very generously. The smaller the gherkin, the more delicate the flavor. The snake cucumber, considered a botanically distinct species, is also sometimes grown as a gherkin. The fruit of this cucumber, when allowed to grow to its full size, becomes very long and bendy, hence its nickname. Picked at finger size, it makes very good pickles, which retain their green color in vinegar better than the fruit of the small green cucumber. As a gherkin, the snake cucumber is less advantageous to grow than the common species, because it branches less and does not produce as many meshes. CURRANTS. - A very large number of squash varieties are grown in southern countries, and play an important role in the vegetable garden and in cooking. In our central departments, especially around Paris, only the large squash, better known by its vulgar names of pumpkin and potiron, is widely grown. The flesh of this plant's very voluminous fruit is used to make refreshing soups whose health-giving properties are so well recognized that the administration of the Paris hospices uses huge quantities for convalescents. Just to supply the hospitals, a large number of pumpkins are grown on the outskirts of Paris by market gardeners who are awarded contracts. To produce pumpkins that ripen very early in the summer, the seeds are sown on a bed in March and April; these seedlings produce plants that can be transplanted in the open air from May 1 to 15. Most often, pumpkins are sown in the first fortnight of May, in holes similar to those used for growing gherkins and cucumbers, but larger, deeper and more widely spaced, depending on the size of the varieties to be grown. The largest pumpkins are grown in holes one meter in diameter and 50 centimeters deep. As far as possible, pumpkins should only be grown in more or less sheltered areas; open ground exposed to strong winds is unsuitable. As for the quality of the soil, this is of little importance, as the plant should live only at the expense of the manure and good soil with which its holes are filled. If the pumpkin seed has not been sown in place, the plant suffers to a greater or lesser extent from transplanting; some of it would be lost to the drying action of the sun's rays if, when planting, you neglected to shelter it under counter-soils which, without intercepting air or light, only provide as much shade as the pumpkin plant needs, until its roots have taken hold of the soil in the holes, which is a matter of a few days. In the absence of counter-soil, three sticks can be planted in the ground, joined at the top by a rush tie, and a bit of long litter or a piece of old doormat thrown on top. Pumpkins require a lot of water at all times of growth; their excessively long stems cannot be pruned; each plant is usually left with two fruits; the stem is stopped two or three leaves above the fruit. If the part of the stem crawling on the ground between the root collar and the fruit is partly covered with soil, as would be a grapevine, it roots quickly, and this kind of layering contributes significantly to increasing the volume of fruit. All squash species can be grown in the same way, except for the size of the holes, which varies with the volume of fruit in each variety. Species and varieties. - The old pumpkin from the Paris area, with its almost smooth rind, not very pronounced ribs, and very often enormous volume, has been gradually abandoned today; it is rightly preferred to the sub-variety known as the Siam ball, which keeps better and contains as much edible substance as the former, because it has almost no interior void, which makes it easier to preserve, and less prone to mildew. The Hungarian green pumpkin is also widely cultivated in vegetable gardens. It is the same shape as the Siamese ball pumpkin, with extremely sweet flesh; it is the variety from which sugar is made in Hungary and Poland, just as beet sugar is made here. This variety is also known as the Spanish pumpkin, and should be preferred by those who value quality over extraordinary fruit size. Giraumont, also known as turban or Turkish bonnet, because of the unusual shape of its fruit, is smaller but sweeter than pumpkins; it is used for the same purpose. Gourds proper, whose flesh is eaten fried in oil with garlic, onion and strong pepper, do not ripen well and are generally only used in the South of France; the most esteemed varieties are the Marseilles or nutmeg squash, and the Messinaise squash, from Sicily, both of which have almost no inner cavity. The Ohio squash, originally from America, and the marrow squash, a very nourishing English variety, do well in the Paris climate. The latter merits the name "vegetable marrow" given to it by the English. We mention only for the record the congourde or pilgrim's gourd, the club gourd, and a host of others whose fruits are not edible. You can, however, make room for them in the vegetable garden, by supporting their climbing stems on trellises or long poles. Their dried and emptied fruits can be used to store all kinds of vegetable seeds. TOMATO. - For the past thirty years, the tomato or "pomme d'amour", which was once only found in the kitchens of wealthy gourmets, outside the departments of the Midi where it has been eaten by everyone from time immemorial, has entered the consumption of the most modest households, which has given its cultivation great importance. Tomatoes don't stand the cold any better than melons and squash; they can only be sown in the open air around the middle of May. Plants sown at this time of year do not always ripen before the first autumn frosts, in which case some of the fruit is lost. The gardener can only count on harvesting tomatoes when the seed has been sown on the bed in February and March. The edges and empty parts of the beds on which melon plants are grown are used for this purpose; the same temperature is suitable for both plants. Tomatoes are excellent transplanters; to bring them forward, they can be bedded out until the temperature allows them to be placed in the open air. In all cases, the plant should be uprooted and transplanted to 13. make it stocky and reduce its tendency to form a profusion of branches, which is detrimental to fruit production. As soon as the season is far enough advanced, tomatoes can be transplanted into the open ground; the plant is naturally very rustic and not very fussy about soil quality; with a little water and manure, it can grow almost anywhere; but it can only bear fruit at the foot of a wall facing south, or under the protection of a temporary espalier of straw mats supported by stakes and facing south. As soon as the fruit has formed, the stems above it are pinched off. Without this precaution, the stems would continue to flower and bear fruit too late to ripen; as the sap rises to the top of the plant, the fruit formed first would not grow, and the harvest would be nil. As the fruit grows, the plant's soft stem can no longer support itself; it needs to be trellised, either at the bottom of an espalier trellis, or on poles placed transversely for this purpose. Great care must be taken to cut back any shoots that appear at various points on the stem, either above or below the reserved fruit. When, despite all the care taken to speed up vegetation, tomatoes still bear only half-ripe fruit when the first cold weather arrives, even though the drop in temperature does not allow us to hope that they will reach full ripeness, they should not be considered as lost. Tomatoes, provided they have only just begun to change color, can become fully ripe in two or three days on a shelf in the kitchen, near the fireplace, in a warm greenhouse, or in any other room where the temperature is sufficiently high. It is understandable that tomatoes ripened artificially in this way are never as good as those that have ripened on the plant during the summer, but they are of good enough quality to find buyers and can be delivered for consumption without any inconvenience. In all our departments in the Midi, tomato seeds are sown on irrigable land; the plant is thinned out to give it suitable spacing; it is never transplanted, and later the plant is left to grow freely. The stems are simply supported by a few branches, without being trellised in the true sense of the word, and the fruit is harvested as it ripens. Forced calture. - In the Paris climate, tomatoes can be forced very easily by sowing the seed on a warm bed from the first days of January. The seed is sown in the same layer, then transplanted to another warm layer, and planted on a third, which is surrounded if necessary by heaters that are renewed once or twice. In this way, the tomatoes reach the time when the glass frames can be raised for most of the day, and then removed altogether. Only a few fruits are left on each plant, which are supported on strings stretched from one edge of the trunks to the other, so that the fruits don't get dirty from contact with the potting soil. Forced tomatoes begin to ripen around June; the more severely they are pruned, the less fruit is left on each plant, and the sooner the fruit ripens. AUBERGINE. - Eggplant, also known as melongene, is an essentially southern fruit, suited to the stomachs of the Midi, but causing frequent indigestion in those who are not used to it. In Paris, eggplants are mainly grown for the large number of southerners who live in the capital. They only produce good fruit by forced cultivation, which is carried out in exactly the same way as forced tomato cultivation, with the only difference being that to increase the volume of the eggplant fruit, it is transplanted three times, eight or ten days apart, before it is finally planted. Eggplants can also be sown on a bed in March, then transplanted in April, and then grown in the open air, at the foot of a south-facing wall. But in this case, the fruit, which ripens late and imperfectly, is even more difficult to digest, however it is prepared. In Paris, only the violet eggplant is grown for cooking purposes. In the Midi, we also eat the fruit of the white eggplant, known as an ornamental plant under its vulgar name of egg plant, because of the oval shape of its fruit. It is more or less unwise to eat these fruits when they have not ripened under the influence of the Midi sun: they are much more indigestible than those of the purple eggplant. CHILI. - As a seasoning, pimento or long pepper also belongs to the cuisine of the Midi, as its fruit has a fiery flavor similar to that of pepper. Peppers are grown in the same way as eggplants. Except in our southern departments, where it grows without difficulty in the open air, this plant is not actually considered a vegetable; it is grown more as an ornamental plant, due to the color of its fruit. There are several varieties with elongated, red, yellow and violet fruit. There is another species with a very large, irregularly shaped fruit, called "tomato chili" for this reason; the fruits of all chili peppers have the same properties; it is an excessively hot seasoning, which should only be used in moderation. CHAPTER XXVI Mushrooms. Edible mushroom. - How it grows. - Mycelium. - Mushroom cultivation. - Production of mushroom spawn. - Preservation of spawn. - Manure preparation. - Choice of manure. - Placement of layers. Layering. - Larding. - Goptering. - Production time. - English method. - Mushroom greenhouse. - Properties of cultivated mushrooms. - How good ones become bad. - Research into the production of muskmushrooms. - Morels. - Truffles. The mushroom's mode of vegetation differs completely from that of all other vegetable plants; it is not a complete plant, but a portion of a plant, which can be taken strictly as neither a flower nor a fruit, but which nevertheless seems to be more like a fruit than any other vegetable product. The plant that produces the mushroom is a kind of subterranean moss, with white, filamentous, highly branched stems, from which the mushrooms grow; this plant is called mycelium; it is what gardeners call mushroom spawn. Mushroom cultivation. - The whole purpose of mushroom cultivation is to place the mycelium, or mushroom spawn, in the best conditions for fruiting. Mushroom spawn can only live at the expense of horse, donkey or mule manure. Horse and donkey manure are the best; however, they are the least used, because in areas where mushroom production is most advantageous, only horse manure is available in considerable quantities. In the countryside, when you only want to grow a moderate quantity of mushrooms for household consumption, there is no better manure than donkey or mule manure, wherever it is available. Production of spawn. - Before starting to cultivate mushrooms, even on a small scale, the first thing to do is to obtain good spawn. Mushrooms cannot be multiplied either by sowing their seeds (they don't produce any) or by taking cuttings from their outer parts. Mushroom spawn can be purchased in Paris from seed merchants, who are now able to ship it to all parts of France, thanks to the railroad network of which Paris is the hub. Mushroom spawn can also be made yourself, using a very simple process. Open a pit in dry soil, and fill it with good horse, donkey or mule manure. This manure must not be too mixed with straw; it must 1 above all be dry rather than wet, and contain no i foreign bodies, nor dry or fresh hay; all these materials are detrimental to the formation of the spawn. Once the pit is full and the manure moderately trodden, about half of the soil extracted from the pit is placed on top, and the operation is complete. After three to five weeks, depending on the season, when the pit is reopened, the manure is converted into a mass of mushroom spawn. You can also, as market gardeners in Paris often do, build up a small layer, as for mushroom production, and when it is well penetrated by the mycelium, dismantle it without giving it time to produce, and sell the substance or use it as mushroom spawn; the spawn obtained by the latter process is considered the best; it's what gardeners call blanc vierge. Whichever way it is produced, blanc has the singular property of being able to be preserved almost indefinitely, provided it is kept dry. If after a year, or even several years, dry spawn is returned to the right growing conditions, it behaves like fresh spawn and can give rise to abundant mushroom production. Manure preparation. - Around Paris, mushroom production is an important and lucrative industry, carried out exclusively by people who have no other occupation. To get an idea of this, it's enough to remember that several mushroom growers, as they are called, build layers several kilometers long in the immense underground tunnels from which the stone for Paris buildings is extracted. As they pay a high price for the manure they are obliged to buy to build layers of this size, it is clear that their industry requires considerable capital. Large-scale mushroom cultivation would not present any serious difficulties, if it were possible to build mushroom beds in the same way as hot, warm or dull beds are built for any other crop. But here, the role played by manure in the operation is no longer the same: it does not serve to produce the artificial heat necessary for the vegetation of this or that forced plant; it must be completely penetrated by the filaments of the mycelium, and these filaments must live at its expense, acquiring, by absorbing its substance, the property of producing mushrooms. If the layer is too hot, the spawn burns as it passes from white to black, and produces nothing; if it is too moist, the spawn cannot attach itself to it, and mushroom production is also nil. Experience alone, acquired through practice, gives the mushroom grower the knack for the correct preparation of the manure he uses, so that he almost always succeeds, whereas by apparently doing as he does, many inexperienced gardeners fail. Choice of manure. - First of all, manure must be chosen with discernment; manure from animals that eat too much fresh fodder does not always give good results. Manure from very large draft horses, which work hard and are therefore heavily fed, is to be preferred to any other. The first thing to do is to form a 1.30-meter-high, squarely-dressed millstone, from which hay, long straw and any foreign matter accidentally mixed in are carefully removed as it is put up, and which is levelled by compressing its surface by trampling. When operating in dry weather, the manure must be kept well moistened as the millstone is raised. After a strong burst of heat, it begins to turn white after a period of time which may vary according to the state of the temperature, but which never exceeds 12 days. The grindstone is then dismantled and immediately restored; 10 to 12 days later, it is reshaped for the last time, remade as at the start of the operation, and left to rest for a week. If all this work has been carried out correctly, the manure can be used to build up the mushroom beds. Location. - Layering. - The best location is a cellar free of humidity, a storeroom, or any other similar room where the air is renewed, but where light does not penetrate. Layers are given a height of around G0 centimeters, with a hump-backed shape, wide at the base and narrow at the top. After three or four days' rest, the layer is ready for larding. Barding a mushroom bed means inserting slabs of spawn, usually cut in a diamond shape, while lifting the manure. Two parallel rows are barded, equally spaced from each other, and equally distant from the base and the top of the layer. If the white takes well, the mycelium invades all parts of the layer within eight or ten days. We then sprinkle it with pulverized dry earth, which we gently tap on the surface of the layer with the back of a spade; this is what we call Goptering the layers, a final operation after which all we can do is wait for the mushrooms to show up. In the subterranean areas around Paris, the layers are gopted only with local tuff limestone, grated and pulverized. Production time. - When all goes well, a mushroom bed will produce for about three months, provided that, when harvesting the mushrooms, care is taken not to disturb the surface, not to remove mushrooms in the process of developing, and to put back a few handfuls of soil wherever the harvest has caused it to fall. When the layer is exhausted, if it is at a short distance from the wall of the room where it was established, and if this wall is salpêtré, a very curious vegetation phenomenon often occurs. The mycelium emerges from all sides of the layer, climbs up the walls or subterranean walls and produces a final crop of mushrooms, after which it dries out and dies. Based on this indication, attempts have sometimes been made, with success, to prolong mushroom production by watering the beds with various saline dissolutions; ammonium sulfate seems to have given good results; we recall these attempts because they are very worthy of repetition and can be expected to produce very useful progress in mushroom production. Manure from completely used mushroom beds is almost worthless as fertilizer; it has even acquired properties that are harmful to certain vegetable plants. If, for example, this manure is used to mulch strawberry beds, it poisons them in a way; the strawberry leaves turn red, the fruit hardens and no longer grows, and the strawberry plants have to be uprooted and the plantation renewed. The mushroom production method just described is unquestionably the most economical of all for large-scale cultivation, enabling Paris mushroom growers to deliver prodigious quantities of mushrooms for consumption at very reasonable prices. It is the only process that can be recommended to gardeners who want to produce mushrooms for sale. Those who have not seen professional mushroom growers at work will only succeed after some trial and error. Apart from our indications, there are modifications in practice which are easily discovered by experience, and which cannot be specified in advance because they depend on the feed of the animals whose manure is used, the local climate, the state of the temperature, and a host of other accidental causes. It should be noted that, in order to reach the precise degree of decomposition necessary for the production of the fungus, the manure must ferment in fairly large masses; for this reason, it is more difficult to achieve success when working in small quantities. I - 4 English method. - When you intend to produce only a moderate quantity of mushrooms for a family's use, you can, with less embarrassment and more certainty of success at the first attempt, use the following ab process, very common in England. In a pile of manure recently removed from the stable, from animals fed mainly on oats, broad beans and dry fodder, lia sort the value of two or three wheelbarrow loads of crot-- tin, without any mixture of bedding. The dung is crumbled by s hand to form as homogeneous a heap as possible. It is immediately used, without any further preparation, to form layers which are given a thickness of around 20 to 25 centimetres without treading; so that after a few days, their thickness is reduced to 12 or 15 centimetres by al packing. Then, towards the middle of their thickness, the white mushrooms are introduced, which take hold of them almost immediately and soon produce beautiful mushrooms whose production lasts for six weeks or two months, without ever being, all things considered, of an abundance comparable to that of the mushrooms on the layers laid out by the champigno--on nists. In the English method, layers of this kind are never established on the ground; they are placed on shelves with a rim, like those on a fruit tree, in a room that is both warm and dark. Often, the gardener of a large house where mushrooms are widely consumed will have a closed shed built against the wall of a single-sided greenhouse. Mushroom racks are arranged along this wall like the shelves of a library, one above the other; heat pipes communicating with the greenhouse heating system maintain a good temperature in what may be called the mushroom greenhouse. When only a small quantity is required, the layer is established, again by the same procedure, in a sufficiently wide and deep drawer, which occupies the underside of the kitchen table; the mushrooms are thus born as close as possible to the stove on which they are to be cooked. Without doubt, this method of production means that mushrooms do not fetch as low a price as those produced by mushroom growers, but the purpose for which they are cultivated is no less achieved. The properties of cultivated mushrooms. - We know how many deplorable accidents can occur when wild mushrooms of varying degrees of poisonousness are confused with those of harmless species. The danger of such misunderstandings does not exist, however, when we confine ourselves to cultivated mushrooms, between which no bad ones can be found. However, it is very useful for everyone to know that the best mushrooms, both cultivated and wild, are only perfectly good when they have just hatched. If the cultivated ones have aged enough before being picked for their inward-rolled edges to have unfolded, and the blades on the underside of the cap to have turned from purplish pink to brown or black, the mushrooms, although all belonging to the best species, have become bad. While retaining their pleasant, delicate flavor, they can cause, if not actual poisoning, at least cruel indigestion. Mushrooms should therefore be harvested every day, or twice a day when their rapid growth seems to require it, so that they can be delivered to the kitchen in the least harmful state; for mushrooms are a difficult food to digest, which many delicate people cannot tolerate, and which, when used without moderation, give rise to serious stomach ailments often attributed to other causes. In recent years, the truffle has been the subject of a large number of propagation attempts, which have not led to any serious results; but they have made it increasingly probable that the truffle is not a fungus. Indeed, there is every reason to believe that the truffle, whose internal context examined under the strongest microscope bears no resemblance to the tissue of true mushrooms, is an outgrowth analogous to the beguar and the walnut, and that this outgrowth is produced on the thinnest filaments of the root tips of some species of oak, in particular the chénevert, by the sting of an insect of the order Diptera and of the genus Cynips, in exactly the same way as the sting of another Cynips 2 2 3 - E 2 produces on the veins of the oak leaf the external "excrescence" known as the Galle nut. We know that many female insects bury themselves in order to deposit their eggs within reach of the substances necessary for the nourishment of the worms or larvae to which these eggs must give birth; there is therefore nothing but natural in admitting that the female of a cy8 I S nips buries herself in order to deposit her eggs in the roots of the oak, whose extravasated sap would be the truffle. At the Great Exhibition of 1855, a landowner in the Vaucluse department claimed the proposed price for the artificial multiplication of truffles; the commission delegated to visit his truffle fields found vast areas planted with holm oaks, known as chènes truf- fiers. Truffles were indeed found at the foot of some of these oaks, but since there was no evidence that they were the product of cultivation, the commission declared that the solution to the problem remained to be found. Twenty-five years ago, Count de Noë obtained truffles in considerable quantities by burying truffle peelings under oak trees; but he obtained them by chance, never at will. If it is to be solved, it will be through the study of the truffle cynip, its habits and its particular mode of multiplication. CHAPTER XXVII New or little cultivated vegetables. New or little cultivated vegetables. - Diff̂cultés of their introduction. - Sweet potato or batate. - Calture. - Cutting out shoots. - Spacing. - Tuber preservation. - Species and varieties. - Dioscoree or Chinese yam. - Suitable soils. - Multiplication by bulblets. - Precautions for uprooting. - Oralide crenelée - Tuber planting. -- Spacing. - Stem layering. - Tuberous nasturtium. - Poor quality of tubers. - Psoralier or piquotiaue. - Its uses in America. - Di Eculte of its cultivation in Europe. - Uhuco. - Chervil bu beur. - Tetragon. - Its superiority over spinach in summer. - Cultivation. - Pe-tsai, Chinese cabbage. - Quinoa. New vegetables are making slow and difficult headway in European vegetable gardens. The amateur gardener is never in a hurry to adopt a dish whose flavor deviates even slightly from those to which he is accustomed; the professional gardener is in even less of a hurry to cultivate any vegetable, however excellent, whose sale is uncertain. A few new varieties, some recently introduced, others of long standing, are nevertheless making progress, and seem destined to take a distinguished place in our vegetable gardens in the not too distant future. The plants we have yet to discuss. In order of their relative importance, among the root vegetables, sweet potato or batato, tuberous nasturtium, edible psoralier, ulluco and bulbous chervil; among the edible leaf vegetables, tetra one and pe-tsai or Chinese cabbage; and among the plants whose seeds are used, dotique and quinoa. SWEET POTATO. - Despite its health-giving properties, the voluminous, nourishing root of the sweet potato has not taken a very important place in the diet, even in the south of France, where its cultivation is assured of success. This is due to its particular flavor, which is excellent, but too sweet; it can only be eaten in the same way as all sweet foods, from time to time as a delicacy; it cannot form part of the usual diet, like carrots or potatoes. It is also difficult to store over winter. Cultivation. - The potato can only be grown in the open air in the climate of our southernmost departments; it cannot withstand even moderate cold. In the climate of Paris, beautiful tubers are planted in a warm layer of potting soil towards the end of March or in the first days of April. They soon begin to sprout and give very numerous sprays, which are successively removed from the 10th to the 15th of May, to be placed in the open air in good garden soil that has been deeply ploughed and generously smoked. Ordinary spacing is 30 centimetres in rows spaced one metre apart; 1.30 metres may even be left between rows for species whose tubers are to become very large. Potato shoots grow with the greatest of ease, whether or not they have roots when they are first planted. In no time at all, their bindweed-like stems cover the whole ground; the potato suffers no vegetation other than its own. Tuber preservation - When the harvest is small, the best way to preserve tubers is not to pull them out. At harvesting time, at the end of September or during October, depending on the temperature, glass frames are placed on the potato beds to prevent autumn rains from drying out the soil; wilted stems are removed and the surface of the soil is cleaned. During the frosts, the frames are covered with litter and straw mats, as if they were housing a crop of delicate plants that needed to be protected from the cold. Tubers are harvested only in proportion to consumption needs. The most valuable provisions are kept in the cellar in dry sand, but despite all possible precautions, some are always lost. Species and varieties. - Several varieties of sweet potato have been introduced into European gardens, some of which have flowered and produced fertile seeds. Sowing these seeds has produced some fine new varieties, all more or less widespread in cultivation. The most esteemed are the white from Ile de France, the long red, the violet, the ovoid and the yam. Some of these varieties can, with very careful cultivation, produce tubers weighing several kilograms. DIOSCORÉE or CHINESE IGNAME. - One cannot make the same objection to dioscorea or Chinese yam as to sweet potato; it is not sweet; its slightly prolonged flavor, although different from that of potato, associates it with all kinds of seasonings; it is at least as nourishing as potato. But a serious difficulty slows down its propagation; it only produces considerable yields after two years of cultivation, and although these yields are equal to or greater in quantity than those of two potato crops, it is more or less inconvenient for the gardener for part of his land to be "occupied for two years, without interruption, by the same crop. As it plunges its tubers vertically into the earth, it needs very deep soil; it thrives there even when the soil is only moderately fertile, and with a moderate dose of fertilizer. he stems produce a profusion of bulblets in the leaf axils, which serve to multiply the plant. These bulblets are planted in rows spaced 25 centimetres apart, 18 centimetres apart in the rows. The following year, finger-sized tubers from these bulblets can be lifted and planted in rows spaced 30 centimetres apart, 20 centimetres apart in the rows. Extraction requires great care, as the tubers, which are never very large in diameter, are often quite long. Cultivation of the Chinese yam is still in its infancy in Europe; among recently introduced food plants, it is the one that seems to have the most future, both in the field and in the kitchen garden. CRENELLATED OXALIS. - The slightly acid taste of crenellated oxalis tubers is not to the taste of most consumers; potatoes are preferable in every respect. The very small tubers only become numerous through continual cultivation, and the real value of the harvest does not sufficiently reward the gardener. All these disadvantages explain why the crenellated oxalis fell into neglect in France, after a period of favor when it was first introduced. Oxalis tubers are planted in April, in the open air, in rows 50 centimetres apart and 1 metre apart.
