As the stems lengthen, they are marcotted, leaving only their tips above ground, so that they eventually occupy the whole ground. The tubers are harvested as late as possible, in autumn, and stored in the cellar like potatoes. They can only be eaten if boiled in the first water, which is then discarded, and which strips them of some of their acidity, after which they are prepared like potatoes. TUBEROUS NASTURTIUM. - Tuberous nasturtium is only mentioned here because a few horticulturists are still working on it, in the hope of improving its tubers. In their present state, tubers of tuberous nasturtium have only one defect: they cannot be eaten. Cultivation of this plant is similar to that of the potato. Those who wish to try to improve it should force it into the soil, so that it flowers early, produces ripe seeds and multiplies seedlings, which can produce good sub-varieties. PSORALIER OR PIQUOTIANE. - This plant, long known to botanists, was the subject of great expectations a few years ago. M. Lamarre-Piquot, who substituted his own name for that of the psoralier by naming the plant piquotiane, saw in it a plant worthy of rivaling the potato, and here's why. The continent of North America still contains immense deserted regions, periodically battered by fur hunters. The edible psoralea, which grows abundantly in these solitudes, is the great food resource of the hunting bands. Although small, its root is very nourishing, with an excellent taste, suitable for accompanying meat, and for all kinds of fatty and lean seasonings; it was hoped that, as the plant's mode of vegetation was not known, cultivation would increase the volume of the roots, and that the psoralier, now known as piquotiana, would be a good acquisition for the vegetable gardens of Europe, as well as for large-scale cultivation. But when the cultivation of the psoralier was first tried, it turned out that, on the one hand, the plant required several years of cultivation to reach a diameter of only a few centimetres, and that, on the other, it was very difficult to produce fertile seeds in Europe, the sowing of which could have given rise to improved varieties; it therefore had to be abandoned after many unsuccessful trials, trials which have not, moreover, been entirely abandoned. ULLUCO. - Great hopes had also been pinned on ulluco, the main food of part of the Peruvian population; but in Europe, ulluco tubers, quite similar to small yellow potatoes, form late, remain small and are not worth cultivating. In fact, from a gastronomic point of view, they are so bad that, like nasturtium 14, they have the slight defect of not being edible. BULBOUS CHERVIL. - Cultivation of this chervil is straightforward, but its tubers, so far not very large, are no more suitable for cooking than those of the preceding plants. However, gardeners with time on their hands and land to spare can multiply seedlings of bulbous chervil and test their cultivation under a variety of conditions. They will probably obtain a very good food plant for livestock, and will be able to sell the tubers with advantage, to introduce it into field cultivation. TERRAGON. - This plant, whose beautiful green leaves have the same properties as spinach, does not have the disadvantage of climbing in summer as spinach does. Provided it is sufficiently watered, it continues to produce abundant fresh leaves throughout the summer. It is grown for this reason in many gardens; its taste differs so little from that of spinach, when its leaves are prepared in the same way, that it is difficult not to be mistaken. Tetragonia seed is sown in March and April, 60 to 80 centimetres apart in all directions between the holes, because the plant radiates outwards and occupies a very large space. The leaves grow back as they are picked, especially when the ends of the stems are pinched from time to time. Tetragonia is so easy to grow, and its health-giving properties are so well established, that it deserves a place in every well-tended vegetable garden in summer. PE-TSAI, or Chinese cabbage. - Pe-tsai, widely favored at the time of its introduction to Europe, is completely forgotten today. Strictly speaking, it is neither a cabbage, nor a salad, which apples with difficulty, or rather never apples in the true sense of the word, and which is not distinguished by any superiority over our European vegetables for the same uses. Pe-tsai seed is sown either in the field or in a nursery for transplanting; the plant is so tender that the same precautions must be taken when transplanting it as when transplanting a romaine plant grown under a bell. The two-year-old seed is the one that most easily produces apples of passable volume; but, despite the most careful cultivation, it almost always happens that most of the plant flowers and goes to seed in the first heat of its first year, which deprives the petsai of any nutritional value. DOLICA. - The cowpea, originally from Egypt, does not tolerate the Paris climate; even when we manage to grow it at the foot of a south-facing wall, it is so unproductive that the least esteemed of our bean species are far superior to it. It is also grown as a dwarf bean without oars. It is a highly esteemed vegetable in our southern departments, especially in the Var, where it is grown on a large scale in open fields; it is as productive there as in its native country. QUINOA. - Quinoa is easy to grow: the seed is sown in spring in good, ordinary garden soil, where it thrives without any special cultivation care. But quinoa seed, whose flour is one of the main foods of the inhabitants of South America, has an unbearable bitterness in Europe, which is very difficult to get rid of. Nevertheless, quinoa can still be cultivated in the vegetable garden, either for its leaves, which are eaten as spinach but are no match for tetragonia, or for its extraordinarily abundant seeds, which are used to feed all kinds of poultry. CHAPTER XXVIII Medicinal plants. Medicinal plants. - Marshmallow - Propagation. - Harvesting and drying of roots and stems. - Patience. - Cultivation at the water's edge. - Harvesting and drying of roots - Soapwort. - Cultivation. - Desiccation and preparation of roots. - Peppermint. - Round-leaf mint, or balsam. - Pouliot mint. - Lemon balm. - White-flowered dracocephalus - Wormwood. - Dwarf variety. - Sage. - Harvest leaves. - Violet. - Natural seedlings. - Lavender. - Suitable location. - Roman chamomile. - Bittersweet. - Harvest and dry stems. - Angelica. - How to candy the ribs. - Anise. -Coriander. - Harvesting their seeds. As a complement to the cultivation of vegetable plants, we can consider the cultivation of a certain number of medicinal plants taken from among those used in greater quantities by pharmacists, herbalists, perfumers and distillers, and which are not found in the wild; most of these plants belong to the field of domestic medicine; they are therefore perfectly at home, albeit in lesser quantities, in vegetable gardens whose produce is not intended for sale. The most commonly used medicinal plants in this category are: marshmallow, soponaria and patience, grown for their roots; mint, lemon balm, wormwood and sage, for their stems and leaves; violet, lavender and Roman chamomile, for their flowers; bittersweet and angelica, for their stems; aniseed and coriander, for their seeds. GUIMAUVE. - Marshmallow thrives in all types of soil; the lightest, even when of mediocre fertility, is particularly suitable when it is deep enough for it to establish itself with its long, strong roots. If you sow marshmallow seeds in the spring, the roots won't be ready for harvesting for two or even three years; this is why gold generally prefers to propagate marshmallow by dividing clumps in the spring, which gives an abundant harvest of roots every two years. Fresh roots are stripped of their bark before being dried, suspended in bundles in a well-ventilated room, where they can be stored indefinitely. The stems and leaves of marshmallow, rich in mucilaginous principles, are used fresh or dried for emollient decoctions; they are no less useful than the roots themselves. SAPONAIRE - Single-flowered soapwort is frequently found in the wild, and its leaves, crushed in water, give it some of the properties of soap water. The roots of this plant are used medicinally as a depurative. A double-flowered variety whose roots have the same properties is cultivated in gardens; it thrives in damp soils and must be watered abundantly in summer; it is propagated exclusively by separating its many offspring at the onset of winter, the most suitable time for harvesting the roots, which can be kept dry and used in herbal teas, after being split lengthwise and cut into pieces a few centimeters long. PATIENCE. - The dried root of the Patience plant is one of the most widely used and effective bitter teas. In gardens with a water feature, or with a trickle of running water, a few clumps of Patience growing along the water's edge produce a beautiful ornamental effect with their ample green foliage, when combined with other aquatic plants. In the absence of such a location, Patience can only be planted in cool, deep soil; it requires no cultivation care and multiplies by splitting the clumps to harvest the roots, which are best harvested in spring rather than autumn. The best way to preserve Patience roots for medicinal use is to cut them into small sections 14. so that they dry quickly, and to preserve them carefully from humidity, which could cause them to mildew. MINT. - Among the aromatic plants of the Eupean climate, mint is unquestionably the most useful. For domestic medicine, it is the infusion that provides the most immediate relief from nervous affections and stomach diseases. It succeeds only in fresh, light soil, where it forms very voluminous clumps. It should not be cut to dry until the tops of the stems are ready to flower. If well watered, it grows back immediately and can be cut a second time before the end of the summer. The commonly cultivated species has narrow, pointed leaves and a penetrating scent; it's known as peppermint, and is to be preferred to all others when you intend to grow it for sale. However, if you only wish to have a small supply of mint for household use, you may prefer round-leaf mint, known in the countryside by the vulgar name of balsam; this mint is found throughout France on the banks of still waters. By transplanting a few vines, either to the banks of a body of water, or to fresh ground that is wetted thoroughly every day in summer, round-leaf mint produces strong clumps that can be cut at least three times from spring to autumn. The sweet smell and delicate flavor of this mint make it eminently suitable as a substitute for tea. An infusion of round-leaf mint is taken in the evening, not only when digestion is difficult, but also in perfect health. Pouliot mint is also often found in uncultivated areas, on the underside of ditches. This small, very minty species can be grown anywhere and, like most other mints, does not need humidity. You can carry clumps of it from distance to distance in the grass borders of flowerbed compartments, or in a corner of the vegetable garden. Its properties are the same as those of other mints; its scent is closer to that of round-leaf mint than peppermint. MINT. - The most sought-after plant for making lemon balm water, whose properties and uses are well known to everyone, is not lemon balm, but a plant of a related genus, commonly known as melissa, although its real name is dracocephalus. There are two varieties, one with red flowers, the other with white; the white is the more highly regarded. Dracocephalus is an annual plant whose seeds are sown in spring in good, ordinary garden soil; it requires no special cultivation care. It is cut when in full bloom and sold to perfumers and distillers, who use it fresh. ABSINTHE. - Although some of the wormwood used in large quantities by distillers in Paris and the large cities of the départements comes either from Switzerland or from the French Jura mountains, growing wormwood in the dry, hot soils that are particularly suited to it is no less advantageous. The best wormwood for sale is the black variety, very fragrant, frankly bitter and less persistent than grand wormwood. The plant is perennial; it is multiplied by dividing clumps in spring, and cut when its foliage is most abundant, just as it begins to flower. SAGE. - Sage, of which several beautiful varieties are grown in flowerbeds for the brilliance of their flowers, some red in various shades, others dark purple, is very little used today in European medicine, even though it possesses properties similar to those of mint: in a few localities in our maritime departments, sage is grown on a fairly large scale for export to China; the Chinese value our sage as much as we value their tea. As the leaf is the only useful part of the sage plant, the young shoots are pinched off to make them branch out, and the flower stalks are removed before the flowers bloom, in order to obtain a greater abundance of leaves, which are dried in the shade and retain their sweet, aromatic scent for a very long time. When these leaves are properly dried, sage cultivation is very lucrative. VIOLET. - In spring, pharmacists, confectioners and perfumers use considerable quantities of violet flowers, not including those kept dry for use in pectoral infusions. Large-scale cultivation of violets in the vicinity of major cities therefore has no shortage of outlets for its products. It is rather difficult to propagate violets by sowing their seeds, whose capsules open when ripe, making them difficult to harvest. Nevertheless, young plants can be obtained easily enough to renew plantations by natural sowing. If you don't harvest the last violet flowers, the seeds that follow them spread to the ground and germinate immediately. As soon as the young plant is strong enough, it should be uprooted and transplanted in a row, 20 to 25 centimetres apart in all directions; the plantation flowers abundantly in spring the following year. LAVENDER. This plant is one of the most commonly used in the perfume industry for the preparation of various cosmetics, including lavender eau-devie. These flowers are also sold in large quantities in bundles, which are wrapped in clean linen to give them a pleasant scent and keep insects away. The gardener can multiply lavender in borders around vegetable garden squares with southern exposure, with the certainty of selling the flowers at a decent price. Lavender flowers well only in warm, dry soils; it can be propagated by dividing clumps in spring. ROMAN CHAMOMILE. - The flowers of Roman chamomile are frequently used in stomachic infusions in common medicine; the presence of this plant is also an ornament to the vegetable garden, where it can be grown in borders, in the same soil and exposure conditions as lavender. Roman chamomile is particularly sensitive to excess humidity, which is detrimental to its flowering. If you wish to harvest a large enough quantity of its flowers for sale, you should avoid growing it around those vegetable garden squares that need to be watered often and generously. Roman chamomile can only be propagated by dividing clumps. BITTERSWEET. - The stems of bittersweet, split lengthwise, dried and cut like soapwort roots, are frequently used medicinally. The plant, which is sarmentose and climbing, is best placed along a trellis or cradle, where it decorates with its dark purple flowers, followed by clusters of bright red berries. Stems to be dried for medicinal use should be cut in autumn, when the leaves have fallen. ANGELICA. - Angelica cultivation is confined, so to speak, to a few localities where confectioners use large quantities. Everywhere else, angelica cannot be cultivated for sale; but a few plants can be grown in the vegetable garden, in order to preserve the ribs or petioles of the leaves, which are quite similar to those of celery. All you need to do is put them in boiling water for a few seconds, then, after letting them drain well, dip them several times in a very-cooked white sugar syrup. Candied angelica is very stomachic. The seed is sown either in spring or summer, as soon as it reaches maturity. The plant is placed 80 cm apart in all directions in cool soil. The plant flowers after three years, after which it must be replanted. ANISE AND CORIANDER. - These two plants are grown in much the same way as fennel; they belong to the same family. The seeds, harvested as they mature, can be stored dry in a dry place. (See FENNEL, chap. XX.) When the placement of the seed is assured, the cultivation of anise, which is used more frequently than coriander, is quite advantageous. re=eTROISIÈRE PARTIE ARBRES FRUITIERS CHAPTER XXIX Preliminary notions. - Multiplication. Benefits of fruit tree cultivation. - Current state of fruit tree cultivation in France. - Necessity of propagating the right species. - Classification of fruit trees. - Fruit tree cultivation. - Multiplication. - Sowing. - Overview of the Van Mons system. - How to obtain good seeds and pits for sowing. - Preparing the soil. - Fertilization. - Pre-cultivation of vegetables. - Core stratification. - Sowing seeds. - Transplanting seedlings. - Grafting. - Need for regular order in the nursery. - Transplanting grafted plants. - Spacing. Man has all the more reason to enjoy cultivating fruit trees and harvesting their fruit, since fruit, more than any other garden product, is his work. In the cold or temperate countries "inhabited by the most active and enlightened part of the Roman race, nature does not produce any really good fruit on its own. Pears and apples, for example, as found in the wild in our woods, are small and unpalatable. It's by happy sowing, by effe, by accidental cross-breeding, by cultivation in the most varied conditions of soil, climate and exposure, that good fruit has come into being, and continues to improve day by day. Is there any fruit that can be eaten with greater pleasure than that of a tree that has been grafted, planted and cultivated with assiduous care, from the moment when the seedling or stone destined to become a tree was entrusted to the earth, to the moment when the first fruit of this tree appears on the table, at dessert? As for the professional gardener, it can be said that it is the cultivation of fruit trees, either to sell them fully trained, or to sell their produce, that most surely leads him to an honest ease, acquired through intelligent work. A glance at the current state of fruit tree cultivation in France shows how much remains to be done in this branch of horticulture, especially now that the completion of our railroad network means that fruit can be easily transported from the places of production to the markets where they are sure to find buyers. In most cantons, however, where the soil and climate are particularly favorable to all kinds of fruit trees, gardens and orchards produce only fruit that well-behaved cows and self-respecting rabbits would refuse to eat. Yet there is no shortage of good fruit, and horticulturists are not to be blamed for laziness or carelessness. Since the beginning of this century, they have provided gardening with a wide choice of excellent novelties in every series of fruit trees. But few of these novelties, some of which are no longer in their infancy, have so far migrated from the amateur's garden to that of the professional gardener; if their fruits appear on the market, it is only from time to time, and by exception. The amateur who sets his sights on propagating the best species of fruit, according to the soil and climate conditions of his canton, is therefore doing something that is both pleasing to himself and useful to society as a whole. Horticultural fruit trees naturally fall into three divisions, the first of which includes seeded fruit trees, the second 2 u 3 - 9 4 9 4 5 11 1 I 1 9 9 2 9 4 stone fruit trees, and the third berry fruit trees, including fig trees and edible fruit bushes that deserve a place in the fruit garden. Fruit trees that don't fit into any of these divisions, such as walnut, chestnut and beech, are not, strictly speaking, part of gardening. The cultivation of fruit trees includes, in the broadest sense of the term, a number of important operations, the most important of which are: the multiplication, pruning and management of these trees. Fruit tree multiplication. - Multiplication of fruit trees is the nurseryman's most important industry, from the moment the tree is born from the germ of the seed or stone, to the moment it is sold, fully trained, to be planted permanently in the place where it is to complete the entire course of its plant life. Seedlings and grafts are the main means of propagation used by nurserymen. Seedlings. - It is a great and generally accepted mistake to believe that the propagation of fruit trees by sowing is the exclusive province of the professional nurseryman. Anyone with a large enough garden can sow a certain quantity of pips and stones every year, with the well-founded hope of obtaining novelties of great value. This hope is based on a fact whose significance has only been fully understood since the successes achieved in Belgium and France, which have given rise to the best fruit now found in orchards all over Europe. Many excellent species of fruit trees flower at the same time, and insects, flitting from one flower to another, inevitably make accidental crosses. Fruit seeds from trees in a fruit garden containing a selection of the best species can always give rise to new sub-varieties, which can be recommended for various reasons. If it happens 15 that the seedling obtained by sowing these pepins does not live up to expectations, this seedling is still used to graft the good species, and the gardener's trouble is not wasted. On this subject, we think it useful to mention the system of the famous Professor Van Mons (from Louvain), a system which was the starting point of a whole revolution in fruit gardens. Van Mons began sowing seedlings and pits at a very early age, and found that trees sown for the first time only ever produced bitter, inedible fruit. However, by sowing seedlings or pits from the fruit of these trees, the second generation already produces fruit superior to that of the first; the improvement progresses from generation to generation, and we end up with fruit that is as good, often better, and in any case almost always entirely different from that which provided the seed for the first sowing. We know that it takes a young seedling a considerable number of years before it decides to show its first fruit, so that a man's lifetime is too short to see the results of seedlings according to the Van Mons system, which doesn't say its last word until the fourth generation. But if we graft twigs only two years old onto vigorous trees sacrificed for this purpose, they quickly bear fruit, so that we reach the fourth generation without suffering too long a delay. Those wishing to complete an experiment of this kind should, to give themselves the best possible chance of success, initially sow only pepins of the finest fruit of each species. If they make a small sacrifice, they can obtain sufficient quantities either from restaurant owners in large towns, or from the main fruit merchants. The latter have a vested interest in keeping fruit of the best species for as long as possible, in order to sell them at a higher price; by prolonging their preservation, they inevitably lose some of it to rot. The pepins contained in fruit corrupted by over-ripening are the best of all for seedlings; these pepins can be collected from fruit trees for next to nothing. When you're working on a large scale, and just want to grow the plant you intend to graft by sowing seedlings, as is the practice in large nurseries, you sow seedlings selected from the pomace of fruits used to make cider, and from the stones of various fruits.

bought from confectioners, at a time when all stone fruits are used by them in considerable quantities to make jam. We also sow almonds and Damask and St Julien plum kernels; all these seedlings provide subjects for grafting. Preparing the terraïn. - The soil in which fruit tree seeds and kernels are sown must not be overly fertile. When sown in soil that has been brought to maximum fertility by the use of highly energetic fertilizers, young trees will grow rapidly in a short space of time; but their roots will be prone to canker disease, and if the purchaser places them in soil that is much less fertile than that of the nursery, several years will pass before they decide to vegetate properly. When the soil in the nursery needs fertilizing, it can be given quite abundantly a year before sowing. This fertilizer can be supplemented by a vegetable crop, which absorbs some of the fertilizer, and the following year the soil is in the best condition to start the nursery. Stratification. - Precious time would be lost if fruit tree cores were sown in the spring without preparation. To encourage their woody hulls to open up and obtain good emergence, they are laid out in alternate beds with fresh sand, in a cellar or other frost-free location: this is called stratifying the cores (see chap. VII, page 68). Piles of stratified cores are dismantled at the end of March or beginning of April, depending on the temperature. The kernels are then planted one by one, at the appropriate depth for each species, in gutters opened for this purpose; the radicle end that has already emerged from the kernel during stratification is trimmed, and you can be sure of obtaining a plant from each kernel. If sown without prior stratification, many kernels would not have emerged before the second year. Sowing seeds. - They are kept dry for sowing in March, in rows or broadcast. Amateurs whose seedlings are aimed at conquering new varieties should keep their seedlings of pits or pepins in order, giving repeated numbers to each compartment in their register, and not mixing seedlings from known species. At the end of autumn, when young trees lose their leaves and need to be transplanted, set aside those whose wood and foliage resemble those of the right species, and reserve them until they show their fruit by grafting onto old trees, carefully preserving the trace of their origin. Transplanting. - When a seedling loses its first leaves, it is not always of equal growth. Many nurserymen transplant just about everything, eliminating only the most defective and unwanted subjects; the amateur, who is not guided by the same considerations, may transplant only the best-conformed and most vigorous seedling, and sacrifice everything else. Transplanting distances vary according to the dimensions the trees are expected to acquire before being grafted in the nursery; no space should be spared so that the young trees, once grafted, receive the unobstructed air and light from all sides necessary for their development in good conditions. Once the grafting process has been resumed, the grafted trees are transplanted, i.e. transplanted one last time to the place they must never leave, until they leave the nursery. Grafting. - The various types of grafting applicable to fruit trees have already been described (see Grafting, ch. IX). In the nursery, it is best to delay grafting until the tree is strong enough to feed the graft. No less important is the careful matching of subject and graft. The professional gardener, and even the amateur, provided he is in the habit of observing fruit trees, knows the temperament of each species well enough; he is careful not to give grafts that are too vigorous to feed to more or less delicate subjects; likewise, he does not entrust grafts of delicate species to robust subjects; if he makes this mistake, the sap arrives in too great abundance and it is said, with good reason, that the subject has drowned his graft. It is especially among young, recently grafted trees that the most regular order is essential; without order, the gardener himself is unaware of what he has in his nursery; he can lose his reputation and alienate his clientele, by delivering cooking fruit trees for dessert fruit trees, and vice versa. When the grafts have taken hold, the grafted trees are transplanted one last time to the place where they are to finish growing. Nowadays, they are given much more space than they used to have in the nursery. The fact that fruit trees can be transplanted, so to speak, at any age, has been perfectly demonstrated, and wealthy enthusiasts prefer fully trained trees in the nursery to the very young trees planted in the past. The nurseryman who has his interests at heart must always be able to supply trees that are already strong, prepared for the various most advantageous shapes, so that the gardener only has to direct these trees without embarrassment, continuing a job well begun. It is easy to see that, in order to prepare the trees properly in their various forms, they must be spaced sufficiently apart when they are last transplanted to the nursery. CHAPTER XXX The fruit garden. - Planting. Fruit garden. - Spacing. - Trees requiring southern exposure. - Palissage à la loque. - Circumstances in which it should be preferred. - Oak trellis. - Advantages and disadvantages. - Palissage à la baguette. - Wire trellising. - Wire netting. - Fruit tree beds. - Distribution of fruit trees of various sizes - Spacing. - Site preparation. - Choice of trees in the nursery. - Transportation. - Root dressing. - Orientation. - Planting. - Most favorable times. - Root placement - English system of platforms for espaliered peach trees. Fruit garden. - The previous chapter showed how fruit trees can be multiplied at will by sowing and grafting. Before showing how each species of fruit tree should be pruned and trained in the various forms that suit it, it is necessary to give an idea of how to distribute these trees in a garden that is supposed to be devoted exclusively to this kind of cultivation, although most often fruit and vegetables are produced in the same garden, which in this case is both a vegetable and fruit garden. Espaliers. - In France's average climate, some of the best species of fruit trees can only produce good produce under espaliers and counter-espaliers; this is why the fruit garden, in addition to the walls it must be surrounded by at least on the north and east sides, must contain several dividing walls pierced with false doors, in order to multiply the surface area at the most favorable exposures for espaliered fruit trees. Peach trees, a few early apricot and cherry trees, and vines producing the best table grapes, cover the well-exposed surfaces of all these walls. The vines can be planted on the opposite side, so that their roots and those of the other fruit trees don't interfere with each other. The arms of the vine, which are to extend in cords at the top of the wall, are introduced on the southern side through openings made in the masonry for this purpose. The north side is planted with late-succession apple trees. Palissage à la loque. - To take full advantage of the warmth reflected by the wall, espaliered fruit trees need to be fixed or trellised, as the saying goes, either immediately on the surface of the wall, or on a trellis. Immediate trellising is only possible when the gardener can have the walls plastered solid enough for nails to be driven into them without detaching them. In Montreuil-aux-Pêches and the surrounding communes, where vast tracts of land are devoted exclusively to the cultivation of espaliered fruit trees, trellising is unheard of. The abundance of plaster, of which there are large quarries in the area, means that walls can be plastered solidly at low cost, and trellises can be placed on the plastered surface without any intermediary. If tree branches, especially those of peach trees, which are softer and less woody than others, were tied to the nails with rush or wicker ties, they would be more or less damaged. To avoid this inconvenience, gardeners get cloth clippings from the tailors' workshops, which women and children cut in their spare time, in pieces the shape of a long square. The gardener takes the branch of the peach tree he wants to trellis from one of these pieces, folded in half, then presses the point of a nail onto the two joined ends, and hammers it into the plasterwork of the wall. This highly economical and convenient method has serious drawbacks when espaliered trees need to be removed, either for annual cleaning of the surface of the branches facing the wall, or when it is necessary to give strength to a weak shoot by temporarily holding it in an inclined position in front of the wall, a method which never fails to produce results. In this case, when the tree has been trellised, there would be too many nails to move. Trellises. - Most gardeners, either out of taste or necessity, not having the elements of good trellising to hand, trellis their trees in espalier, either on a trellis or on wire. The most commonly used trellis is made of oak slats that cross to form square or diamond-shaped meshes; this type of trellis is fastened to the wall with large hooked nails. Trees attached to the wooden trellis by wicker ties are easily stripped in whole or in part. The trellis itself, divided into medium-sized pieces, can be easily removed for cleaning on both surfaces. This prevents the proliferation of insect pests that make their habitual home on the rear surface of the trellis, which can be periodically repainted to make it more durable. Compensating for these advantages, wooden trellis is very expensive. More often than not, when planting young espalier trees, the owner, simply to give his fruit garden a more pleasing appearance, has the entire surface of the walls covered with trellis. Many years must pass before the new planting reaches the top of the wall. In the meantime, the trellis remains exposed to the inclemency of the seasons; it deteriorates from year to year, so that its upper part is worn and must be renewed before it is used. In the part of Walloon Belgium that includes the provinces of Liége, Namur and Luxembourg, a more economical and rational method is used for trellising espaliered fruit trees. Rods of dogwood, or any other strong yet flexible wood, are fixed to the wall with hooked nails, behind the space covered by the young tree. As the tree grows, new rows of rods are added, suitably spaced out. If one of the rods is accidentally broken, it is replaced without disturbing the rest of the trellis; cleaning and untrimming are equally easy; trellising with rods, as it is called in countries where it is in use, prevents immediate contact between the wall and the espaliered trees; it combines all the advantages of wooden trellises, and has none of their disadvantages. Wire. - Wire can be used for trellising in two different ways. The most commonly used consists in fixing wooden uprights vertically along the wall. These uprights, set 2 or 3 meters apart, are pierced with holes through which horizontally-tensioned wires pass, in tiers spaced 25 to 30 centimeters apart; the spacing between the wires can vary according to the shape of the trees to be trellised on them. In townships close to factories, where wire is readily available at moderate cost, the entire wall surface is covered with a mesh similar to that of a rabbit warren; each mesh is 10 to 15 centimetres square, and nails with hooks from distance to distance secure this mesh to the wall; it has the advantage over all possible trellises of solidity and durability. It is not widely used, however, as it is very expensive to build. Clumps of fruit trees. - Once the two surfaces of each wall have been planted with espaliered trees, suitably matched, trellised according to local resources and spaced out in proportion to the dimensions they are to acquire, the rest of the fruit garden should be filled with the best fruit trees that the nature of the soil and the local climate will allow. The first step is to place a certain number of these trees on the edge of the flowerbed at the foot of the walls. These trees are directed as if they were espaliered; poles, some straight, others horizontal, hold the branches of the counter-spaliered trees in a direction parallel to the wall. If the garden has a higher, drier area than the rest, this is the place to plant a few high-stemmed open-air trees, mainly cherry, plum and apricot 15. trees, including one or two blackberry bushes, taking care not to place them on the edge of a pathway, as their fruit, which falls at every moment as they ripen, can stain clothing. Pyramid pear trees, which should be given as much space as possible in the fruit garden, should be placed at a sufficient distance from the walls so that their shade does not interfere with the ripening of the fruit of espaliered trees. To ensure that air and light, essential elements of fertility, circulate freely around the pyramid-shaped pear tree beds, the central part of the fruit garden is occupied by dwarf apple trees grafted on doucin, and dwarf apple trees grafted on paradis. These are combined with squares of raspberry, redcurrant and currant bushes. With this arrangement, everything is in its place in the fruit garden; open areas alternate with shady ones, and the available space is put to the best possible use, with each tree placed in the best conditions to grow and bear fruit without harming its neighbors, and without suffering from their proximity. Spacing. - The spacing of trees in a fruit garden can only be given as a rough guide; trees grow more or less because of the more or less vigorous nature of each variety, according to the degree of fertility of the soil. In soil of average fertility, high-stemmed open-air trees should be planted at 8 meters; pyramid-shaped trees at 4 or 5 meters; half-dwarf apple trees on doucin at 1.50 or 2 meters, and dwarf apple trees on paradise at only 1 meter. Preparing the ground. - Planting a fruit garden is a necessarily costly operation, the results of which, good or bad, must be prolonged indefinitely. Nothing must therefore be neglected if it is to be carried out in the best possible conditions. If the soil is clayey, compacted and lies on impermeable subsoil, drainage is essential. The soil must then be broken up to purge it of any stones and roots it may contain, mark the spot for each tree, open holes that are larger than they are deep, and leave the soil removed from the holes exposed to the air for at least a few months before planting the fruit trees. The amendments that fruit-garden soil may need should not be spared; calcareous amendments, mainly plaster, are always very useful for planting stone-fruit trees; on the contrary, a soil that is stronger than light is suitable for seed-fruit trees. If the soil in the fruit garden is very light, due to an excess of silica sand, it should be amended with a little lime and a heavy dose of clay. All these measures having been taken, we can proceed with the planting of fruit trees; but first of all, we must choose the trees that are going to be planted. Tree selection - Transport. - If, as is often the case, the gardener has not raised the trees he wants to plant himself, he should choose them early in the autumn at the nursery. If he waits until the planting season is over, he may no longer find the species and varieties he wants in good samples at the nursery. He will try to be present at the uprooting, to ensure that the young trees retain all their roots; he will supervise the packing, so that the trees don't have to suffer during transport. As a general rule, and except where it is impossible to obtain fruit trees for a large plantation from a distance, it is preferable to go to the nearest nursery, especially now that the practice of planting very young trees has been more or less abandoned. Trees bought fully trained from a nursery, however well packed they may be, always suffer to a greater or lesser extent during the journey from the nursery to the place where they are to be planted. The less time that elapses between uprooting and planting, the greater the chances of success. If the garden where the trees are to be planted is in a situation more exposed to the cold north and east winds than the nursery, it is useful to mark on the bark, with a brush stroke and an oil color, the side that faced north in the nursery, so as to preserve the same orientation for the planted tree. When the trees have suffered more or less from being transported in dry or cold weather, cow dung is mixed with a very small amount of water in a large tub, so as to form a clear broth in which to soak the roots at the very moment of planting. As for what we call root dressing, anyone planting fruit trees must be convinced of the truth that a fruit tree can never have too many roots. Root dressing should therefore be limited to removing the parts of the roots damaged by uprooting and transport, while retaining all the rest. Planting. - Advances in modern horticulture have completely changed people's ideas about planting fruit trees. For a start, people are no longer reluctant, as they once were, to pay prices commensurate with their real value for fairly large trees, which produce a fairly good crop of fruit the year they are planted, or at the very latest the following year. If, for construction or any other reason, fruit trees of a fairly advanced age have to be moved, they are no longer considered as lost. We know that by raising these trees in rootballs, and taking the wise precaution of coating the trunk and main branches with the mixture of cow dung and clay known to gardeners as Saint-Fiacre's ointment, the trees can be planted, so to speak, at any age. For those whose position does not oblige them to look too closely at the expense, it is always much better to plant well-developed trees that have been in the nursery for 4 to 6 years, than to plant very young trees whose first fruits will be delayed for several years. The best time to plant fruit trees is from the end of November to the end of April. In winter, trees can be planted as long as it doesn't freeze; in spring, trees in full sap, already with leaves, can be planted with as much chance of success as winter planting, provided they are protected from the effects of drought by timely watering, and evaporation of the sap is prevented by coating the bark with Saint-Fiacre ointment. This coating gradually falls off, when the tree no longer needs it and has taken possession of its new place with young roots. When planting a fruit tree, care must be taken not to bury its roots too deeply. The first thing to do is to line the bottom of the pit with a sufficiently thick layer of good soil, suitably amended as necessary, then spread the roots in all directions over this layer, avoiding forcing them and causing them to burst on insertion. The aim of this part of the work should be that, after planting, the roots run horizontally between two layers of soil, close enough to the surface to take full advantage of atmospheric influences. When the roots run too deep into the ground, not only does this result in excess vigour, causing the trees to produce a useless luxury of wood and hindering fruit production, but the fruit itself does not possess the qualities proper to its species, to the same degree as if the tree's roots had felt, during fruit formation, the beneficial action of alternating rain and sunshine. If the soil in the fruit garden has been properly drained, the roots of fruit trees can be spread a decimetre or two below the level of the surrounding soil, and then covered with the rest of the soil taken from the hole where the tree is planted. But if the soil has not been drained, or if, despite drainage, there is reason to fear excess moisture in the subsoil, then there is no need to fear planting flush with the ground, except to form a mound of good soil around the foot of the tree, so that its roots are sufficiently covered. Flats. - The same rules apply to the planting of espaliered fruit trees. A very rational method is followed in England, which should be imitated wherever espaliered peach trees are to be planted in soil and climate conditions similar to those in Great Britain. At the point where the peach tree's roots are to be planted, all the soil in the bed in front of the wall is removed; a pit is thus opened that is the full width of the bed, 50 to 60 centimetres deep, and 1.5 to 2 metres long. The bottom and sides of the pit are lined with paving and light brickwork, then some of the soil is replaced, and the peach tree is planted according to the principles outlined above. The peach tree's roots are prevented from overgrowing in this kind of embankment, which English horticulturists call a platform; the tree's vegetation then grows steadily; the equilibrium of the sap in all its parts is easy to maintain; the result is a regular production of the best possible fruit. Peach trees planted on a platform should be kept to an average size; allowing them to cover too much surface area on the espalier would give the trapped roots too many branches to feed, and the aim of this type of planting would be missed. CHAPTER XXXI pruning and management of trees with pepino fruits. Necessity of pruning fruit trees. - Pruning fruit trees. - Principles of pruning fruit trees - Balance of sap. - Natural pear tree vegetation. - Annual budding. - Staves. - Buds. - Twigs. - Pruned pear branch. - Various forms of pear tree management. - Simple horizontal cordon. - Trees à la Jamin. - Pruning. - Pinching. - Disbudding. - Their effects on pear trees. - Double horizontal cord. Necessity of pruning fruit trees. - Fruit trees planted in our gardens cannot be left to grow naturally. Although they belong to long-established breeds that have been modified by cultivation, if left unpruned, these trees would set fruit late, exhaust themselves producing a multitude of confused branches, and in no way meet the gardener's requirements. What matters to him is that the fruit tree should produce regular harvests for as long as possible, and that it should occupy precisely the place assigned to it in the garden, retaining the form in which it was originally established; this, in a nutshell, is the main aim of pruning, an aim that could not be achieved if the tree were left to its own devices. Pruning seeded fruit trees. - From an economic point of view, seedling fruit trees rank first in the fruit garden. The art of pruning them to develop and maintain their productive force is one of the most essential skills for both professional and amateur gardeners. For a very long time, this art was regarded as one of those mysteries accessible only to the initiated; the old gardeners, guided more by routine than by reasoning, carefully kept to themselves whatever notions they might possess in this respect, and passed them on to a small number of pupils only with great diffidence and circumspection. Butret's and Dalbret's treatises, remarkable for their time, published at the beginning of this century, paved the way for other works in which the correct methods for pruning fruit trees were brought within reach of the general public. The first to really set out the true principles in this field, and the sound applications of these principles, was Count Le Lieur, Intendant of the Crown Gardens, in his justly famous work entitled Pomone française. Since the publication of this work, the principles of pruning have become increasingly popular, so much so that nowadays it is impossible for anyone involved in gardening not to be familiar with them. Principles of fruit tree pruning. - These principles are based on a vital fact, which everyone can verify by direct observation. Each tree has its own particular mode of vegetation, its own way of successively forming its wood and fruit products, and of directing the course of the sap that sustains it; it is therefore impossible to prune according to uniform rules trees whose vegetation does not follow the same course; any rational method of pruning fruit trees must be based on observation and in-depth knowledge of the way they vegetate when not pruned. Another principle from which we must never deviate is to always seek to direct the sap without antagonizing it, and to distribute it as evenly as possible in all parts of the tree; this is what we call the principle of sap equilibrium, a principle equally applicable to all fruit trees, whatever the form in which they are trained. So, before considering pruning any tree, it's important to have an exact idea of the nature of its branches, its fruit production, the way it forms, and its overall vegetation. Natural vegetation of the pear tree. - Careful examination of a fully-formed pear branch reveals various branch sprouts, each easily distinguished from the others by the eyes or gems it bears, and by the characteristics of these eyes. The one-year-old branch or budFig. 54. Woody branch of the pear tree. Fig. 55. Bunch of pear tree flowers. nual (fig. 54), is the simplest of the pear tree's branches; it bears only woody eyes, all similar to each other; each of these eyes is formed in the axil of a leaf; this shoot is born during the summer, from a woody eye developed in spring; from the following year, it will show other characters. The upper eyes will open with wood, as the gardeners say; buds will emerge that are similar to the first, except for their length; the terminal eye will be used to extend the shoot. The lower eyes will not open like the upper ones; they will grow larger or smaller, protected by persistent scales, and their base will be surrounded by two or three leaves. Every year, the number of leaves will increase, the bud support, marked by as many scars as there have been successive leaves to protect it, will lengthen more or less; the bud will grow and eventually open into a bunch of flowers followed by fruit (fig. 55). All this, if the tree is not pruned, will take a considerable number of years. Not all branches of a pear tree are buds. Others, short and stubby, ending in a fruit bud more or less advanced in its development, bear only fruit buds; these are called Lambourdes (fig. 56); they are the most valuable of the pear tree's fruit-bearing branches. On lambourdes, groups of fruit buds are often very close together, placed on a common, swollen, fragile support, furrowed across the scars left by the fall of the leaves that helped feed it; these are called bursae (fig. 57). DuringFig. 56. Pear tree stalk. Fig. 57. A pear tree bursa. a woody eye is found among the fruit buds, the gardener is quick to encourage its development, even if it means sacrificing part of the fruit, in order to rejuvenate the bursa by attracting sap, in the hope of giving birth to a lambourde. In addition, the pear shoot has small, thin branches bearing buds of varying degrees of development; these are called twigs (fig. 58); the fruit buds are very evenly distributed on all the branches of the shoot, at their lower end. Here is an unpruned pear shoot, three years old; all its terminal branches are buds bearing exclusively woody eyes. The other, older parts have a few buds which will eventually flower, but not one of which is yet ready to blossom; there is no bursa, lamburd, twig or branch ready to become one of these productions (fig. 59). Opposite this branch is another of the same age, pruned regularly from its first year. The woody branches that were not useful for fermation of the framework, and from which no fruit-bearing branches could be expected, were removed; the terminal bud extended the branch, not Fig. 58. Twig Fig. 59. Unpruned pear tree. branch, as if it had been left to its own devices, but moderately, by pruning each year on a good wood eye. The sap, no longer diverted by a multitude of superfluous buds, has flowed back to the bottom of each branch, many of which bear fruit buds, bursae and short twigs that will become lambourdes. A comparison of these two branches gives an exact idea of the application of the principles of rational pear pruning, and the results of this pruning (fig. 60). Fig 60. Pruned pear tree branch. Ge 9 3 Various forms of pear tree management. - Pruning, as has already been pointed out, is not only intended to regulate the growth of a tree and the production of fruit; it must also give it a specific shape, according to the gardener's views, and maintain it in this shape: this is what we call training a tree. Pruning and training of fruit trees therefore run in parallel. Each type of fruit tree can be trained in a number of ways, depending on its purpose and the place assigned to it in the fruit garden. The main shapes suitable for pear trees are the horizontal cordon, single or double, the pyramid, the cattail, which differs little from the horizontal cordon, the spindle, also known as a colonnade, and the vase or basket. When pear trees are planted in espaliers or center-espaliers, they are nowadays only grown in the two forms of simple and double palmette. The other forms in which pear trees were once grown in espaliers have been completely abandoned, to such an extent that it would be difficult today to find pear trees for espalier or counter-espalier that are not prepared for simple or double palmette. Horizontal cordon. - Of all the forms in which pear trees can be trained, the horizontal cordon is the most recently introduced. Pear trees in this form are known in the horticultural trade as Jamin trees, after the skilled horticulturist to whom this new form is due (fig. 61). Like all possible forms, the horizontal cordon starts from a young tree grafted the previous year, better on quince than on franc, i.e. on a subject obtained from pear seedlings. The latter is generally unsuitable for trees grown in a form that allows them to develop very little. The graft has been pruned on a good eye, and this eye, opened into a vigorous bud, has given rise from spring to autumn to the shoot which is to become a tree (fig. 62). If the horizontal cordon is to be simple, as soon as the twig begins to drop, i.e. to pass from the herbaceous to the woody state, after the movement of the second sap in August, we incline it without forcing it, on the side where it should be established horizontally, in which position we fix it 50 centimetres from the ground, either on a wire stretched by means of two stakes, or on a strand of trellis supported by the same Fig. 62. One-year-old pear tree. If, when first planted, the horizontal cordon were allowed to grow too long, the eyes on the one-year-old branch, all of which are woody, would be too slow to develop into fruit buds or fruit production. It is therefore pruned according to its strength at a certain distance from the bend. The following year, the shoot coming from the eye on which we have pruned is also shortened according to its strength; the cordon thus gradually reaches a length of 1.50 to 2 meters, which it must not exceed. The buds that develop along its length, between the bend and the terminal shoot, are pruned shorter or shorter to obtain fruit-bearing branches, or removed altogether if they are badly placed and disturb the harmony of the tree. Pruning is not the only resource available to the gardener for the management of his fruit trees; he also has pinching, almost as useful as pruning itself, disbudding and breaking to regulate their vegetation. The application of these operations to pear trees in horizontal cordon, the least complicated form of all, makes their usefulness perfectly clear for trees pruned in any other form. Pinching consists in cutting back, in summer, the part of a bud that has not yet passed from the herbaceous to the woody state; it stops the useless extension of the bud, enlarges its lower eyes, and hastens their conversion into buds or fruiting branches. When the horizontal cordon pear tree has reached the length it should retain, its extension bud is pinched off, then pruned in winter, and the bud that follows it is treated in the same way in subsequent years, so that the horizontal cordon ceases to lengthen. If buds develop along the cordon, they are pinched off in the same way, so that their lower eyes can benefit, becoming fruit buds. Disbudding, whose effects differ from those of pinching, consists in suppressing a herbaceous bud at the start of its growth. On regularly pruned trees, many buds are always born here and there from misplaced eyes, which the gardener cannot allow to develop, even halfway, because he does not expect them to produce fruit. These buds must be completely removed, which requires a great deal of care and discernment, so as not to have, later on, reason to regret buds removed inconsiderately. Breaking is the complement of pinching; it consists in breaking buds that have become woody after the second sè́ve. If they were pruned instead of broken, these buds would not be stopped completely enough in their extension; the eye below the cut would immediately start again in a new bud, and the desired effect would not be produced. On the broken bud, the eye below the break does not start up again; the entire sap turns to the benefit of the eyes that are to become fruit buds. Thus, by pruning proper, the gardener has given the horizontal pear tree the shape and length it needs; by pinching, he has prevented the buds from developing too much, preparing them for fruiting; by disbudding, he has eliminated buds that would have disturbed the harmony of the tree's shape; by breaking, finally, he has prepared the retained buds that have already become woody to take on fruit production. 1 The same operations performed on trees grown in any other form produce the same results. We had to demonstrate the first applications on a pear tree in a single horizontal cordon, for the sake of clarity. To form the double horizontal cordon, more commonly used than the single, we let two buds develop on the right and left of the tree being pruned for the first time, instead of one, and we bend these two buds, one to the right, the other to the left. They are usually allowed to grow to a length of 2 meters from their starting point; the rest of the pruning and handling is the same as for the single horizontal cordon uia pear tree. CHAPTER XXXII Pruning pear fruit trees (continued). Pyramid pear trees. - Pyramid formation. - Spiral arrangement of lateral branches - Gradual extension of the spire. - Cattail. - How it differs from the pyramid. - Why it is abandoned - Spindle or column - Size of roots - Mandatory stakes. - Pear tree in vase or basket - How to establish it - Its disadvantages - Pear tree for espalier in simple palmette - Shaping the palmette. - Double palmette. - Pruning and training. - Half-dwarf apple on doucin. - Dwarf, on paradise. - Normandy. - Freeing dwarf apple trees when their graft is buried. The indications given in the previous chapter contain all the principles of pruning trees with pear fruits, and their application to the simplest form in which the pear tree can be trained. When it comes to pruning pear trees into any other shape, the same principles are applied in practice. Pyramid-shaped pear tree. - The pyramid shape (fig. 63) is the most commonly used for pear trees, either in the fruit garden proper, or in the beds that frame the vegetable garden squares. Pear trees in this form do not cast too thick a shadow on their neighbors or on the surrounding parts of the vegetable garden; their fruit, receiving air and light in all directions, possess all the qualities proper to their species; once well established, pyramid-shaped pear trees easily maintain good vegetation; Unless they are left to careless hands, these trees never tend to become overgrown with superfluous wood or to take on greedy branches, whose excessive vigor would disturb the harmony of the form. Today's preference for the pyramid is therefore entirely justified. Pyramid formation. - The pear tree destined to become an unc pyramid starts, like all possible pear trees, with a single shoot born from a graft pruned over a good eye. If you examine the woody eyes distributed along this shoot, you'll see that they are naturally arranged in a spiral; it's also in a spiral that the pyramid's lateral branches should be established. Start by pruning on a good eye, leaving four or five buds below the cut. To ensure that the sap benefits mainly the lateral branches, on which the tree's future depends, the central shoot, known as the arrow of the tree, is only allowed to grow moderately, by pruning on a vigorous eye until it has gradually takenFig. 63. Pyramid-shaped pear tree. the height it should not exceed. The side shoots are likewise shortened to a greater or lesser extent, to give them sufficient strength to retain their share of the sap that continually tends to flow towards the top of the tree. If they do not naturally take the desired direction, the lower lateral branches are tied with wicker ties either to the tree's spire, or to the stake which supports them during the first few years and prevents them from deviating from the vertical line. Nowadays, for planting purposes, it is customary 16 to buy ready-made trees from nurseries, which the gardener only has to continue, and which effortlessly maintain perfect regularity. Cattail. - The only difference between the pyramid and the cattail is that, starting from the ground, the first tiers of lower branches widen out to around two meters; from this point on, the shape of the tree becomes entirely pyramid-like. This shape has the disadvantage of not giving enough strength to the lower branches, which bear almost no fruit, the sap being attracted by the tiers of lateral branches immediately above. Spindle or column. - The spindle or column pear tree is only suitable for small gardens where a large number of pear varieties need to be grouped together in a limited space. These trees have virtually no lateral branches, or at least their branches, pruned very short, are used exclusively to support fruit production. Trees can only be preserved in this somewhat unnatural form by allowing them to increase in height, and by subjecting them from time to time to root pruning in autumn, after the leaves have fallen. To this end, the tree is dug up all around its base, and all the slightly extended roots are cut off, leaving only sections of length proportionate to the tree's strength. Plant physiology recognizes that roots make branches, and that branches make roots; a tree that retains almost no roots, and whose underground part is pruned regularly, forms almost no branches. However, in well-amended, naturally fertile soil, spindle- or column-grown pear trees, while not very long-lived, are, all things considered, as productive as others and produce excellent fruit, depending on their species. For the duration of their life, unless they are planted in clumps in a perfectly sheltered position, these trees need to be supported by good stakes, without which the shortness of their roots would expose them to being blown over by strong winds. Vase or basket pear trees. - This form is mentioned here because it is still in use in some amateur gardens; vase pear trees take up a lot of space because of the interior void that must be created; they offer no real advantage either for abundance or for the quality of the fruit, which compensates for this disadvantage. To obtain a well-formed vase, the young pear tree is pruned on two eyes, each of which produces a good bud. The following year, these two buds are each pruned over two eyes, giving four buds, the basis of the tree's framework. As they continue to grow, adding lateral branches, these main shoots are attached, at equal distances from each other, to circles of barrels, which are chosen to be larger and larger from tier to tier, until the fully-formed vase can support itself. Only pear trees grafted on quince should be grown in vase form, as these species are not inclined to grow too much; vases that are too high are bound to have a very large diameter at the top, making them bulky and very inconvenient. Simple palmette pear trees. - As mentioned above, this is the most common form of espalier and counter-espalier at present. Among the species of pear trees that give the best fruit, there are varieties that are more or less sensitive to cold, or that flower very early in the spring, so that late cold often prevents the fruit from forming. These are the varieties that should be placed on the espalier with good exposure. To establish the simple palmette pear tree, which is almost the only form used for the espalier pear tree, the spire, or central stem, is pruned successively over two eyes, one on the right and the other on the left; the buds born from these eyes are lowered and trellised in an almost horizontal position; they begin a tier of the framework. The following year, the spire, which has been allowed to grow only moderately, is pruned to produce a second tier, and so on. Great care must be taken to regulate the growth of the lateral branches or cords of the palmette, so that they are all of equal strength and grow with the most perfect equality, a condition easily obtained by pinching. If one side of the young tree gets carried away and becomes much more vigorous than the opposite side, the weaker side should be depressed while the stronger side remains attached to the trellis; this is enough to restore the balance. When you buy pear trees prepared by a skilled nurseryman, nothing is easier than to make the well-started framework take on all its growth, maintaining symmetry and regulating the distribution of sap in all parts of the tree. On an espalier pear tree, the buds born from the woody eyes are almost always too numerous; by disbudding, without giving them time to absorb any of the sap unnecessarily, we remove the poorly-placed buds, as the cords of the simple palmette pear tree should only present a bare surface to the wall, and have all their growth in front of the wall. Retained buds, if they show excessive vigor that prevents the tree from setting fruit, are pinched off, then broken, to obtain fruit production from their lower eyes; in this way, the trees quickly reach their highest degree of fertility. The procedure is the same for counter-espalier trees in the same form, but as both surfaces of the counter-espalier are exposed to air and light, whereas the espalier receives light and air only from one side, the gardener allows buds to develop equally on the two opposite surfaces of the cords of the single-palmette counter-espalier; he can thus obtain a double number of fruit productions (fig. 64). Double palmette pear tree. - This shape begins with pruning on two eyes, leaving the buds to grow vertically. On each of these buds, which have become the base of the tree's framework, we let them grow, Tig. C4. A simple palmetto tree, pruned in much the same way as the simple palmetto, with equally spaced lateral cords. This form offers no real advantage over the simple palmette; it is best suited to species with a Fig. 65. Pear tree prepared for the double palmette. high vigor, destined to cover a large area on the espalier (fig. 65). 16. Pruning and training. - The apple tree's natural growth habit, woody branches, fruit production, pruning principles and applications are exactly the same as those of the pear tree. However, these two trees, which are so close to each other, cannot be joined by grafting in a lasting way, which indicates more radical differences in their constitution than the complete identity of their way of vegetating. Apple trees grafted on franc or doucin form pyramids and vases, in the same way as pear trees. The vase shape is more advantageous for apple trees, which are less inclined to get carried away than pear trees. The apple tree also has the ability to graft by approach when its branches are crossed, either on its own branches or on those of another apple tree; when it is trained as a single horizontal cordon on wire, 50 centimetres from the ground, the end of one cordon is grafted by approach, by means of a slight notch in the two barks, on the bend of the following cordon. The result is a continuous line of indeterminate length, through which the sap supplied by the roots of all the plants circulates, making them more durable and productive. In fruit gardens where space is at a premium, you can devote a square of a certain size in the fruit garden to establishing what is known as a normandie. This involves planting alternate rows of half-dwarf apple trees, in small pyramids, 1.5 meters apart, and dwarf apple trees only 1 meter apart; the rows are evenly spaced at 1.5 meters apart. Great care must be taken when planting a Normandy composed of doucin and paradis apple trees, not to bury the grafts. When the grafts of these trees are buried, they act as cuttings; they emit roots of their own, they become free, according to the very apt expression used by gardeners. In fact, grafts rooted as cuttings are no longer nourished exclusively by the subject; as a result, instead of remaining small, in half-dwarf pyramids or dwarf bushes, apple trees grow unevenly and excessively, and behave exactly as free-standing trees would, i.e. born from seedlings of pepins, or grafted onto free-standing subjects, which would not allow them to be kept in Normandy. We can take advantage of the ease with which all species and varieties of apple trees can be grafted, or, to put it more accurately, welded by approach, to form hedges and arbors that are both very solid and very productive. For example, to separate the fruit or vegetable garden from the other sections of a large garden, instead of a bower or a fence of dogwood or hawthorn, plant a row of apple trees and direct them so that their branches intersect in diamond shapes, either with each other or with those of their neighbors. At each point where two branches meet, the bark is incised, and the two parts that are to be welded together are held together by a ligature. From the first year, the weld is consolidated; apple trees trained in hedges in this way are just as productive as if they were trained as counter-paliers. The same procedure can be used to establish an arbor of diamond-shaped welded apple trees within a few years. CHAPTER XXXIII Pruning and management of stone fruit trees. Stone fruit trees. - Natural vegetation of peach trees. - What happens to unpruned peach trees. - Pruning and management of peach trees. - Various peach tree branches - Disbudding and pinching. - Various peach tree shapes - Egyptian pyramid peach tree - Single palmette peach tree. - Double palmette. - Its advantages in small gardens. To rationally apply the pruning and management principles set out in the previous chapters to stone fruit trees, it is first necessary to understand exactly how each tree grows, so as to be able to act with full knowledge of the facts. The stone fruit trees grown in our gardens are: peach, apricot, plum and cherry. The peach tree is the most important, either because of the high value of its fruit, or because of its fecundity and the number of good species that horticulture has been able to obtain from it. Natural vegetation of the peach tree. - If you sow a peach pit and leave the resulting sapling to grow untouched, it will form a stem of its own, branching out at a certain height and ending up with an irregular head made up of young branches that follow one another from year to year. Some of these branches blossom and bear a certain number of fruits; those that have borne fruit once can never bear fruit again, however long the tree lasts; they can only produce young branches that can blossom and bear fruit once, never more. All the sap flows towards the top of the tree, the lower branches remain entirely bare, and no young wood is ever naturally produced there, so powerful is the suction of sap by the upper part of the branches in the peach tree: such is the invariable course of the natural vegetation of this tree; this course is exactly as it has just been described in a grafted peach tree, left to its own devices, without being pruned or guided in any particular way. Pruning and training. - The preceding notions already show what the main aim of peach tree pruning and training should be. Those known as vine peach trees, which are not grafted and which grow in vineyards as God pleases, can be left to the natural course of their vegetation without great inconvenience, until they die of exhaustion; this is what happens in the United States of America in the immense orchards planted with peach trees of this kind, whose fruit is used to supply alcohol by fermentation and distillation. In gardens, it's not possible to give peach trees the same freedom. In the climate of Paris and the whole of France, from the Loire basin to our northern frontier, the peach tree only produces good fruit when cultivated in espaliers; the counter-espalier is not even sufficient for it; peaches ripen too late, or not at all, due to lack of heat. When a peach tree is grown in an espalier, it's easy to understand that, even without taking into account the beauty of the view, a wall lined with trees whose branches are all bare at the bottom and bear only a little greenery at the top, would be a cause of serious loss for the gardener. When pruning, therefore, he must combat the tendency of the sap to leave the bottom of the peach tree, retain it and distribute it evenly to all parts of the tree, which he must know how to make equally productive, and knowing that a peach fruiting branch flowers and bears fruit only once, provide uninterrupted replacement branches for the branches he removes after they have borne a crop (fig. 66). The annual bud on a peach tree branch is not, like that of the pear and apple tree, furnished exclusively with woody eyes; these eyes are accompanied by one or two flower buds, on which the fruiting branch rests (fig. 66). Peach fruit branch. Fig. 67. Flowering branch of the peach tree. of the year's harvest (fig. 67). Sometimes, small, very short branches bearing a tuft of leaves and several flower buds are produced on the branches of the peach tree: these are called bouquets. Bouquets are to the peach tree what bursas are to peach trees; they are carefully tended as the best of fruit production (fig. 68). When a young peach branch, born from a woody eye from the previous year, has borne fruit, it is pruned on its lower eye, so that it opens onto a bud which will be a fruiting branch in its turn. The result of this repeated pruning every year is a certain number of short branches, evenly distributed throughout the tree's structure, whose function is to produce fruiting branches; these are what gardeners call spur branches (fig. 89). When one of these branches produces a good eye with inferior wood, this eye should be pruned to rejuvenate the branch, thus preventing excessive extension and exhaustion. Fig. 68. Bouquet The whole art of pruning the peach tree, apart from the peach tree itself, consists in providing it with a sufficient number of evenly distributed spurs to ensure that all its branches are Fig. 69. Peach tree spur branch. parts of the espalier give their quota of good peaches, and to give birth, at the bottom of the branches that have just borne fruit, to a certain number of replacement branches. Peach trees planted in the right conditions are more likely to produce too many annual shoots than too few. It often happens that a very vigorous annual bud doesn't stay simple, and the eyes of the upper leaf axils open prematurely to form false or early buds. Whatever the vigor of the peach tree, the gardener should never be embarrassed by it; as long as he doesn't allow the sap to carry more to one side than the other, and maintains the balance of vegetation, the peach tree will never have too much strength, since it will always be possible to use this strength for the benefit of the production and quality of the fruit. Disbudding and pin¬- cementing are the easiest ways for the gardener to prevent disorder and maintain harmony of form. Unnecessary or misplaced buds should not be removed too early; they should be allowed to grow to a certain extent, so as not to have to regret an ill-considered retran¬ chection later on. It's for the same reason that the gardener doesn't give the peach tree its main annual pruning until the tree begins to flower; he can see his job more clearly and knows better what he's doing. If he were to carry out this pruning in winter, while the peach tree was asleep, he would often compromise the harvest, by keeping only those branches with a wooden eye too weak to attract sap and nourish the fruit, and by pruning the extension branches on an eye which is strong enough in appearance, but which would only give the shoot a defective bud." Various shapes of peach trees. - As we have seen, the high-stemmed open-air peach tree, or vine peach tree, is not pruned; it is only pruned to remove dead, diseased or accidentally broken branches; it is not trained in any regular shape. The pyramid shape is not used for peach trees; only one species, the Egyptian or Syrian peach tree, of very modern introduction, lends itself to the pyramid shape and produces passable fruit in this form; the framework is established according to the principles set out for forming the pyramid pear tree; all other peach trees want the espalier shape. The main shapes of espaliered peach trees are the single palmette (fig. 70), the double palmette, the Vouvert, the square shape, the Demoustier shape, and the single or double oblique cordon.  ~ Fig. 70. Peach tree with single palmette. Simple palmette peach tree. - To grow peach trees in this form, the one-year-old peach tree is grafted onto a good eye, which produces a single shoot destined to be the central stem or arrow of the palmette. The first pruning produces two lateral arms, one on the right, the other on the left, at the same height; these are the first lower cords of the palmette. Carefully managed by pinching and disbudding, they are easily maintained in the necessary state of vigor and equality; the following year, the spire, sufficiently prolonged, gives two more cords which are treated in the same way, and the tree is completed year after year by the same process, until it has reached the top of the wall. When the arms are spaced far enough apart to give them a filling of sprigs and fruiting branches on both sides of each cordon, care is taken not to trellis them quite horizontally, so that the small branches on the lower part can attract their share of the sap. When the cords are closer together, they are trellised more or less horizontally (fig. 71); but then they only have spur and fruiting branches on their upper part; the first of these two methods is by far the most common. Fig. 71. Single palmette tree with horizontal strings. Double palmette peach tree. - This form is started with two buds of equal strength, trellised vertically facing each other, on each of which cords are placed at the same level on each side. The double palmette offers no advantage over the single palmette, and is almost never used. It is especially suitable for very vigorous species, whose cords are very long and cover a large area on the right and left of the espalier.
