Open V shape. - This shape, referred to in older treatises as the Montreuil shape, no longer merits this name; it is mentioned here only as a reminder; Montreuil gardeners have gradually abandoned it in favor of the square shape, now gradually replaced by the palmette; the reason for this is obvious. When a young peach tree is established on two diverging branches which are made to produce a complete regular framework, a fairly long time always passes before the gap left on the espalier by the divergence of the two arms of the open V can be filled. The result is a very noticeable loss in fruit production. The palmette offers the undeniable advantage of covering the entire surface and using all of it for fruit production. Square shape. - This shape has long enjoyed great favor; it is superior to the open V shape in that it covers the surface of the wall more quickly; it is inferior to the palmette in that some of the branches of the frame, beginning like the open V with two divergent first arms, are in a position too close to the vertical. Any peach tree branch in too upright a position sucks in too much sap and continually tends to get carried away at the expense of the others; the balance of vegetation can only be maintained with care and attention. Nevertheless, many espaliers still feature square-shaped peach trees, which are very beautiful and productive; but they are successively replaced by simple palmettes, as they die of old age. Demoustier shape. - This form, now completely abandoned, starts with an open V; the first branches are given a very large extension, so that the framework is only complete after a large number of years. Undoubtedly, in the garden of an amateur who values the beauty of the view, and who has a lot of free surface on his walls, it's a very beautiful thing to have an old peach tree trained in the Demoustier style, 6 to 8 meters long on each side of the trunk, and equally covered with flowers in spring and fruit in summer throughout. But for a professional gardener, who pays the rent for the land he cultivates and can lose neither precious time nor most of the espalier surface, this shape cannot be adopted and its complete abandonment is fully justified. Oblique cordon shape - The oblique cordon peach shapeFig. 72. Simple oblique cordon peach tree. oblique cordon peach tree is one of the most successful innovations in modern horticulture (fig. 72). The peach tree in this form does not, strictly speaking, require pruning to establish its framework. Once a vigorous bud has emerged from a good pillar, it is allowed to grow without being shortened, simply by being inclined at an angle of 45 degrees. The eyes that open up along the cordon give more than enough spurs and small fruiting branches. The inclined position of the cordon allows it to extend so far that it stops of its own accord before reaching the top of the wall; otherwise, it is contained by the pinch. Peach trees in this form can be planted 75 centimetres apart; the way they fit one on top of the other leaves no gaps in the espalier, and not 1 square centimetre is lost to fruit production. The double oblique cordon (fig. 73) is formed in the same way as the single, with two buds which are allowed to grow, taking care to 2 9 9 9 2 "maintain them at equal strength by pinching, disbudding and trellising; they are spaced at Fig. 73. Double oblique cordon peach tree. 40 centimetres apart, at the same angle, as the single cordon. Double-cord peach trees should be planted 1 meter apart. One of the main advantages of the oblique cordon is that it enables a whole collection of peach trees to be grouped together in a small espalier, which is not possible when each individual tree occupies 2 or 3 running meters of surface area to the right and left of its base. As you can see, pruning and managing the peach tree, in whatever form it takes, in accordance with its particular mode of vegetation, offers no serious difficulties. On the contrary, this tree is extremely docile, quick to set fruit and regularly productive when enough care is taken to direct it according to the requirements of its temperament and the natural course of its sap. CHAPTER XXXIV Pruning and management of stone fruit trees and vines. Pruning and management of apricot trees. - In the open air. - Espalier. - Fan shape. - How to establish it. - Difficulty of maintaining it. - Apricot tree branches. - Pruning and management of plum trees. - In the open. - Espalier. - Pruning and training cherry trees. - Espaliered cherry, simple palmette. - Vine pruning and management. - Natural vegetation. - Bushes. - Weeds. - What happens to them if they are not pruned. - Necessity of pruning. - Its purpose. - Its effects. - Spurs, similar to peach tree spurs. - Their rejuvenation. - Summer pruning. - Vine training. - Thomery shape. - Advantages. - Vertical cordon shape. Pruning and management of apricot trees. The fruit of the apricot tree only really possesses all the qualities that give it its merit, when it is harvested from trees trained in the open air, with high stems. These trees are established on four main branches, following a similar pattern to that used to form vase or basket pear and apple trees. For the first few years, the branches of the framework are kept at the desired distance, by attaching them to one or more barrel hoops; they are then left to grow freely. Apricot trees, whose wood is always extremely gummy, are among those which, as gardeners say, don't like iron, and want to be pruned as little as possible, once they are well established in the desired shape. Espaliered apricot trees. - In a fairly large fruit garden, apricot trees are always given a place on the espalier, either to have ripe apricots very early, or to have very beautiful apricots, even though we know in advance that they will never be as good as full-wind apricots. The form usually adopted for espaliered apricot trees is the fan shape, which, when complete, consists of 5 or 7 regularly diverging branches, like the pins of a fan. Like the peach tree, this form begins with two buds facing each other, diverging like the first shoots of a Vouvert peach tree. In the second year, these two branches, pruned to 25 or 30 centimetres on two good eyes, form the lower branches of the framework, which continues in the same way in subsequent years, allowing the extension shoots to take on only a moderate length each year, so as to fix the sap in the lower branches before allowing those in the center, whose direction is closer to the vertical, to develop. Moreover, however much care is taken to establish perfect symmetry between all the parts of an espaliered apricot tree, this harmony of form, even in the hands of the most skilled gardener, never lasts very long. The apricot tree is more prone than any other tree, at least in the climate of Paris, to sudden loss of a part of its framework from which, without any apparent cause, and without it being possible to prevent it, the sap suddenly withdraws, or which is struck dead by an engorgement of extravasated gum. These fairly frequent accidents do not prevent many espaliers from growing very productive old apricot trees. The generous nature of this tree quickly makes up for its losses with vigorous sprays that emerge from the old wood below the stricken part, and the gaps in the framework are filled in no time. Apricot tree branches - This tree sets fruit of its own accord; its two-year-old branches bear both woody shoots and flower buds, both supported on a sort of very strong bracket (fig. 74). These branches remain fertile for several years, after which they usually wither and die. On full-wind trees, they should only be touched to prune them when they are dead, leaving nature to develop new ones. On espaliered apricot trees, superfluous or misplaced buds are removed as soon as they are born; buds that tend to grow too far forward are contained by moderate pruning, pinching and trellising; a certain number of small branches, 10 to 15 centimetres long, are always formed, loaded with fruit buds only; these branches are to the apricot tree what bunches are to the peach tree; they are not pruned; fruits follow one another on these short branches, for several years without interruption. Pruning and management of plum trees. - Once established on three or four good branches, and left to its own devices, this tree naturally takes on a regular shape, with a fairly high head if it belongs to a species with oblong fruit and hard wood, and a spread, rounded head if it is of a variety with round fruit and soft wood, such as the Reine Claude. The pruFig. 74. Flowering branch Apricot trees are less prone to losing parts of their framework through sudden death or gum engorgement. It also easily repairs its losses through the vigor of its vegetation. The most sought-after plum species, contrary to what happens with apricot trees, are better espaliered than in the open air; but even the best plums are never sufficiently valuable for plum trees to be given part of the available surface area on walls with good exposure. As a result, plum trees are only grown in espaliers by way of exception, in the gardens of a few enthusiasts who make a special case for plums. The pruning and management of espalier plum trees is very similar to the pruning and management of espalier apricot trees. Cherry pruning and management. - Full-wind cherryFig. 75. It is hardy and vigorous and requires no particular direction, once its head is formed on four branches that are as equal as possible (fig. 75). Espaliered cherry. - In the vicinity of towns, it is often very advantageous to devote part of the wall surface to a few cherry trees of the earliest and most esteemed species; the fruit of these trees, which are ten to fifteen days ahead of fruit of the same species harvested from trees in the open air, sell very well, and the cherry tree adequately pays the rent for the space it occupies on the espalier. It can be trained either as a very wide fan, like the apricot tree, or as a simple palmette, like the peach tree, by subjecting it to the same pruning, to which its vegetation lends itself quite readily. When space is at a premium, the arms of the framework, which are more numerous and closer together than those of the apricot and peach trees, do not need to be pruned for extension; the arms can be allowed to grow without 17. shortening them; it is enough to ensure that they remain as equal as possible; these arms fill themselves with small fruit-bearing branches along their entire length, and their fertility is virtually inexhaustible. Pruning and vine training. - It is impossible to find even the remotest analogy between the nature of the fruit of the vine and that of the peach tree. However, by a remarkable coincidence, the vegetation of the vine and that of the peach tree are exactly the same, and follow the same progression from point to point, so that the pruning of these two fruit trees is based on the same principles. To convince yourself of this, you need to carefully observe an unpruned annual vine shoot, and see how it behaves when left to its own devices. Natural vine vegetation. - The eyes of the vine are born on the shoot, in the axil of a leaf; they are known by the special name of "bourres", because they are surrounded by a thick, russet-colored down or "bourre", which helps to protect them from the effects of frost. In spring, when the temperature is sufficiently mild, the bud opens to produce an annual bud, or shoot. Like the fruiting branch of the peach tree, once it has borne fruit, it can never bear fruit again, no matter how long it has been in existence. As the shoot lengthens, the bunches on its lower part blossom, followed by grapes. After the second movement of the sap, in August, most of the shoot passes from the herbaceous to the woody state. When it is only imperfectly so, which happens when summer and autumn have not brought their normal quota of warmth and fine days, the grape ripens badly or not at all; this is why the winegrower rightly says that the vine must ripen its wood at the same time as its fruit. At the end of the year, the vine shoot, left to its own devices, bears downwards a number of more or less ripe bunches of grapes, topped by a branch that is usually very long, woody at the base, herbaceous at the top. During the summer, a wad is formed in the axil of each leaf. In the spring of the following year, each of these buds, if the shoot has not been pruned, opens to produce a bud whose annual vegetation reproduces all the phases just described. But, as the shoot has a very large number of buds to feed, some abort, others can only grow to a mediocre length; only a few of them bear one or two seedlings whose very light-colored seeds cannot reach the normal volume of their species. If the experiment were extended for another year, the shoots would be even more numerous, even shorter, and the harvest would be reduced to a few grapes. Pruning is therefore just as necessary for vines as it is for grapevines; its purpose on both of these fruit trees is to create replacement branches for those which, having once borne fruit, can never bear fruit again, and from which we must consequently seek to obtain eyes or buds capable of giving fertile shoots, if we want to have any harvest at all the following year. Vine pruning. - Whatever the form in which the vine is trained, pruning is always based on the same principle; it infallibly gives the same result. In winter, when there is no frost, when the leaves have completely fallen off the vine, and when the vegetation is as asleep as it can be, each shoot is cut back over the two eyes closest to its base; these eyes will be more or less productive shoots in the spring. Since the vine only has to feed a number of eyes proportionate to its strength, it produces regular harvests, and the grapes retain all the qualities specific to their species. In this way, the shoots are born successively on a first shoot which served as their starting point, and which is called the spur, just as, on the branches of a peach tree, the spur supports the annual branches or small branches which alone can bear fruit: the identity in this respect could not be more perfect. As the vine gets older, the spur, after having supplied a large number of fertile shoots, ends up being too long, so that the grapes are formed too far from the main branch of the vine on which the spur is inserted. The gardener has to watch carefully for the birth of an inferior eye on the spur, which always appears from time to time; he pulls the spur back over this eye at winter pruning, and at the sacrifice of a year's or two's harvest, the spur is rejuvenated, without the overall harvest being very noticeably diminished, because the spurs are only rejuvenated one after the other if necessary. Summer pruning. - When the vegetation of vines properly pruned in winter follows its regular course, the bunches flower in June and the grapes are formed immediately after flowering. However, in very fertile soil, or when the summer is particularly favorable to vine shoot growth, the shoots continue to overextend, drawing most of the sap to themselves. The vine then "sinks", as the saying goes, meaning that most of the flowers don't produce grapes, and some of the bunches abort and turn into tendrils, reducing the harvest to nothing. This is also known as summer pruning, and must be repeated when the eyes below the cut have opened into false buds. If the gardener were to allow these false buds to grow as they wish, they would delay the ripening of the grapes, which would lack flavor and quality. Even in the best-governed espalier vineyard, in the best soil and under the most favorable climatic conditions, the vine shoot should always be kept in check by disbudding once the grapes are well formed; after the top of the shoot has been cut back, not only will the grapes grow bigger and ripen more fully, but the eyes at the bottom of the shoot, on which the winter pruning should be based, will become stronger and better prepared for the following year's harvest. Vine management. - Since the beginning of this century, there has been a complete reform in the way vines are trained to produce table grapes. In the Paris climate, the vines that produce the best dessert grapes only produce really good fruit under espalier and counter-espalier conditions. Everyone knows the justly-deserved reputation of the Fontainebleau chasselas, the largest quantity of which is produced near Fontainebleau, in the village of Thomery. Since the superiority of the Thomery gardeners' method of espalier vine training cannot be disputed, and since good horticultural treatises have made knowledge of this method commonplace, the entire Fig. 76. Vines à la Thomery. ionde started to grow vines, not in the same way everywhere, but according to the same principles, which has greatly improved the production of good table grapes. Thomery shape (fig. 76). The vine shoot used as a base for this type of vine training is first brought by successive prunings to the height where its two horizontal arms are to be started, on the trellis of the espalier or counter-espalier. If we were to take advantage of the natural vigor of this vine shoot to bring it to this height in a single spurt, the wood of its upper part would not be well enough balanced, and the shoots on which we would have to prune to create the two arms of the trellis would not have enough Fig. 77. Vertical cordon vine. By asking only a moderate amount of growth from the shoot each year, the vine at the point where it is to bifurcate is I robust, and the pruning is based on two shoots as vigorous as the gardener may wish. These two treads give rise to 9 two shoots which are lowered to the right and left, and which, by successive prunings, always based on the same principle, are extended very evenly to 1 meter or 1.50 meters at most, from the bifurcation. When the vine, planted on the north side of an espalier wall, is introduced through a hole in the masonry, on the southern surface of the wall, its arms or horizon cords R rates are established on the right and left, in the same way. The above contains the key to the entire Thomery method of vine training, which anyone can apply without error. On an espalier wall of any height, cords of vines à la Thomery are staggered one above the other, the first 30 centimetres above ground level, the others 50 centimetres apart. The wooden or wire trellis is laid out so as to be able to conveniently trellis the cords or framework arms of the vine, and the annual shoots responsible for the harvest. The most important point is that the canes, on each of which two shoots are allowed to develop every year, should be spaced as evenly as possible, and not too far apart. With the Thomery method, there can be no question of these huge vines covering immense surfaces, or running in cordons of indeterminate length. It's easy to see that, when vines are allowed to acquire these exaggerated dimensions, the equal distribution of sap and spurs is no longer practicable. The Thomery-style arrangement of rows of vines on an espalier, which the rows cover from top to bottom, means that the vines have to be planted very close together; this prevents them from being carried away by useless wood, and their grapes are much better for it. Vertical cordon vineyard (fig. 77). - The layout of walls to be planted with espaliered vines does not always allow us to adopt the Thomery shape, although it is obviously the best. Based on the same principle, vines can be trained with the same ease in vertical rows, which are allowed to grow slowly so as to space their spurs appropriately, and which are pruned, disbudded, trellised and managed according to the principles set out above, principles which are easy to apply and infallible in their results. The cultivation of vines has undergone a complete revolution thanks to the recent work of Doctor Jules Guyot, applicable to vines in gardens as well as in large vineyards. Instead of the usual vine training, each vine is pruned in winter to leave a shoot with all its buds, which alone will bear the current year's harvest. In spring, this shoot is trained horizontally on a wire, forty or fifty centimetres from the ground. The other buds, as they develop, are trained straight onto a stile or vertical wire. This arrangement is as easy to adopt in a garden as in a vineyard. Trees trained with a single fruit-bearing shoot are just as productive and take up just as little space in the garden as trees trained horizontally; mats can be laid in the event of late frosts to preserve budding, or in times of storms that may be accompanied by hail to preserve the harvest. Isolated vines can be planted in rows behind the flowerbeds, alternating with apple or pear trees in horizontal rows. The result is a series that is as productive as it is pleasing to the eye, on which pears, apples and grapes of the best species are harvested in abundance. CHAPTER XXXV Shrubs with edible fruit. - Fig tree. Shrubs with edible fruit. - Currants. - Multiplication. - Cuttings. - Marcottes. - Sowing. - Pruning and training. - Head shape on a single stem. - Counterpallet or candelabra shape. - Species and varieties. - Gonduin currant. - Cherry currant. - Thorny or gooseberry; - bush; - palmette; - fan. - Species and varieties. - Raspberry. - Pruning and management. - Species and varieties. - Fig tree. - Multiplication. - In the South of France. - Cuttings in pots. - Pruning and management. - Wintering in the Paris climate. - Species and varieties. The products of edible fruit bushes play a fairly important role in the domestic economy; the confectionery and distilling industries cannot do without them, and they are widely consumed. Near large population centers, the cultivation of these shrubs is in itself a lucrative branch of gardening; everywhere else, it can be considered an indispensable accessory to any fruit garden. This series of shrubs includes currants, gooseberries, raspberries and figs. Although in southern countries the fig tree is a medium-sized tree, it is really only a large shrub in the average French climate. Currants. - Multiplication. - There is no shrub whose propagation is quicker and easier than that of the currant. When grown as a bush, each clump produces numerous rooted offshoots every year, which are ready-made subjects for renewing plantations. If these offshoots are not numerous enough to meet needs, we lay down part of the lower branches and cover half their length with soil; they root by layering with the greatest of ease; they are weaned at planting time, in the spring of the following year. In nurseries, cuttings are taken from the annual pruning of redcurrant bushes; these cuttings root very quickly and almost anywhere, and are particularly well suited to cool, light soil. The best time to take redcurrant cuttings is at the beginning of spring, when the bush resumes its annual growth. Redcurrant seedlings can also be sown in spring, in light soil in a shady location, but only in the hope of obtaining new sub-varieties. As it is important for the seedlings to be as perfectly ripe as possible, a number of the best bunches are left to dry on the branch. When the currants are completely dry, they are soaked in water for a few hours, then crushed between the fingers, and the seeds separated from the pulp by washing. These seeds can be sown in autumn, or kept dry for sowing the following spring. Pruning and management. - In most gardens, the currant bush is not pruned regularly; it is simply left to grow into bushy, irregular bushes, and branches are removed if their productive force seems exhausted, or if they are dying of old age. However, professional gardeners who grow large numbers of currants for sale regularly prune them in winter, at the same time as they winter-prune all their fruit trees. The redcurrant bud, given over to the natural course of its vegetation, begins to produce in its second year; it is in full ratio by its third leaf, and begins to wear out by the fourth. Accordingly, if you want to keep redcurrants at their most fertile, you need to prune to encourage the birth of young replacement branches, so that you always have shrubs made up of two- and three-year-old branches, the most productive of all. It's because of this pruning practice that gardeners in villages around Paris, where this shrub is widely cultivated, sell redcurrant branches full of ripe fruit to Parisians on country feast days. These are the three- or four-year-old branches that should disappear during winter pruning, and which are removed in summer because they can be disposed of with advantage. In both fruit and landscape gardens, gooseberries are planted in clumps, not as bushes, but as rounded heads on a single stem. Redcurrants can easily be grown in this form by propagating them with cuttings made from one-year-old twigs, cut in an upright position. The eyes along the entire length of these branches are removed, with the exception of the three closest to the top; the terminal eye is also removed. In this way, the cutting becomes a young currant bush, with a head of three branches, each pruned over two good eyes. Within two years, the head is sufficiently supplied with fruit-bearing branches, which are removed, like bushes, before they are exhausted, and replaced by buds which pruning brings out from their base in more than sufficient numbers. The fruit harvested from currants grown in this way is always very clean, having been born far enough from the ground not to be splashed by it during the heaviest rains. Currants trained on a single stem are very productive and last indefinitely, so to speak. Trellises separating different divisions of a garden can also be planted with currants. In this case, the best shape to give them is that of a candelabra. This shape is obtained by pruning a redcurrant cutting over two eyes, to the desired height. The buds born from these eyes are inclined, one to the right, the other to the left, and trellised horizontally on the trellis. Vertical branches are formed by subsequent pruning on the horizontal cords or arms, at a distance of 15 to 20 centimetres from each other. When their fertility is exhausted, these branches are replaced by buds sprouting from their base. Candelabra-shaped currants are long-lasting and graceful (fig. 78). Fig. 78. Redcurrant candelabra. Species and varieties. - In addition to the two old species, one with red fruit, the other with white, seedlings have produced one with light pink fruit, a little too acidic, though of good quality. The Gonduin and cherry currants, two species much sought-after as dessert fruit because of the extraordinary volume of berries in their bunches, are due to the same propagation method. Nursery catalogs list numerous sub-varieties with red, pink and white fruit, most of which are indistinguishable from one another, with the exception of those mentioned above. Gooseberry. - This shrub multiplies like the currant, either by cutting or by separating its rooted suckers. If you wish to propagate it by sowing, in the hope of acquiring good new varieties, you should only sow the seeds of fruits whose skin bursts as a result of over-ripening. These seeds are sown under the same conditions as those for currants. For a long time, thorny currants were left to grow as they pleased, without any care other than cutting back dead or exhausted branches. For some years now, it has been trained on trellises, either in a palmette or fan shape, a form to which its vegetation lends itself perfectly. By giving the thorny currant this shape, you can harvest the fruit without pricking your fingers; it's bigger and tastier than fruit of the same species harvested from thick bushes, which are difficult for air and light to penetrate. Species and varieties. - The two common species, one with yellowish-green fruit when ripe, the other purplish-red, were for a long time the only ones known and cultivated in France. By persistent sowing and careful cultivation, English and Belgian growers have conquered a large number of varieties of various shades, with walnut-sized fruit. These varieties, which have a stronger flavor than the common species, are now widespread in all gardens; they are both very rustic and very productive. To avoid having to wash some of the gooseberries, which fall off in the slightest wind when ripe, it's a good idea to lay a bed of clean, fresh straw at the foot of the thorny currant bushes when the fruit ripens. Raspberry. - The European climate produces no fruit with such a delicate fragrance as the raspberry. Thanks to a happy elasticity of temperament, the raspberry can grow and bear fruit in a wide range of climates.

Like the bramble, of which it seems to be only a variety, the raspberry tree is found in both northern and southern Europe; it is in temperate climates that it produces the most abundant and fragrant fruit. All soils are suitable for the raspberry, in the sense that it does not refuse to grow even in the most ungrateful ones; but it is only very productive in good soils. When you want to prolong the raspberry harvest, you plant raspberry bushes with southern exposure, and others with northern exposure; the fruit of the latter is no less abundant than that of the former, but they ripen only when the harvest of the former is exhausted. Raspberry pruning and management. - Although a good raspberry plantation can last indefinitely, if it is not denied a sufficient ration of manure every two years, this sub-shrub is perennial only through its roots, which produce annual stems every year. These stems die after bearing a single crop; they are replaced by the year's suckers or offshoots, which in turn die after bearing a single crop. Raspberry bushes are planted either in clumps, staggered 40 to 50 centimetres apart in all directions, or in rows spaced 40 to 50 centimetres apart. Raspberry bushes may be planted 20 to 25 centimetres apart. Raspberries can only be propagated by separating suckers, which are always produced in overabundant numbers; they can also be easily propagated by cuttings. In row plantations, which are the most advantageous for large-scale cultivation, cross-pieces consisting of long, thin poles supported by stakes 60 centimetres above the ground are established; all the raspberry stems are trellised along these poles, giving them a slight curve that favours fruit production. Every year, from the first days of February, raspberry bushes are pruned. This involves cutting back the upper part of the previous year's shoots, so as to reduce their length to 90 centimetres or 1 metre. If retained, they would needlessly absorb part of the sap, to the detriment of the eyes in the middle of the stem, which alone can produce an abundance of beautiful raspberries. To avoid losing some of the fruit, which doesn't adhere well to its support when ripe, the soil between the rows of raspberries should be covered with fresh straw. At the start of winter, the most vigorous shoots are reserved for production of the following year's crop; the others are removed and used, if necessary, to renew or maintain the plantations. Raspberry plantations require shallow ploughing with an iron-tined fork at the onset of winter, when superfluous suckers have been removed. It is also at this time that the fertilizer needed every two years is ploughed in. Species and varieties. - Only four distinct varieties of raspberry are grown in gardens: the small-fruited or woodland raspberry, the large-fruited red raspberry, the large-fruited white raspberry and the remontant or four-season raspberry. Those that appear in fairly large numbers in English growers' catalogs, including the Falstolff raspberry, are barely distinct sub-varieties of the large-fruited red. Fig tree. - The fruit of the fig tree plays an important role in the diets of the peoples of the Midi, but mainly as dried fruit; little is eaten fresh; the species and varieties whose fruit is best for drying are the most widely cultivated for this reason in the departments along the Mediterranean coast. To the north of these departments, figs are sought after only for fresh eating. As far as the Loire valley, from Roanne to Nantes, the fig tree needs no protection from the cold; it overwinters in the open air in well-sheltered places; it is mainly planted in large courtyards and gardens at the inner angle formed by two walls, one of which faces south. In the climate of Paris, it requires a more effective guarantee against the rigors of winter. Fig tree plantations in the south of France are maintained using seedlings obtained from cuttings and raised in nurseries. For some years now, fig trees have been cut in large pots, where they form an abundance of foliage and grow more rapidly than cuttings taken from the ground. Around Paris, the small number of fig tree varieties grown as bushes in the best-sheltered locations, at the foot of hills facing due south, multiply only by separating their rooted suckers. These suckers can be used directly in plantations, without the need for nursery cultivation. The best fig trees propagated by layering are available from professional nurseries. Planted in good, deep, light soil, they soon form thick bushes, usually leaving only three sprigs per clump; the others are removed even when they are not needed for planting. Even in the Paris climate, the fig tree can bear two harvests a year; the second only matures in years with exceptionally favorable temperatures. In ordinary years, if you have a large enough number of fig trees, you sacrifice the first harvest of some of them by removing the half-formed fruit; this speeds up the formation of autumn figs, which can almost always become, if not perfectly ripe, at least edible before the end of the summer. Fig tree pruning is reduced to cutting off the top shoots of fruit-bearing branches, and periodically removing tired or exhausted stems. In large-scale cultivation, fig tree clumps are planted in staggered rows, four meters apart in all directions. Every year, before the first cold spells arrive, the fig bushes are tied together with wicker ties, then carefully laid down to the bottom of an open pit in very dry soil; they are maintained in this position with the earth extracted from the pits, which is refilled to prevent frost from reaching them. In this position, fig trees grown in the open fields of Argenteuil and neighboring towns only freeze in excessively harsh winters. When a similar disaster occurs, the stumps don't freeze; they give offspring the following year, enabling the plantations to be repaired. In gardens where you wish to have a few fig trees for household consumption, they should only be planted close to a wall facing south, and should not be allowed to exceed a height of one and a half to two meters. Instead of bending them all the way down to the ground and burying them to overwinter, you can then simply bundle the stems together with wicker ties, and wrap them in a thick jacket of fresh straw, held together with twisted hay ties. This method, which would be impractical for large-scale cultivation because it would be impossible to obtain the necessary quantity of straw without enormous expense, is the best way of keeping a small number of fig trees frost-free. In spring, fig trees should only be uncovered when the last of the late cold returns are no longer to be feared. Species and varieties. - Only two species of fig tree are grown for fresh consumption, one with white fruit inside, pale green outside, the other with purple fruit inside and out. The white fig is the better and more widely cultivated variety, with two sub-varieties, one with round fruit, known as the white round fig, and the other with oblong fruit, which gardeners call the long white fig. Both are vigorous and highly productive. CHAPTER XXXVI Cultivation and rejuvenation of fruit trees. Cultivation care required for fruit trees. - Manuring. - Fresh manure harmful to stone fruit trees. - Potting soil. - Compost. - Watering. - Ponding. - Their useful effect. - Cleaning. - Lime milk for old fruit trees. - Scabbing. - Ring-shaped incision. - Fruit thinning. - Removal of part of the leaves from peach trees. - Vines. - Rejuvenation of trees with pome fruits - By grafting onto the main branches - By pruning. - Crown grafting. - Rejuvenation of stone fruit trees. Having followed the fruit tree from the earliest stages of its vegetative life, and having led it in the form best suited to its nature, until it is in full strength and bearing, it remains to describe the various cultivation treatments it requires to prolong the duration of its productive strength and to rejuvenate it, when it is exhausted by a long age. These include fertilizing, watering, ponds, cleaning, arching, ring pruning and rejuvenation. Fertilization. - Like all cultivated land, fruit garden soil retains its fertility only through periodic fertilization. However, the gardener would be making a mistake that would be highly detrimental to his fruit trees, if the soil in which their roots grow were smoked indiscriminately. In general, stone fruit trees, whose wood is gummy, do not tolerate the immediate contact of their roots with fermenting manure. Anyone who, for example, makes the mistake of burying horse, horned-beast or sheep manure on the roots of peach, apricot and cherry espalier trees, as 18 it is removed from under these animals, will lose most of his trees in the same year; all of them will be affected by gum and red blight, and those that do not immediately succumb to these two diseases will remain languid and unproductive for several years. When the soil in the bed in front of the espaliers is tired, and it is deemed necessary to revive its natural fertility, it should only be fertilized with compost from old, broken layers. The best potting soil for this purpose is the one that is the most past its prime, as gardeners say, i.e., the most completely denatured, so that it retains no trace of its primitive state. If you're growing large stone fruit trees, either espaliered or in the open air, there's no better manure to give them than compost made up of alternating beds of good garden soil and all the plant debris that comes from weeding the vegetable garden squares and dressing the vegetables. The compost heap grows throughout the summer; it is dismantled and reworked with a spade to mix the elements well, once or twice during the winter. In spring, the soil covering the roots of stone fruit trees is very gently tilled with an iron-tined fork. Some of the divided soil is removed and transferred to other squares, where it is replaced by an equal quantity of compost. Soil and compost have the property of maintaining the fertility of the soil without transmitting the seeds of any disease to stone fruit trees. Trees with stone fruit tolerate better the contact of their roots with fresh manure; however, it is better to give these trees, as well as vines, only half-decomposed fertilizers, which are the ones from which their vegetation benefits most completely. For espalier and counter-espalier vines, when they alone occupy the whole plot, the best fertilizer is herbaceous leaves and shoots, coarsely chopped with a spade and buried on the vine roots. This type of entirely plant-based fertilization maintains the soil's productive capacity and in no way alters the quality of the products of the vine, which produces the best dessert grapes. Watering and irrigation. - With the exception of the southern departments of France, where fruit tree plantations are exposed to five to six months of uninterrupted drought, and would dry out if not irrigated, fruit trees rarely need to be watered. However, it is useful to pour a bucket of water every two days at the foot of espaliered peach trees, when the drought continues into midsummer. It's a good idea to use only water that has been sitting for at least a day in underground barrels, and whose temperature has equilibrated with that of the surrounding soil. You always run the risk of causing some of the fruit on espaliered trees to fall off when you pour very cold water on their roots in soil heated by the rays of a scorching sun. When the fruit is half full-grown, espaliered fruit trees are occasionally given not watering, but "bassinages", i.e. sprays of fresh water distributed over the entire surface of the tree with a portable pump. Drenching removes the dust that clogs the leaf pores, helping to keep the trees in good health; it helps the fruit acquire its normal volume; and it prevents the fruit stalks from drying out and falling off before ripening. Don't hesitate to drench espaliers thoroughly in summer, as soon as eight to ten days have passed without a beneficial shower of rain. Cleaning. - The gardener is well advised to keep the bark of his trees perfectly clean at all times, especially that of espaliered trees. It's easiest to remove the bark from peach and apricot trees without damaging the buds, and to clean both sides of the branches and the trellis supporting them, as well as the surface of the wall. In this way, as far as possible, we prevent the multiplication of harmful insects; we encourage transpiration, so necessary to the health of the trees; and we give them a vigor of vegetation that is never observed in trees for which this care of cleanliness is neglected. Older fruit trees, especially those growing in more or less damp soil, are covered with grey, white or yellowish moss and lichen, which must be removed at the same time, after a good gentle spring rain. If their bark is chapped and overgrown with moss, at the onset of winter, as soon as the leaves have fallen, the trees should be whitewashed from top to bottom with a whitewash similar to that used to whiten ceilings. This very flimsy coating will not withstand the action of winter rains and alternating frosts and thaws; it will have carried off part of the old bark and with it the insects, their eggs and larvae, which are always lodged in large numbers in the cracks. This easy and very inexpensive operation immediately revives the vegetation of old pear and apple trees in all forms, and makes them produce abundant harvests. Scabbing. - This outdated procedure, long since abandoned by the most skilful practitioners, is mentioned here only because they may find themselves having to manage fruit gardens whose trees, long badly pruned and poorly managed, resist the ordinary means of bringing them to fruit by pruning, pinching and disbudding. Arboring is only practicable on large pyramid-shaped trees whose branches have been allowed to get carried away and produce a profusion of useless wood. In this case, once the tree has been pruned back, the main branches are bent into an arc, with their ends attached either to the trunk or to the larger branches below. By counteracting the natural flow of sap, the arching keeps it at the bottom of the branches, giving rise to fruit buds. It also has the serious disadvantage of deforming the tree and hastening its exhaustion. If, in order to bring vigorous, but previously misguided trees back to fruit quickly, we are forced to resort to bowing, we should stop using this method as soon as it is no longer essential and we have obtained the desired effect. Ring incision. - The annular incision is mentioned here only because it can give rise to experiments which are not devoid of interest. It consists in detaching a very small ring of bark from the branch of a fruit tree or a vine shoot, with the aim of promoting fruit enlargement. This aim is not always achieved, but it seems that it can be, and indeed sometimes has been. This alone is enough to recommend that gardening enthusiasts multiply ring incisions under a variety of conditions. When done with due care, using cutting pliers specially designed for the purpose, ring-shaped incisions can give certain fruits extraordinary volume and prevent grape run-off, without in any way compromising the health of the fruit trees or vines. De-stemming. - In large fruit gardens, pruning is a very delicate operation, involving the removal of part of the leaves from vines and peach trees, in order to give the fruit a lighter color and enable it to fully benefit from the sun's heat and light to ripen. Pruning is only ever necessary on fruit trees and espaliered vines; in years of great abundance, it should be preceded by thinning out the fruit. It's a bad idea to leave too much fruit on espaliered peach and apricot trees. If the same trees are planted in full wind, hurricanes and insect bites take care of dropping the overabundant fruit, which almost never happens on the espalier. When the fruit has reached about a third of its normal volume, a portion should be removed from all overloaded branches, and only as much should be left. 18. branches, leaving only enough to make a good ordinary crop. By doing so, you not only spare the tree, which excessive production would exhaust before its time, but you also ensure that the fruit retained retains all the qualities proper to its species. When vines grow in cool, very fertile soil, the bunches often become enormous and the grapes, packed too tightly together, ripen only imperfectly. To prevent this, one in three grapes is removed with a pair of sharp scissors, when the grapes have about a quarter of their normal volume. This removal produces no appreciable reduction in the harvest; the retained seeds, not being hindered in their development, become very large, and as they receive air and light freely in all directions, they become as ripe and as sweet as they can be. Peach trees, when the time comes for the peaches to ripen, must be stripped of their leaves in order to uncover the fruit and expose it to the sun; this operation is carried out twice, eight or ten days apart: if all the leaves to be removed were removed at once, both the fruit and the tree would suffer. Vines are also pruned twice, as the grapes approach maturity. Shucking gives the grapes more color and quality than they would have if they remained hidden under the vine's ample foliage. Replanting fruit trees. - When a gardener, who has himself reached a fairly advanced age, sees old fruit trees that he has tended and cultivated for many years withering away through exhaustion, and which he regards almost as old friends, he feels a real sense of sorrow and spares no effort to prolong their life by reviving their productive strength. The resources at his disposal for this purpose are quite numerous, and vary according to the nature of the trees to be rejuvenated. Rejuvenation of trees with pome fruits. - The trees the gardener should be most concerned about rejuvenating are the best espalier-grown pear trees. After a certain length of time, the main branches of these trees cease to produce woody buds. At this point, their fruit production is exhausted and cannot be replaced; for every fruit-bearing branch began as a wood-bearing branch, and the moment the production of new wood stops in a fruit-bearing tree, it is in decline. For a few more years, it will flower abundantly, but it won't have the strength to tie its fruit, and all this luxury of flowering will be sterile, and then the arms of the framework will die successively. To cure the disease, you mustn't wait until it has progressed too far. At winter pruning, the arms of the partly exhausted tree are stripped of all fruiting and other branches, converting them into long, completely bare poles. From distance to distance, grafts taken from trees of the same species in full vegetative vigor are placed along the entire length of the arms. The energy with which these grafts suck in the sap imbues the tree with new life; young buds with well-formed wood are born on all sides, and it still has a long future of robust, productive plant life ahead of it. When this most rational method of rejuvenation is too long overdue to be practicable, we can simply resort to "recepage", folding the framework arms back onto the trunk and waiting for buds to emerge that can serve as the starting point for a new framework. This expectation is often misplaced; after exhausting its remaining strength by producing a few delicate branches, the tree succumbs and cannot be rejuvenated. But it's a very different story when, after "ivoir recepé", the base of the frame's branches are grafted with crown grafts. In this way, each graft that takes over gives a well-constituted shoot, so that in a few years the tree can be completely re-established and have as much future as it had when it was planted. The above applies to both apple and pear trees. Berrying of stone fruit trees. - When an old peach tree is exhausted, since the natural life span of this tree is never very long, we must not try to rejuvenate it; it is better to sacrifice it and replace it, and this is what professional gardeners do. But if you're an amateur who cares about your old peach trees, and you know that it's the older ones that produce the best peaches, even if they don't produce many, you can try to rejuvenate them by receiving them. The peach tree is reluctant to produce young shoots on old branches, to reject old wood, as the saying goes; but it nevertheless produces them often enough for us to always take the chance when it comes to prolonging the life of old peach trees that we don't want to sacrifice. If you succeed, it's almost never completely; the young wood obtained is never abundant enough to give the peach tree a regular structure; but it can still, for several years, give fair harvests of excellent peaches. Plum, apricot and cherry trees rejuvenate themselves in the sense that the natural energy of their vegetation provides them with the means to replace dead or exhausted branches with young wood for a long time. From the moment they lack the strength to repair these losses, they can be considered as lost; no practicable procedure can be successfully employed to rejuvenate them. CHAPTER XXXVII Forced cultivation of fruit trees and pineapples. Forced cultivation of fruit trees. - Fruit trees forced in situ. - Peach trees forced on site in the temporary greenhouse. - Day and night temperatures. - Part of leaves removed. - Apricot trees forced with peach trees. - Fruit trees forced into greenhouse. - Vines forced. - Stocks planted outside the greenhouse to be forced. - Vine forced on site. Forced vine in temperate greenhouse. - Forced dwarf fruit trees. - Pre-cultivation in pots. - Pineapple cultivation. - Multiplication of eyelets and crowns. - Length of pineapple growth. - Suitable temperature. - Root amputation. - Open-ground cultivation in the greenhouse. -Sowing pineapple seeds. - Duration of seedling growth. - First fruiting. The forced cultivation of fruit trees, when well managed, can be a source of considerable profit for the professional gardener, and a source of varied pleasure for the amateur gardener; moreover, it is of real interest only in temperate and northern countries; in the Hidi climate, this branch of gardening becomes completely useless and is not practiced. For France in particular, forced cultivation of fruit trees begins at our northern border 11 and stops at the Loire valley. Fruit trees can be forced either on site or in the greenhouse. In Paris, this method of cultivation applies only to espaliered vineyards and orchards; trees that are still young, but nevertheless vigorous and in full bearing, are forced into place. These trees are pruned, trellised, pinched and disbudded as if they were to flower and bear fruit in the open air. Peach trees forced into place. - From the end of January, or 1 at the latest during the second half of February, movable glass frames are hung from the top of the wall lined with espaliered peach trees, and held inclined in a fixed positiono by means of a board, placed on the field, on the edge of which the lower edge of the frames is placed, so as to form a single-sided temporary greenhouse, the width of which at ground level is equal to that of the flowerbed in front of the espalier wall. Both ends are closed by two plank partitions, one of which has a door. Near the end opposite the door, a heating unit is set up outside, with heat pipes running inside the temporary greenhouse on brick supports. When a large number of espaliered peach trees are heated in this way, there's no more advantageous appliance than the thermosiphon; to heat just a few, a simple cast-iron stove can be used; In this case, however, during the often very cold nights of February and March, you have to stay up all night to feed the stove's fire, which must give a gentle, uninterrupted heat; whereas, when using the thermosiphon, this heat is produced by lighting the fire in the evening, without having to worry about it during the night. The thermometer should not mark more than ten to twelve degrees centigrade during the first week. As flowering approaches, which develops rapidly under the protection of the temporary greenhouse, the temperature is gradually raised to 15, then 20 degrees centigrade. When the temperature is mild outside, the fire is left off during the day; the sun is enough to maintain the desired temperature inside the greenhouse; more or less fire is always needed in the evening, to avoid cooling during the night: this is the crucial point on which the success of the operation depends. Don't forget that when peaches are at about half their normal volume, a cold night is all it takes to make most of them fall off. You need to start removing some of the leaves that prevent the peaches from coming into contact with the light, as soon as they are about their full size, so that they can color properly; forced peaches always have a little less color than peaches ripened in the open air. The frames dismantled and tightened after harvesting forced peaches can last a very long time, provided they are I well maintained. As for forced peach trees, although most treatises recommend letting them rest after they have been forced, experience shows that, if they have only been asked for a moderate quantity of beautiful peaches when forced on the spot, they are no more tired after being forced than if they had not been; they are, on the contrary, in better condition than those of the same species, whose spring vegetation may have been more or less thwarted by the sudden variations in temperature so frequent at this time of year in the Paris climate; they can therefore be heated in the temporary greenhouse for several years running, without the slightest inconvenience. Apricot trees of the best species, grown in espaliers 4 alongside peach trees, can be subjected to the same kind of forced cultivation; but they are less amenable to it than peach trees; it is very important to strip them of a good part of their fruit when they have reached a quarter of their size, when they are too full. By leaving the whole crop on the branches, the gardener runs the risk of seeing them die suddenly at the moment when the shell of the stone should become woody, so that for wanting to have too much, he could lose not only the crop, but the trees themselves. The forced cultivation of espaliered trees, as described above, is so inexpensive and easy to implement, that it is surprising that it is not widely practiced. The high price of peaches on the big city markets, a month or six weeks before the natural ripening time of this excellent fruit, provides the gardener with an adequate return on his efforts and advances. What's more, he can use the soil of the bed converted into a temporary greenhouse, without harming the forced trees, and obtain strawberries, peas, cauliflower and tomato plants very early on, so as to make a very profitable profit. Fruit trees forced into the greenhouse. - When building a greenhouse designed exclusively for forced cultivation of fruit trees, fill the flat strip along the greenhouse wall with the soil best suited to the vegetation needs of the trees to be forced into it. This wall is then filled, not with young trees that need to be trained and raised in the greenhouse, but with fully-formed trees which, with the precautions indicated in the description of fruit tree planting, are highly transplantable and immensely productive. The pruning and management of these trees in the forcene greenhouse is no different from the pruning and management of the same trees in the open air. Heating starts at a more or less advanced stage, depending on the desired degree of earliness of the fruit harvest; usually, the forcing greenhouse is divided by glass partitions into several compartments which can be heated one after the other; this arrangement means that the forcing fruit does not all reach maturity at the same time, which is a favorable condition for placement when the fruit is to be sold. Cultivation care is the same as for espaliered trees grown under the shelter of the temporary greenhouse. After harvesting, keep the greenhouse panels open at all times; you can even remove them altogether, and only put them back in place towards the end of the summer season. Prolonged contact with the outside air fortifies the forced trees, and sets them up to produce abundant harvests every year. Forced vine. - Vines that are to produce forced grapes indoors are usually planted outside the greenhouse. The vines are planted in the bed, along the wall supporting the greenhouse glass, and inserted through holes made for the purpose in the macom nery of this wall; they are led in cords on thick wire, parallel to the glass. Pruning, training, trimming, thinning out over-tight bunches and removing shoots around the time of ripening are all carried out in the forcing greenhouse according to the same principles as for cordon vines grown in the open air. In winter, during hard frosts, it's a good idea to cover the soil in the outside flowerbed with litter or long manure, which supports the roots of the vines grown in forced cultivation. Around Paris, professional gardeners who force vines to sell their produce do not force them into permanent greenhouses. The vines are planted at the foot of a south-facing slope. In the year when they are to be forced, a plank placed on the field at the top of the slope serves as a support for an inclined glazing system, which covers the vines very closely; a ditch, deep enough for the gardener to move around freely, runs in front of the vines, which are planted horizontally in two rows, very close to the ground; thermosiphon pipes heat the inside of this sort of long, low and narrow temporary greenhouse, which is therefore easy to set up and heat very economically: it's the least expensive way of forcing vines. The amateur gardener, guided by other considerations, introduces vines of the best species from outside, into a temperate greenhouse intended mainly for the cultivation of ornamental plants. In this greenhouse, he forms elegant arches which, without in any way harming the vegetation of exotic plants, the main object of his solicitude, give him an ample harvest of excellent grapes, and enable him to invite his friends to come and harvest in his greenhouse, at a time of year when there are still neither flowers nor fruit in the gardens. Forced dwarf fruit trees. - Several stone fruit trees, especially cherry, apricot and several plum trees, can easily be forced into the temperate greenhouse and produce very good quality, ripe fruit from May onwards. For this purpose, dwarf fruit trees are planted a year in advance, in fairly large pots filled with good ordinary garden soil, and are available in small cattails from any nursery. These trees, well cared for, watered and cultivated for a year in the open air, are successively introduced into the greenhouse, starting, for those that are to bear the first fruit, as soon as the leaves have fallen. The pots should be placed as close as possible to the windows, turned over frequently so that the trees receive light from all sides, and watered as much as necessary so that the soil they contain is constantly fresh, without excess moisture. Some watering is useful for the foliage of these trees, to keep it clean, and for the fruit, to encourage its growth. Dwarf trees forced into pots are very heavy; their small size means they can be placed on the table, where each guest can pick his or her own share of the dessert, helping to make the fruit taste better. Redcurrant and raspberry bushes, treated in the same way, are forced with the same ease; they produce ripe fruit at the same time. Pineapples. - Although the pineapple is the fruit not of a tree, but of a simple herbaceous plant, its cultivation must be included here because it is entirely forced, from the beginning until the fruit is fully ripe. The pineapple can be propagated by the eyelets at the bottom of its stem, by the tufts of leaves called crowns that crown the fruit, and by sowing its seeds. The first two methods are the only ones used in normal cultivation; seedlings are only used in the hope of obtaining new varieties. Carnations and crowns should not be planted as soon as they are detached; they are true cuttings which should be treated like all cuttings from succulent plants, allowing the cut at the heel to dry out in the air before planting, otherwise they may rot and fail to put down roots. Since pineapple cultivation is forced from one end to the other, it can be started in any season. To calculate the time at which the fruit will reach maturity, and to make this maturity coincide with consumption needs, it is necessary to be aware of the exact duration of its cultivation. It does not last, as most treatises say, for three whole years, but for eighteen months to two years, including two months of the first year, the whole of the second, and four to six months of the third. If the carnations are planted in the first week of November, they should be in pots filled with heather soil, immersed in a very hot layer, the heat of which is maintained by frequently renewed stoves. For the first winter, this layer is usually set up under a simple frame; the plants make little progress there, but they take root firmly and are ready to take on great growth when the fine weather returns. The temperature should not be allowed to fall below 20 degrees centigrade at night, and 25 degrees during the day. In spring, a new, equally hot layer is added, in which the pineapples, ready for rapid growth, are immersed until the pots can no longer contain the roots, which have become too voluminous. We then resort to a highly energetic treatment, which few other plants would withstand, and whose constant success proves the pineapple's essentially robust temperament. All the plant's roots are cut off at the collar, and the pineapple, after the wound has dried for twelve hours in the open air, is cut out again in a large pot filled with a mixture of potting soil, heather and good garden soil, in equal parts. The pots are then immersed, no longer in a warm layer, but in the tannin of a forcing greenhouse. For large-scale cultivation, very low greenhouses are specially built for pineapples, with just the right height for indoor use; the tarpaulin containing the pineapple pots is arranged so that the plants are as close to the glass as possible; pineapples can never have too much light or too much heat. Provided it is watered and sprinkled from time to time with a watering can with a sheaf of very fine holes, in the form of a very divided shower, the pineapple can be constantly exposed to a temperature of 35 to 40 degrees; it will only vegetate more vigorously. Pineapple's long leaves often need to be cleaned on both surfaces with a fine cloth pad or a slightly damp piece of sponge. Quite often, the plants are invaded by insects, which need to be killed with strong tobacco fumigations. Towards the end of summer, the pineapple, planted in November of the previous year, begins to develop its flowering stems; it is then in reed form, as the gardeners say. They don't all come up at the same time, even though they were planted at the same time, and it's to the gardener's advantage that this is so, otherwise all his pineapples would ripen at the same time, making placement less easy. When flowering is over and the fruit has formed, many gardeners subject the plants to a second complete cutting of the roots; this amputation is only necessary when the soil in the pots seems exhausted, and the pineapples are not growing vigorously enough. In this case, the pots are filled with new soil, and the pineapples, treated as for the first amputation, are placed back in the tanned soil, with only a slight delay in their growth. Following the English method, now adopted in France by the most skilful practitioners, rooted pineapples can be planted immediately after their first winter, in the soil of a 60 to 80-centimeter-deep tarpaulin, strongly heated by the thermosiphon. Here, they are as if they were in the ground, and must not suffer any further displacement or amputation of the roots. Watering should be plentiful throughout the formation of the fruit, unless, to satisfy his customers, the gardener finds it necessary to hasten ripening. All he has to do is keep the plants warm, and only water them as much as is necessary to prevent them from dying of thirst. Pineapples treated in this way ripen very quickly and are as good as their species requires; but they never reach their normal volume. If you want to try your hand at conquering a new, recommendable variety by sowing pineapple seeds, you need to be very patient. Seeds sown in spring in heathery soil soon sprout and produce a very weak plant that takes five long years to grow and produce its first fruit, which rarely lives up to the gardener's expectations. However, a fairly large number of very good pineapple sub-varieties are due to the persevering care zealously practised in England, Belgium and France since 1815, when pineapple cultivation, after a long interruption, was resumed in the greenhouses of Europe. CHAPTER XXXVIII Fruit trees grown in pots. - Fruit harvesting and preservation. Growing fruit trees in pots. - Gardens à la Rivers. - How to establish them. - Their advantages. - Parts of France where they can be useful. - Fruit gardens on terraces. - Fruit trees in groves. - Harvesting stone fruit. - Cherries. - Apricots. - Plums. - Peaches. - Careful brushing. - Harmful properties of their down. - Harvesting fruits with seeds. - A sure sign of the right time. - Fruit trees. - Fruitier à la Dombasle. - Grape preservation. - Hollow tin cylinders for this purpose. In recent years, the cultivation of fruit trees in pots has been applied in so many ways that it is essential to go into a few practical details, which are indispensable to those who find themselves in conditions where this way of growing fruit trees can be both pleasant and advantageous. The successes obtained in the cultivation of fruit trees, grown in columnar form and subjected to periodic root pruning, so that a fairly large number can be gathered on a very limited surface area, demonstrate first of all the possibility of making fruit trees of various species live and bear fruit, in sufficiently spacious pots, filled with very substantial soil, the fertility of which is maintained at its maximum by frequently repeated watering with liquid fertilizers. These trees, placed in pots a year or two after being grafted, are pruned very short, in columnar form; their spire is only allowed to grow to about half the height it would reach in the open ground. In this way, while they produce very little young wood, they carry a lot, and if they are not very durable, it costs little to replace them when they are exhausted. Rivers-style gardens. - A skilful English horticulturist, Mr. Rivers, has based a whole system of fruit gardens on the pot culture of fruit trees, for use by those who, in a severe climate, have only a very small space at their disposal. The first step is to surround with a living hedge, regularly trimmed in the shape of a wall, the square of garden you intend to devote to potted fruit trees. Stakes 20 to 25 centimetres higher than the hedge itself are placed at intervals in the hedge. These stakes should be strong enough to serve as a base for a very light wooden frame, but strong enough to be covered with straw mats in the form of a gable roof. In this sheltered space, the potted trees are arranged in three or four parallel rows, with internal paths in the center of the square and along the hedge, for service. During the summer months, the mats disappear and the trees are more or less in the open air; nevertheless, the protection they receive from the hedge, equal to two-thirds of their height, is enough to prevent them from being knocked over by violent gusts of wind. As soon as the leaves have fallen and the trees have entered their dormant period, the mulch roof is put back in place, where it remains until the potted fruit trees have flowered and set fruit the following spring. The aim of this method of cultivation is not to obtain earlier fruit than would be possible in the open air; but, in a Rivers-style garden, the potted trees, protected from snow and ice during the winter, and from hail and late frosts while flowering, produce regular harvests of the best fruit every year, which would not be the case if they were exposed to the open air and grown in the ground all year round. Growing in pots also makes it easier to renew the soil in which the tree roots live, when there is reason to assume that its fertility has been exhausted. In France's average climate, Rivers-style gardens offer no real advantage; but north of the Seine valley, especially in the Aisne, Ardennes and Nord departments, they can be established in many localities where, under ordinary conditions, fruit tree cultivation would yield only uncertain results. Terrace fruit gardens - There are quite a few horticultural enthusiasts in Paris and other major cities in France who have no other resource for satisfying their taste for gardening than a window garden, sometimes on a terrace level with their apartment. When this terrace is not too small, it's easy to convert it into a cradle covered with ornamental climbing plants, to which you can add three or four vines grown in pots like those used for forced cultivation. These vines, in pots that are deeper than they are wide, filled with very good soil, can live for several years, and every year produce bunches of excellent Chasselas that a city dweller is always happy to be able to harvest in the heart of a city where ordinary gardening is impossible. On a terrace, in the same conditions, you can also grow in pots or crates a few pear trees grafted on quince and apple trees grafted on paradise; these trees give some very beautiful fruit every year, especially the paradise apple trees, whose roots don't spread very far. We sometimes see pear trees on the dessert table, only 1.5 meters high, loaded with the most beautiful pears possible; these pear trees are in pots, but you wouldn't expect them to have reached such a degree of vigor and fruitfulness with the little soil contained in the pot, even if this soil were of the highest fertility. At the start of winter, these plants are placed in large pots with holes in them, in the most favorable conditions, on a garden bed. The roots, emerging freely from the bottom of the pot, lived off the soil of the bed, which provided them with the means to feed the potted tree amply, and to make it bear a full load of magnificent fruit. These trees make a very pleasing effect on a dessert table, where they appear after having been uprooted; they are left with only the few roots that remain inside the pot: they very rarely survive such an amputation. But, without claiming to obtain results of this kind, you can keep Madeleine, Savings, Rousselet de Reims and other pear trees in pots for several years, and have them produce a small number of very good pears every year. Fruit trees to be placed in groves. - A number of fruit trees belong more to the flora of woodlands than to that of gardens, but they nevertheless produce products that are well suited to the dessert, and which should not be overlooked by anyone with a large garden, even though these trees cannot be conveniently placed in the fruit garden. These include medlar, corm, hazelnut and even chestnut; in a grove of sufficient size, these trees, interspersed with ornamental trees, in no way detract from them; they add to the pleasure the lady of the house takes in visiting the grove at the start of autumn. These trees don't need pruning; they don't require any special cultivation. This concludes all the useful information we have given on fruit trees and the various procedures for multiplying them, raising them and managing them during all the phases of their existence. At the end of this volume, you'll find a list of the best species of each seric. of fruit trees and shrubs with edible fruit. Fruit harvesting. - Fruit trees are cared for all year round, but their precious produce is harvested only once a year. It is therefore of the utmost importance that fruit be harvested in the best possible conditions, so as to get the most out of them. 19. Cherries, the first ripe fruit of the season, should only be picked when they have reached the precise degree of ripeness appropriate to each species. The professional gardener who picks his cherries a little before they are perfectly ripe, to please his clientele who are willing to give a good price for cherries when, without being completely ripe, they are only ripe enough to be eatable, cannot be severely blamed. If they are ripe enough, the health of those who eat them will not be compromised. However, the true connoisseur must impose upon himself the law of picking cherries, like other fruits, only when they are precisely at their peak, i.e. when they have acquired to the highest degree the edible properties of the species to which they belong. Apricots and peaches should be picked only when, by gently turning them on their support, they come off easily. Plums, for the most part, can be safely waited for until they fall of their own accord; simply lay a bed of clean straw under the trees at the presumed time of natural plum fall. In general, we pick plums with very watery flesh, such as Claude cherries and mirabelle plums, and wait for drier plums to fall, especially those that are to be dried in the oven to be made into prunes. The most important fruit harvest is the stone fruit, peaches, which are often worth a great deal near the big cities. Before being taken to market or served on the table, peaches of all varieties must be brushed with a very soft brush. Women, who are usually in charge of this work because their fingers, lighter than those of gardeners, don't wrinkle ripe peaches, must take care to place themselves in a current of air strong enough to carry away, as it detaches, the down that covers the skin of the peach. This down is a poison which it would be dangerous to breathe in, and from which it is very useful to rid the peach skin. So it's not just to make them look better that peaches need to be brushed. Apples and pears, especially those that can be kept from one year to the next and which form the basis of desserts throughout the bad season, should be picked with every precaution to ensure their good preservation. Nothing is less rational than the all-too-common practice of harvesting all pome fruits on the same day, even though they are at very different stages of ripeness. You shouldn't even pick all the fruit from the same tree at the same time, because it can happen that the inner fruits, in a pyramid-grown tree for example, are 15 days or 3 weeks later in ripening than the fruits on the outer branches. As for the best time to start harvesting, there's a precise indication that's never wrong. If, on a calm morning, you find perfectly healthy fruit on the ground at the foot of a pear or apple tree, free from any insect bites that might have caused them to fall prematurely, it's time to harvest most of the fruit. We choose a fine autumn day with bright sunshine for this operation, and don't start until the heat of the day has fully evaporated the night's dew. As the fruit is harvested in medium-sized baskets, before being taken to the room where it will be stored, it is laid out on the dry, well-cleaned floor of a room where it will remain for at least two or three days. The doors and windows of this room are kept open to ensure good ventilation, so that the fruit loses some of its excess water through evaporation. They are then in the best possible condition to be stored in the fruit-box, where they will keep as well and as long as each species requires. Fruit box. - In any dwelling with a fruit garden of any size, a room on the ground floor must be dedicated to fruit storage: this is called the fruit room. When built specifically for this purpose, the fruit room should be a vaulted chamber, cool but perfectly free from humidity, and much longer than it is wide. The walls, on the right and left, are lined with horizontal trays, supported by cleats and fitted with a rim one or two centimeters high. Fruit is distributed over the surfaces of these trays, avoiding mixing species, which should, as far as possible, be classified according to their order of ripeness. Dessert fruit is carefully separated from fruit that cannot be eaten raw. Many people believe that they are doing the right thing by wiping pears and apples with a dry cloth as they are placed on the fruit stand; this is more harmful than helpful to the preservation of the fruit; it removes the very light ceramic coating on the skin of the fruit; this coating certainly slows down the action of the air on the fruit, and it is important not to strip it. In homes where fruit harvesting is not so important as to require a separate room, the fruit tree itself can be replaced by the following process, developed by the famous agronomist Matthieu de Dombasle. The carpenter makes inexpensive square white wooden crates, about 1 meter long by 80 centimeters wide and only 8 to 10 centimeters deep. These drawer-like boxes have no lids. The first is laid flat on the floor, with the fruit arranged as it would be on a fruit stand. The second, which must have been adjusted by the carpenter to fit without leaving a gap around the edges of the first, is filled in the same way and covered with the third crate, and so on. A stack is usually made up of 12 similar crates, leaving the last crate empty. Stacks of crates take up little space; their number is proportionate to the quantity of fruit you intend to preserve; they are perfectly protected from contact with air and dust; from time to time, you dismantle the stacks to rebuild them immediately after inspecting their contents, either to remove fruit that is beginning to spoil, or to deliver for consumption fruit that is ripening successively. It's a very misguided economy to only ever deliver for consumption fruit that's beginning to spoil; all fruit must end up that way: there's no such thing as fruit that lasts forever. Many housewives consider it a kind of sacrilege to eat healthy fruit; the result of this parsimonious mania is that, in many good houses where there is an ample supply of very good fruit, only very bad fruit is ever eaten. It is a real deception not to eat each fruit at its precise point of ripeness, when it reaches the degree of perfection proper to its species.
