It's at this point that the fruit should be placed on the dessert table. Harvesting and storing grapes. - Table grapes should be harvested as late as possible. When the grapes have taken on a beautiful color on the trellis, and are fully ripe, they can be twisted off the stems and placed in bags made of horsehair, canvas or simply unglued paper, to protect them from the stings of flies and the beaks of birds. When the temperature no longer allows the grapes to remain on the cane, they are harvested in fine, dry weather, and hung from the edge of the fruit tree's shelves, fitted with hooked nails for this purpose. The bunches should be attached to these nails with thick wire ties, in the opposite position to their natural position. No matter how much care is taken to remove all spoiled grapes, and no matter how often the grapes are visited to ensure that they are well preserved, grapes hanging in the orchard can never be kept for very long. After a certain length of time, either rot takes hold of the skin of the grapes, or they dry out to the point of having only an excessively arid skin, stuck to the pips; in this state, they are not spoiled, but they are no longer edible. When you want to keep a small quantity of beautiful table grapes fresh until well into the bad season, you can use the following procedure: The shoots are cut with their load of ripe bunches of grapes; their lower end is plunged into a vase filled with water, placed in a cool place where frost cannot penetrate. As long as the vine shoot retains its vitality in the water, the grapes remain in very good condition; they can stand comparison with the best grapes harvested from forced vines, and are far superior to grapes preserved by any other method. Hollow tin cylinders, placed on a trestle of the same shape as those used by wood sawyers, but lower and more elongated, are used to create this excellent means of preserving grapes. Each cylinder has a tap at one end and a funnel at the other, so that the water can be renewed at will. From distance to distance, thumb-sized tin tubes are welded to the outer surface of the cylinder; the vines are passed through these tubes, which support them with their load of bunches; they thus receive all the moisture they need to keep the grapes fresh for as long as possible. PART FOUR OUTDOOR ORNAMENTAL PLANTS. CHAPTER XXXIX The Parterre. The parterre. - Soil amendment. - Compartment layout. - Borders. - Boxwood. - Bellis. - Carnation. - Edging for large beds. - Grass. - Irish ivy. - Its advantages. - Second row borders. - Flower distribution in the parterre. - Mixing shades. - Size classification. - Multiplication of outdoor ornamental plants. - Sowing. - Transplanting. - Growing plants in nurseries. - Cuttings. - Marcottes. - Separation of offspring. - Cloves of bulbous plants. - Bulbils. - Cuttings from plants that do not overwinter in the open air. The most attractive part of gardening is undoubtedly the cultivation of ornamental plants, a source of elegant pleasure that can be enjoyed at any age and in all conditions. The harmless passion for flowers is at the forefront of those that an honest man can confess to; it precedes all the others, it outlives them, and the better half of the human race can take its share without fear of becoming satiated; for, in the practice of floriculture, something new is constantly being produced, and each season brings its quota of flowers, all the more precious for having taken the trouble to make them bloom which is a pleasure. In order to bring the necessary order and clarity to our instructions on the cultivation of ornamental plants, we will deal first with those which grow in the open air in the average climate of Central Europe, and which are used from spring to autumn to decorate our gardens. The space devoted to this branch of gardening is, as we know, known as a parterre. Parterre. - A well-kept parterre is always a delightful thing, even when it consists simply of one or two flowerbeds close to the house. For the parterre, like the fruit garden, is not always distinct from the rest of the garden. Many gardening enthusiasts, placed in an intermediate condition as far from hardship as it is from opulence, are obliged to devote most of their garden to the production of vegetables and fruit. The flowerbeds closest to the house, and part of those surrounding the squares, are, in this case, the only ones devoted to growing flowers: they act as parterre. In the most favorable hypothesis, the idea is to create a new, suitably exposed parterre. There are no precise rules for the design of a parterre; if it comprises several flowerbeds, anyone can give them the most graceful shape to suit his or her taste, according to the shape of the land; each has its own center. If the soil in the parterre needs to be amended, you should add whatever amendments you deem useful, in sufficient quantity to ensure that you never have to come back to it. In general, good ordinary garden soil, provided it is not too compact, is suitable for almost all the ornamental plants that are to decorate the parterre. When it is too strong, i.e. when there is too much clay, a little lime and a large dose of sifted wood ash, mixed with an equal quantity of potting soil, should be added. All that's left to do then is to maintain the fertility of the bed's soil by adding a sufficient amount of manure every year, either very early in the spring, or in late autumn. Edging. - Before filling the compartments of the parterre with ornamental plants, once the design has been determined, the contours of the paths should be marked with borders. For centuries, dwarf boxwood was the only border used in the parterre; it is still one of the most commonly used. Boxwood has a number of serious drawbacks, however, in addition to its sad appearance and bad odor. It is a haven for slugs, snails and a multitude of harmful insects, whose multiplication it encourages. To compensate for its shortcomings, it has only one advantage: it supports the soil very well, taking up very little space, because it can be mowed with scissors once or twice a year, and thus kept very close to the ground, without its vegetation having to suffer. For small flowerbeds, the choice of plants that can be used as borders is fairly limited. After dwarf boxwood, we frequently use large-flowered brunnelle, perennial bellis or double-flowered daisy in various shades, and dwarf carnation, better known by its vulgar name of mignardise. The most beautiful variety of mignardise carnation is white with a purple spot at the base of each petal, giving it the name crowned mignardise. As borders, perennial bellis and carnation mignardise have one major drawback: they recede from place to place, spread wide at other points, and consequently form very uneven borders. For this reason, carnation borders must be cut every two years. Several species of oxalis can also be used to form attractive borders around the compartments of a small parterre, especially Deppe's oxalis, a charming, very floriferous little plant. When the parterre includes a bed of heather, after surrounding it with boxwood, a border of alternate clumps of cuphea and blue-flowered lobelia can be planted in the second row (fig. 79). These two pretty plants are naturally maintained at small sizes, and their flowering season is virtually inexhaustible. Fig. 79. Blue winged lobelia. Borders for large flowerbeds - The borders of the compartments of very large flowerbeds are usually made of either turf or ivy. The best variety of ivy, which has become very popular in recent years, is what gardeners call Irish ivy. Ivy is preferable to turf as a border, because of its firmness and the ease with which it can be kept constantly covered with young shoots of a very beautiful green: its leaves only turn a rather sad dark green when they have aged. Grass, on the other hand, due to the mixture of wild plants whose seeds are continually transported by wind and birds, never forms, around the compartments of a large parterre, green bands as uniform as the ivy borders, which perfectly bring out the brilliance of the varied groups of flowers they frame When the compartments of the parterre have a large lar8 L Fig. 80. In addition to their perennial border of dwarf box, turf or ivy, these are edged with small bulbous plants, such as crocuses of various colors, and with annuals sown every spring in the second row. The plants most commonly used for this purpose are semi-double dwarf larkspur and Mahon julienne. The latter plant, cut back to ground level after it has flowered once, without giving it time to ripen its seed, comes up again and flowers abundantly a second time. One of the most graceful borders, of fairly recent introduction, is a creeping verbena, very floriferous, with corollas striped in light purple and pure white (fig. 80). Flower distribution in the parterre. - Once the compartments of the parterre have been designed, suitably amended if necessary, and edged in accordance with their dimensions, the gardener must make the best possible use of the resources at his disposal, so that there will be flowers all year round, even in the middle of winter. As they approach flowering time, seeded plants, cuttings and nursery cuttings follow one another into the flowerbed. For these plants, as well as for the annuals that must be sown in situ, because they cannot be transplanted, the gardener must first take care to choose the right colors. Without this care, at a given moment, the flowerbed will be all red, all blue, all yellow, or all white, instead of being, as we say, enamelled with flowers of mixed shades matched with discernment. The gardener also needs to be aware of the size of ornamental plants to be planted in the ground, so as to stagger them by placing the largest in the center, the medium-sized in the second row, and the smallest in front, immediately after the border. There's a charming little saxifrage with exceptionally elegant foliage and white corollas regularly punctuated with bright red, which the peasants of northern France have nicknamed : The more I see you, the more I love you. Many small flowering plants are in the same situation as this saxifrage; the gardener must assign them a place in the flowerbed where they can be seen from close enough, so that we can fully enjoy all the charms of their bloom. Many flowers of different seasons, such as pansy and heliotrope, for example, follow the apparent march of the sun over the horizon by a movement of their own. When poorly placed, these flowers can only be seen upside down by the walker in the parterre's alleys, which causes them to lose much of their ornamental value; this is a fault that a careful gardener must never commit. Multiplying outdoor ornamental plants. - The most important aspect of good flowerbed maintenance is to organize the propagation of the most beautiful ornamental plants, in accordance with the local climate and available space. If this space is limited, it's even better to sacrifice part of it for propagation, so as never to run out of good plants to replace those whose flowering is exhausted, than to leave the flowerbed compartments empty or sparsely stocked. Seedlings, cuttings and marcottes are the main means of propagation used for plants destined to feature in the parterre. Seedlings of hardy plants such as mathioles, campanulas, foxgloves and a host of others are best sown in well-tilled and, above all, well-smoked soil. Enough space should be set aside for this type of nursery, so that biennial plants such as poet's carnations and many campanulas can be transplanted and, as they strengthen, wait for the time to take their place in the flowerbed. Seedlings of more delicate plants or Fig. 81. Pink petunia. Fig. 82. Coréopsis. demanding plants, such as balsamines, margueles, petunias (fig. 81), corćopsis (fig. 82), can only be sown on a muted layer of potting soil. The fibrous roots of these plants, stimulated to branch out by the penetrable nature of the potting soil that feeds them, form a great abundance of hair. When they need to be put in place, we carefully pull them out; they take a good handful of the potting soil from the layer adhering to their roots, and, with a lot of wetting, they take up again immediately, which ensures the beauty of their flowering. Cuttings can be made partly in the open air, partly under a bell or under a frame, under the same conditions as seedlings on a deaf layer. Layers of cuttings are made in place, around the mother plant, in the bed where it has flowered. However, if the marcots - those of garden carnations, for example - need to be cultivated for some time after being weaned, before being transplanted to the place where they are to flower, they should be pricked out in a nursery. Many ornamental plants are not propagated by any of these three means; they provide more rooted offshoots each year than are needed to renew old clumps. These offshoots are detached either in spring or autumn, to be planted immediately if they are strong enough, or to be transplanted to the nursery if the gardener feels they need to develop further. Flowering bulbous plants must also be multiplied very carefully by sowing their seeds: these sowings are used only in the hope of obtaining new varieties superior to the old ones. Every year, after they have flowered, onions are lifted to remove the young bulbs or cloves, which are transplanted to a nursery and cultivated as young seedlings would be, until they are ready to contribute to the decoration of the flowerbed. Many of the most beautiful bulbous ornamental plants produce eyelets called bulbils in the leaf axils of their flowering stems, which take root as they fall to the ground and become perfect plants in time. This is a supplementary means of propagation that can be used mainly to propagate some very fine species of lily. The gardener can also propagate a wide variety of ornamental plants during the summer months, at no cost other than a layer of soil, one or two glass frames and a few glass cloches. Although these plants are not grown in the open air because they do not overwinter in the Paris climate, they nevertheless add variety to the decoration of the flowerbed from spring to autumn. These include several species of calceolaria, fuchsia and petargonium. All you need to do is overwinter a few strong vines of each of these plants in a cold greenhouse or orangery, and in spring they will produce numerous shoots suitable for making cuttings, which will root quickly and flower throughout the summer. With all these resources at his disposal, the professional gardener in charge of maintaining a flowerbed is never at a loss to decorate it with the most beautiful flowers according to the season. As for the amateur gardener, if he wants his parterre to be irreproachable, he must look after it all year round without interruption. CHAPTER XL Ornamental plants for various purposes. Plants and shrubs with climbing stems. - Irish ivy. - Its uses - Its cultivation - Cissus or Virginia creeper - Its use as a wall covering. - Glycine from China. - Vigorous vegetation. - How to moderate it. - Bignone, Virginia jasmine. - White jasmine. - Italian honeysuckle. - Evergreen honeysuckle. - Clematis. - Henderson clematis. - Aristoloches. - Ample foliage. - Annual climbers. - Saxatile plants for rock gardens. - Spring barberry. - Yellow-flowered Corydalis. - Alyssum corbeille d'or. - Evening primrose with large fruit. - California Zauchsneria. - Its little-known merit. - Heather. - Saxifrages. - Sedums. - A curious experiment on the vegetation of acrid sedums and Rhodiola. In the advanced state of modern horticulture, the gardener in charge of a complete parterre must have at his disposal a wide variety of plants, each with its own special merit and purpose. The most useful to know are arranged in six main divisions, including climbing plants and shrubs, for covering trellises and cradles; saxatile plants, for decorating rocks and rockeries; aquatic plants, suitable for decorating the edges of ponds and water features; forest plants, to decorate areas shaded by tall trees or buildings; fragrant plants, with the special function of perfuming the flowerbed; and evergreen or evergreen plants, whose presence conceals the nudity of the flowerbed during the bad season. Plants and shrubs with climbing stems. - There is almost always a section of wall in sight of the parterre, either enclosing it or belonging to a neighbouring building, which cannot be left uncovered and must be concealed under a mantle of greenery and flowers, also essential for lining the cradles often built behind the parterre's compartments, on a point from which you can see all the groups of flowers that make up its finery. The most common climbing plants used for this purpose are: ivy, cissus or Virginia creeper, Chinese wisteria, bignone, Virginia jasmine, white jasmine, honeysuckle, clematis and aristoloches. Ivy. - We have already mentioned the value of Irish ivy as a plant that can form very good borders around the compartments of large flowerbeds. It is no less useful for covering walls and trellises completely and in a very short time. However, as this plant does not flower and its greenish flowers are insignificant, it should only be planted on its own in a northern exposure, or in such a shady situation that you cannot hope to successfully grow other flowering climbers with a rich ornamental effect. Irish ivy can be propagated either by sowing its seeds, or by taking cuttings; the latter is the more common method. Cuttings are made from young shoots, almost always more or less covered with hooks, which become roots; they can be rooted in any season, as ivy's vegetation never slumbers; but they are most successful in spring and autumn. Cissus or Virginia creeper. - This plant, also known as five-leaf ampelopsis and Virginia creeper, blooms as little as ivy; it grows in the same conditions and at the same exposure as ivy. Towards the end of autumn, Virginia creeper leaves turn from green to wine-red, producing a rather picturesque effect, especially when the plant hangs in graceful festoons from tall trees or high walls. It is rarely necessary to worry about propagating Virginia creeper. The seeds that escape from its ripe berries propagate it sufficiently by natural sowing, and give abundant seedlings 20 that can be planted immediately, or raised for a year in a nursery. The numerous offshoots growing at the foot of each older plant are also used as a means of propagation; whether or not they are rooted, these offshoots take root with extreme ease. Chinese wisteria. - Chinese wisteria, acFig. 83. Chinese wisteria, commonly called visteria by botanists, is one of the prettiest, most floriferous and most useful of ornamental climbers. It can be planted in all exposures and in all types of soil; it flowers most abundantly in fertile, light soil, with a southern exposure; in less favorable exposures, it flowers a little less and much later, but it nevertheless produces very beautiful clusters of papilionaceous flowers, of a very delicate amethyst shade, with a most pleasant fragrance. Although not precisely a remontant plant, Chinese wisteria always produces, one or two months after its first flowering, which precedes the leaves in spring, a second flowering that is just as beautiful and fragrant, although less abundant; the clusters of flowers then stand out against its elegant foliage (fig. 83). In European climates, wisteria very rarely produces fertile seeds, so it is almost never propagated by sowing; cuttings taken from the current year's twigs, and marcottes taken from the previous year's twigs, root very easily. In its use as an ornamental climber, wisteria has only one drawback, which is its excessively vigorous vegetation. On the island of Hong Kong, when the English took possession of the island, they found wisteria spreading from tree to tree in the woods, over a width of 8 to 10 meters and a length of more than 300 meters. In Europe, wisteria doesn't grow so fast or so far, but it does invade very large areas in a short space of time; fortunately, the flexibility of its branches, when they're still young, means that they can be folded in on themselves and contained within a limited space. This is what needs to be done when a strong wisteria plant, after having sufficiently lined a cradle, is trained along a trellis of limited extent. The main stem, first extended to the very bottom of the trellis, is brought back on itself to its starting point, then led in successive strings up and down, until the whole trellis is covered. Using the same method, it can be led over wire to completely cover the surface of a wall. The life of this plant is indefinite; Fig. 84. Virginia jasmine. combined with ivy, it produces a most graceful effect, with its bright flowers and pale green foliage contrasting with the dark green of the ivy leaves. Bignone, Virginia jasmine - Virginia jasmine (fig. 84), remarkable for the brilliance of its terminal tufts of large, beautifully red flowers, as well as for its cut foliage of a very beautiful green, does not flower well in northern exposures; all other exposures are suitable. All this beautiful plant lacks is a little perfume: its flowers are completely odorless. Virginia jasmine, classified by botanists in the tecoma genus, multiplies by cuttings and the separation of rooted offshoots; its life span, like that of wisteria, is indefinite. White jasmine. - In the climate of Paris, white jasmine should only be planted in a southern exposure; it combines graceful foliage with the sweetest fragrance; but its flowers, small in relation to the size of the plant, are never very abundant, except in a southern climate. It is propagated either by cuttings or marcottes, in spring and autumn. White jasmine flowers are always born at the ends of the twigs; after the leaves have fallen, the clumps should be clipped with scissors, to bring out as many young flowering shoots as possible. Honeysuckle. - Honeysuckle is one of the hardiest climbers in the European climate, and should be treated accordingly. When you plan to plant it in a cradle, you can plant strong, old plants, provided their sap is not moving at the time of planting. If you take care to raise the plant in a clod with as much soil as possible, and to thoroughly wet the soil in the hole dug to receive it, recovery is assured. Italian honeysuckle, a variety cultivated in preference to all others in gardens, can be combined with evergreen honeysuckle, whose fragrance-free blossoms are yellow inside and bright red outside; mixing the flowers of these two honeysuckles produces a very pleasing effect on a cradle or a wall covered with trellis. Honeysuckle is propagated like jasmine, but with even greater ease. Clematis. - Horticulture has a large number of species and varieties of clematis, all more or less climbing, very floriferous, and many of which can be grown outdoors in the Paris climate. The two species most commonly used to cover trellises and cradles are the fragrant clematis, which flowers from late July to September, and the Henderson clematis, which doesn't begin to bloom until autumn. Through cultivation, the fragrant clematis has given rise to a very pretty sub-variety, slightly tinged with pink, which is less widespread in gardens than it deserves to be; it is as fragrant as the pure white common species. All clematis are propagated by cuttings and the separation of rooted offshoots. Aristoloches. - Several species of Aristoloches are cultivated for their extensive foliage and bizarre flower shapes, most of which give off an unpleasant odor. Aristolochia siphon is the only one that can overwinter in the open air in the climate of Paris; its powerful vegetation provides impenetrable shade from the sun for very large cradles, where the thickness of its ample heart-shaped foliage produces a very pleasant coolness in summer. Although Aristolochia siphon produces ripe seeds in our gardens, we generally prefer to propagate it from two-year-old wood. For this purpose, we select a twig with two knots, cut it in half and cover it with soil, leaving a good eye out. These marcottes, made in spring, can be weaned in autumn. Aristolochia is one of the hardiest and most durable of ornamental climbers. Annual climbers. - Annual climbers, such as volubilis, nasturtiums, sweet peas and runner beans, are among the most common; sown at the end of March or in April, they grow almost everywhere. However common these plants may be, they are no less worthy of propagation, especially the sweet pea which, as its name suggests, has a very strong fragrance, similar to that of the orange blossom; it is particularly suitable for lining the bottom of trellises and cradles in the parterre, and for forming very floriferous 20. clumps around the bare stems of roses grafted onto high-stemmed rosehips. Saxatile plants. - Although this kind of ornamental planting is not always in the best taste, many amateurs like to build an artificial rock in a corner of the flowerbed, with hollows to accommodate various types of plants suited to this special purpose and therefore known as saxatile plants. The list of plants that can be grown in the open air in the Paris climate is long, but not all the plants it includes are ornamental to the same degree. The best for maintaining an artificial rock lined with flowers or greenery all year round are : arabette, yellow-flowered corydalis, alyssum corbeille d'or, aubrietia, large-fruited oenothera, California zauchsneria, several heathers, three or four saxifrages, and a dozen sedums. SPRING CRESS. - The flower of the Arabica is simple and undistinguished; its merit lies in its earliness combined with an extreme hardiness that enables it to be grown in the most ungrateful soils. A handful of soil mixed with an equal amount of sand, thrown into a crevice of the artificial rock, is enough to make a large clump of cattail grow and flower abundantly. Although this plant can also be propagated by sowing and by separating its rooted offspring, it should not be sown in place when used to decorate rock gardens; it is better to place fully-formed clumps in autumn, to obtain a more beautiful bloom. Similarly, clumps of cottongrass should be raised in a nursery, and should bloom in flowerbeds from the first days of March, when other flowers are not common. CORVDALE WITH YELLOW FLOWERS. - Corydalis, also known as bulbous fumitory, flowers abundantly and for a long time, and forms clumps of rare elegance, due to the fine indentations in its foliage, which is similar to that of officinal fumitory. Seeds can be sown in the field, but lose their germinative properties immediately and must be sown at the same time as they are harvested. When given a place in flowerbeds, it's a good idea to mix a few fragments of broken stone with the soil in which their bulbous roots should grow; clumps can be split every three years in late autumn. ALYSSUM CORBEILLE D'OR. - This is the saxatile plant par excellence; its half-woody stems brave drought and flower well only in dry, slightly stony soil; it flowers best in rock crevices, but should not be sown there. Seeds sown in May, as soon as they are ripe, produce seedlings which are transplanted in the autumn and not planted until the spring of the following year. However, if they are vigorous enough, they can be planted immediately before the first cold weather arrives. AUBRIETIA. - This plant, of a very similar genus to the previous one, has become very popular in recent years, due to the brilliance and duration of its abundant blooms, of a charming amethyst hue. There are three varieties, including one with pink blossoms; their cultivation is very similar to that of the alyssum corbeille d'or. ÆNOTHÉRA A GROS FRUIT, ONAGRE A GROS FRUIT. - This plant is only suited to fairly large rock gardens, on which it spreads its stems laden with yellow flowers of unusual brilliance and size. The best way to propagate it is to take cuttings in summer, using young shoots that are covered with a glass cloche and planted as soon as they have taken root. CALIFORNIA IAUCHSNERIA. - This pretty plant, which at the time of its introduction into Europe was greeted with unsustained favor, is now almost nowhere to be found. The neglect into which it has fallen is in no way justified; it forms elegant little bushes between rockeries, which withstand the winters of the Paris climate very well in the open air, and produce a profusion of pretty red flowers throughout the summer. California Zauchsneria is easy to propagate; its seeds, which are abundant, should be sown in May; the seedling flowers in its first year; all soils and all exposures, except due north, are equally suitable. HEAVEN. - Most heathers in horticulture belong to the temperate greenhouse. Those grown in the open, i.e. those that can overwinter in the open air in European climates, are ideal for rock gardens. The hardiest species are: common heather, ash heather, multiflora heather and broom heather, which, despite its less than poetic usage, is no less elegant. They are easily propagated by cuttings, marcottes or sowing their seeds. These plants thrive only in light, sandy soil, known in gardens as "terre de bruyère". This is the soil with which to fill the crevices of artificial rocks where heathers are to be grown in the open air. SAXIFRAGES. - The name saxifrage, which means "breaker", indicates that it thrives in rock gardens. Among the many varieties of saxifrage cultivated in gardens, those that best decorate rock gardens are the ombreuse saxifrage, also known as amourette, and the sarmenteuse saxifrage, whose loose stems run over rocks in all directions, and emit, from distance to distance, bouquets of leaves and flowers, making them eminently suitable for decorating hanging vases in inhabited apartments. The shady saxifrage, whose small flowers are regularly punctuated with bright red, is what we call, in northern France, plus je vous vois, plus je vous aime (the more I see you, the more I love you). Saxifrages are propagated by sowing their seeds in spring. SEDUMS. - The sedum genus is rich in very rustic, very floriferous species, which, living mainly on air which they decompose by means of their fleshy leaves, need very little soil, very little water, and are particularly happy on rock gardens; some even flower with difficulty in any other position. They can be propagated by dividing clumps early in the spring. The most attractive species for rock gardens are: acrid sedum, with star-shaped yellow flowers, very numerous; poplar-leaved sedum, with white flowers washed with pink, with a pleasant fragrance; rhodiola sedum, and houseleaved sedum; this last species is more sensitive to cold than the others; it should only be planted on rock gardens with a midday exposure. All these sedums, but especially the acrid and rhodiola sedums, can be used in a curious experiment of interest to sedentary people, or those deprived of the means to indulge their taste for flower cultivation. These plants, picked a month or so before flowering and suspended by a thread from a nail in the wall of an inhabited room, vegetate there without soil or water, and flower as if in the open ground. CHAPTER XLI Ornamental plants for various purposes. Aquatic plants. - White water lily. - Yellow water lily. - Sagittaria or water arrowhead. - Flowering rush. - Flowering willowherb. - Fireweed. - Purple loosestrife. - Austrian loosestrife. - Round-leaved mint. - Forget-me-not. - Virginia lobelia. - Virginia ponteideria. - Forest plants. - Suitable for decorating shady areas of the flowerbed. - Foxglove. - Large-flowered Hypericum. - Mimulus. - Nemophilous. - Lily of the valley. - Violet. - Gentian. - Periwinkle. - Anemones. - Hepathic. - Hallelujah. - Hoteya. - Tiarella. - Fragrant plants. - Reseda. - Musk mimulus. - Sage. - Thyme. - Lavender. - Hyssop. - Growing them. - Reseda tree management. - Wallflower. - Mathiole. - Evergreen ornamental plants. - Advantages of growing them in the flowerbed in winter. Aquatic flantes. - A pond or water feature often accompanies a large parterre; this pond is not always enclosed in a lining of cut stone or marble, a condition that is de rigueur only for ponds in public promenades or very large parks. If it's only surrounded by a gently sloping grass belt, its edges look much more pleasant when lined with clumps of ornamental aquatic plants chosen from those that have nothing to fear from our cold winters. Some of these plants are perennial only in their roots, which spend the time they are dormant in the mud at the bottom of the water; others, with equally annual stems, live at the water's edge; all are extremely easy to grow. The former spread their leaves and flowers over the surface of the water; the latter erect their flowering stems, most often ending in spikes, on the very edge of the water where they seem to be reflected. The native flora provides 2 S - 9 Thorticulture with white water lily, yellow-flowered water lily, arrowhead, flowering rush, purple loosestrife, round-leaved mint and the humble forget-me-not. When the water in the pond is deep enough that frost can never reach the bottom, you can add to all these native plants Virginia lobelia, which blooms abundantly throughout the summer, and ponteideria from the same country, which blooms for an equally long time. WHITE NYMPHEA. - This plant is one of the most beautiful adornments of still waters, on whose surface come

It requires no special cultivation care, and is propagated by dividing its long, fleshy rhizomes or false tubers. If there is reason to fear that the shallow water in the pond will freeze over in winter, remove the rhizomes from the bottom of the water after flowering, store them all winter in damp, frost-free mud, and replace them in the pond when summer returns. YELLOW WATER LILY OR NUPHAR. - This plant is associated with the white water lily, whose large, buttercup-like flowers create a graceful effect. The foliage of these two plants is the same; they are propagated by the same method. WATER ARROWHEAD. - The brown, gnarled rhizomes of the water arrowhead are found everywhere in still waters, its leaves taking on the shape of an arrowhead as they rise above the water level, while those that remain underwater are simple, very long green strips; the three-petaled white flowers, grouped in spikes three by three, are not lacking in grace; but it is above all through the singular shape of its leaves that the water arrowhead contributes to the ornament of water features. Same cultivation as the water lily. BUTOME OR FLOWERING RUSH. - The umbellate arrangement of the butome's very pink flowers, borne on a single, straight, very high stem, makes this plant one of the most ornamental of Europe's aquatic flora. Butoma is propagated by dividing clumps pulled up towards the end of autumn, separating each into three or four and replanting immediately in the mud. Butoma seeds can also be sown at the bottom of the water as soon as they reach maturity. However, this type of sowing is not practicable in ponds or bodies of water where pairs of swans live. These birds, with their long necks, search the bottom of the water for butoma seeds and those of a host of other aquatic plants, just as the act of germination develops the sweet principle that makes them particularly agreeable to swans. This, incidentally, is the reason why no wild aquatic plant spreads naturally on the surface of a pond frequented by a pair of swans. LARGE-FLOWERED WILLOWHERB. - Two varieties of willowherb are used to decorate the edges of water features, both of them very long-flowering; the larger of the two is also known as flowering willowherb; it is a tracer and is quick to invade more space than it is given; the smaller willowherb, known as narrow-leaved willowherb, is non-tracking; we have a very good sub-variety with wider corollas than those of the two preceding varieties, which we prefer to plant; the root sinks into the mud, right at the water's edge. Willowherb is propagated by dividing clumps in autumn. SALICARIA - Although it blooms abundantly and for a long time, purple loosestrife, with its long spikes of purplish-red flowers, is rarely planted in beds on the edge of a pond. Loosestrife, like willowherb, is planted with its roots in the water at the very edge of ponds; it is perennial and, once it has taken possession of the land, will remain there in perpetuity. ROUND-LEAVED MINT. - Although its small but very numerous flowers, of a very pale purplish hue, lack grace and brilliance, the round-leaf mint, with its sweet fragrance, deserves a place among the ornamental aquatic plants, all of which are odorless. It is a tracer and spreads by itself wherever it has been planted under the same conditions as fireweed and purple loosestrife. MVOSOTIS. - The forget-me-not plant, unrivalled in the flora of European waters for the grace of its form and the purity of its azure blue hue, cannot be overemphasized at the water's edge. Like the round-leaved mint, the forget-me-not propagates itself by its offspring, and quickly forms large clumps, provided that in summer the water level doesn't drop and leave it dry; for, if it ceases to have its foot in the water, it yellows and dies; but, when water is not lacking in any season, it is virtually indestructible. VIRGINIA LOBELIA. - The surest way to grow this pretty plant, which produces beautiful spikes of elegant blue flowers, is to plant it in pots filled with peaty soil, sunk into the mud a short distance from the edge of the pond. In this way, as winter approaches, the pots can be removed from the pond and the lobelia can be overwintered in a frost-free location; it requires no further cultivation care and can be multiplied by dividing the clumps at the start of winter. VIRGINIA PONTEDERIA. - This aquatic plant, equally remarkable for the singular shape of its thick leaves and the elegance of its pretty blue flowers, should be treated in exactly the same way as the Virginia lobelia. Forest plants. - The parterre is often close enough to the buildings or groups of tall trees that make up the groves, so that at least part of its surface receives very little sunlight, or is even completely deprived of it. Compartments in this situation cannot be decorated with the same flowers as the others; the gardener, in anticipation of this necessity, must cultivate a certain number of plants that grow naturally in the shade of forests, so 21 that not one part of his parterre is devoid of flowers. In the Paris climate, the most beautiful and hardy plants for this purpose are foxglove, large-flowered hypericum, several species of mimulus, nemophilia, lily of the valley, violet, periwinkle, gentian and several anemones; If a certain amount of heather soil is available, these plants can be joined by Japanese hotteya and tiarella, two charming plants that grow and flower perfectly in the shade. PIGITALE. - If you're content with foxglove, which grows wild in all woods, transplant young burrs into shady flowerbeds in the parterre; they stay there in perpetuity, with the sole proviso of splitting them when they become too large. If you wish to add the large-flowered foxglove, which is not very different from the foxglove but flowers more profusely, sow the seed in spring; the plant, transplanted in a nursery, flowers in summer the following year. HYPERICUM. - This plant, which traces and spreads rapidly in all directions, is covered from June to September with large, golden-yellow flowers. It should only be planted in situations where it can form fairly large clumps, otherwise it has no effect. The large-flowered Hypericum is propagated exclusively by the separation of rooted suckers, which each clump always produces in overabundant quantities. MIMULUS. - The mimulus genus is very rich in beautiful species with abundant and prolonged flowering, which grow very well in the shade. The cardinal mimulus, with its bright red flowers, and the variegated mimulus, with its purple spots on a yellow background, are the two species that do best in this situation. They are propagated mainly by dividing clumps in spring; seedlings do not always reproduce the variety from which the seed was harvested. NEMOPHILES. - Nothing is more graceful than this pretty plant with its finely cut foliage, pale blue corollas and rare elegance of form. The two or three varieties Fig. 85. Nemophila. cultivated in gardens are propagated exclusively from seed. Nemophilus seed is sown in spring, in the most shady locations. As its name suggests, this woodland plant blooms abundantly in the shade of tall trees without needing direct sunlight. LILY OF THE VALLEY. Because of its fragrance and the graceful shape of its bell-shaped corollas, the lily of the valley cannot be grown too much in shady areas of the flowerbed. If you wish to plant large areas, sow the seed in the garden, either in autumn or spring. If you wish to have only a few clumps, plant the roots removed after flowering; the plants flower the following spring. In the parterre, you can combine wild lily of the valley from the woods with pink-flowered lily of the valley and the double-flowered variety; all are equally fragrant. LILY OF THE VALLEY. - As hardy as quackgrass, the fragrant violet, with single or double flowers of various shades, should have a large place in the flowerbed. A few clumps are planted at the foot of a wall facing south, so that the violet can be picked very early in March; the majority is planted in the shade, where it blooms from April to September, when the remontant, four-season varieties and the very long-blooming, pale-blue double Parma violet are adopted. GENTIANE. - The common species of gentian, widespread in the wild on uncultivated heaths throughout northern Europe, adorns very well the compartments of the shadiest flowerbeds, where its goblet-shaped, dark-blue flowers produce a very beautiful effect; it is multiplied by dividing clumps in spring; it is content with the least fertile soils. PERVENCH. - The two species of native periwinkle, the large and the small, grow and flower in the shade without any cultivation care; they are no more difficult than acaule gentian, as far as soil quality is concerned. The clumps must be contained by frequent splitting, otherwise they will quickly invade a large area. Periwinkle is invaluable in shady areas of the flowerbed, because of its early flowering. ANEMONES. - The little white wood anemone, the false buttercup anemone and the blue-flowered Alpine anemone can all be planted in the shade of a flowerbed; all are equally hardy and will perpetuate themselves in the soil they have once taken over. They can be multiplied by splitting clumps every three years; but the detached roots, with a good eye, should be put in place immediately: the less time they remain out of the ground, the more assured their recovery. These native anemones can be joined by the hepatic anemone, also known as alleluia, because it blooms at the time when the Catholic Church begins to sing Alleluia. We have two simple varieties, one pink, the other blue, and two others that correspond to them, with very double flowers. All these anemones have the same fault: they bloom very early for a very short time, and occupy the ground uselessly for the rest of the year. They should not, therefore, be lavished on; but, as soon as the first fine days arrive, we like to see their graceful flowers, which precede all others, blooming in the shady compartments of the flowerbed. JAPANESE HOTEYA. - Japanese hoteya, also remarkable for its elegant foliage and beautiful, long-lasting white flowers, can be grown in beds of little space, where one shaded compartment can be broken up and filled with heather soil. The clumps of this plant grow large enough every year to be propagated by splitting them before spring vegetation resumes. TIARELLA. - Like its predecessor, tiarella thrives in shade and heathery soil, but if the soil is not naturally damp, it needs frequent and ample watering. It can be propagated like the hoteya, by sowing its seeds. Fragrant plants. - The two most fragrant, musk mimulus and reseda, produce flowers that are barely visible, but their presence is revealed by their sweet smell, and the gardener must not forget to associate them in sufficient numbers with plants with brighter flowers, but no scent. In addition to the two main ones just mentioned, sage, thyme, lavender, hyssop, yellow wallflower, single and double, and mathiola are also grown in the parterre, mainly because of their pleasant scent. Sage, thyme, lavender and hyssop belong to the Labiatae family; all plants in this family require dry soil and southern exposure; they multiply by dividing clumps. Reseda and musk mimulus are annuals; they are propagated by sowing their seeds in spring. Gardeners know a variety known as English reseda, with broader leaves and more developed flowers than the common species. If a single plant of reseda from seed is transplanted into a pot filled with good garden soil, half-mixed with potting compost from broken layers, and instead of forming a clump, it is trained on a single stem, using a straight stick as a support, it can be made hardy and extend its life indefinitely, provided it is not allowed to go to seed. To achieve this, start by pinching the top of the stem when it is ready to flower. This pinching causes lateral shoots to develop, three or four of which are retained. When these shoots are close to flowering, they are pinched off again to give each one two flowering shoots. The lower part of the stem has become woody; the reseda has taken on the form of a small shrub, with a head made up of eight branches held evenly apart by a thin wicker rod bent into a circle. Once the shoots have flowered, they are cut off before the seeds are formed; they thus follow one another without interruption. Keeping the reseda perennial in an inhabited room at a mild temperature during the winter is enough to keep it in perpetual flower for an indeterminate number of years. The pots containing the reseda plants, grown in this way, can be buried in the flowerbed in spring, helping to perfume it throughout the summer. The simple yellow wallflower is propagated by sowing its seeds; the plant often freezes in winter; it should only overwinter in a well-sheltered position, to be placed in the flowerbed in spring, where its flowers spread a scent similar to that of violets. The double-flowered mathiole, improperly named white, red and violet wallflower, can only be propagated from cuttings, as can the double-flowered yellow wallflower. These clove-scented plants become perennials when potted at the start of winter, to be overwintered in a frost-free location, and put back into the flowerbeds in spring. Evergreen ornamental plants - In winter, you can hide the nudity of the flowerbed by temporarily placing a few evergreen plants and shrubs, some in pots, others in the ground. We bury large pots containing a few plants of variegated holly, cotoneaster, aucuba and Japanese medlar. These shrubs are removed in spring to make way for a first assortment of flowering plants, which would not be possible if they were planted permanently. Clumps of thick-leaved saxifrage, pansies, snapdragons and a few other plants whose foliage only freezes in exceptionally harsh winters are planted in the ground. Thanks to this resource, the appearance of the parterre is still brightened up by a little greenery during the rare fine days of the worst months of the year. One of the most valuable evergreen shrubs for decorating the parterre in winter is Forlythia viridissuca, now common and inexpensive. This very cheerful green shrub, as its name suggests, is also adorned, from the first bearable days of February, with a profusion of golden-yellow flowers, reminiscent in shape of the flowers of the Spanish jasmine. These flowers seem all the more pleasing in the parterre, as they even precede those of the Japanese coignassier and other precoee flowering ornamental plants and shrubs. CHAPTER XLII Collectible ornamental plants. Collector's ornamental plants. - Tulips. - Forming beds. - Soil renewal. - Onion planting. - Spacing. - Requirements for a beautiful amateur tulip. - Cultivation care. - Multiplication by cloves. - By sowing the seed. - Hyacinths. - Soil preparation. - Onion planting. - Winter cover. - Stakes. - Dutch hyacinths. - Parisian hyacinths. - Forced cultivation. - Cultivation in water on the chimney. - Ranunculus. - Seedlings. - Transplanting claws. - Soil preparation. - Cultivation care. - Watering. - Resting claws. - Harvesting and storing ranunculus seeds. Among the ornamental plants that can be grown in the open air, there are some that belong to genera rich enough in species, varieties and sub-varieties to form numerous groups. For this reason, they are referred to by the special name of collector's plants; these are the ornamental plants whose cultivation is of most interest to the true amateur. Each of these plants, in order to combine all the properties that make them worthy of the connoisseur's attention, must possess a certain number of very special qualities, which the horticulturist strives to preserve by developing them more and more. As with all things of taste and elegance, fashion has an influence on ornamental plants; many ornamental plants have had their periods of favor, before being abandoned and forgotten. We feel it is our duty here to describe the cultivation of all those ornamental plants that possess unquestionable merit, even if some of them are temporarily and unjustly disdained by a whim of fashion. The main ornamental collection plants that can be grown without the help of a greenhouse in the Paris climate are: tulips, hyacinths, buttercups, anemones, carnations, auriculas, pansies, glayeulas, peonies, dahlias, chrysanthemums, phlox, petunias and, among flowering shrubs, roses. TULIP. - Of all collector's plants, the tulip is the one that has been the object of the most passionate favor, a favor that is very much appreciated, but which nonetheless persists among a fairly large number of enthusiasts. To form a fine collection of tulips, instead of acquiring all the s0o varieties of which complete collections are made up, an expense that few enthusiasts can afford, we are content with a hundred or even fifty first-choice onions; we must choose them that are smooth, firm, well-conformed and, if possible, of a volume that slightly exceeds the movenne of their species. Every year, these carefully cultivated onions produce a certain number of cloves which can be raised separately in a nursery, and which, as their blossoms reach perfection, can be used for exchanges to complete the collection, without incurring excessive expense. Tulip beds should be set up in an open, well-ventilated position; the tulip is originally from the East, and grows wild in meadows near the sea; shade from trees and buildings can only be harmful. Its best exposure is west or south-west. The tulip onion thrives in clean garden soil mixed with a third of potting soil from broken layers; if the soil is stronger than light, half the potting soil can be added. Manure, even when very advanced in decomposition, is fatal to tulip onions as long as it has not been turned into potting soil. The soil quickly tires of this type of cultivation; the soil in which tulip collections are grown must be renewed in its entirety every two years, or every three years at the latest, otherwise the beauty of the flowers is considerably impaired. Tulip onions are planted in the first fortnight of November, taking advantage of the last few days of what gardeners call St. Martin's summer, a period of mild, bearable weather which, in the Paris climate, rarely fails to precede the bad days of early winter. The soil in which the tulip onions are to be planted is removed to a depth of 40 centimetres, placed on a rake, amended if necessary with potting soil mixed in very thoroughly, and immediately put back into place. The surface is carefully levelled with a fine-toothed rake, then the onions are placed in rows spaced 20 centimetres apart, and 12 centimetres apart within the rows. If the tulip bed were given a greater width, the result would be that, at flowering time, the last rows, too far from the edge of the row, could not be seen from close enough to admire the beauty of detail that is this flower's greatest merit. When all the onions have been placed, the rest of the soil held in reserve is spread over them, taking the necessary precautions not to disturb them; then the surface of the bed is levelled with a rake. This surface should not be completely horizontal, but slightly inclined forwards, so that the tulips, arranged in an amphitheatrical fashion, with the tallest at the back, present themselves with all their advantages. The main requirements for a good amateur tulip are : regularity of form, purity of shade (at least two shades on a very pure white or light yellow background), height of stem (no less than 50 centimetres), with a diameter in proportion to the volume of the flower, and finally, a goblet-shaped corolla, perfectly upright on its stem; those whose stems are flexuous, or lean forward or sideways, even if perfect in other respects, are excluded from collections. Once planted in good conditions, with care taken to match colors for the fullest possible ornamental effect, onions require little attention to cultivation. Although they originate from a much warmer climate than ours, tulips can withstand the cold of our unprotected winters in the open air. The leaves emerge very early in the spring, and the tulip beds can be lightly hoeed, taking care not to injure the onions, after which the weeds can be removed if they accidentally appear in the bed. It sometimes happens that icy rains and gusts of wind accompanied by snow and sleet, known as March sleet, fill the cone formed by the tulip's leaves, at the bottom of which is the bud, with very cold water; this water, which is very slow to evaporate, more or less harms the bud by its prolonged stay. Enthusiasts who want their tulips to flower in the best possible conditions erect a very light, green-painted iron frame over the beds, over which a canvas is thrown in case of bad weather. During flowering, this tent prolongs the blooming period, protecting the flowers from direct sunlight. The fruits that follow the flowers must be removed as soon as the divisions of the colored calyx, which replaces the corolla in the tulip, have fallen; only a few are reserved from the most perfect flowers, whose seeds are left to ripen when they are to be used for propagation by sowing; usually, tulips are propagated exclusively by chockers, who reproduce the sub-varieties without any alteration. It always takes several years of nursery cultivation to obtain flowers worthy of a place in the collection, so anyone wishing to maintain a complete collection of tulips, or to increase it by exchange, must raise a fairly large number of cloves every year, planted at the same time, in the same soil and cultivated under the same conditions as the fully-formed onions. Once the fruit has been removed, wait until the leaves have begun to wilt and the flower stalks have lost their rigidity, then lift the onions from the ground, allowing them to lose some of their vegetative water by exposing them to a brisk draught in a place where the sun cannot penetrate. As soon as they are considered sufficiently dry, they are placed in numbered crates, so that when the time comes to plant them, the colors can be matched at will. The cloves reserved for propagation must be treated in the same way, and classified with the same care. Seedlings do not reproduce sub-varieties identically. You need a great deal of patience to propagate tulips in this way; you can only expect to see the final result of sowing after 10, 12 and sometimes 15 years of cultivation, and after this long wait, the flowers obtained are more often than not inferior to those you already have. Tulip onions sometimes produce nothing but leaves for a year; in this case, they are said to be resting, and will flower all the more beautifully in subsequent years. From time to time, without any regular periodicity, the most beautiful onions in a collection give imperfect flowers, entirely different from their ordinary bloom; but by continuing to cultivate them under the conditions described above, we can count with certainty that their flowers will become again as perfect as they could have been before. JACINTHES. - Although the hyacinth originates from the same countries as the tulip, it is of a slightly different temperament, and much more sensitive to the effects of the cold; it does not overwinter without a blanket in the Paris climate; this is why many amateurs only plant their hyacinth onions in spring, when the most severe cold has entirely passed. However, hyacinths treated in this way do not flower as well as those planted in September or, at the latest, in the first fortnight of October. Hyacinth onions that are not planted in autumn are exhausted in their efforts to vegetate in the drawer where they are kept; by the time they are planted in spring, they have already lost some of their vigour and can only give mediocre flowering. Hyacinth onions fear contact with manure even more than tulip onions; they need light, soft, slightly sandy soil, mixed with a third of heather soil, or an equal quantity of potting soil; leaf compost is much preferable for this crop to layer compost, unless the latter is completely decomposed. The bed does not need to be broken up to the same depth as for tulip planting; it is sufficient to remove the soil to a depth of two decimetres, and to replace it after having passed it through a rack and amended it with the prescribed dose of potting soil or heather soil; the same soil can be used for cultivation several years in succession; the breaking up and amendment of the bed need only be renewed after 3 or 4 years. Onions are planted 16 centimetres apart in all directions and, like tulip onions, are covered with one decimetre of good soil reserved for this purpose. Hyacinth onions don't all flower at the same time; if you want to get the most out of your hyacinth collection, plant the singles and semi-doubles, which flower together from the first half of March, and the doubles, which continue to flower until the end of April, side by side. To protect hyacinth onions from frost, they should not be covered with manure or litter soaked in cattle urine, as this would result in the loss of most of the onions. Only perfectly dry litter or, better still, a thick layer of leaves, held in place by slats or poles to prevent the wind from blowing them away, should be used as a covering. The most beautiful hyacinths, with their very double, very numerous flowers, do not have strong enough stems to support themselves; they are staked with small green-painted rods, fitted with two or three horizontal rings of thin wire; the stems, passed through these rings, cannot be blown over by the strong winds of the spring equinox, and the violent rains that are always frequent when hyacinths are in bloom. Hyacinth collections are made up of two series: Dutch and Parisian; the former are unquestionably superior to the latter, but they degenerate, and after a few generations, their cloves give only unrecognizable flowers; collections of Dutch hyacinths can only be maintained by continual acquisitions of onions bought in their country of origin, which necessarily ends up being very expensive. Parisian collections of hyacinths, though less beautiful and less developed in flower, are hardier and less prone to degeneration. They are very easily maintained in their entirety, with their cloves cultivated like fully-formed onions. After flowering, when the leaves begin to fade and turn yellow, it's time to remove the hyacinth onions from the ground, leaving them to dry out in the open air for a day or two, weather permitting. They are then placed in drawers and wrapped in numbered paper, so that they can be mixed at planting time. Collection hyacinths produce single, semi-double and double flowers; the single flowers are the only ones that produce seeds; they are broadcast in September in soil prepared in the same way as onions. Very small seedling onions are not lifted from the ground until their third year; for the first two years, cultivation is limited to protecting the young plant from frost in winter, by covering it with litter or dry leaves, and cutting back the leaves to ground level as soon as they begin to wilt. Hyacinth onions from seed only flower perfectly after ten to twelve years; this method of propagation is only used in the hope of obtaining new sub-varieties of merit, a hope that is not very often realized. 3 Hyacinth onions lend themselves extremely well to forced cultivation. From late autumn onwards, and successively throughout the winter, hyacinth onions are planted in pots filled with potting soil, kept in a constant state of moderate humidity, and brought into a temperate greenhouse; they immediately come into vegetation and show their flowers in the dead of winter. Everyone can enjoy forcing a few beautiful potted hyacinths onto the mantelpiece of an inhabited apartment. You can also place the onions in the opening of a suitably shaped carafe, in white or blue glass, filled with pure water. You'll soon see the roots forming, sinking to the bottom of the vase, and the plant developing and flowering. The vigorous vegetation of the hyacinth makes it possible to carry out a curious experiment with this plant. Two onions are planted in a glass vase full of potting soil, one with the top down, near the bottom of the vase pierced for the purpose, the other, in its natural position, near the vase opening. With these arrangements in place, the first vase is fitted over a second, filled with pure water. The upper onion follows its ordinary course of vegetation; the lower onion does the same, but in reverse; the leaves, stem and flowers grow from top to bottom, in the middle of the water, and the bloom is no less beautiful or colorful than that of the onion that vegetates in contact with the air. BUTTERCUP. - Buttercups are the simplest and easiest of all collector plants to grow. It is easy and inexpensive to multiply by sowing its seeds, so that you can create fairly large beds in your flowerbeds which, when they bloom, are a delight to look at, thanks to the graceful shape of the flowers and the variety of their colors. Very numerous sub-varieties cannot be reproduced by sowing without modification; they lack fixity. But if you take the precaution of sowing only seeds harvested from plants with dark flowers or bright shades, you can count on a rich mixture, where light and dark colors will be in just the right proportions, because some of the dark-flowered seeds only produce flowers of light shades. Ranunculus seed can be sown in the open ground, either in spring or autumn; but it is much preferable to sow only in terrines, which allows sowing to take place virtually all year round, as the terrines can easily be moved into a frost-free place in winter. To obtain a vigorous plant ready to flower very early, fill the terrines, not with soil, but with dried cow dung, pulverized, then moistened to a suitable degree and kept fresh by moderate but frequent watering. When the leaves of the young ranunculus plant begin to turn yellow, watering is stopped; the contents of the terrines, having become sufficiently dry, are crumbled and passed through a wire sieve. Very small tubers, or buttercups, remain on the sieve, each consisting of just two or three fingers. From the first fine days of spring, these young clumps can be planted in good, soft, substantial garden soil, rather light than strong, amended with good loam. When first transplanted, the young claws can be spaced a decimetre apart in all directions; if you take care to water them twice a day, except when it rains heavily, almost all show their flowers in the first year. Once you've obtained an ample supply of fully-formed claws from seedlings, plant them at the distance and under the conditions indicated above, twice, once at the beginning of spring and again in summer. In this way, the flowerbed is decorated twice during the summer season, with two beautiful clumps of flowering ranunculus. Once they've bloomed, when their leaves turn from green to yellow, we lift them from the ground. Those whose seed is to be harvested remain on the ground a little longer; the ripe seed is not detached immediately, but adheres to the scales in rounded tufts that follow the flower; these tufts are tied into bundles and hung in a dry place until the time comes to use the seed for sowing. Like tulip and hyacinth onions, griffez do not have a natural tendency to vegetate when they are not planted in spring. They can be kept for a year in the drawer; gardeners then call them griffes reposéts; they give a more beautiful bloom and wear out less quickly than griffes that are planted every year. Watering is the most important part of growing collector's ranunculus. To avoid compacting the soil too much on the claws, water only with a watering can with a sheaf pierced with very fine holes, and, especially during flowering, give little water at a time, unless you renew the watering several times a day. CHAPTER XLIII Collection plants (continued). Anemones. - Harvesting the seed. - Cultivation care. - Flemish carnation. - Requirements. - Carnation soil. - Incised cuttings. - Marcottes. - Plant rejuvenation. - Auricle. - Conditions for admission to collections. - Cultivation in pots. - Multiplication. - Pansy. - Sowing seeds. - Seedling wintering. - Seed harvesting. - Gladiolus. - Onion planting. - Uprooting. - Clove separation. - Peony. - Montan peony. - Cultivation. - Seedling propagation. - Root grafting. - Dahlia. - Qualities of a beautiful dahlia. - Tuber germination. - Tuber planting, uprooting and storage. - Chrysanthemums. - Main series. - Late flowering. - Cutting propagation. - Phlox. - Drummond phlox. - Petunia. ANEMONE. - After many years as the most sought-after collector's flower in France, Germany and England, the anemone is now only cultivated by a small number of enthusiasts. During the plant's heyday, connoisseurs invented a whole vocabulary of special terms to designate its various parts: pampers, fane, baguette to designate the leaves, collar and stem, mantle, breeches, cordon, beguillons and plush, various parts of the flower, each of which must satisfy the conditions of a very demanding program. Today, any truly beautiful flower, even if it lacks some of the qualities once considered indispensable, is accepted in collections; the most important point is that the shades are vivid and the shape regular, with the center rounded in the shape of a bud, and the outer petals well developed, the flower being double, or semi-double. A few single-flowered anemones should always be allowed in, in order to harvest seed for sowing; semi-double flowers don't provide any. As anemone seed is very light and can be blown away by the wind, you need to keep an eye on its maturity to harvest it in good time. Seedlings can be sown under the same conditions as buttercup seeds, and cultivation care is the same for both plants. Anemone claws can be stored in the same way as buttercup claws, but they are somewhat sensitive to excessive drought during the winter. To obtain a beautiful bloom, they must be planted regularly every year in spring; almost all of them would be lost if left to rest from one year to the next; this is the only point on which their temperament differs from that of ranunculus. CARNATION. - The carnation has been cultivated to produce a large number of varieties, with flowers that are as beautiful as they are fragrant; only the Flemish carnation is considered to be a collector's item, its main distinguishing features being a perfectly round flower, evenly rounded at the center, full petals with no cut edges, and one or two very sharp longitudinal stripes of color on a pure white background. The Flemish carnation, which combines all these qualities, is quite expensive; in Belgium and the Nord department, where it has been cultivated since time immemorial, fully-formed plants obtained from cuttings or marcots fetch a very high price; the seeds themselves, harvested from prime double-flowered plants, are sold for a franc a seed; it's true that very beautiful double carnations yield very few. Nevertheless, the less fortunate amateur can, with care and patience, build up a fine collection of Flemish carnations, starting with a few marcots of 5 or 6 of the finest species, which he can then propagate, either by cuttings and marcots, or by sowing their seeds; This inexpensive method will give him ample opportunity to make exchanges with carnation enthusiasts, completing his collection in just a few years, and maintaining it in its entirety by the same process. Collector's carnations should only be grown in pots, so that the plant can easily be sheltered from the weather, should the need arise. It's not that the carnation is extremely sensitive to cold; it tolerates very well a dry cold of 10 to 12 degrees; but, in spring, when light frosts follow rainy thaws, and frost or ice attaches itself to its leaves, it can't resist these alternatives, which are much easier to preserve when it's not in the ground. Various gardening treatises give more or less complicated recipes for composts, known as carnation soil; these recipes are perfectly superfluous; good ordinary garden soil, mixed with a small amount of compost when it seems a little too compact, is the best carnation soil. Flemish carnations are staked with a thin stick, as the stems are too delicate to support themselves; they are watered moderately during the summer, and if the pots containing the carnations are sheltered during the winter, they are given just enough water to prevent them from dying of thirst. The Flemish carnation is propagated mainly by cuttings and slips. Cuttings don't root easily, unless you make incisions in the buried nodes that penetrate to half their thickness and encourage root formation. Young shoots suitable for marcotting are usually born along the stem, at various heights, so that marcotting at the foot of the plant, by the ordinary method, is not possible. Small pots with side slits are used, or zinc cones attached to the carnation's stake at the height of the marcotte. After removing the lower leaves and cutting the nodes of the part to be buried, fill the pot or cone into which the lower part of the shoot is to be placed with good soil. Take care to keep the soil fresh, but not excessively damp, and do not dry out the marcotte until you are sure it has formed good roots. Elite carnation plants can live for a very long time, but as they age, they are prone to degeneration. In this case, the flowers become deformed, diminishing in volume, and the petals lose the purity of their white background, which becomes washed with pink or light violet. The plant in this state is not lost; it should be de-potted in spring and planted in the garden; there, during the summer, it forms a vigorous clump of lower shoots, which are used to multiply it with cuttings or marcottes; at the same time, the flower regains its normal volume, its rounded, domed shape, and the purity of its coloring; it can then be potted again and resume its place in the collection. Among insects, the Flemish or collector's carnation has a bitter enemy in the forficula or earwig, which devours the buds as they are about to open. To destroy this insect, freshly slaughtered sheep's hooves, commonly known as sheep's dewclaws, are hung from the top of the stakes supporting the carnation flower stems. During the night, forficulas attracted by the particular odor of these ergots retreat there in droves; they are caught early in the morning, and the carnations are freed from them. AURICULA. - The auricula, better known by its common name of BEAR'S EAR, has, like the anemone, fallen from favor, having long been the flower of choice for a very large class of connoisseurs. The cultivation of the auricle has given rise to a particular vocabulary; connoisseurs distinguish between the following parts of the flower: the nail (pistil), which occupies the center; the spangles (stamens), which must graze the corolla without rising above it; the eye, or central part, of a light yellow or pure white, which must contrast sharply, forming a regular circle, with the rest of the corolla's limb, whose hue must be very dark with velvety reflections. In general, people are less demanding nowadays than they used to be, as regards the perfections required of a collector's auricula; even double-flowered yellows are accepted, although their merit is disputed by many connoisseurs. The auricula is no more sensitive to the cold than the Flemish carnation, but it is even more prone than the latter to perish from rotting of the fleshy stump that bears the leaves and flowering stems; this is why its cultivation is only successful in pots filled with the same soil in which carnations are grown. It's especially important in late autumn and spring, during the March sleet season, to place auriculas in pots, either under a cold frame, or in some other place where the cold humidity that this plant fears above all else cannot reach it. When we're not trying to create new varieties, which is only possible by sowing, we're content to multiply auriculas by detaching, in autumn, the eyelets or rooted offshoots that grow at the foot of the old clumps; these eyelets, grown in the same conditions as fully-formed plants, grow very quickly and flower as early as their first summer. Although auriculas are not remontant, in the strictest sense of the term, plants properly cared for during the winter and watered not excessively, but every day, morning and evening, during the heat of the summer, usually give a second flowering at the beginning of autumn. BENSEE. - This plant is the easiest of all collector's plants to grow. Simply sow the seed in August, in a bed with a southern exposure, so that the seedling can overwinter in the open air without suffering from the cold, which the pansy fears very little, unless it is exceptionally severe; In this case, the slightest shelter of straw mats or dry litter thrown over the plants during heavy frosts and removed as soon as the weather eases, ensures the pansies' preservation. Plants from seedlings sown in August always show some flowers before winter; we take advantage of this circumstance to keep only the best flowers in our collections, eliminating the others (fig. 86). In a beautiful collector's pansy, connoisseurs demand: a well-spread corolla, free of folds, well rounded in shape, a dark cenFig. 86. Pansy. from which equally dark stripes emerge on a yellow or white background, and a symmetrical distribution of spots over the five divisions of the flower. Seed harvested from plants whose flowers satisfy all these conditions does not constantly reproduce similar flowers; but if a good choice of pansy seed has been sown, all the sub-varieties of any value are found, without exception, in the seedling, which renders useless the propagation by cuttings indicated as a means of propagating the most distinguished varieties. This method is defective in that it only produces delicate seedlings, which are difficult to overwinter, so that in the spring, collections of pansies grown from cuttings are incomplete and bare, which is not the case with seed multiplication. It is important that seedlings that develop during the hot August weather are watered often enough to ensure that their growth is not slowed down by drought. Pansy seed is rather difficult to harvest, due to a peculiarity of the plant's vegetation. 1 As long as the seed has not formed, the stem supporting the pansy flower bends at the very point of insection of the corolla, so that the latter is in an upright position; if this were not the case, with rain or just dew falling on the reproductive organs, fertilization could not take place, and the pansy would not produce seeds. But once fertilization has taken place, the curvature of the flowering stem gradually disappears; the stem becomes completely straight, so that when the seeds ripen, the capsule enclosing them points upwards, without tilting to either side, and suddenly opens, letting the ripe seeds escape in all directions. If the capsule had retained the position occupied by the flower during blooming, it would have the tip pointing sideways; all the seeds would fall to the ground, in a bundle, one on top of the other, and the pansy would be almost impossible to reproduce by natural sowing. It is important to keep an eye on the maturity of the seeds, and to pick the capsules with their full-length stems, leaving them to mature on a sheet of paper in a well-ventilated room. Otherwise, it would be difficult to obtain a sufficient quantity of pansy seeds to maintain collections. The seedlings, well cared for during the winter, are transplanted in groups into the flowerbed, where they flower abundantly throughout the summer. GLAIEUL. - The gladiolus (fig. 87) is one of the most recent of the collector's plants. It is not difficult to grow, but its flattened onions, which are very voluminous in some species, only flower well in pure heather soil. In a pinch, this soil can be replaced by a mixture of light sandy soil and leaf compost; but then, gladioli are not as perfect as they can be when planted in heather soil. Onions should only be planted in the open air in April, when the last of the late frosts no longer seem to be a threat; they should be lifted in November when the weather is dry, and the cloves removed, as they only ever produce a small number of cloves. Gladioli make up numerous collections, the main series of which, with their magnificent, very long flowering period, include: the parrot gladioli, the cardinal gladioli, and the Grand gladioli, with several subdivisions, all rich in Gladioli. Fig. 87. in sub-varieties of great beauty of form and color. PEOPLE. - Peony collections consist of three distinct series, comprising: tree or woody-stemmed peonies; herbaceous-stemmed peonies with hairy fruit, and herbaceous-stemmed peonies with smooth fruit. The tree peony or Montan peony is the basis of our collections, the true amateur peony. We have some forty very fine species, all remarkable for the size, abundance and brilliant color of their flowers, which bloom very early in the spring. The number of species or varieties of Montan peony is increasing slowly, because we can only hope for beautiful new varieties by multiplying this plant by sowing its seeds. On the one hand, it produces few, and on the other, the seedling only says its last word after twelve or fifteen long years, and the final result rarely meets the gardener's expectations. Nevertheless, there are always persevering sowers, and a few new Montan peonies of real merit are put on the horticultural market from time to time. The Montan peony is hardy; it overwinters very well in the open air in the climate of Paris, where it forms clumps of bushes one to two meters high; although the icy north and north-west winds sometimes roast its first leaves in spring, it is very rare for late cold to seriously damage its buds.
