All peonies can be grown in a mixture of garden soil and heather; seedlings do best in unmixed heather. Peonies are mainly propagated by dividing the clumps in spring or autumn, splitting the more or less tuberous roots. To obtain earlier flowering, choice varieties can also be grafted onto the roots of more common species. DAHLIA - When the dahlia was imported from Mexico to Europe at the beginning of this century, its brilliant destiny was not foreseen. It is said that Mexicans eat it; what is certain is that those who first tried to eat it in Europe were not tempted to try again. As a collector's plant, the dahlia (fig. 88) has been one of the most sought-after ornamental plants for large flowerbeds. Its popularity was sustained for as long as the old varieties were succeeded by ever more beautiful ones, thanks to successful sowings; at that time, dahlia cultivation alone made the fortunes of several horticulturists in France, Germany, Belgium and England, who worked exclusively with it. Today, the popularity of this plant has passed; the number of beautiful varieties is so great that it seems superfluous to try to create new ones; the ease with which they can be multiplied by dividing tubers and grafting onto roots is such that their price has become extremely modest. The dahlia remains what it should be: the king of autumn, as the English say, and indeed it's hard to imagine what could replace its beautiful clumps of brilliant flowers in the most varied shades, if it were to disappear from our flowerbeds; but it is no longer, so to speak, an amateur plant, in the true sense of the term. A beautiful dahlia should have a regular globular shape, symmetrically arranged horn-shaped ligules or florets, and a stem long enough to overflow the foliage, strong enough to ensure that the flower does not fall back and show itself upside down. Fig. 88. Dahlia. Dahlias thrive in free-flowing garden soil, which should be a little strong rather than too light. Tubers should not be planted in the open ground before the first fortnight of May, as young shoots can be killed without remedy by a few hours of morning frost of just one degree. At the end of March, however, the tubers are placed close together in a cold frame at the foot of a south-facing wall. There, immersed up to the root collar in soft soil mixed with loam and kept very slightly moist, they slowly come into vegetation, with nothing to fear from the late cold. By the time they are placed in the open air, each tuber, which is also planted with a sturdy support, already has a good shoot that consolidates on contact with the air and flowers by late summer. Without this precaution, the dahlia would bloom too late, and the first frosts of late autumn would kill the plant, still full of buds ready to open. For some years now, it has been customary to leave a single stem in each clump, which becomes very solid and almost woody at its base. This single stem branches regularly, producing clumps that are strong, full of flowers and without excessive foliage. Not all dahlia varieties develop in the same way; when forming clumps without combining them with other plants, care should be taken to match them by color, and to place in the center of the clumps those varieties whose stems should exceed those of the other dahlias in height. Tubers should be removed before frost and stored in a cool place. INDIAN CHRYSANTHEMUM. - Although the Indian Chrysanthemum Fig. 89. Chrysanthemum. has, like the dahlia, pretty much said its last word, and because of the ease with which it can be propagated, either by sowing or cutting, or by splitting clumps, its price has become very moderate, the plant's popularity has not waned; it is still at the top of the list of collector's plants. Chrysanthemum collections are made up of four series, including buttercup-flowered, daisy-flowered, acorn-flowered and dwarf chrysanthemums (fig. 89). All these plants are equally hardy and easy to grow; any good garden soil is suitable for them; they ~ flower abundantly and form strong clumps in a short time; but they all have the same fault, that of flowering very late, in November and December, so that the first serious frosts destroy them in full bloom. This is why the most beautiful species of the first and fourth series are most often grown in pots, which are brought into the orangery or cold greenhouse when the cold season approaches; these pretty plants flower there for part of the winter. More than any other plant, the collector's chrysanthemum has the ability to take cuttings at any time in its vegetation. If you cut branches with well-formed buds, these branches will root and flower at the usual time, but they will no longer grow. This simple procedure can be used to form very small clumps of Chrysanthemums from cuttings, suitable for decorating small flowerbeds and for wintering in apartment window boxes. 9 PHLOX. - Today, phloxes have produced so many beautiful varieties that they have become collector's items. Strictly speaking, they are sub-shrubs with woody annual stems, perennial roots and abundant, prolonged flowering; the collection comprises some forty distinct species, each of which continues to produce beautiful sub-varieties from seed. One of the most interesting species is Drummond phlox (fig. 90), which reproduces every year from seed and produces low but very floriferous clumps. Drummond phlox thrives in a mixture of free soil and heather; Fig. 90. Phlox of a good ordinary garden soil is sufficient Drummond. to other species. PETUNIA. - The number of varieties of this charming 2, 2 plant, whose abundant, essentially remontant flowers have a very pleasant, sweet fragrance, is increasing daily; we now have enough to form collections that remain in constant bloom throughout the summer. The most remarkable species are the white-flowered petunia, the first to be introduced from its native South America; the plain pink petunia, with sub-varieties ranging to the darkest velvety purple; the central black-eyed petunia, and the purple-veined petunia on a pink background, one of the most sought-after by lovers of this beautiful genus. Petunia seeds can be sown in trays or in the open ground on potting soil, in a south-facing position, giving early seedlings that can be planted at a very young age, and which vegetate vigorously, provided they are well watered, in good ordinary garden soil. At the end of this volume, you will find lists of the most beautiful species, varieties and sub-varieties of all ornamental plants grown in open ground. Roses. 391 Classification of roses. - Hundred leaves. - Provins or damas. - Ile Bourbon. - Portland or perpetual. - Hazelnut. - Bengal roses. - China. - Tea roses. - Sarematious roses. - Uses of various roses in horticulture. - Propagation. - Sowing. - Hybridization. - Grafting. - Choice of rosehips. - Side slit grafting. - In escutcheon. - Pruning. - Its purpose. - Direction of buds. - Growing roses in the open air. - Moving them to force them to flower. - Forced cultivation. The multiplication, cultivation and pruning of the rose should be studied separately from that of other ornamental plants in open-ground collections, not only because the rose has not ceased to be the queen of flowers from biblical times to the present day, but also because rose collections contain no less than 3,000 species, varieties and sub-varieties. It's true that many of these are more or less questionable, and can only be distinguished by characteristics that can only be appreciated by consummate connoisseurs; but the number of roses that are indisputably distinct and cannot be mistaken for one another can be increased from 1,800 to 2,000. Classification of roses. - To recognize each other and establish a regular order among so many shrubs all belonging to the same genus, various classifications based on botanical characteristics have been proposed; the most widely used is that of the famous English botanist Lindley. For the gardener, it's enough to distinguish clear-cut series of roses, classified according to their most remarkable properties from a horticultural point of view. Considering them in this light, we find that roses naturally fall into eight series, comprising : 1° The hundred-leaves, the oldest type of rose perfected by cultivation; all with very fragrant, very double flowers, but which do not ascend; 2° provins, with semi-double flowers, a very strong fragrance and generally very vivid shades, which should be called Damascus roses, as they were brought back from the Orontes valley at the time of the Crusades by a Count of Champagne who propagated them in the gardens of his Provins castle; 3° bourbon or Bourbon Island roses, which have produced the largest number of the most sought-after varieties by sowing their seeds; 4° portland or perpetual roses, the most remontant of all cultivated roses; 5° hazelnut roses, which flower abundantly in autumn, but are odorless; 6° Indian roses, which include Bengal and Chinese roses, which are very remontant, have a double, but faint odor, and only semi-double flowers, and are more sensitive to cold than any of the above; 7° tea roses, sufficiently distinct by the particular fragrance of their roses and the delicacy of their shades; 8° climbing roses, suitable for decorating trellises and cradles in the company of ornamental shrubs with climbing stems (Voy. ch. XL). Uses. - In horticulture, roses lend themselves to a host of different uses. Species which, like most hundred-leaf roses, grow naturally and form thick bushes from the numerous offshoots their stumps produce, are perfectly at home at the entrance to groves, at the edge of clumps of tall ornamental trees. This situation is particularly suitable for the painter's rose, the York rose, white at the heart and flesh-colored, and the charming little pompon rose, which, despite its small size, blooms on very large bushes. The smaller provins make a very attractive addition to the shrubbery that forms the transition from grove to parterre in landscape gardens. The portland, ile Bourbon and hazelnut series are especially suitable for grafting onto tall rosehips; they form the basis of amateur collections; their flowers offer the richest variety of shades, including pure yellow, of rare freshness in the yellow Persian rose; pure blue is lacking, although ancient Arab authors speak of the blue rose, without possible equivocation, as a common flower in gardens at the time of Moorish domination in Spain and Portugal. Bengal and Chinese roses, perennial only by their roots, are suitable for gardens where there is little space to spare; they must be pulled back on the stump at the onset of winter, and covered with leaf-covered soil to protect the stump from the effects of frost; their annual shoots flower profusely all summer long; in the Midi, they are large evergreen bushes, forming flowering hedges of the most pleasing effect. This quick overview shows just how much horticulture can gain from roses in the most diverse conditions. Propagation. - Rose propagation, especially that of collectors' roses grafted onto wild roses, is an important branch of horticulture in its own right; those who take special care of it are referred to in gardening parlance as rose growers. All solid-wood roses, especially those in the hundred-leaf series, produce a profusion of rooted offshoots every year. These are separated in spring or at the onset of winter, before the leaves have fallen; they are raised in a nursery and trained into the desired form. There is no need to use any other means of propagation for these roses. The Bengal and Chinese rose series, with their soft, pith-filled wood, are very easy to root from cuttings, and are best grown in a greenhouse, under a frame, rather than in the open air. Roses taken from cuttings flower very young and grow very fast; this method, for this series of roses, is used in conjunction with the separation of offshoots which, placed in favorable conditions, provide good plants, even when they have few or no roots at the time they are detached from the stock. Seedlings are the most interesting way of propagating roses; all varieties in all series, even the most double varieties, give a few fruits or rose hips containing fertile seeds every year; the stamens are so numerous in the rose that they never all turn into petals, and there are always enough left for fertilization. The seeds enclosed in the rose hips should only be removed and sown when the fruit is a little past full maturity, after the first frosts. Seedlings should be sown in spring, in heather soil, to encourage root development as much as possible. Young seedlings are then transplanted to more substantial soil, where they are raised in nurseries. They sometimes have to wait a long time for their first flowering; this is why those whose behaviour and foliage give reason to hope for a good novelty, are grafted onto rosehips in their second year; they flower immediately there, which makes it possible to judge whether they should be eliminated or kept in the collection. Hybridization. - It was at the beginning of this century that the Marquis de Villarési, in Italy, realized the possibility of creating new rose varieties by hybridization. The process he used was primitive. The roses he wished to cross were trellised closely together at the bottom of an espalier trellis. At flowering time, M. de Villarési intertwined their branches, leaving it to the winds and insects to carry pollen from one flower to the other. The seedlings from the rose hips produced by these roses gave him some very fine novelties among a multitude of insignificant flowers. This success had a resounding effect, and M. de Villarési had a large number of imitators. Numerous crosses, practiced with greater care and method, gave more frequently successful results. Today, without directly hybridizing one sub-variety with another, it is possible to sow the seeds contained in rose hips harvested from roses in a collection of well-chosen varieties; wind and insects will inevitably have produced accidental crosses; many of today's most famous roses have no other origin. When you intend to artificially fertilize two roses of very different species, in the hope of creating an intermediate sub-variety, you should shake one of the roses over the other at the moment of full bloom, and then wrap the rose with gauze, so that the hybridized rose cannot accidentally receive pollen from another species, which would cause the operation to fail. The hybridization of roses sometimes gives expected but highly unusual results; for example, the hybrid cross between a very red rose and another of pure white has created the Prince Albert rose, each petal of which is white on one side and red on the other. Grafting the rose. - The majority of roses propagated by grafting are grafted onto tall rosehips. This type of rose grafting is so widely used that, in a large radius around Paris, Lyon and other major cities, the breed of rosehips suitable for grafting the most sought-after species has been completely exhausted. Several growers have already made substantial profits simply by raising rosehips from seed, which they sell when they have developed to the point where they are suitable for grafting. When wild rosehips are purchased for grafting, the portion of the stump that usually adheres to the bottom of the stem must be cut off at the time of planting. The stem must be straight, free of knots, wounds and branches. If rosehips were to retain a portion of their stump, they would produce a multitude of offshoots, which would exhaust the shrub to the detriment of the beauty of its flowering. Rosehips require at least a year's nursery cultivation before they are ready for grafting; a certain number must always be planted in excess of the precise quantity needed; some are always missing when they are taken up again, and grafts never succeed in their entirety. Grafting takes place in spring, when the sap is most active, either on two or three branches at the top of the rosehip, each of which receives a shield graft, or on the main stem, stripped of all its branches. In this case, grafting is done sideways, using Fig. 91. Rose split grafting Fig. 92. Grafting with a rose escutcheon. for grafting, a branch from the previous year with two good wills (fig. 91); or, two escutcheons are placed close to the top of the stem, facing each other (fig. 92). This last method, the most widely used of the two, is the one which, when each of the two escutcheons has given a good shoot, offers the easiest way of forming a regular, elegant and floriferous head for the grafted rose in a short space of time. Pruning the rose. - When the rose, grafted or not, belongs to a hardy, vigorous species, as are all leafy and climbing species with sar mente stems, they are pruned with scissors, just as if a hedge were being mown. But more delicate species, especially those grafted onto wild roses, need to be pruned more carefully and methodically. After removing dead wood, accidentally broken branches and those that cause confusion, these roses are pruned with the dual aim of obtaining abundant flowering and keeping them in good shape. In terms of flowering, each variety has its own particular way of vegetating, depending on whether it is remontant or not, or only biferous. As each well-conformed eye should produce a more or less floriferous branch, the gardener, who must know perfectly well the temperament of the roses in the collection he manages, adjusts his pruning accordingly, leaving each branch with one, two or three eyes, which will be as many branches. As for preserving the shape, this is easy for the gardener who, when pruning, pays sufficient attention to the place occupied by the eyes of the rose's branches. These eyes produce buds which, as they grow, always elongate at a very open angle, usually at right angles to the branch bearing them. Thus, when the gardener needs to fill a gap in the inner part of the head of a grafted rose, he prunes on an eye whose point is directed inwards, and even, if it is necessary for the bud that will be born from this eye to grow with great vigor, he removes the eye inferior to the one on which he has pruned, by disbudding it, before it has begun to elongate. If, on the other hand, he needs a good outer branch to give the rose a harmonious shape, he prunes on an eye with the tip outwards, and knows in advance what place the branch he is pruning on will occupy. In order to avoid the mistakes that can result from miscalculated bud direction, many rose bushes, whose eyes are naturally not very visible, are not pruned until spring, when vegetation is sufficiently advanced for the gardener to clearly distinguish in which direction the buds born from each of the eyes reserved for pruning will elongate to complete or maintain the regularity of the head of these shrubs. Growing roses in the open air - When roses raised, grafted and pruned as indicated above, have been planted in 23 good conditions, those grafted on rosehips in soil that is stronger than light, those from India, raised as bushes, in heather soil mixed in equal parts with free garden soil, they require little further cultivation care. If it is not possible to move them periodically for lack of space, every three or four years part of the soil in which their roots grow should be removed, and replaced with new soil of the same type as that removed. The soil of roses grown in pots should, by virtue of the same principle, be renewed every two years. When aphids invade roses in summer, following a long period of drought, tobacco smoke is used to rid them. For this purpose, a glass bell (melon bell) is hung over the head of the rose, so that the tobacco smoke dissipates only after the aphids have been asphyxiated, without harming the rose's vegetation. It sometimes happens that roses grafted on rosehips, which are apparently healthy, hardly flower at all. In such cases, when they start to bloom again, they must be pulled out and immediately replanted close to their previous position; this move is enough to persuade them to bloom. However, the success of the operation is more assured when the lay of the land allows the roses to be transported some distance from their first location. Forced cultivation of roses. - All roses, potted and moved into a temperate or hot greenhouse, can be forced into rich bloom long before their natural flowering time; but only remontant roses are usually forced. Professional gardeners, whose customers demand large numbers of ball bouquets, force bushes of Tea roses, Bengal and China roses, and Portland or Perpetual roses into the open ground under frames heated by manure heaters or a thermosiphon; they thus have roses to pick all winter long. Rose lovers who do not need such a large quantity of roses in winter, simply pot up a certain number of roses of the same species a year in advance and bring them successively into the temperate or hot greenhouse, so as to have a certain number of roses to pick from late autumn to early summer of the following year. CHAPTER XLV Growing annuals, biennials and perennials in the open ground. Growing annual ornamental plants. - Sowing. - Plants to be sown in clumps. - Annuals to be transplanted. - Herbaceous perennials. - Turfgrasses. - English ryegrass. - Seed mixes for dry soils. - Quantity of seed per acre. - Weeding lawns. - Isolated plants. - Palma-christi. - Acanthus. - Siberian heracleum. - Yucca gloriosa. - Shrub groups. - Lilacs. - Spiries. - Buddleyas. - Summer pruning of Persian lilac. - Grafting josikœa onto ash, and lilac onto josikœa. - Ornamental trees. - Landscape gardens. - Isolated trees. - Catalpa. - Tulip tree. - Suitable situations. - Fruit trees used as ornamental trees. After describing in particular the cultivation of ornamental ground plants and shrubs suited to various special purposes, and that of collector's plants, it remains to indicate the general cultivation care required by annuals, biennials and perennials, in the climate of central France, to contribute all year round to the decoration of the parterre. Annual plants. - Sowing. - The seeds of most outdoor annual plants should be sown in the spring; many of them vegetate quickly enough that, after a first flowering, sowing can be repeated with the certainty of seeing the same plants flower a second time before the bad season. There are a few important rules to be observed when sowing seeds of annual plants. The smaller the seeds, the less they are buried; the smaller seeds, such as double coguelicot, are not buried at all. After scattering them on the surface of the soil, they are mixed into the surface layer with a close-toothed rake, and then a very thin layer of dry, powdered loam is spread on top. The young plant born from a very small seed is too weak to lift the soil with which it is loaded, when the seed has been buried too deeply. Those of these plants which are individually poorly developed and not very floriferous only appear to their advantage when they are grouped together in large enough groups. Clarkia, for example, produces no effect in the parterre and seems a 2 - Fig. 93. Graham's schizante. small, insignificant flower when two or three isolated strands are seen; on the contrary, it offers the most graceful appearance when the seed is sown in a circular space 35 to 40 centimetres in diameter, ¿ either when the two varieties, one white, the other pink, are sown separately, or when they are mixed to form very long-flowering clumps: the same applies to eutoca, schizantes (fig. 93), brachycomes and a host of others of more or less recent introduction, all valuable additions to our garden flora. At the time of the main annual plant sowings, in March and April, the flowerbed still has only a small number of plants in bloom. The gardener needs to know exactly how much space he or she has, so as not to give too much to seedlings in situ, and to avoid running out later on when transplanting nursery-raised plants. Although most of these plants are biennial, such as marigolds and most campanulas, several annuals, such as marigolds and balsam ferns, do not flower well when sown in situ. As their presence is essential in the flowerbed for most of the summer, it is important to note in advance the place they should occupy, either in the intervals between seedlings of annual plants, which must not be transplanted, or to succeed the plan Fig. 94. Large-flowered Brunelle. Fig. 95. Pentstemons. with very early flowering, such as thick-leaved saxifrage, arabette and abyssum or golden basket, which leave early gaps to be filled in the flowerbed compartments. Among annuals grown in the open ground, there are many, such as clarkia, eucharidium, gillia, nemophilæ, silenes, coreopsis, large-flowered brunelle (fig. 94), Chinese carnation and several beautiful species of delphinium. These can be treated as biennial plants by sowing them in autumn, so that they germinate during the last fine days of the year, and the young plants born from these seedlings can withstand the cold winters of the Paris climate very well. It is Fig. 96. Cosmos. Fig. 97. Cantua. It is always wise to sow the seeds of these plants fairly lightly, in a sheltered position, with a southern exposure, so as to be sure of finding them in good condition in spring, when they are to be transplanted into the flowerbed compartments. For truly biennial plants, such as carnations, pentstemons (fig. 95), cosmos (fig. 96), cantua (fig. 97) and rudbeckia (fig. 98), if you want them to flower well, sow their seeds not in autumn, but in spring, in March and April, and nurture them throughout the year so that they flower the following year. Herbaceous perennials - Many of these plants, such as irises, white and yellow lilies, Phlox, aconites and dielytra (fig. 99), need to be split quite frequently, otherwise their clumps, which have become too voluminous, recede in the center and give only degenerate flowers. Fig. 98. Rudbeckia. By splitting the clumps in spring or autumn, you renew the soil in which they have grown, or, before putting them back in place, you give this soil Fig. 99. Dielytra. N with a well-consumed fertilizer. With this care, and by not letting them run out of water during hot spells, these plants, with their robust temperament, will remain in the flowerbed in perpetuity. Lawns. - Thanks to the humid climate of their island, the English were the first to achieve the highest degree of ornamental value with these admirable lawns, real carpets of green velvet that gardens all over Europe have adopted as their example. Today, carefully tended lawns no longer belong exclusively, as they once did, to large landscape gardens; they have been introduced with great success into medium-sized gardens, especially those that are both fruit-growing and landscape, where groups of fruit trees are arranged in compartments designed in the picturesque style, separated from one another by lawns. In good soils, English ryegrass is the basis of the most beautiful lawns; provided it is sufficiently watered, and mowed often enough not to let it flower, it does not recede from the base and remains in very good condition for a long time. For dry soils that are difficult to water abundantly, we sow mixtures in varying proportions of several grasses combined with a few forage plants from the legume family. The most commonly used proportion is 600 seeds of ryegrass per are; mixtures of grasses and leguminous forages can be sown in a slightly lower proportion; 500 grams per are is sufficient. After the grass seed has been lightly mixed into the soil by shallow harrowing with a rake, a roller is used to firm up the soil; if rain is not forthcoming after the grass has emerged, water moderately, and mow as soon as the grass is tall enough for the scythe to work. Whatever care is taken to maintain a lawn, it will always end up being more or less infested with weeds, whose seeds are carried by the wind and birds; for this reason, it is necessary to weed the lawn once or twice every 23. year, to prevent the multiplication of weeds, especially those whose seeds have a tassel, such as dandelions, which must be removed before they flower. In spring, if the turf seems to have been lifted by alternating frosts and thaws, spread a good dose of well-consumed compost mixed with dried and pulverized pond silt over the entire lawn, then roll it in; roll it in a second time after mowing. Isolated plants. -- In patches of lawn of mediocre size, nothing produces a better effect than an isolated tuft of one of those plants with ample leaves like those of the large rhubarb species, or elegantly cut, like castor-oil or palma-christi, soft acanthus and Siberian heracleum. A few vines of yucca gloriosa can also be used for the same purpose: its clumps of evergreen leaves, similar to those of agaves, and its flowering stems laden with a multitude of greenish-white bell-shaped flowers, stand out most picturesquely against the uniform green of the lawn. These plants are perennial, with the exception of the castor bean, whose seeds are sown every year in situ, when the last late spring frosts are no longer to be feared; their elegant or bizarre forms and the power of their vegetation serve to break up the monotony of lawns; in small and medium-sized picturesque gardens, they create the same effect as clumps of shrubs and isolated ornamental trees in landscape gardens of the greatest style. Shrub groups. - It wasn't until quite recently that the English began planting groups of shrubs in large gardens. These were called schrubbery, a word for which we would have to introduce the equivalent term arbusterie, which is missing from our language. These groups, contained in compartments designed like those of a parterre, but on a larger scale, now include a very rich variety of shrubs, among which lilacs, spireas and buddleyas rank first. Lilacs have given rise to a fairly large number of beautiful varieties, the most widespread of which are Macly lilac, the most beautiful of all, white lilac and Persian lilac. If you don't intend to use lilac seeds by sowing them in the hope of obtaining new sub-varieties, you must remove all spent flowers immediately after flowering, without allowing them to be replaced by seeds. The production of seeds exhausts the lilac and makes it almost impossible to flower in the year following that in which the seeds matured in too great abundance. This care, and that of ridding lilac bushes of the multitude of offshoots they produce each year, which are their main means of multiplication, is enough to obtain a splendid bloom every year. For some years now, Persian lilacs have been treated in a very special way, with the aim of regulating flowering. As soon as the flowers have wilted, we immediately remove not only the flowers, but everything on this shrub that gives the appearance of greenery; we give it a summer pruning as severe as winter pruning is for grafted roses. The gardener, in giving this pruning to Persian lilac, is counting on the vigor of the second sap in this shrub. In just a few days, Persian lilac pruned in this way starts to produce buds that, before the leaves fall, give it an elegant head, with all the terminal shoots giving their bouquet of fragrant flowers the following year. This method of pruning the Persian lilac was devised by M. Hardy, head gardener at the Luxembourg Gardens. A very similar shrub to the lilac, syringa josihœa, whose flowers are less spread out and a little darker than those of the lilac, blooming a fortnight later, can give rise to a curious experiment that can easily be repeated at very little cost. After a year or two, when the vegetation of the graft seems vigorous enough, a fine species of lilac can be grafted onto the josikœa. In a few years, the lilac forms a clump as floriferous as if it were free-standing; the result is flowering shrubs at a fairly high elevation above the ground which, in an avenue of very tall trees, where lilacs grafted in this way alternate with elms, beeches, lime trees or horse chestnuts, produce a most picturesque effect in spring. Spirea arborescens, of which a charming pink-flowered variety contrasts with other species with satin-white flowers, are ornamental both for their flowers and for their foliage, which is quite similar to that of Virginia sumac. Most spireas produce flowers exclusively at the end of the current year's twigs; the shrub must be pruned every year after leaf fall, in order to obtain as many young flowering shoots as possible for the following spring; spireas grow in all types of soil and exposure. Lindley's Buddleya (fig. 100), less well known and less Fig. 100. Lindley's Buddleya, less cultivated than it deserves to be, stands up well to the ordinary winters of the Paris climate; if the shrub freezes during very harsh winters, it is quickly replanted by offshoots from the stump that does not freeze. The slender, gracefully drooping stems end in long spikes of dark purple flowers. Lindley's buddleya grows well in a box on a balcony, providing pleasant, fragrant shade all summer long. Some of the most graceful shrubs, whose beauty lies in the abundance of their dark-red berries (cotoneaster), blood-red berries (various species of holly) and fiery berries (crotaegus pyracantha), can be combined with flowering shrubs in the formation of flowerbeds. All are equally hardy and undemanding in terms of soil quality. Ornamental trees. - Ornamental trees complete the series of outdoor perennials. Some are prized for their flowers, such as the catalpa, the tulip tree, the numerous varieties of robinia or false acacia, and the paulownia imperialis, or Ki-Ri from Japan, a modern introduction; others, such as the plane tree, the virgil tree, the maples and the sycamore, owe their place in groves to the shade provided by their abundant foliage. Still others are cultivated for the quirkiness of their foliage, with its unusual shapes in the case of Gingko bilobo, and its strange color in the case of black beech. Two other series, that of coniferous trees, whose leaves are replaced by needles, and that of weeping trees, with naturally drooping branches, also deserve a place in landscape gardens. The art of designing and creating parks or landscaped gardens, the practice of which constitutes the profession of the garden architect, includes the construction of terraces, kiosks, bridges and fountains; the placement of vases, statues and monumental columns; and the digging of lakes, basins, water features and artificial rivers. - In medium-sized gardens, where the picturesque style is adapted to a small space, a variety of ornamental trees are frequently planted in isolated situations for specific purposes. Trees with drooping branches, such as the weeping ash and saphora, are planted on the highest points of the site, where they offer a view of the landscape, as are trees with dense foliage that can act as cradles, such as the thornless locust and the umbrella medlar. Just one of these fully-grown trees covers a large enough space to accommodate a table and chairs. At the water's edge, the weeping willow is virtually indispensable. Depending on the layout of the land and the size of the garden, flowering trees such as catalpa, paulonia, tulip tree, cercis (Judas tree), and other trees remarkable for their shape and the elegance of their foliage, are planted on the edges of large lawns. These trees are usually given over to the natural course of their vegetation; they therefore grow in their natural form, which makes them particularly suitable as models for painters, especially when they are framed in massifs composed in such a way as to highlight their advantages. The tulip tree only grows and flowers abundantly when it is surrounded by other trees as tall or taller than it, which protect it from violent gales; of all the inclemencies of the seasons, this is the one it fears most. With this precaution, tulip trees can be successfully planted even north of the Seine valley. The catalpa should only be planted in situations as well sheltered as those suitable for the tulip tree. It's not that the catalpa fears the cold; but its wood is excessively brittle; the slightest gust of wind causes it to split from top to bottom, and frequently strips it of its most beautiful branches, all the more easily because its broad leaves give the wind a lot of grip, and contribute to making its wood more fragile. The paulownia and the Judas tree can be planted in all types of soil and exposure; their wood is as strong as that of the catalpa is fragile. A few large fruit trees, especially apples and apricots, can also be planted in isolation in positions where their shape can contribute to the picturesque effect of the garden as a whole. PART FIVE ORANGE AND GREENHOUSE PLANTS CHAPTER XLVI Orangery plants. Orangery. - Orangery plants. - Orange trees. - Growing in the open air in the South of France. - For its fruit. - For its flowers. - Multiplication. - Sowing lemon tree seeds. - Raising the seedling. - Slot grafting à la Pontoise. - Twig cuttings. - Leaf cuttings. - Marcottage. - Growing orange trees in pots. - In crates. - Renewing the soil. - Exposure to the open air. - The great Frederick's orange trees in Potsdam. - Orange tree products. - Flower harvesting. - Pruning. - Pinching. - Species and varieties. - Growing small orange trees in the apartment. - Pomegranate. - Layering. - Grafting. - Species and varieties. - Cultivation care. The orangery is a type of construction that has been all but abandoned in modern horticulture. It no longer meets the needs of modern gardening as completely as the cold greenhouse does, which is why the cold greenhouse is constantly preferred (see part one, chap. Vl). Nonetheless, many old country homes still have orangeries that can't be put to any other use. It is therefore essential to take a look at orangery plants, i.e. those which, in the Paris climate, can overwinter in an orangery and remain there in good condition. The list is not very long; it includes orange, pomegranate, myrtle, oleander, kalmia and the varieties of azalea and rhododendron that cannot overwinter in the open air. ORANGE. - The cultivation of

In France, open-air orange growing is only possible in a fairly narrow area, parallel to the Mediterranean coast, particularly in the Var department. Vast plantations are found around Hyères, where the fruit is abundant but of mediocre quality, and around Grasse, where the flowers are harvested for distillation. Most of the orange blossom water consumed in France is distilled in the Var department. In the happy climate of Provence, all the orange tree needs is light rather than strong soil, and sufficient irrigation to prevent it from suffering from drought. In exceptionally harsh winters, Provençal orange trees sometimes freeze, but never so completely that they can't be pulled back onto their main branches and restored in a few years by the natural vigor of their vegetation. In the rest of France, orange trees need an orangery to spend the winter safely, which can be replaced by a fairly spacious room on the first floor, with large windows facing south. Multiplication. - Only professional horticulturists in Paris and the major cities of the départements that do not belong to the southern region are regularly involved in propagating orange trees. This branch of gardening is highly advantageous, because the orange tree is one of those shrubs that everyone loves, and that everyone can obtain at a moderate price by buying it young to give themselves the pleasure of watching it grow. We have varieties which, while flowering abundantly, grow very little, and are suitable for amateurs with little space to spare. Nevertheless, sedentary people with enough patience, ease and leisure can propagate the orange tree themselves, to enjoy watching it go through all the phases of its growth under their guidance. The orange tree is propagated by sowing its seedlings, grafting, cuttings and marcottes; sowing and grafting are the two most common means of propagating the orange tree. In Paris, several of the horticultural establishments that specialize in the cultivation of orange trees bring in seedlings ready for grafting, or already grafted a year ago, from the vicinity of Genoa and Nice, in the Kingdom of Sardinia; they are also beginning to receive seedlings from several parts of the Var department. The others mainly sow lemon seeds. The reasons for this preference are based on the considerable quantity of lemons used every year by Paris confectioners, which makes it very easy for gardeners to obtain the pepins they need for their seedlings. Lemon seedlings can be grafted onto any variety of orange or lemon tree. For growing orange trees in pots or crates, they perform better when grafted onto lemon trees than when free-standing. Only three or four varieties of orange can be propagated free-standing by sowing their fruit pips; all the others, especially the most sought-after in the horticultural trade, must be grafted. Lemon or orange seeds are sown, but lemon seeds are preferred, in very light soil, composed of half leaf compost and half heather. Many gardeners sow immediately in orange potting soil, but this is too strong for the young seedling, and it is difficult for it to form good roots. The pots in which the seedlings are sown, either singly in small pots, or two or three in each medium-sized pot, are buried in potting soil in a warm layer; they spend their first and second winters there; they are given air only during fine weather; they are not fully exposed to the open air, from spring to autumn, until their third and sometimes fourth years. Grafting. - Some orange trees can be grafted as early as the end of their first year; others can be successively grafted from the second to the fifth year, depending on the height at which you intend to start forming a regular head. Those belonging to dwarf or underdeveloped species can be grafted at a very young age, with the head starting at a height of 25 to 30 centimetres above the soil of the pot; those which are to become first-generation orange trees can only be grafted much later, with the head only being established at a height of 1.5 to 2 metres. Orange trees can be grafted in slits; the most common grafting method is known as Pontoise grafting. This is a side-slit graft, in which the subject, inserted in the axil of a leaf in place of an eye, is a branch of roughly the same diameter as the subject. This branch, detached from a fully-formed tree, can be chosen with a full complement of flowering buds; the graft is no less successful. This is how we obtain these charming little orange trees covered with flowers, in very small sizes. The ease with which they can be propagated by grafting à la Pontoise means that professional gardeners can supply them at very reasonable prices. Young grafted orange trees should be tied only slightly tightly, and loosened as soon as the graft is well attached to the tree. Since the sap is never completely stationary in orange trees, especially in young plants raised from seed and kept constantly under a warm frame, they can be grafted in any season, but the most favorable time is when the sap is most active, in spring. Horticultural enthusiasts who do not have an orangery and are forced to live in a large city, and to make do with a portable greenhouse, either cold or heated by a spirit-of-wine lamp, can, according to the preceding indications, sow pepins of lemons or oranges bigurrades, graft the subjects at Pontoise, at two years at the latest, and see them flower abundantly in relation to their small size, the year following that in which they were grafted. Cuttings. - Orange trees can be cuttings in the same soil used for seedlings, using as cuttings not only young twigs, but even simple leaves whose stalks are buried and which eventually root and give rise to a young tree. This method is not suitable for professional horticulturists, as young orange trees grown from cuttings are slow-growing and generally lack vigor. However, amateur horticulturists can use cuttings to build up a whole collection of orange trees, which remain small for a long time; for those who only have a small room to use as an orangery, this is an advantage rather than a drawback. Layering. - By attaching a side-split pot filled with heather soil to a sufficiently sturdy stake, you can use it to marcott an orange branch placed in such a way that it can be removed without destroying the harmonious shape of the tree. This method of propagating the orange tree is even less common than cuttings, although a few enthusiasts use it from time to time, as they have had strong enough plants for one year, which flower immediately and do not need to be grafted. Growing orange trees in crates. - Small dwarf orange trees, or species that are slow to grow and need to be kept small for a long time, thrive just as well in pots as in crates, provided their roots are not buried too deeply, they are watered generously in summer and very sparingly in winter, and the soil in the pots is changed when the condition of the shrub indicates that it is exhausted. Larger orange trees need crates with a capacity proportionate to the volume of the tree's roots. These crates are built in such a way that their sides can be removed at will, one after the other, so that, without moving the tree or removing all its roots at the same time, the soil can be partially renewed as often as necessary to keep it in good vegetative condition. In large orangeries, the date on which the soil was renewed is marked on one side of the box, so that the gardener cannot lose track of it. In Paris, orange trees are put out in the open on May 15th, and put back in the orangery on October 15th. These dates are not the same south or north of the Seine valley; each gardener must act according to the local climate. We know that in Potsdam, in 1755, the great Frederick lost all his orange trees to a late frost, having forced his gardener to put them out, against his advice, a little earlier than usual. So whenever the king gave his gardener an order that the latter didn't think was reasonable in terms of his competence, the gardener simply replied: "Sire, we had some beautiful orange trees in Potsdam in 1755! Cleanliness is the orange tree's first need at any age; the leaves of young orange trees must be frequently washed on top and underneath, one by one, with a wet sponge to loosen the dust; large orange trees receive frequent basins given from bottom to top, so that the underside of the leaves is washed like the top. Produce. - Well-cared-for orange trees produce a fairly valuable product through the sale of their flowering branches for bouquets, and of their detached flowers, which confectioners and distillers pay a fairly high price for. When in bloom, the flowers must be harvested every day, and even twice a day. In the south of France, this harvest is virtually uninterrupted; orange trees here bear ripe fruit, green fruit and blossoms almost all year round. Orange trees whose main product is flowers for distillation are not allowed to bear fruit. Pruning. - An orange tree that is not pruned, simply because it is in a box and its roots cannot extend more on one side than the other, naturally takes on a symmetrical shape; for it, as for all possible trees, the roots make the branches, and conversely, the branches make the roots. Unless the small size of the orangery or of the premises in which it is located makes it imperative to do so, the young orange tree should be left to grow on its own, merely removing the languid or superfluous branches that needlessly clutter up the inside of its head and prevent air and light from penetrating freely. Once the orange trees have grown, it becomes necessary to prune them moderately every year, to keep them in the desired shape, and to obtain a reasonable quantity of young flowering shoots. This general pruning of orange trees should only be carried out a month or so before they are due to be returned to the orangery; this time, in the Paris climate, coincides fairly regularly with the middle of September. However, regardless of the September pruning, if there are important gaps to be filled in the head of the orange tree, and it would be useful to have some of its outer branches branched for this purpose, these branches should be pinched off as soon as the tree is brought out into the open air, around the middle of May, as this is the time when the tree's vegetation is most active. The above applies only to large, fully-formed orange trees; small orange trees, it cannot be stressed too strongly, must not be pruned. Species and varieties. - In countries where oranges and lemons are widely grown as major fruit trees, there are a multitude of varieties. The gardener to His Majesty the King of Naples has drawn up a monograph containing over two hundred and fifty perfectly distinct varieties. In France, orange groves admit some twenty species, divided into the two main series of orange trees, the most widespread of which are: the orange tree proper, the myrtle-leaved Chinese orange tree, the grapefruit orange tree and the sour orange tree; and the lemon trees, to which the lime and citron trees belong. All should be propagated, managed and pruned according to the principles set out above. For small orangeries, the two most pleasant species to grow are the bigaradier, which flowers abundantly and whose fruit ripens quite often in orangeries, and the small myrtle-leaf orange, whose green oranges, when half their size, are candied in sugar and brandy, and highly esteemed by a large class of consumers, under the name of chinois. If you wish to keep a few small orange trees in an apartment, keep them close to a window during the winter and turn them over frequently, so that they successively receive light from all sides, the deprivation of which makes them suffer greatly. Avoid leaving them in a room where there is a fire; in general, it's always warm enough in an orangery or similar, as long as the frost doesn't penetrate. Otherwise, if the temperature is too mild, the orange tree's sap is set in motion, and in spring it produces only weak shoots and aborted flowering. During the summer, orange trees that have overwintered in an apartment are placed in the open air on a balcony; if they are in pots, it is important that the outside walls of the pots do not come into direct contact with the sun's rays, otherwise the roots that touch the inside wall of the pot will be exposed to too much heat, which could cause the death of the young orange trees. A piece of board, placed between the pot and the balcony, prevents this excessive heat from occurring. GRENADIER. - In our southern departments, the pomegranate is a very hardy shrub, growing naturally as a bush and producing a multitude of offshoots from the foot, which serve to multiply the plant. In the climate of Paris, it is an orangery shrub cultivated for its abundant flowering. The pomegranate tree can be propagated by sowing pomegranate seeds, or by separating offshoots from the stump; but these two methods are seldom used, as the offshoots do not vegetate vigorously enough. Pomegranates lend themselves better than any other shrub to this method of propagation; the formation of roots on the marcotte branch should be encouraged by binding with brass wire, which forms a strangled marcotte. The marcotte can be placed in pots in spring and weaned at the end of summer, so that the young plants can overwinter in suitable pots or crates in the orangery. Three species of pomegranate are grown in orangeries: the single-flowered red, whose small, very acid fruits sometimes ripen in the Paris climate; the double-flowered red, the most widely grown; and the double-flowered white, less common than the other two. Among professional gardeners, the single-flowered pomegranate is multiplied by layering, to provide a large number of plants for grafting the other two. The pomegranate is grafted in slits, but not in the Pontoise style, a type of graft to which its mode of vegetation does not lend itself. It grows perfectly well in orange soil, and requires the same cultivation care and winter temperature as the orange tree; it is pruned only to remove dead wood; no attempt should be made to give it a symmetrical shape; it naturally forms an irregular, but graceful and very flowering head. Its life span, like that of the orange tree, is indefinite. CHAPTER XLVII Orangery plants (continued). Myrtle. - Its temperament in the wild. - Modifications it undergoes in the orangery. - Multiplication from seed. - Second flowering in winter. - Watering. - Ponding. - Repotting. - Oleander. - Vegetation in its natural climate. - Winter sleep. - Multiplication. - Sowing. - Cuttings in soil. - Cuttings in water. - Wine lees mixed with oleander box soil. - Grafting choice species onto common species - Kalmia. - Multiplication - Cuttings. - From seedlings. - Seedlings. - Forced cultivation. - Open-ground azaleas. - Indian azaleas. - Summer cultivation in the open air. - Rhododendrums. - Seedling propagation. - Transplant at two years. - Four years old. - Suitable soil. - Repotting. MYRTE. - Myrtle, the common species of which grows wild on the uncultivated hills of our departments bordering the Mediterranean, is classified as an orangery plant, although some of the most beautiful species, notably the small-leaved myrtle and the cottony myrtle, belong to the temperate greenhouse. Single-flowered varieties can be propagated by sowing their seeds, which must be brought in from the south of France, because in the Paris climate, myrtle hedges rarely produce fertile seeds. The double-flowered species are propagated from marcottes and cuttings, both of which are slow to root. In the orangery, the myrtle never reaches large dimensions; but it flowers abundantly and for a long time, and when it is not in flower, it stands out for the elegance and very pleasant resinous scent of its glossy foliage. In its native country, myrtle grows on stony slopes where it can withstand very high temperatures and continuous droughts lasting several months. In the orangery, however, its temperament seems to change completely. It thrives only in a mixture of equal parts of heather soil and good 24-garden soil; in summer, it needs an open position and full southern exposure, so that it never gets too hot. It requires frequent watering, even in winter, and when its roots are imprisoned in a box, it cannot withstand the deprivation of water that it tolerates so well between the cracks in the rocks of its native land. This anomaly is due to the fact that on the shores of the Mediterranean, if the heat is excessive during the day, the dew at night is very abundant and compensates to some extent for the persistent drought. In orangeries where it lacks this resource, myrtle needs to be watered more often during the winter than other plants, whose sap must more or less slumber from autumn to spring. Often, after the first flowering in summer, myrtle produces a large number of young shoots which are covered with buds, so that it is ready to give a second flowering as beautiful as the first, when the arrival of the first cold weather means it can no longer be left in the open air. In the orangery, the buds usually fall off before they have had a chance to bloom; but if the myrtle is ready to bloom again and has been placed in a temperate greenhouse, or simply in an inhabited apartment where a good fire is kept burning, it will give its second bloom at the beginning of winter, provided it is not allowed to suffer from thirst. When a gentle rain falls, it can be left to receive it for a quarter of an hour to clean its foliage of the dust that clings to it. From time to time, it's a good idea to place the pot or crate at an angle above the washing stone, and give it a drenching with a watering can with a sheaf pierced with very fine holes. This removes the dust clinging to the foliage without over-wetting the soil in the pot or crate, which would necessarily happen if, while the gardener was giving the myrtle this drenching, the shrub remained in its natural position. As a young myrtle grows, it should be given a larger pot, and finally placed in a crate proportionate to its size, with the soil renewed every two years at the latest. It is all the more necessary to place the myrtle in the best conditions for it to vegetate energetically, as the more young shoots it produces, the more it flowers. When the soil in its box is exhausted and not renewed in good time, it doesn't die, but it stops flowering. Myrtle does not want to be pruned; if, in order to add one of its flowering branches to a bouquet, or to regularize its shape, we deem it necessary to cut off a branch at its head, this procedure should only be used with the greatest care, as it is slow to repair the losses of this kind that we have caused it during its flowering period. ROSE LAUREL. - This shrub, one of the most graceful of all those growing wild in southern countries, has given rise by sowing and cultivation under various conditions to a fairly large number of beautiful varieties with single, semi-double, full flowers in various shades of pink and purple, pure white, light yellow and orange-yellow. In all hot climates, starting with the south of France, the oleander, known to botanists as nerium, grows abundantly by the water's edge, where it flowers for several months at a time; its roots, which are almost as long as its stems, plunge into dry stream beds throughout the summer, seeking the abundant moisture it cannot do without at great depths. In winter, the roots and lower part of the stems are in the water, and do all the better for it. These indications are useful to the gardener for the prosperity of nerium collections in orangeries. The oleander needs the same soil as the orange tree, and very abundant watering throughout the time it spends in the open air. In winter, on the other hand, it's in the gardener's interest to give the oleander in the orangery the most complete plant sleep possible, because in its native country, its vegetation doesn't suffer any downtime. To this end, once the plant has returned home, watering is gradually reduced, and eventually water is given only once a week, just enough to prevent it from drying out. When the time comes for the outside temperature to allow it to be put back in the open air, the oleander needs to be watered a little less infrequently; it then seems to come back to life as it emerges from the orangery, and its young shoots from the previous year flower abundantly. Oleander is easily propagated by sowing its seeds, taking cuttings from its young shoots and grafting. Seedlings used only in the hope of conquering new varieties should be made with seeds from our southern departments; several white and yellow-flowered varieties reproduce fairly constantly by sowing their seeds. Sow in spring, in heather soil; the plant should be pricked out very young, singly, in pots deeper than they are wide, filled with orange-tree soil. Cuttings taken in spring, under a frame in a northern exposure, or at least in a shady spot, are always successful; they are potted as soon as their renewed growth indicates that they have taken root. In towns and cities, if you only have one or two oleanders to keep on your balcony during the summer months, you can propagate them by cuttings using the following highly successful procedure. Around mid-April, the shoots which do not interfere with the shape of the shrub are cut off at the level of the main stem; each of these shoots is immersed in a bottle full of water, so that the tip of the cutting does not touch the bottom of the bottle. Water being, as we have seen, the indispensable element in the vegetation of the oleander in the wild, the cuttings are quick to form roots, especially if the bottles are placed on a windowsill facing south. As soon as they have sufficient roots, the cuttings are planted in pots deeper than they are wide, filled with soil mixed with compost; they should be watered generously throughout the summer, and placed in larger pots as soon as their development indicates the need. In Paris, at the wine warehouse and at the door of several wine merchants, passers-by admire magnificent bushes of oleanders; the vigor of these shrubs and the truly extraordinary abundance of their blooms are due to a small quantity of wine lees mixed with the soil where their roots grow. Wherever this type of amendment can be obtained and mixed, in moderate doses, with the soil in which oleanders grow, it gives them remarkable strength and abundance of bloom. Grafting is mainly used to propagate the more sought-after varieties on the common species. Oleander is successfully grafted with herbaceous split grafts in spring, when vegetation is most active. In the south of France, enthusiasts whose gardens border on a stream, which is often lined with a wide border of wild oleanders, graft semi-double and double oleanders, as well as yellow, white and orange-flowered varieties, onto these shrubs in this way, creating clumps of mixed shades with the richest ornamental effect. KALMIA - It's a mistake for the most popular gardening treatises to list the kalmia as being very hardy and able to overwinter in the open air in the Paris climate. This beautiful shrub, so remarkable for the profusion of its white flowers washed with pink, should be considered as belonging to the orangery. Those that are planted in the open air in heathery soil don't always freeze; but the first harsh winter takes them away when, after a great deal of effort and care, you've succeeded in obtaining elegant, highly-flowering bushes. Kalmia can only be left outside until late autumn, and then brought back into the open early in the spring. Kalmia can be propagated by both marcots and seedlings. Marcots are rarely used, as they take two long years to root. Sowing, which is almost the only method in use, is done in summer, as soon as the seed is ripe, as it immediately loses its germinative properties and must be sown as soon as it has reached maturity. As the seed is 24. very fine, it is only mixed with the surface of the soil, which should be made up of equal parts of sand and heather. It is much more sensitive to the cold than fully-formed shrubs, so its first winter is spent in a frame, and it is gradually acclimatized to air contact by lifting the frames. The three distinct species of kalmia - the broad-leafed kalmia (latifolia), the most commonly cultivated, the narrow-leafed kalmia (augustifolia), and the glaucous or rosemary-leaved kalmia - multiply in the same way; these shrubs are cultivated in the same way as the orange tree; they naturally take on a good shape and need no pruning. The last two species, brought into a temperate or warm greenhouse in late autumn, lend themselves easily to forced cultivation and are in flower before the end of winter. AZALEAS. - The azalea genus comprises two clear-cut divisions, the first of which contains only ground-hugging shrubs, with flowers that usually appear before the leaves, and in which we find all shades of pink and red, from pink-washed white to bright red, and all shades of yellow, from straw-yellow to orange-yellow. These shrubs require pure heather soil and a northern or very shady exposure; their cultivation is the same as that of all outdoor shrubs; they produce a beautiful effect in spring with their abundant, early flowering, especially when combined with later-flowering evergreen shrubs. The second division includes all azaleas that do not overwinter in the open air. In a pinch, these shrubs belong in the cold greenhouse; but when you have an orangery large enough to give azaleas a place near the windows, so that they are never deprived for too long of the light they need, they overwinter well in the orangery. Those who have only a small number of azaleas can also winter them in a well-lit room, where the temperature should not fall below zero, nor rise above 3 or 4 degrees. Indian azaleas are easy to grow if you have land where they can spend the whole of the summer season, from the beginning of May to the end of September, in the open air in heather soil. In this case, you don't need to take any more care of them than you do of the other shrubs in the open ground, until you put them back in pots or crates of the right size, to bring them into the orangery. When, for lack of space in the garden, you have to keep Indian azaleas constantly in pots, the soil in the pots must be changed every year. The soil in which they thrive best is made up of equal parts of heather soil and leaf compost. Each time they are repotted, the bottom of the pots is lined with a layer of broken pot fragments, to ensure rapid drainage of excess water from watering. Although Indian azaleas need to be watered frequently, prolonged root growth in an excessively humid environment causes them serious damage; this is one of the reasons why these shrubs are much more vigorous and give a much more beautiful bloom when planted in summer in the open air. The flowers of Indian azaleas are so abundant that, during the blooming period, which lasts no less than a month, the foliage is completely masked. White, brick-red, bright red and light violet are the dominant shades of Indian azalea flowers. These well-cared-for shrubs can live for a very long time, and can be pruned into all sorts of shapes, without becoming less floriferous. RHODODENDRUM. - The rhododendrum genus, like the azalea genus, is rich in species and varieties that are perfectly suited to overwintering in the open air in the Paris climate; but it also contains a large number of the most sought-after, which cannot do without shelter during the winter. Professional horticulturists who grow large numbers of rhododendrons devote a cold greenhouse to them; those who have only a small number can treat them as orangery shrubs. These shrubs produce such a beautiful effect in gardens, especially those belonging to the species known as Rhododendrum arboreum, which has produced a multitude of sub-varieties through cultivation, that their multiplication and breeding form a distinct branch of horticulture. Many large horticultural establishments are devoted exclusively to rhododendrons. This shrub is propagated by sowing its seeds, which often produce beautiful new plants; the seed of the hardiest of the rhododendrums grown in the open ground, rhododendrum ponticum, is also sown, and can be grafted onto all the other varieties, so that those that rarely produce fertile seeds, and those whose flowers we fear will not reproduce unaltered by sowing, can be propagated at will. Successfully propagating rhododendrons from seed requires a great deal of perseverance. Seedlings are sown in terrines filled with finely sifted heather soil. As the terra cotta in the terrines is always more or less porous, they are immersed in a tub full of water, so that the water in the tub is constantly flush with the level of the heather soil in the terrines. The humidity that seeps through the thickness of the terrines is enough to ensure that the heather soil, on whose surface the rhododendron seeds are spread without being covered, is always at the degree of freshness needed to determine germination. The seeds must be sown lightly, as young plants of any variety cannot be transplanted before their second year, when they are still small and very delicate. They are transplanted for the first time, in rows only a few centimetres apart, in beds laid out to receive boxes covered with glass frames at the start of winter. The young rhododendrums spend a further two years in this situation, after which they are transplanted a second time, either in pots, to be returned to the orangery in winter, or in heather soil, 30 centimetres apart in all directions. Those that don't overwinter in the orangery need a shelter of glass frames in winter, which are removed in spring. They only begin to grow rapidly after this second transplanting, after which they show their first flowers in their fifth or sixth year. Rhododendrons need good, loosely-packed but substantial heather soil, renewed at least every two years for those grown in pots. The orangery, in addition to the shrubs whose cultivation has just been described, can in a pinch house in winter the less delicate species of fuchsia, pelargonium and several other plants destined to appear in the parterre in summer; but these plants are really only at home in the cold greenhouse. CHAPTER XLVIII Cold greenhouse plants. - The Camellia. The cold greenhouse. - Plants it can accommodate. - Its superiority over the orangery. - Camellia. - Its introduction into Europe. - First camellia introduced in France. - Climate in which camellia grows in the ground. - Propagation. - Sowing. - Conditions for success. - Their results. - Cutting. - How to do it. - Cutting in the greenhouse. - Faucheux graft, the only one used for camellia. - Favourable time for this graft. - Camellia soil. - Repotting. - Watering. - Water temperature for watering camellias. Pruning. - Not necessary for camellias in pots. - Growing care. - Ponding. - Thorough cleaning. - Aeration of camellias. - Need for a humid atmosphere in summer. - Growing conditions in their native country. The cold greenhouse. - Nothing proves better the progress made by modern horticulture in the cultivation of exotic ornamental plants, than the incessant increase in the number of cold greenhouses in Europe, wherever gardening is in honor, both among amateurs and professional gardeners. As the temperament and mode of vegetation of the most beautiful plants borrowed from the flora of warm countries have been better studied, we have come to recognize this long-unnoticed truth, that most plants raised in temperate greenhouses can overwinter in the cold greenhouse, and that to see them flourish and bloom, it is sufficient to place them out of the reach of frost in winter and sunburn in summer. As a result of this important advance in gardening practice, a whole assortment of the most beautiful ornamental plants have been able to migrate from the temperate greenhouse to the cold greenhouse. This revolution in one of the most interesting branches of horticulture led to another, which we have already I 2 - 2 2 had occasion to mention. Thanks to the fall in the price of iron and glass, the building blocks of the cold greenhouse, the number of plants that could be accommodated during the winter months more than doubled, at the same cost, by substituting the cold greenhouse for the orangery. In addition to the assortment plants, each of which has its own special purpose, the cold greenhouse admits, as the essential base of its plant population, a certain number of collection plants which not only contribute to its decoration, but some of which, from spring to autumn, increase the gardener's resources for ornamenting the parterre; these are: camellia, fuchsia, calceolaria, pelargonium and cineraria. CAMELLIA. - When Father Kamel, a German missionary, introduced the shrub that bears his name to Europe in 1739, no one foresaw the future of the camellia or the role it was destined to play in European greenhouses. It was a fairly pretty shrub, with a simple, attractive red flower that was not in itself much superior to that of the Japanese quince. It was not until much later, first in England, then in France, Belgium and the rest of Europe, that the camellia, so long relegated to the category of plants cultivated solely for the study of botany, rose to the forefront of collector's shrubs. This is due to the graceful shape it assumes naturally and without pruning, to the beauty of its lustrous evergreen foliage, and to the length of its flowering period, which can be enjoyed from late November to late April. Except for the fragrance, which the camellia flower completely lacks, it would be worthy of rivaling the rose. The first camellia imported into France was sent from England to Madame Bonaparte, wife of the First Consul, at the time of the Peace of Amiens. Tamponnet, gardener at Malmaison, received a small branch from the hands of the future Empress Josephine, and took a cutting from it. It was a double-flowered white camellia (alba flore pleno), the ancestor of countless posterity. This camellia is still admired today, preserved as a precious souvenir in the establishment of Mr. H. Courtois, Tamponnet's successor. The camellia belongs to the cold greenhouse only in departments outside what is known as the olive limit; between this limit and the sea, it lives and flowers in the open ground in places sheltered from the north winds, particularly in the Var. Southern Italy returns it to the climate of its native land, where it can grow to a height of seven or eight meters; trees of this size can be seen in the gardens of the palace of the King of Naples at Caserta. In Italy in particular, the primitive camellia with its single red flower and several semi-double varieties bear fruits the size of small apricots, filled with fertile seeds. The eight hundred camellia varieties in our collections have been conquered by the persistent sowing of these seeds. Botanists consider some of these varieties, including Camellia reticulato, to be distinct species. Seedlings are not the most common method of propagation, even in the south of France; they are only sown in the hope, often realized, of increasing the number of varieties of merit. Cuttings, marcottes and grafting are the most widely adopted methods of propagation. Camellia seeds are sown in heather soil, which must be kept constantly moist, otherwise the seed will not germinate. When a large quantity of seeds is available, there's no risk of wasting effort by multiplying seedlings. Once the young plants have produced their first flower, even if it has no particular merit, they can still be used by grafting onto the most sought-after varieties. Camellia cuttings. - Camellia can only be successfully cut from the previous year's shoots. These branches rarely belong to the choice varieties that we generally prefer to propagate by grafting; only grafted camellias are to be found in amateur collections or those of professional gardeners. The sole purpose of cuttings is therefore to propagate plants suitable for grafting. Nevertheless, some old varieties whose merit has not been diminished by the passage of time, including Camellia fimbriata and Camellia alba flore pleno, multiply very well from cuttings. Horticulturists and hobbyists who have large collections of camellias to maintain in their entirety cultivate single-flowered camellias for this special purpose, pruning them to produce a large number of young shoots which are cut off each spring and used as cuttings. To save space, these cuttings are planted close together in terrines filled with good heather soil; if this soil seems too compact, fine silica sand is mixed in, in the proportion of a quarter or even a third. The terrines, filled with Camellia cuttings, are covered with a glass cloche and placed either under a shaded frame, or in the best-shaded part of the cold greenhouse, as the slightest sunburn, or even prolonged contact with bright light, would compromise the success of the operation. Ladies who take pleasure in cultivating a few pretty exotic plants in a small portable cold greenhouse, a charming piece of furniture that can be placed on a pedestal table or console in a living room, can cut out shoots from the previous year from a single camellia with assured success; after forty to forty-five days, they will be rooted and begin to grow. As soon as the camellia cuttings show signs of good root development, they are transplanted individually into small-diameter pots, where they grow until they are ready for grafting. When there is no particular reason to hurry, it's best to wait to graft Camellia cuttings until they have reached a certain stage of development; they feed their graft better and form better shrubs than those that have been grafted too young. Grafting. - The various grafting methods used to graft 25 camellia have been successively abandoned in favor of side grafting, known to gardeners as Faucheux grafting. A notch is made in the axil of a leaf so that a branch can be inserted, wedge-shaped at the base, leaving the upper part of the plant until after grafting. Only then is the head removed and used as a cutting. The Faucheux graft differs from the Pontoise graft in only one respect. In Pontoise grafting, the subject and the graft are of the same size; the graft is loaded with flowers and buds; in Faucheux grafting, the subject is much stronger than the graft, which consists of a shoot from the previous year, still a long way from the time when it is due to start flowering; moreover, there is no difference between these two methods of grafting. Camellias are only grafted in spring, when the sap is most active; the grafted plants are placed under cloches, either in a cold greenhouse or under a frame, until the graft begins to grow; the young camellias are then repotted into pots larger than those in which they were grown. In general gardening treatises and in special treatises on camellia cultivation, we find various recipes for camellia soil, including all kinds of ingredients. All these recipes prescribe that the mixtures should be allowed to age before use, with the result that they revert to a simple mixture of soil and potting compost, because after a certain length of time, any fertilizer will inevitably reduce to potting compost. It's quicker to mix a third of leaf compost directly with a third of powdered charcoal and a third of good garden soil. Camellia exhausts soil very quickly, and imperiously requires rich, very substantial soil. The best way to satisfy this requirement is to incorporate a high dose of non-disinfected human fertilizer into the compost prepared for potting camellias. The usual proportion is one tenth to one eighth of the total mass. When this fertilizer has been properly incorporated into the compost and reworked several times over a three-month interval, it no longer gives off any bad odors; it imparts extraordinary vigor to the camellias. Compost fertilized with human manure must be renewed every two years; this means of maintaining camellia vegetation is preferable to manure broths prepared with cow dung, sheep dung, or a small quantity of guano diluted in water; waterings of this nature are very fertilizing, but too short-lived. June is the best time of year for repotting fully-formed camellias, which are awaiting flowering. Watering with plain water, which the camellia needs more or less all year round, should be given with water at the temperature of the soil in the pots. If too cold water is used to water camellias in autumn, while the flowering buds are forming, the buds will detach and fall off; the frequent bud drop of apartment-grown camellias often has no other cause. Over-abundant flowering is very tiring for the camellia; it often happens that in a group of three or four buds attached to the same branch, not a single one is able to open. In such cases, we must not be afraid to remove part of the buds to save the rest. But, as the camellia stalk is extremely delicate, it would be dangerous to detach any of them; they would almost all fall off. What remains after this removal dries out and falls off, without compromising the buds that are due to flower.
