Pruning. - When camellias are raised in pots or crates, they can be left to do as they please, as long as they are well-managed; their natural shape, which they take on of their own accord, is as good as any that can be made to take on artificially. Those planted in full heather soil in the Winter Garden can be pruned into any shape you wish; their generous vegetation lends itself to pruning with perfect docility (see Winter Garden, chap. XLIX). Camellias, grown in large crates, are usually trained in a pyramid shape according to the principles described for pruning trees with seeded fruit. Cultivation care. - The Camellia's highly active vegetation is sustained almost as much by its foliage, which decomposes atmospheric air in order to appropriate its principles, as by its roots themselves. It is therefore of the utmost importance for successful cultivation that both leaf surfaces be kept in a constant state of cleanliness, so that they can freely perform their functions. As long as camellias remain in the greenhouse, dust causes them little damage; but as soon as they leave the cold greenhouse to spend part of the summer in the open air, if the drought continues, they are infallibly invaded by dust. Watering with a fine-holed watering can only half-cleans camellia foliage; every now and then, all the leaves must be wiped off one by one with a fine, slightly damp sponge. Several treatises on camellia cultivation recommend thorough cleaning once a year. It's understandable that the gardener in charge of looking after a very large collection of camellias doesn't have the time to repeat such an operation often; however, if he keeps to a single cleaning a year, this work won't be of much use. But the amateur who has only a limited number, or who doesn't care about the well-being of his shrubs, even when he has a large number, must clean them thoroughly, leaf by leaf, as often as he deems necessary. Those living in an inhabited apartment, where the need to clean the rooms every day inevitably raises more or less dust, cannot be wiped less often than once a week, and not once a year. In the Paris climate, the Camellia, which is not much more sensitive to the cold than the orange tree, is not taken out of the greenhouse to be exposed to the open air until mid-July, when the annual shoots are almost fully grown. It has been observed, or thought to have been observed, that in the cold greenhouse, the young wood can acquire greater vigor, which contributes to making camellias more floriferous. If you have a well-sheltered area for airing your camellias, you can take them out in the first days of June, and they'll grow all the better for it. But amateurs with a collection of several hundred camellias, and professional growers with several thousand, are rightly afraid of exposing them to the open air during the stormy season, because a few minutes' hail can destroy everything. So they leave their camellias in the greenhouse all year round, limiting themselves to removing the glass panes during the summer months, which can be put back in place and covered with straw mats in no time at all when a violent storm approaches. According to the reports of botanical travellers, camellia in its native country forms coppice woods in cool, fertile soils; care is taken to prune it to make it suitable for its special purpose, which is to serve as broomsticks. This shows that a more or less humid atmosphere is favorable to it; for the more a foreign plant, whatever it may be, finds outside its home country the vegetation conditions of its native land, the more the success of its cultivation is assured. This is why, as long as the camellias live in the open air, either outside the cold greenhouse, or in the greenhouse with its panels removed, water must very often be sprinkled on the soil, in the spaces between the pots and crates, independently of the watering and bedding given to the shrubs themselves. Placed in this way in an atmosphere that is both warm and moist, they benefit visibly and prepare to flower profusely. CHAPTER XLVIX Cold greenhouse plants. - Fuchsia. - Pelargonlum. - Calceolar. - Cinerea. Fuchsia. - Their origin. - Hybrid varieties. - Edible fuchsia globosa berries. - Growing fuchsia in a cold greenhouse. - Its place in the flowerbed in summer. - Growing in a temperate greenhouse. - How to propagate. - Seedlings. - Cuttings. - Suitable soil. - Wintering temperature for fuchsias. - Pelargoniums. - Species that take their place in the parterre in summer. - In hanging vases. - Wintering in the cold greenhouse. - Calceolaria. - Multiplication. - Sowing. - Clump division. - Cineraria. - Separation of offspring. - Sowing. - Care to harvest the seed. - Nursery in the cold greenhouse. - Lantanas. - Verbena. - Mexican agerat. - Remarkable speedwell. - Henderson speedwell. FUCHSIA. - This pretty plant is one of the most remarkable examples of the power of hybridization to modify plants cultivated by man. For almost a century, its flower, which in shape resembles no other, remained stationary and very sparsely distributed in collections, never leaving the temperate greenhouse. Then, when species with very elongated flowers and large, woody stems were introduced, horticulturists and amateurs began to make hybrid crosses between these fuchsias and the old ones with short, rounded flowers, with the greatest success, especially nowadays; for the introduction of the most beautiful hybrid fuchsias goes back no further than 1837. The number of varieties, apart from the distinct species accepted by botanists, is several hundred, of which only two belong to the New Zealand flora; all the others come directly from South America, or have been obtained by hybridization (fig. 101). A curious and unexpected development in fuchsia cultivation The berries that follow the elegant flowers of Fig. 101. Fuchsia, Venus victrix. this shrub are bland in flavor and small in volume; they are not considered edible in either America or New Zealand. Twenty years ago, an English gardener emigrated to New Zealand; having settled near one of the emerging towns of this colony, whose climate corresponds fairly closely to that of the south of France, he was astonished to see a fuchsia globosa he had brought from Europe, and planted in an espalier in the open air, cover itself with red berries the size of a cerisc. He found the berries excellent, consulted the colony's doctor to make sure they were free of any harmful properties, and, on the doctor's word, began selling them to his neighbors. Today, fuchsia fruit is sold by the pound, like cherries, in markets all over New Zealand. It is highly probable that the same result could easily be achieved by growing fuchsia in the open air in Italy, Spain, southern France and Algeria. Although in special treatises and most horticultural works, fuchsia is classed as a temperate greenhouse plant, it is essentially a cold greenhouse plant which, like camellia, can be grown in the winter garden (see Winter Garden, chap. L). Passionate fans of the fuchsia genus never allow collections of these shrubs to leave the temperate greenhouse, where they are content to leave the panels open day and night during the summer to aerate the fuchsias. They believe, quite wrongly, that the shades of the corolla and colored calyx are darker and less delicate when the flowers bloom in the open air; our own observations have not confirmed this remark. Throughout the summer, in public gardens and in the most beautiful private gardens, groups of fuchsias globosa, serratifolia, fulgens and a few others can be seen, their pots buried in the flowerbeds of the parterre; these shrubs grow in strength and flower no less well there than in the cold greenhouse or the temperate greenhouse. It's not that, from a cultivation point of view, the custom of never putting fuchsias in the open air is to be blamed; it saves their very loose flowers from the danger of being blown off by strong winds, and the plants from being chopped up by hail during thunderstorms; it makes flowering, if not more beautiful, at least longer-lasting; it saves the trouble of moving the many shrubs that make up complete collections in and out of the garden; it is also very favorable for fuchsias when they have just been repotted. But it's worth warning the general public that, on the one hand, the cold greenhouse is sufficient for fuchsias, and that, on the other, it would be a mistake to deprive the flowerbed of one of its most decorative features in summer, by not burying potted fuchsias, which are perfectly at home in the open air throughout the summer. Fuchsia thrives in the soil we've indicated for camellia; however, you can remove the powdered charcoal from this compost and replace it with an equal proportion of good garden soil, when the latter is light rather than strong. Human fertilizer is no less useful for all varieties of fuchsia than it is for camellias; above all, it makes them flower abundantly and continuously until the end of summer. Propagation. - Seedlings and cuttings are the two most common methods for propagating fuchsias. The very fine fuchsia seed is sown just above ground, with the sole aim of conquering new varieties. As the young plant is very sensitive to the slightest cold, when sown in late autumn, when the berries containing the seed are fully ripe, it can only be sown in trays filled with heather soil mixed with sand, and placed in a warm, or at least temperate, greenhouse. This is how it's done by professional horticulturists, whose establishments contain all types of serrc. Amateurs who don't have a warm or temperate greenhouse at their disposal can only sow in spring, at the end of April, on a muted layer of sandy heather and covered with a glass frame. The plant can be transplanted very early, and will flower the following year. Subjects with insignificant flowering are immediately sacrificed; they can be of no use because fuchsia is not grafted; not that this shrub does not lend itself very well to this method of propagation, but because grafting offers no real advantage for fuchsia, as all varieties can be propagated from cuttings with equal ease. Fuchsia cuttings. - Among cold or temperate greenhouse plants and shrubs, none is more easily rooted by cutting than fuchsia. One- or two-year-old shoots are used as cuttings, and annual pruning provides an ample supply. They can be cut immediately in the same soil that is suitable for 25. fully-formed plants, in a shady spot in a cold greenhouse. Some are so perennial that they can be cut, like chrysanthemums, at any age, in any season, and in any type of soil, except those that are too compact. Once sufficiently elongated shoots have been cut, they are held in place by a perfectly straight stake, and all side shoots are removed as they grow, except for the three or four closest to the top. These serve as the base for a graceful, drooping head, the natural form of the fuchsia. Pruning consists in cutting back the newly-flowering branches every year, just a few centimetres from the main stem, when the plants are taken into the cold greenhouse for wintering. This pruning will encourage the emergence of young, highly-flowering branches the following spring. Once the fuchsias have been brought in and pruned, we gradually reduce the amount of watering, eventually giving them only enough to prevent their death from drying out. In spring, as soon as the buds lengthen and vegetation starts to grow again, watering becomes more frequent and abundant. The best temperature for overwintering fuchsias is between two and five degrees centigrade; below two degrees, they suffer more or less from the cold. This temperature is easily maintained in the cold greenhouse by means of a stove placed outside and lit only during the most rigorous cold; a few degrees too much heat, provoking the movement of the fuchsia sap at the wrong time, will harm them as much or more than a temperature of one or two degrees too low. Fuchsia collections, regardless of botanical differences, are classified into three series, according to calyx coloration: white or meaty fuchsias, pink fuchsias and red fuchsias. (See the lists of fuchsias at the end of this volume.) PELARGONIUMS - Collector pelargoniums, almost as rich in varieties as fuchsias, belong to the temperate greenhouse; many of the horticultural public refer to them as geraniums, as they were originally known. (See temperate greenhouse, chap. LII.). However, in addition to the collection Pelargoniums, there are still a number that belong to the cold greenhouse and whose dazzling flowering produces a very beautiful effect in the parterre in summer. The most remarkable of this series are Pelargonium inquinans and Pelargonium zonale, of which we have varieties with scarlet flowers, pink flowers and pure white flowers. All can be propagated from cuttings taken in spring, in heather soil, under a bell or in a frame, and planted out in May as soon as they are ready to bloom. As long as these Pelargoniums are in the open air, they are treated in the same way as annuals grown in the ground. They require abundant watering throughout the summer, so that their flowers follow one another uninterruptedly until autumn. They form elegant belts along the edges of ornamental shrub and tree beds in the landscape garden; in large gardens, they are used to line marble or cast-iron vases with white or pink petunias; finally, the drooping-stemmed varieties, mixed with Chinese saxifrage, create a pleasing effect in vases hung as ornaments from windows or apartment ceilings, or from the glazing of a cold greenhouse. At the start of winter, if the garden is large enough, the gardener must have such a mass of pelargoniums in the ground that it would be impossible to overwinter them all. He chooses a number of beautiful plants proportionate to the presumed needs of the cuttings for the following year, and sacrifices all the rest. The reserved plants are placed in pots and returned to the cold greenhouse, where they overwinter by keeping them close to the windows and watering them sparingly. CALCEOLARIAS - The calceolaria genus is made up of two distinct series, one of which includes several pretty species that spend the winter in the cold greenhouse and the summer in the parterre, and the other the innumerable sub-varieties of which enthusiasts form rich collections. The cultivation of all these plants is very similar to that of fuchsia. Soil that is suitable for fuchsia is also suitable for calceolaria; the time of repotting is the same for both plants. Fig. 102. Calceolaria with rough leaves. Calceolaria is propagated by sowing its seeds, under the same conditions and with the same precautions as for fuchsia seedlings. Seedlings are only used to obtain new varieties, which have become so numerous that, if a few of the old ones were not eliminated every year to make way for the most beautiful, calceolaria collections would include thousands of sub-varieties, each distinct by the color of its flowers. These sub-varieties are so fleeting that not a single one reproduces by sowing its seeds. Calceolaria almost all produce a multitude of rooted shoots, which are separated in autumn and potted separately, as if they were young plants from seed, to overwinter in the cold greenhouse. Many growers cultivate calceolaria in a temperate greenhouse without letting them out, in order to prolong their flowering; the cold greenhouse is perfectly sufficient for them, provided that in winter the temperature never drops below two or three degrees centigrade above zero. The cold greenhouse, dedicated exclusively to a collection of calceolaria, must be carefully shaded as soon as the sun begins to produce a high temperature inside; this care must be continued throughout the summer. Calceolaria need frequent and abundant watering, especially those such as the rough-leaved calceolaria, which should be placed early in the bed and flower until autumn (fig. 102). CINERARIES. - Cineraria, which botanists refer to the genus senecon, have several important advantages over a host of other cold greenhouse plants that deserve to be mentioned. Their flowers offer the most beautiful shades of blue and purple to be found in the entire plant kingdom; they bloom very early and continue without interruption for three months; they adapt very well to the concentrated atmosphere of an apartment, where they are completely at home, their flowers being totally odorless. Like calceolaria, cineraria are propagated by dividing clumps and separating the offspring, which are usually not rooted. They are placed singly in pots filled with a mixture of heather soil and potting compost in equal parts; they are then treated as cuttings. Provided they are watered abundantly, the offspring quickly become strong plants ready to flower immediately in the cold greenhouse they adorn at a time of year when most other cold greenhouse plants are not in flower. Those with white rays, edged with purple-purple, and those of a plain but very bright shade are particularly sought-after. Once the cineraria have finished flowering, a few precautions need to be taken with regard to those whose seed you intend to harvest. The seeds of cineraria are provided with aigrettes and arranged like those of the common dandelion, which children have fun scattering by blowing on them. If seed-bearing cineraria were left in the open air, the slightest gust of wind would blow their seeds away as they matured. So we bring them into the cold greenhouse and harvest the seed-laden corymbs without waiting for them to reach full maturity. These corymbs, or deflowered tops, are tied in bundles and hung in a dry, draught-free place; the seed completes its ripening and can then be easily collected without loss. Cineraria seedlings are sown in the same soil given to offshoots detached from clumps; they germinate quickly; the seedling is transplanted at a very young age, and later treated like the offshoots. Seedlings of cineraria do not always reproduce the color of the flowers from which the seed was harvested; but sowing the seed of first-choice cineraria produces a very fine mixture, often giving rise to remarkable novelties. To bring out the merits of the cineraria, they are arranged on tiers, assorted in shades from the lightest to the darkest; for three months, they offer a rich appearance and such vivid tones that the palette of the best painter cannot reproduce them. Cineraria are perennials; but, in order to always have a complete collection in very fine specimens, plants must be renewed by sowing or separating offspring, and never be more than two or three years old. Nursery in the cold greenhouse. - The amateur, who must attach equal importance to the upkeep of the flowerbed and that of the cold greenhouse, should always reserve a place in the latter, without detracting from the beauty of the view, for the propagation of a few plants which must be nursed in the cold greenhouse to be placed in the flowerbed when they are ready to flower. These include lantanas, verbenas, Mexican ageratum or celestine, remarkable speedwell and Henderson's speedwell. Lantanas (fig. 103) multiply by sowing seeds supplied in abundance by plants placed in the ground Fig. 103. Lantana camara. Fig. 104. Mexican bergamot. where they flower profusely from mid-May to the end of September, and into October if there are no early white frosts in the autumn. Seedlings can be sown in trays on a deaf layer, or in heather soil in terrines placed in the cold greenhouse. Care of the young plant, which starts flowering immediately, is the same as for calceolaria and cineraria. At the end of September, a few clumps of lantana are returned to the cold greenhouse; they continue to flower and provide cuttings for propagation in the spring of the following year. Lantana cuttings flower immediately like seedlings. Varieties with yellow, fire-colored, pink, lilac and white flowers are mixed in beds. Verbenas, whose scarlet, pink, violet and white flower varieties are almost collector's items, are propagated from cuttings taken under glass in the cold greenhouse in spring; they are placed in Fig. 105. Remarkable speedwell. in the open air in the flowerbed when they begin to bloom. A few plants of each variety are potted in late autumn to overwinter in the cold greenhouse; they provide cuttings for propagation. Mexican ageratum, or blue celestine (fig. 104), very fashionable these days, can also be propagated from cuttings in the cold greenhouse, and then placed in the parterre, where it flowers uninterruptedly until the first frosts; it overwinters in the cold greenhouse. The remarkable speedwell (fig. 105) can also be cut in the cold greenhouse and flowers in the flowerbed in the open air for as long as the celestine. When kept constantly in a cold greenhouse, in suitably large pots or crates filled with heather soil, it can be raised on a single stem and form large, very flowering shrubs in a few years. Henderson's speedwell is treated in the same way as the preceding one, differing only in that its long flower spikes are a beautiful light lilac at the top and white at the base, whereas the spikes of the remarkable speedwell are of a single color. The assortment of other cold greenhouse plants and the benefits that can be derived from them are described in the next chapter, devoted to the winter garden, the most pleasing form of the cold greenhouse. CHAPTER L The Winter Garden. The winter garden. - Its origin. - Interior layout. - Orangery trees and shrubs in the ground. - Camellia espaliers. - Pruning and management. - Cultivation care. - Mustang peony. - Yucca. - Bengal and Chinese roses. - Trees and shrubs with ornamental foliage. - Arancaria. - Norfolk pine. - Sicilian palm. - Flowering climbing shrubs. - Clianthus. - Grafting on baguenaudier. - Passiflora. - Passion flower. - Blue passion flower. - Grafting other species onto blue passionflower. - Its place in the winter garden - Fragrant flowering plants. - Wood carnation. - Remontant violet. - Reseda tree. - Fragrant Chimonant. - Madeira clethra. - Its multiplication of marcottes - Brazilian Lycopod. -Cyclamen -Lobelia. Origin of the winter garden - The English call the cold greenhouse the green house, or the conservatory. This last expression is the most accurate of all, and would have deserved to be adopted in all languages if it wasn't already accepted there, as it is in French, under a different meaning. In the two previous chapters, we considered the cold greenhouse from the point of view of winter preservation of ornamental plants which, in the European climate, cannot overwinter in the open air. It remains for us to highlight its advantages as a winter garden. The first winter garden was established in Paris some forty years ago, by a skilled horticulturist, Mr. Fion, who cultivated a small walled garden. He had the bright idea of erecting rows of pillars supporting iron arches, which enabled him to cover the entire garden with glass, and to grow the finest orangery and cold greenhouse plants in the open ground, without using any heating equipment. Since that time, the number of winter gardens has increased year by year; Berlin and several other major German cities have extensive winter gardens that serve as public promenades. Interior layout. - The interior of a cold greenhouse intended for use as a conservatory should be designed as a miniature pleasure garden, with winding paths, seats at intervals, a basin for watering, vases hanging from the arches and lined with hanging plants, completing the layout and interior decoration of the conservatory. Orangery shrubs in the ground. - Orange, pomegranate, myrtle and oleander blossom in the open ground and take on great proportions; each is given the soil best suited to it in the flowerbed or bed of which it forms a part, and the composition of which has been indicated. Since their roots can spread freely, there's no need to periodically replenish their soil, as is the case for the same shrubs in boxes. There's also no need to prune them, except to remove dead, diseased or confusing branches. In the winter garden, it's much more pleasant to see an orange tree, which never stops growing as long as it's sufficiently watered, cover itself with flowers and fruit almost all year round, and take on its natural shape, than to prune it into a symmetrical form. In the orangery, this shape is a necessity, because on the one hand space is at a premium, and on the other, a tree whose roots are imprisoned in a box, cannot retain its natural habit; in the conservatory, on the other hand, a grove of exotic trees and shrubs is all the more pleasing because these plants are less hindered and deformed by pruning. The same applies to all other orangery and cold greenhouse trees and shrubs that may be included in the winter garden decor. CAMELLIA in the ground in espalier. - Of all these shrubs, the Camellia is the most beautiful, in terms of variety, abundance and duration of flowering. Planted in the winter garden, the camellia looks only remotely like it does when its roots are cramped in the soil of a pot or crate. Brought up in espaliers in the form of a simple palmette with horizontal cords, along the windows, in just a few years it becomes the rival for pruning of the most beautiful pear trees grown in espaliers in the open air. Its framework is established by the same pruning and according to the same principles as those of the pear tree. It is in this form that old camellias are most floriferous, and are quickest to repair the losses that gardeners inflict on them in order to use the flowers and flowering branches for ornaments and ball bouquets. Care must be taken not to plant winter garden camellias too close to other shrubs which, like laurel, for example, require very abundant watering; the camellia's roots, which need much more moderate humidity, could suffer as a result. Throughout the summer, from around June 15th to the end of August, the windows of the conservatory, behind which the espalier camellias are planted, should remain open day and night; they should only be closed when thunderstorms are approaching. It is useful, for ease of service, if the espaliered camellias in the conservatory are trellised, not directly on the glazing, but on a coarse wire mesh that is independent of it. Open-ground plants for the conservatory. - A number of plants that can withstand wintering in the open air are an essential part of the winter garden ornamental scheme, such as mustang peonies and vuccas. The latter plant in particular, with its foliage similar to that of American agaves, produces a very picturesque effect, even when not in flower. It is in its temperament to emit its flower stalk without a fixed period, very often at the onset of winter, so that the first frosts, without compromising the existence of the plant itself, kill the budding flower without giving it time to bloom. In the winter garden, at whatever time they decide to bloom, yuccas bloom completely, and their flowering v is much more prolonged than it would be in the open air during the most favorable season. In the winter garden, beds of Bengal and Chinese roses, which have to be replanted every year when they overwinter in the open air, produce roses all year round; verbenas hardly interrupt their brilliantly hued bloom; Lantanas, yellow calceolaria, Henderson veronicas and Mexican agerats bloom all winter long, and collections of Indian chrysanthemums display the luxury of their richly varied shades, while ice and snow have wiped out all traces of plant life outside. Ornamental shrubs. - In addition to flowering shrubberies, the winter garden, where the diversity of shapes and foliage must contribute to the overall effect, includes other tree clumps whose foliage alone is ornamental, especially arancarias, Norfolk pine and chamgrops humilis or Sicilian palm, whose presence gives an African aspect to the vegetation of the beds, while the host of flowering plants and shrubs, which never leave the winter garden devoid of flowers, preserve the character of the European grove and parterre. Climbing plants. - The pillars, usually made of cast iron, which support the arcades of the conservatory, need to be concealed by clumps of climbing plants, the most beautiful of which deserve special mention. Yellow-flowered jasmine and Spanish jasmine, with their equally fragrant flowers, can be grown there without any care other than directing and shortening their flowering branches as needed; they don't require any particular cultivation procedure; they are easily propagated from cuttings. Clianthus and passiflora can be combined with them for the same purpose, chosen from species of these two genera that do not urgently need a temperate greenhouse. CLIANTRUS. - The Dampierre and purple-flowered clianthus, both remarkable for the beauty and singularity of their flowering, do not need a temperate greenhouse, even though they are listed as belonging to it in most treatises and catalogs. The temperate greenhouse is so unnecessary that we can take pleasure in growing and flowering them in the open air in espaliers, aumidi, in the climate of Paris, with the only precaution of bundling their flexible branches at the onset of winter, and wrapping them in a straw blanket which is sufficient to protect them from the effects of frost; All the more so, clianthus can overwinter without difficulty in the conservatory, in open heather soil, where they belong at the foot of pillars to which they are attached as their stems lengthen. Clianthus can be propagated from cuttings, but those wishing to have robust, abundantly flowering plants very soon should plant in the winter garden strong plants of baguenaudier (colutea), an extremely hardy shrub that is readily available everywhere. The following year, in spring, the branches of the baguenaudier are cut back onto the main stem; each receives a cleft graft of purple-flowered clianthus or Dampierre clianthus; the two species can even be grafted onto different branches of the same plant. The grafts flower immediately and abundantly. Pruning consists in removing old, exhausted wood and replacing it with young, vigorous, flowering branches. PASSIFLORES. - Passionflowers, better known by their vulgar name of passion flowers because, with a little good will, you can find the instruments of passion in their flowers, are all plants of great vegetative energy. The blue passionflower and the stalked passionflower alone belong to the cold greenhouse, and therefore to the winter garden; the other species or varieties, numbering around twenty, belong to the temperate or warm greenhouse, where many of them ripen their large, edible fruits. Leu passionflower, the hardiest of them all, is planted at the foot of pillars, in a mixture of heather soil and 455 good garden soil; no other plant less vigorous than itself should be planted within reach of its roots, as it would suffer from its proximity. The best way to get blue passionflower to bloom abundantly in the winter garden is to let its stems grow up the pillars to half their height, and then lead it from that point on wires stretched horizontally from pillar to post. All other species with red and purple flowers of the most brilliant color are grafted onto this passiflora. Passionflowers cannot overwinter in the winter garden; but if you graft them early, in spring, onto the blue passionflower, they grow vigorously and flower all summer long. In autumn, they should be cut back on the subject, which can then be left to grow and flower, or grafted a second time with a branch of a more sought-after species. All passionflowers propagate equally well from cuttings; but cuttings of the most delicate species vegetate painfully and never flower well in the winter garden, where they are never warm enough, and where, moreover, they cannot overwinter; whereas when grafted onto blue passionflower, they flower satisfactorily, if treated as annuals. Plants with fragrant flowers. - In the winter garden, we shouldn't forget a few clumps of very fragrant flowering plants, particularly the wood carnation, which blooms during the worst season, reseda tree, remontant violet, and a few shrubs whose blossoms are not at all brilliant, but which spread a very sweet perfume, which, like that of orange blossom, cannot bother people of a nervous disposition. Two cold-greenhouse ornamental shrubs, fragrant chimonanthe and Madeira clethra, are ideal for this purpose in the winter garden. The fragrant chimonanthe should not be planted alone, as it has the disadvantage, like many azaleas and rhododendrums, of not taking its leaves until after it has deuriated. The fragrant chimonanthe doesn't bloom brightly, but it does bloom for a very long time, lasting more than two months, starting in December and ending only in February, when the shrub begins to put on its foliage. It therefore belongs in the winter ¡garden, among the evergreen shrubs with brilliant but odorless blossoms, and is propagated by cuttings. Madeira clethra is less widespread than it deserves to be, because it is very difficult to propagate from cuttings and, as it rarely produces fertile seeds in European greenhouses, it cannot be propagated from seed. However, Madeira clethra marcots can only be weaned after two years. Those who have the patience not to shy away from this slow process are rewarded by the flowering of this graceful shrub, which blooms abundantly in late summer and part of autumn and spreads a most delicate fragrance in the winter garden. Borders. - The compartments of the conservatory, like those of the open-air parterre, absolutely need to be bordered. The more or less concentrated atmosphere inside a conservatory makes it impossible to use as borders the same plants that are successful in the open air; grass borders in particular are difficult to maintain in a satisfactory state. This is why, for some years now, all winter gardens have generally adopted Brazilian lycopods as borders. This pretty little plant never gets carried away and, with a few waterings, remains incomparably fresh and green all year round. The Brazilian Iycopod is propagated by dividing clumps; its use has become so widespread that its cultivation constitutes a distinct branch of horticulture, so to speak, and many gardeners in the vicinity of large cities make the propagation and sale of the Brazilian Iycopod, for borders in winter jardius, the mainstay of their industry. There are several varieties, all suitable for the same purpose. 9 2 9 4 8 9 CYCLAMENS - In a well-designed winter garden, light areas should always be set aside, far enough from the clumps of trees and shrubs to receive as much light as if they were uncovered in the open air; these areas are devoted to growing small and medium-sized plants in the ground. The number of plants that can be grown in this part of the conservatory is considerable; among the smallest, which are planted at the edge of the paths immediately behind the border, special mention must be made of cyclamen, which has been elevated to the rank of collector's flower by the large number of pretty varieties that have been conquered from seed in recent years in France and Belgium, where cyclamen cultivation is very popular. Cyclamen is propagated exclusively by sowing its seeds, which ripen quite easily, both those of spring-flowering varieties and those that flower only in autumn. Seeds sown as soon as they are harvested produce seedlings that only need to be transplanted in the spring of the following year, whatever the time of sowing. In the winter garden, if there's enough space, cyclamen seed can be sown in a corner and immediately transplanted into the ground to form clumps or beds, planted at a distance of only 5 or 6 centimetres for the smaller species, and 8 to 10 centimetres for the larger ones. Although the fleshy root is perennial, it's best not to keep cyclamen too long, and to renew the collection frequently with seedlings. If you take care to include spring-flowering and autumn-flowering species in your collection in equal numbers, you can enjoy cyclamen flowers twice a year in the winter garden. LOBELIAS. - Among the medium-sized plants that can be used to advantage in winter garden compartments, lobelias hold a distinguished place thanks to the lively shades of their flowers, in which red, violet and blue dominate. When grown in the open air, lobelia, most of which are perennial only by their roots, flower well, but the roots must overwinter in the orangery or cold greenhouse (26). In the conservatory, they can remain in place all year round. Lobelia can be kept for a long time and form very attractive clumps, but their blooming loses some of its brilliance if they are allowed to age too much. They are easily propagated by dividing clumps; those that produce fertile seeds should preferably be propagated by sowing in heather soil at the very moment when the seeds mature. When choosing between the profusion of beautiful ornamental plants that can contribute to the decoration of the winter garden, the amateur should be particularly careful to multiply, in preference to others, those plants that flower late in autumn, in the middle of winter and early in spring. The presence of flowering plants in the ground, when the flowerbed is devoid of flowers, is what gives the most value to the cold greenhouse set up as a winter garden. CHAPTER LI Temperate greenhouse plants. Temperate greenhouse plants. - Pelargoniums for collectors. - French. - English or fancy. - Their conditions of perfection. - Multiplication. - Sowing. - Cutting. - Cultivation care. - Pruning. - Exposure to open air. - Repotting. - Removal of spoiled leaves. - Ericas. - Growing difficulties. - Multiplication. - Sowing. - Seeds harvested in Europe. - Seeds from the Cape of Good Hope. - Time taken for seed to germinate. - Transplanting the seedling. - Cuttings. - Cultivation care. - Aeration. - Repotting. -Epacris - How they differ from ericas. Fortunate is the amateur who is fortunate enough to be able to afford the luxury of a temperate greenhouse, where he can house a multitude of plants far more varied than in the orangery or the cold greenhouse. He has, so to speak, all the flora of the tropics at his disposal, minus the plants that imperiously require the hot greenhouse. In addition, during the few months that temperate greenhouse plants can spend in the open air, the temperate greenhouse becomes the infirmary for more or less ailing orangery and cold greenhouse plants, especially cenes that are too seriously ill to be expected to recover outside under the influence of the summer sun. Temperate greenhouse ornamental plants include a number of collectors' plants, the most sought-after of which are pelargoniums, ericas, cacti and amaryllis. Each of these series is rich in species, varieties and sub-varieties, all of which are very similar in temperament, and require much the same care in cultivation. PELARGONIUNS. - Pelargonium species have been described as cold greenhouse plants. They overwinter in the greenhouse and adorn the flowerbed with their brilliantly hued flowers all summer long. True collector's Pelargoniums are all temperate-greenhouse plants; they rarely leave the greenhouse, even in summer, unless it's to decorate a window box in an apartment; the extreme delicacy of their flowers, whose petals can be scattered or torn by a strong gust of wind or a heavy rainstorm, means that they can scarcely be exposed to the open air while in flower. They are divided into two series, both of which have a corolla similar in shape to that of the pansy, in terms of the arrangement of the petals. The first series includes French Pelargoniums, with very wide corollas, regularly spotted with more or less dark spots on a light background; the second is made up of English or fancy Pelargoniums, whose character is to have the two upper petals of a plain, very dark purple, framed by a very thin white border. Multiplication. - Almost all collector's Pelargoniums produce fertile seeds, which are used to propagate them from seed in the hope of obtaining good new varieties. Only the most beautiful plants are allowed to go to seed, either because they have been hybridized with other choice species, or because the grower has left it to accidental crosses. Pelargoniums that have matured their seed do not go back up, and therefore only flower once a year; those whose seed is not to be harvested are treated differently: as the flowers wilt, they are removed, without giving the seed time to form; with this precaution, pelargoniums are essentially remontant; the best species flower with little interruption, from mid-May to the end of August. Seeds are sown just as they reach maturity, in terrines filled with heather soil which is kept constantly moist; the young seedling is transplanted singly into small pots; it overwinters in the temperate greenhouse and shows its first flower in May of the following year. Cutting is the most common method of propagating Pelargoniums for collections; cuttings are young shoots detached from clumps that have stopped flowering at the end of July and during August. When growing large numbers of Pelargoniums for sale, lay out a thick bed of good, well-compacted potting soil in a temperate greenhouse or under a stretcher in a south-facing position. The cuttings are placed close together and covered with a glass frame; those taken in the greenhouse are covered with a glass cloche or glass jar, which is removed only when the vegetation of the cuttings shows that they are well rooted. When working on a smaller scale, Pelargonium cuttings intended solely for the upkeep of a collection can be taken in a terrine or a large pot wider than it is deep, filled with well-tilled potting soil and covered with a bell or glass jar. As soon as the cuttings have started to grow, it's time to transplant them separately, like seedlings, into pots kept in the brightest part of the temperate greenhouse throughout the winter, at a temperature of at least 5 to 6 degrees during the coldest weather, and 10 to 12 degrees after hard frosts, without exceeding the latter temperature until the flower buds start to appear, which usually happens in April. From this time onwards, Pelargoniums are occasionally given a little air in the greenhouse, taking care to protect the young plants ready to flower from direct sunlight. To this end, whitewash the inside surface of the greenhouse windows, or lay a very light canvas over the outside. It would be dangerous to shade pelargoniums with a covering of straw mats; too complete an exclusion of light would cause them to wither; the flowering shoots would overextend and their bloom would abort. care of cultivation. - Pelargoniums should be repotted every year in spring, when their vegetation 26. starts up again, but is never completely interrupted. Larger pots are given to those whose size seems to require it; for the others, simply remove about half the clod of soil adhering to the roots and replace it with new soil, putting each plant back into the pot from which it has just been momentarily removed. The best soil for Pelargoniums is made up of half good light garden soil and half broken potting soil. Many growers give their pelargoniums heather soil, either on its own or mixed with potting soil; the plants grow quite well in this, but they are neither as robust nor as floriferous as in the mixture we have indicated. Pelargonium's generous vegetation allows it to be pruned into a regular shape that it would not take naturally, and without which its ornamental effect is incomplete. After wintering, Pelargoniums of the most robust species are established on four branches, each of which is pruned in turn to give it two more, so that the head is made up of eight symmetrically spaced branches, all of equal strength and equally flowering. Those of the more delicate species are established only on three branches, each of which is pruned to two, to form a regular head of six branches. After flowering, Pelargoniums can spend some time in the open air; their pots are buried in a flowerbed with good exposure; the plants are then placed in the soil.

consolidate their wood and gain the strength they need to withstand the winter from which they always suffer to a greater or lesser extent. They should be returned to the temperate greenhouse as soon as the nights start to get cooler, in the course of September. During overwintering, Pelargoniums should be watered moderately and gradually brought up to the temperature indicated for seedlings or cuttings. It's important to give them as much light as possible in the greenhouse, from autumn to spring. Collector Pelargoniums lose some of their leaves, especially in winter, due to lack of light. No matter how they are treated, they always lose part of their leaves; as soon as a leaf begins to yellow, it must be removed without delay by lowering its stalk, which is not very attached to the branch; without this care, which must be taken assiduously by inspecting the Pelargoniums every day, the leaf which dies completely and falls off by itself leaves a scar on the branch which may eventually lead to its own death. ERICAS. - For true connoisseurs, the merit of ericas, better known by their vulgar name of heathers, lies largely in the obstacles that must be overcome in order to multiply them, raise them and form good plants with brilliant and prolonged flowering; those who are completely successful in this cultivation enjoy above all the pleasure of overcoming difficulties. The species and varieties of the Erica genus are very numerous; it is generally believed, on the strength of the most accredited horticultural treatises, that the most beautiful and delicate of temperate greenhouse heathers cannot be successfully cultivated in the average French climate; this error has been mainly propagated among the horticultural public by professional horticulturists. They have in fact eliminated from their heather crops the most beautiful species grown in England, Belgium and Holland, and we can't blame them. The cultivation of these species requires too much meticulous care; it makes people wait too long for results, and, in the final analysis, the number of amateurs willing to pay a suitable price for the most beautiful heathers is not large enough in France for the gardener who cultivates for sale to benefit from propagating them. These considerations, quite right in themselves, should not stop the simple amateur who seeks in the cultivation of temperate greenhouse plants, and in that of ericas in particular, the most pleasant of pleasures. The same applies to the gardener of an opulent home: with a little care, he can maintain a complete collection of heathers in the temperate greenhouse, without excluding those known as English heathers, which in England are the object of a predilection justified by their beauty, and which can succeed in our climate as well as in that of Great Britain. Propagation. - The best way to propagate Ericas is to sow their seeds, which the most beautiful species always produce in sufficient quantities. These seeds are always preferable to commercial seeds shipped from the Cape of Good Hope. Most of the heathers in our collections belong not to simple sub-varieties, but to botanically distinct species, and reproduce unchanged by sowing their seeds; the gardener who sows seeds collected from the heathers in his own collection therefore knows in advance the result of his sowing; if he sows seed from the Cape, he may well, after long and meticulous care, only see seedlings of the least sought-after species grow. Professional horticulturists sow in well-sieved heather soil, in terrines which they bury on a deaf layer under a frame. This procedure is good for them, because they need all the space available in the temperate greenhouse for other purposes; but seedlings are much more successful in the temperate greenhouse, where the terrines are subjected to the influence of an atmosphere that is more even and less variable than that which can prevail under a warm frame, which can never be the equivalent of a temperate greenhouse. The heather seed should be spread over the surface of the terrines, and a little sandy heather soil, not more than one or two millimeters thick, should be sifted over it. The amateur who knows perfectly well the species from which he has collected the seed for his seedlings, should only sow together the seed of those which germinate in roughly the same space of time; this time is very variable: some heathers germinate after five to six weeks, others after five to six months, others after one or two years. Throughout this period, the soil in which the seeds are sown must not be allowed to dry out for a single day. Once the plant has reached a height of 5 or 6 centimetres, it is ready to be transplanted. Its roots are so delicate that young plants are always very tired from transplanting; the pots must be ready in advance and filled with heather soil mixed with a third of leaf compost. The entire contents of the pots are then removed, and the soil carefully crumbled, so as to cause as little damage as possible to the excessively delicate roots, which suffer much less than if they were pulled out one by one. The plant must be transplanted very quickly, so that the roots are exposed to the air for as little time as possible. Cuttings are taken under conditions similar to those for seedlings; one-year old shoots are used, from which the lower leaves are removed, leaving only those at the top; they are planted close together and maintained in moderate humidity, either under a warm frame or in a temperate greenhouse. They are slow to root, but they always end up forming good roots, which can be recognized by the lengthening of their terminal shoots. Transplanting is carried out under the same conditions as for seedlings. A little heat and a lot of light, excluding contact with the summer sun, and assiduous but moderate watering ensure the success of Erica cuttings. Cultivation care. - Ericas should not be put outdoors before the end of May; cold nights are very harmful to them. From April 15th onwards, the panels of the temperate greenhouse occupied by the heathers should be opened all day and then at night, so that the heathers can become accustomed by degrees to the open air before being placed there permanently. Instead of giving each plant all the water it needs at once, it's better to water them three or four times a day in summer, and twice in the off-season. Re-potting also requires a great deal of attention; slow-growing plants don't often need to be given larger and larger pots, but once or twice a year they do need a little new heather soil mixed with leaf compost. When it's time to repot, stop watering for a day or two, so that the rootball can be easily detached from the pot. Remove 1 or 2 centimetres of the old soil, and replace it with new soil, inserted and lightly trodden between the rootball and the sides of the pot. Although heathers don't die under the influence of moderate cold, and the temperature of the cold greenhouse may be sufficient for them in rigorous conditions, they can only be overwintered in good condition and flower well in the temperate greenhouse. In all cases, they should be kept indoors for two or three weeks after repotting. Flowering Ericas withstand the effects of wind and heavy rain better than many other temperate greenhouse ornamentals, because the single-petaled corollas are very adherent and the foliage, which is tiliform so to speak, is not easily damaged by rainwater, which it allows to drip off easily; this is why large collections risk little when they are allowed to flower in the open air. But enthusiasts of this beautiful genus, especially those whose collections contain many delicate species, should not hesitate to keep them in the temperate greenhouse for as long as flowering lasts, except for opening the panels day and night and closing them only in the event of bad weather. Flowering heathers can also be kept in an apartment window box, where they are perfectly at home, flowering as long as they do in the temperate greenhouse. Like ericas, we grow the many varieties of the epacris genus, confused with ericas under the common name of heather, but easily distinguishable by the constant arrangement of their flowers in unilateral spikes, with flowers on only one row and always on the same side. The foliage of epacris is the same as that of heathers; the flowers are generally more elongated, with a slightly curved tube, of a very bright color in which dark red is the dominant shade. Heather and epacryis should not be pruned, as this would spoil them; they naturally take on a good bushy shape; the most delicate, with grey stems, only need to be supported by one or more stakes, which should be chosen thin and painted light green, so that they are as inconspicuous as possible. Sturdy species with very hard wood can do without stakes. CHAPTER LII Temperate greenhouse plants (continued). Cacti. - Singularity of their vegetation. - Cereus or candles. - Peruvian candle. - Mode of growth. - Multiplication. - Seedlings. - Accidental hybridization. - Cutting. - Conditions for success. - Wintering. - Cultivation care. - Species and varieties. - Melocacti - Echinocacti. - Mammillaries. - Epiphylls. - Opuntias. - Nopal - Fig tree. - Fat plants. - Agave. - Rare flowering in the greenhouse. - Aloes. - Abundant flowering. - Crussulan. - Rochea. - Stapelia. - Oddity and particular odor of their flowers. - Proof of flies' sense of smell. - Ficoïdes or mesembrianthemums. -Sowing. - Cuttings. - Dwarf species for living-room shelves. CACTICIES. - The vegetation of cacti is so bizarre, so different from that of all other cultivated or wild plants, that cacti are rightly sought after by a large number of enthusiasts and considered to be one of the most beautiful, if not the most beautiful, adornments of the temperate greenhouse; only a few species belong to the hot greenhouse. Cacti give a very special physiognomy to the flora of South America, from which they originate; the main genera, all rich in species and varieties, of which the collections are composed, are the cereus, melocactus, echinocactus, mammilla, epiphyllum and opuntia. In all these genera, with the exception of epiphylls, leaves do not exist, but are replaced by the stem, which performs the functions of both stem and leaves; in epiphylls, it's the same thing in reverse, with only fleshy leaves, more or less thick, emerging from each other and acting as both leaves and stems. CEREUS or CIERGES. - Cereus are, strictly speaking, the type of the cactus family. Some of the most remarkable are endowed with an astonishing energy of vegetation. In its native country, the Peruvian candle, which grows between rock crevices, leaning against perpendicular bare rock, with a midday exposure, endures alternately prolonged dry heat for several months without interruption and torrential rain for almost as long. Its growth, almost completely stationary during the dry season, takes on extraordinary vigor when the rainy season returns. A very fine specimen has long been preserved at the Museum of Natural History, and a greenhouse - or, to put it more accurately, a glass cage - had been built for it, which had to be raised periodically; it was impossible to foresee where it would end up when it accidentally died. All candles, and generally all cacti, have more or less the same temperament; they all have the ability to remain immersed for a long time in a complete vegetal sleep, to wake up to grow and flower, and then to fall back into the same sleep. Multiplication. - Cierges can be propagated by sowing their seeds, but not all produce ripe fruit in the climate of Paris, in the best-operated temperate greenhouses. The seeds are sown in heather soil in terrines kept in temperate greenhouses. The seedling, transplanted at a very young age into a mixture of equal parts of free-range garden soil and heather soil, does not constantly reproduce the flowers of the species from which the seed was harvested; this is at least what has happened several times in seedlings made with candle seeds harvested in Europe. As pollen is abundant in all cacti, and in candles in particular, the variations observed in the flowers of plants obtained from seed can be considered as due to hybridization by accidental crossing. The most common method of propagation for 27 all cactus species is cuttings. Since these eccentrically-shaped plants have no young branches to detach to make cuttings without damaging the mother plant, a portion of the stem is sacrificed and divided into sections only a few centimetres long. The wound produced by this cutting on the mother plant is quickly healed, and the candle quickly repairs losses of this kind by the energy of its vegetation. Sections destined to serve as a means of propagation must not be cut immediately; if they were placed in the ground at the very moment when they have just been cut, while the cut wound is still fresh, they would rot and not form roots. They are placed on a shelf in a dry place, where they remain for two or three days until the cut wounds have healed. Each cutting is then planted separately in a pot filled with the same mixture used to transplant the seedling. Cactus, like all cacti, can be cut at just about any time of year, but cuttings are most successful in spring and late summer. When the young shoots they produce, as soon as they are rooted, have reached a certain length, which varies from one species to another, their upper extremity is cut off, leaving the wound to dry in contact with the air. This amputation helps them to flower well the following year. Young cuttings rooted in spring spend the summer in the open air, and are returned to the temperate greenhouse by mid-September. The watering they must have received sparingly, but without interruption, throughout their period of vegetative activity, is then gradually reduced, then eliminated altogether during the winter. Stored on shelves in a corner of the temperate greenhouse, they overwinter dry, at an average temperature of 6 to 8 degrees, rising to 12 and even 15 degrees when their growth resumes; most of them flower in their second year. Cultivation care. - To grow well and flower abundantly, fully-formed plants only need to be treated in the same way as cuttings. Water them again in spring only when they show the first signs of regrowth. They should be given very little water at first, and the dose gradually increased until they have bloomed. In most species, flowers last only a few hours and fall without producing fruit; plants that do bear fruit, and whose seed is to be used for seedling propagation, are watered and kept outdoors until the fruit matures, then returned in September to the temperate greenhouse, where they overwinter under the same conditions as the young cuttings. Species and varieties. - The most sought-after species of the Cereus genus in our collections are the Cereus speciosissimus, or magnificent candle, with its vase-shaped flowers and inner petals glistening with a brilliance that no paintbrush can capture, and its hybrids, the Cereus de Quillardet and Cereus toujours en fleur; Then there's the large-flowered cereus, whose flowers, 12 to 15 centimeters in diameter, bloom only at night, wither in the morning, and spread a penetrating fragrance similar to that of heliotrope and vanilla; Passionate amateurs watch for the blossoming of this candle, admiring its beauty and breathing in its truly delicious scent; then the serpentine cereus, whose flower, of the same shape as that of the magnificent cereus, spreads the scent of the rose; and finally the whip cereus, whose long, flexible, cylindrical stems are covered with a profusion of flowers of the brightest pink. The latter is the hardiest of them all, requiring only occasional watering as it approaches flowering and throughout its blooming period. In a pinch, it can overwinter in a cold greenhouse or an inhabited apartment, just as well as in a temperate greenhouse. MELOCACTS. - Cacti of the Melocaceae genus are only accepted in collections because of their unique ball shape, criss-crossed by protruding ribs with tufts of hairs or spines along the edge. Very large species rarely bloom; small and medium-sized species bloom a little more often, but without any regular periodicity, their satin-finished flowers in various shades of red, pink and yellow, arranged in a circle at the top of the plant, with no stems, leaves or anything resembling them. In terms of cultivation care, soil, watering and overwintering temperature, melocacti are grown in exactly the same way as candles. Their propagation is rather difficult; their shape does not lend itself to cuttings; the seed taken from the countries of origin of these singular plants is sown under the same conditions as that of candles, as they rarely bear fruit in European greenhouses. MAMMILLAIRES. - Cacti of this genus resemble melocacti, with the difference that their protruding edges are formed by fleshy nipples, from which their name derives. They flower in the same shades as melocacti, but no more frequently; their cultivation methods are the same from point to point as those of candles. ECHINOCACTS. - The resemblance of these plants to melocacti is striking; most plants of this genus are armed with strong spines that are very hard, but not very adherent. In Jamaica, where echinocactus grows abundantly wild in the mountains, goats detach it with their horns and roll it around as if they were playing with it, until there are no thorns left, after which they can eat it without hurting their lips. Their cultivation is the same as that of the three preceding genera. EPIPHYLLES. - Plants of this genus are similar in appearance to other plants in that their leaves are indented and interlocked; the flowers, which offer the most beautiful shades of red and pink, are born in the indentations at the edges of the leaves, giving the flowering plant an original physiognomy. Epiphylls are easily propagated by cuttings, by taking a whole leaf or a fragment of leaf as a cutting, leaving the wound to heal well before planting it in the ground, in the same conditions as candle branches. Epiphylls are less sensitive to cold than most other cacti; they can be grown in the cold greenhouse, but it is always in the temperate greenhouse that they vegetate best and flower most beautifully. OPUNTIAS. - The opuntia genus is one of the most remarkable of the cactus family; one species of this genus, the nopal, feeds the cochineal, an insect which supplies the most beautiful red for the arts, known as carmine; another, of very large dimensions, known as the Indian fig, because its very sweet fruits are eaten like figs, is used as a fence for inheritances in Algeria and Sicily. A large number of opuntia species are included in collections, all remarkable for their snowshoe-like shape, set one above the other and regularly covered with staggered tufts of hairs or prickles. They propagate like epiphylls, and are grown in the same way as candles. Opuntias produce a good effect in the greenhouse because of the eccentricity of their shape; most species rarely flower; their dull pink flowers are not in keeping with the size of the plants, and amateurs do not take their ornamental effect into account. Succulents. - In horticulture, this name covers a large number of plants that are very different from one another, but which have in common the thickness of their fleshy leaves, and the fact that they live much more from the air, which they decompose and whose substance they appropriate, than from the earth in which their roots live. These plants form a sort of transition between cacti and the rest of the world's vegetation. The most remarkable genera of succulents in the temperate greenhouse are: agaves, aloes, crassulas, stapelias and ficoides. Most of these plants can survive in the cold greenhouse; a few, in a pinch, could even overwinter in an orangery; but their cultivation only gives good results when they can overwinter in the temperate greenhouse, even those indicated as belonging to the warm greenhouse. AGAVE. - Like the rare-flowering cacti, American agaves are grown in greenhouses in France only for the beauty of their elegantly tufted foliage, which in some species is striped with yellow on a green background. As for flowering, it occurs only at very rare intervals; some only bloom every fifty years. In Spain, Italy and Sicily, they form impassable hedges; in the climate of southern Europe, agaves often display their flowers elegantly arranged in a candelabrum on a very high, straight stem. They are propagated by detaching the eyelets from the root collar, and by sowing their seeds, which the trade gets from Mexico or southern Europe. These seeds are sown in a terrine, in pure heather soil, and kept in a temperate greenhouse. Seedlings are treated in the same way as cactus cuttings; like all succulents, they require moderate watering in summer and no water in winter. Agaves leave the temperate greenhouse towards the end of May, and should return in September. ALOES - The appearance of aloes is so similar to that of agaves that the public generally confuses these two types of succulent plant. Their cultivation and propagation methods are the same, with the essential horticultural difference that aloes flower abundantly and regularly in temperate greenhouses. Although their flowers are not very bright, they are very elegant and produce a very pleasing ornamental effect in collections where flowering aloes are combined with agaves, which almost never flower. CRASSULAS. - The crassula genus is rich in species that flower abundantly and over a long period, in brilliant shades in which bright red is the dominant color. All these plants can be grown in heather soil in a temperate greenhouse, and propagated by sowing their seeds. You can also take cuttings from their young shoots, or even from their leaves, taking care to let the wound heal before planting. The cultivation of crassulas is in every respect the same as that of cacti. One of the most beautiful species botanically forms a separate genus under the name of rochéa; it produces clumps of small scarlet flowers that are associated with non-flowering succulents; they brighten up the somewhat severe appearance of the vegetation in temperate greenhouses devoted to cacti and other succulents. STAPELIA. - This plant, whose star-shaped, fleshy, dark-hued flower, bristling with coarse hairs, is one of the most bizarre in the plant kingdom, can flower perfectly well in the temperate greenhouse, although it is indicated everywhere as a hot-greenhouse plant; it requires no special cultivation care, and should be treated like other temperate-greenhouse succulents. Too many stapelia should not be grown in a small greenhouse; their flowers give off a nauseous odor, similar to that of tainted meat. This peculiarity has given rise to a very curious experiment, by which naturalists have been able to confirm the fact that stapelias are endowed with a sense of smell, even though we don't know where it comes from. If you enclose a stapelia flowering plant in a chamber containing large flies, known as blowflies, these insects deposit their eggs in the corolla of the stapelia flowers. They can't be attracted there by the sense of sight, as the flower bears no resemblance, even remotely, to an advanced piece of meat, where blowflies usually lay their eggs; it must therefore be the sense of smell that gives rise to their misapprehension. FICOIDS. - These pretty plants, called mezembrianthemums by botanists, are grown in heathland, and multiply, either by sowing or cutting, with the greatest ease. Only those perennial species that can be collected in large numbers are considered to belong to the temperate greenhouse. Seedlings are sown in spring in heather soil; cuttings are taken in June and July, with the same precautions indicated for cactus cuttings; the seedling or cutting is transplanted into pots in September and returned to the temperate greenhouse; it must overwinter almost dry; it flowers the following year. Ficoids produce a profusion of elegant flowers, some of which are fragrant; they come in all shades of pink, light violet and tan; their place is next to the crassulas in the succulent greenhouse; they can spend part of the summer in the open air, but should be brought in early in September. The dwarf species, planted in small pots of red earth, are the main adornment of living-room shelves, where they appear alongside the equally dwarf species of cactus, agave and aloe, requiring no cultivation care other than a very small amount of water every morning, from April onwards, when they come into vegetation. CHAPTER LIII Temperate greenhouse plants (continued). Amaryllis. - Composition of collections. - Causes of their scarcity in cultivation. - Striped amaryllis. - Multiplication. - Sowing. - Seedling rearing. - Separation of buds. - Cultivation care. - Queen Amaryllis. - Time of flowering. - Amaryllis lis de saint Jacques - China golden. - Equestrian. - Cross-linked. - Various uses of the temperate greenhouse. - Its single-sided construction. - Plants to decorate it. - Living-room greenhouse. - Its distribution. - Suitable plants. - Ixias. - Sparaxis. - Achimenès picta. - Temperate greenhouse converted into winter garden. - Basin for aquatic plants. - Nelumbiums. - Growing them. - Various temperate greenhouse plants. - Growing them. - Maintenance and repairs. AMARVLLIS. - The amaryllis genus is rich in distinct botanical species, all of rare beauty; some belong to the cold greenhouse, others to the warm greenhouse, the greatest number to the temperate greenhouse. Among the latter, two very fine species, the amaryllis vittata or striped amaryllis, and the queen's amaryllis, have nowadays produced a multitude of sub-varieties through seedlings and hybrid crosses, which have elevated them to the rank of collector's plants. Collections of amaryllis are quite rare in greenhouses in France, because of the difficulties involved in growing them, and also because of the high price of fine varieties; this price cannot be reduced much, because most bulbs give few cal̈eux, cloves only flower well with care, and seedling onions only give flowers after a variable time depending on the species, but always a very long time. It's not surprising, then, that few horticulturists are involved in amaryllis propagation, a branch of gardening whose products have little outlet. On the contrary, it's a very attractive crop for any amateur with a temperate greenhouse. Multiplication. - Striped amaryllis, the most widely 27. cultivated, is propagated mainly by sowing its seeds, which ripen perfectly in the Paris climate, since the plant flowers in mid-June, giving the seed a long period of good weather and hot temperatures to complete its ripening. Sow as soon as the seeds are ripe, in pots filled with a mixture of free-range garden soil and leaf compost. The same mixture is suitable for fully-formed plants; however, if the garden soil is poor in calcareous principles, a small quantity of lime compost and turf can be added; cold soil, where lime exists only in too small a dose, is not suitable for amaryllis. By keeping the soil in the terrines at a moderate level of humidity, the seeds germinate fairly quickly; the young plants are left in the terrines until the end of autumn; at this time, the soil in the terrines is left to dry out completely, so that the young amaryllis bulbs can be removed and allowed to air-dry in a well-ventilated room, where they are kept away from the cold, like other flowering onions which should not be planted until spring. Towards the end of winter, as soon as the young onions are ready to start growing, they are planted singly in pots filled with the same soil as for fully-formed amaryllis. These pots remain in the temperate greenhouse until the end of May, and spend the rest of the summer outdoors. When their leaves turn yellow in autumn, it's time to stop watering the amaryllis and treat it as if for its first wintering; this cultivation must be continued with perseverance, giving new soil and larger pots each year as the onions grow; they eventually flower, and good new varieties are often obtained by sowing. The striped amaryllis is listed in most treatises as an orangery or cold greenhouse plant; the seedling can, in fact, be raised in the cold greenhouse; but it does not grow well there, it makes you wait a long time for its first flowering, and its flowering is never as beautiful as when it is treated as a temperate greenhouse plant. The chocks removed from old onions are treated in the same way as onions grown from seed. Cultivation care. - As soon as the striped amaryillis onions have become large enough, and start to flower, they should be given large pots where their fibrous roots can plunge at ease into the soil indicated above. The times for lifting onions from the ground, replanting them and exposing them to the open air are the same for ready-made plants as for young seedlings or cloves. Water moderately at first, then more generously as the amaryllis prepares to flower. When it's time to bloom, bring the flowering plants into the temperate greenhouse to protect them from the heavy rainstorms that are common in June in Paris. Only those plants whose seeds are to be harvested are left in the open air. The others are returned to the open air after flowering, where they remain until the onions are ready to be lifted and dried for wintering. By following this procedure and giving their amaryllis plants a temperature of 8 to 10 degrees from the start of vegetation, gradually raised to 12 or 15 degrees until they are placed outdoors, Dutch and Belgian growers are able to obtain plants whose flowering stems, nearly a metre high, bear six to eight flowers of extraordinary size and vivacity of color; with the same care, they can aspire to the same success everywhere. AMARVLLIS DE LA REINE. - The cultivation of this species does not essentially differ from that of the striped amaryllis, in terms of soil composition and general cultivation care; but, as it only flowers in mid-winter, the onions lifted at the end of summer, and freed of their cloves, are planted in spacious pots before the end of autumn, and placed in the temperate greenhouse, in the best-lit locations. Although amaryllis in this series are listed in many treatises as warm greenhouse plants, they flower very well in the temperate greenhouse. The queen's varieties of amaryllis, all very beautiful and brilliantly colored, rarely produce fertile seeds; the time of flowering does not often allow the flower to produce seeds and for these seeds to ripen; the only means of multiplication in use for these amaryllis is the rearing of the cloves which are detached when the onions are lifted from the ground, and which are treated in the same way as plants made to bring them into flower after several years of assiduous care. As you can see, amaryllis collections, made up of the species and varieties whose cultivation has just been described, flower twice a year: the striped amaryllis in midsummer, and the queen amaryllis in midwinter. The collections also include the amaryllis lis de Saint-Jacques, with its magnificent red flowers, one of the most beautiful of its kind, the Chinese golden amaryllis, the equestrian or scarlet amaryllis, the reticulated amaryllis and a dozen other species. By taking advantage of the coincidence of their flowering periods, these species can be hybridized with each other and with those used as the basis for collections, and their seeds can be sown to give rise to very beautiful sub-varieties, all the more precious to the true amateur as they have cost him more trouble and meticulous care. Various uses of the temperate greenhouse. - When a temperate greenhouse is run by a professional horticulturist, with the aim of selling the ornamental plants he can raise there, everything is sacrificed to utility; temperate greenhouse collection plants, then those most sought-after by amateurs in each locality, are naturally those that the gardener multiplies there in preference. The amateur, on the other hand, seeks above all to make his temperate greenhouse as pleasant as possible, both by the right choice and by the happy arrangement of the plants he takes care to furnish it with. If the greenhouse is built on a single slope, as is often the case in gardens where a south-facing enclosing wall is used to build the temperate greenhouse, this wall, which forms the back of the greenhouse, is lined with ferns at the bottom, and trimmed at the top with strings of Frankentot and grosse perle de Hollande vines, combined with various climbing plants such as mandevillea suaveolens and grenadilla, or red-flowered passionflowers with edible fruit. A few vines of the same type, planted in rows along the windows and in arches across the temperate greenhouse, take no space away from the ornamental plants, and give very early forced grapes of excellent quality. Living-room greenhouse. - If the temperate greenhouse is built on the same level as a ground-floor salon, the advantages of which have already been mentioned (see Ch. VI), an empty space is left in the middle along its longest side, wide enough to accommodate a carpet for a reception. Places are reserved for seats in the center and at either end; chandeliers are hung from the top of the windows, and the amphitheatrical tiers to the right and left of the central aisle are loaded with the prettiest flowering plants, with those, such as the ixias, sparaxis and achimenes, that stand out for their beauty of detail and want to be admired at close quarters. This is particularly true of the achimenès picta, whose tubular corolla, of rare elegance of form, is internally marbled with purple spots on a light yellow background, to the most graceful effect (fig. 106). The Achimenes genus, by the number and variety of its species, the duration of its inexhaustible flowering and the ease with which it can be propagated by cuttings, is one of the most precious for the ornament of the greenhouse. Temperate greenhouse converted into winter garden. - In describing the distribution and stocking of a conservatory, we have had to take the most commonly realized assumption, and represent the conservatory under the conditions of a cold greenhouse. But when the amateur agrees to pay for a good thermosiphon and continuous heating from the onset of the bad season to maintain the temperature of the conservatory day and night between s and 12 degrees, then it's a little corner of Africa or India under glass; the temperate greenhouse, converted into a conservatory, has the spring temperature of the tropical climate in winter without Fig. 106. Achimenès picta. in summer; it's up to the amateur to bring together the rarest and most precious ornamental plants, with the exception only of those that need an exaggerated amount of humid heat and can only thrive in the warm greenhouse. Basin for aquatic plants. - A great deal of water is needed for watering in the temperate greenhouse, and this water can only be distributed to the plants once it has reached the temperature of the greenhouse atmosphere; for this purpose, it must remain for a sufficiently long time in a shallow basin to which it is brought from outside.
