If the basin is located in the center of the conservatory, away from the large plants, so that it receives direct sunlight, it can be filled with the most beautiful aquatic plants, adding a welcome variety to the appearance of this part of the temperate greenhouse. One of the most remarkable of these plants is the nelumbium, whose flowering is unrivalled for beauty in all water flora; it yields only to the victoria regia, the queen of tropical waters, and outshines all other aquatic plants. Cultivation of the nelumbium, of which we have two species, one with pink-tinted white flowers, the other a golden yellow, is extremely simple. The bottom of the pond is lined with pond vase, with no fertilizer or other soil mixed in; the rhizomes or false tubers of the nelumbiums are planted at the bottom of the water in spring; The plant blooms towards the end of summer and sheds its leaves as winter sets in; the roots overwinter in the mud, returning to vegetation the following spring. Ponteiderias, lobelias and a host of other aquatic plants suitable for decorating basins in cold greenhouses or in the open air, thrive and flower even better in the temperate greenhouse basin, where they can be associated with the charming aquatic sensitive, whose roots float between two waters and whose graceful stems are covered with foliage that is retractable to the touch like that of the ordinary sensitive. Various temperate greenhouse plants. - The myrtles, orange trees, pomegranates and oleanders that all those who have traveled to the South have seen in the open air, like the apple trees in the orchards of the North, are replaced here by beds of browallia, brugmansia and other flowering shrubs totally foreign to the flora of Europe. Over these clumps rise clumps of slender bamboo, then, here and there, some of the fruit trees of our West Indian colonies, such as the persea, whose fruit is the avocado pear; the eugenia, with myrtle leaves; the eucalyptus and the metrosidéros, all three representing the flora of Australia, and to crown it all, some of the most beautiful tree ferns of New Zealand and the Sunda Islands. Some beautiful, oddly-shaped plants from the cactus family, especially the large opuntia, complete the foreign look of this collection of precious plants, which can easily be grown in the temperate greenhouse in soil and temperature conditions little different from those of their native land. In front of the clumps of shrubs and large plants, separated by lawns of Brazilian lycopod, compartments forming the parterre of the winter garden, receive, still in the open ground, collections of amaryllis, ixias, sparaxis and alstræmerias with abundant flowers, The vases hanging from the hangers in the temperate greenhouse are filled with the elegant, velvety-blue flowers of torrenia asiatica, along with a host of charming gesneriaceae. The cultivation of all these plants is similar to that of the plants described in detail above; they all thrive in heather soil, either pure or mixed with leaf compost; Those that do not produce fertile seeds are easily propagated by cutting, marcotting or grafting, and the amateur, who always finds ample material for all types of propagation in his temperate greenhouse, can, by making use of these inexhaustible resources, obtain the means to enrich his collections by exchange, not only at no cost, but also with the added pleasure of obliging fellow horticulturists; The exchange of a few rare plants is often all that's needed to foster friendship between people who might otherwise have been hostile or indifferent all their lives. When the temperate greenhouse has not been converted into a winter garden, and does not contain any plants in the ground, the time of year when the greatest number of plants are temporarily in the open air is chosen to carry out the essential maintenance work. Otherwise, if painters and glaziers are to be introduced into the temperate greenhouse winter garden, the owner must personally supervise their operations to ensure that they do as little damage as possible, as it cannot be hoped that they will do none at all. CHAPTER LIV Warm greenhouse plants. Hot greenhouse plants. - Orchids. - Causes of their high cost. - Difficulty of propagation. - Epiphytic orchids. - Terrestrial orchids. - Oncidium. - Oncidium papilio flower. - Oncidium carthaginense. - Aerids. - Vegetation conditions. - Dendrobiums - Cultivation similar to that of aeridia - Lælias - Ornamental plants for wilderness huts. - Miltonias - Stanhopeas - Fragrance and oddity of their flowers. - Cattleyas. - Phalenopsis. - Uropedium. - Vanilla. - Vegetation in pots. - Parasitic on wood or wall. - Artificial fertilization of its flowers. - Nepenthes. - Its urns or Ascidians. - Æschinantes. - Their propagation by cuttings. - Abundance and beauty of their flowers. Hot greenhouses are not very common in France; you need to have both a very pronounced taste for the rarest plants of the intertropical flora, and a considerable fortune, to be able to afford the very expensive luxury of a hot greenhouse, which is very costly to build and maintain, and can only be filled with very expensive plants, due to the difficulty of propagating them. What's more, the hot house cannot be transformed into a winter garden like the cold and temperate hothouses; the lover of exotic plants cannot spend the most pleasant hours of the day there; the temperature is so high all year round that it's unhealthy to stay there too long. All these considerations sufficiently explain why, among horticultural enthusiasts, we find fewer hot greenhouses than cold or temperate greenhouses. Nevertheless, for the wealthy amateur who knows how to weigh up the advantages against the disadvantages, the hot house offers a continual source of pleasure; it's the place to grow plants that could not be given the hospitality they deserve anywhere else; it's the place where you can savour at every moment the lively satisfaction that results from a successful struggle against an obstacle, from a victory won over difficulty. The various series of plants grown in the greenhouse are all difficult to cultivate to more or less the same degree. The amateur who wants to succeed in their cultivation must make it his or her main occupation. Orchids, bromeliads and palms can be considered as collection plants for the greenhouse. ORCHIDS. - Orchids are unquestionably the most eccentric creatures in the entire plant kingdom; their flowers bear virtually no relation to those of other plants; bulbs and tubers are replaced by rhizomes or pseudo-bulbs of bizarre shapes; the roots themselves, instead of plunging into the soil and drawing nourishment from the plant of which they are a part, implant themselves as parasites in the cracks in the bark of tall trees, or, swaying in the air and terminating in green-surfaced nipples, they sustain many orchids exclusively at the expense of the atmosphere, and for this reason take the name of aerial roots. In the interior of the flower, the reproductive organs are no less different from the usual form and function of the stamens and pistils of regular flowers. In general, the functions of these organs are accomplished very slowly in orchids; the presence of the corolla, the most visible part of the flower, is precisely intended to protect these organs during the mysterious act of fertilization. Until this act is accomplished, the corolla persists, which is what sufficiently accounts for the extraordinary duration of flowering in many orchids, some of which have corollas that last for over a month. This persistence, in the eyes of the orchid lover, compensates to a certain extent for the infrequency of flowering of most of these plants, which almost never bloom with a periodicity that can be counted on to return. Orchids are, after palms and almost on a par with them in this respect, the most expensive of all ornamental plants. We have already had occasion to point out that the price of any ornamental plant cannot rise much when, either by cuttings or marcots, or by sowing its seeds, it can be multiplied quickly and easily. Nor can a plant drop significantly in price, when none of these methods can be used to propagate it on a large scale. Orchids give neither stems nor branches that can be cut or marcotted; they almost never produce fertile seeds in European greenhouses, and can only be propagated by dividing pseudobulbs, a method that is not always successful. Moreover, it is impossible to bring orchid seeds from their country of origin; orchids grow naturally only in the shade of forests in the hottest and wettest countries on earth. As soon as the seeds, all of which are excessively small and resemble a highly-divided dust, reach maturity, they disperse in all directions; those that encounter favourable conditions germinate and perpetuate the species; the others lose their germinative properties in a very short time; it would therefore be pointless to try to collect them to send them to the horticulturists of Europe, who would be unable to make use of them. These facts show how and why the cultivation of orchids remains, and should remain, the preserve of a very limited number of wealthy amateurs who can devote to them a greenhouse particularly suited to the requirements of their vegetation. Orchid greenhouses - Orchid greenhouses are usually single-sided, domed and face east or west; they must be abundantly supplied with water for the frequent watering and basking that orchids continually require; they are preceded by a vestibule large enough to ensure that, under no circumstances, can outside air be suddenly introduced before it has reached the temperature of the greenhouse's interior atmosphere. In terms of cultivation, orchids are divided into epiphytes and terrestrials. Epiphytic orchids grow wild on the branches of trees in tropical regions, like mistletoe on our apple and oak trees. They are grown in hot greenhouses either in small hanging wire baskets, where the base of their rhizomes is surrounded by damp moss in which they spread their roots, or on pieces of cork or rough-barked wood, lined with moss; the orchids are held in place by lead wire, until they have attached themselves by their roots. Terrestrial orchids are grown in pots, like all other plants; they are given more or less peaty heather soil, either crumbled or cut into fragments between which air can circulate. The most sought-after orchid genera are the oncidiums, aérides and deudrobiums, followed by lælia, miltonia, cattleya, stanhopea, phalænopsis and uropédium. ONCIDIUM. - The flowers of orchids in the Oncidium genus are most remarkable for their resemblance to various insects. This can be seen by inspecting the flower of Oncidium papilio, which perfectly resembles a butterfly (fig. 107). What completes the illusion in this respect is the fact that the flower stem, 80 centimetres to 1 metre long, almost straight and dark green, looks like a stick at the top of which the flower appears, like a butterfly that has come to rest on it. Oncidiums are partly epiphytic, partly terrestrial; the epiphytic species are grown on pieces of wood suspended from the iron arches of the hot greenhouse, at a temperature of 20 to 25 degrees. As long as their vegetation remains stationary, it's enough to keep them moderately moist; they take their share of the lukewarm humidity that must always prevail in the orchid greenhouse. As soon as they are ready to flower, place them as close as possible to the air vents Fig. 107. After flowering, they are returned to their original position. Terrestrial species are planted in pots in fragments of peaty heather soil, each with a volume of a few cubic centimeters; as far as temperature and watering are concerned, they are cultivated under the same conditions as epiphytic species. The Oncidium genus includes species of incomparable beauty, such as Oncidium papilio and Oncidium roseum; others, without being as brilliant, are commended, such as Oncidium carthaginense, by the pleasant scent and profusion of their countless small yellow flowers with brown streaks, resembling graceful insects. AERIDS. - Aeridia are all considered epiphytes, and indeed they are in the sense that, fixed to the bark of a piece of wood, they flower perfectly there; but this support is not even essential to them. They can be suspended from a lead wire in the best heated part of the greenhouse, and the base of their rhizomes can simply be surrounded with damp moss; their roots will extend into the air on all sides; aerialids require no other means of existence. Contrary to the general tendency of the flowers of almost all plants, whose flower stems rise above their foliage so that flowering takes place in the open air and in full sun, acrids let their flower stems hang from top to bottom, which adds to the singularity of their ornamental effect. We have quite a large number of species, all very pretty, but capricious bloomers which, although very healthy in appearance and in good vegetation, remain stationary for a long time without deciding to bloom. DENDROBIUMS. - Like the Aeridae, Dendrobiums let their flower stems hang downwards in a graceful curve and are covered with flowers of incomparable freshness of color; each plant always produces several flower stems, whose flowers do not bloom all at once, so that they contribute for a long time to the ornament of the warm greenhouse. Dendrobiums can be tied to pieces of wood by surrounding them with moist moss at the base of the rhizomes; but their vegetation works best when planted in wire baskets filled with fragments of peaty heather soil similar to those used to grow terrestrial oncidiums. The way in which dendrobiums throw their long clusters of hanging flowers, coupled two by two, from different sides, makes their appearance much more pleasing when grown in hanging baskets, where nothing hinders the expansion of their stems in the air, which seems to be their element, as it is that of aerialids. LÆLIAS. - The orchids of the Lælia genus are generally terrestrial. In the interior of South America, local people, struck by the beauty of the flowers of lælias and several other orchids of related genera, cover the tops of their huts with them, just as in many of our départements, country dwellers like to cover the outside of their ovens with tufts of iris. Lælias flower for a long time and quite regularly; they provide a fairly large number of young rhizomes which are used to multiply them. Their cultivation is similar to that of other terrestrial orchids; young rhizomes should only be separated from old clumps at a time when their vegetation is at rest; otherwise, the flowering of fully-formed plants would be compromised. MILTONIA. - Most of the plants in this beautiful genus are epiphytic and are grown on pieces of hanging wood; their flowers are as beautiful as they are fragrant, but they bloom less frequently than the lælias. CATTLEVAS. - The orchids of the cattleya genus lack the oddity of form and the strangeness of color of the oncidiums; but they outshine other orchids in the breadth of their corollas, which are dominated by shades of pink, lilac and dark purple. With care and patience, treating them like terrestrial orchids and giving them a mixture of heather soil and moss in sufficiently spacious pots, they eventually form voluminous clumps with brilliant, prolonged flowering. In general, they shy away from the excess heat that is suitable for other orchids, and should be kept in the less heated areas of a warm greenhouse. STANHOPEAS. - The flowers of orchids of the stanhopea genus are more bizarre than beautiful; their coloring is anything but brilliant; the thickness of their fleshy corollas makes them look like flowers imitated in wax or sugar. Nevertheless, they can be found in every orchid collection, where they are considered indispensable for their unmistakable fragrance, reminiscent of both a perfumer's and a confectioner's store. This kind of fragrance is particularly well developed in a stanhopea, nicknamed odoratissima for this reason. All stanhopeas are epiphytes; they flower quite easily in the warmest parts of the orchid greenhouse; they are fixed with lead wire to the bark of hanging pieces of wood, surrounding the base of the rhizomes with damp moss. We have a dozen species with fragrant flowers that last a very long time. PHALENOPSIS. - This genus is not rich in species; but its flowering, instead of being merely strange like that of the stanhopeas, is as graceful as it is singular; it justifies the nickname of amabilis, given by botanists to the species most widely found in collections. It is cultivated as an epiphytic orchid, flowering well only with the help of plenty of humidity and sustained heat. UROPEDIUM. - The few species of the uropedium genus deserve mention only as a sample of the excess of quirkiness in orchid flowering; the one we drew in the greenhouses of Messrs. Thibaut and Ketteleer, borne on a stem about 40 centimetres long, is greenish-yellow, with blackish-brown lines; the corolla appendages reach to the ground. Uropediums are less difficult to grow than many other 48 orchids; instead of peaty heather soil, they can be given good ordinary garden soil, which is lighter Fig. 108. Uropedium. than strong; keep them in the least heated places until they flower, and move them closer to the heat pipes when they are ready to bloom (fig. 108). Apart from collectible orchids, a number of other plants in this family are indispensable additions to the warm greenhouse, due to the different types of merit they possess; the most worthy of the grower's care in every respect is undoubtedly the vanilla plant. VANILLA. - In its native Mexico, the vanilla plant is one of those vines of immense development, which swing from tree to tree and often end up suffocating the tree at whose expense with their powerful parasitic vegetation, implanting in its bark the suckers of their long aerial roots. In the orchid greenhouse, young vanilla plants, obtained from cuttings, are planted in pots in peaty heather soil, where they grow quite rapidly. As soon as these roots appear, the plant can no longer live exclusively on the soil of the pot through its terrestrial roots; it tends to start living as an epiphytic orchid. A tree trunk is then placed within reach, and the plant clings to it; from then on, all it needs is frequent sprinkling and sustained warmth to flower abundantly and grow to its full potential. In the absence of a tree trunk, if the interior layout of the orchid greenhouse makes its use difficult, young vanilla plants can be placed in pots at the foot of the back wall of the greenhouse; they will soon climb the trunk and cover the surface, as an ivy plant would do in the open air. The vanilla plant does not flower brilliantly; its flowers, which are quite large, are greenish-white and have nothing remarkable to offer as an ornament. In 1836, the late Mr. Neumann, then head gardener of the greenhouses at the Muséum d'Histoire Naturelle, had the bright idea of using a brush to take pollen and apply it directly to the female organs of the flowers, which immediately became fertile and produced long vanilla pods whose scent was second to none to that of vanilla harvested in the New World. Today, anyone can use this easy-to-implement procedure with the same success. Vanilla flowers should only be artificially fertilized in this way when they are fully open and their reproductive organs well developed; the most favourable time for this operation is between eleven o'clock and midday. Among the multitude of rare plants that can only thrive in the orchid greenhouse, we can especially recommend nepenthes and æschynanths, some for the singularity of their vegetation, others for the beauty of their flowering and the richness of their color. NEPENTHES. - The nepenthes flower is greenish and has no ornamental value. However, due to its rarity and the difficulties of propagation, it is one of the most expensive of all greenhouse plants. What we try to develop in the nepenthe are not the very inconspicuous flowers, which in any case are of no use, because they do not produce fertile seeds in European greenhouses; they are the ascidians or vases in the shape of an antique urn which terminate the tendrils or spiral filaments with which the plant is equipped. The ends of the tendrils begin by flattening out and folding in on themselves to form a cup; their development is most curious, eventually resulting in a sort of water pot with a lid, sometimes of fairly large capacity. Nepenthes, native to Ceylon and Indo-China, grow in marshy soils; their urn or ascidian is constantly filled with very clear water; the same phenomenon is obtained in the orchid greenhouse, provided that the soil of the pots in which the nepenthes live is abundantly watered. These plants require a very warm but shady location; they can be propagated by separating offspring, which are rather difficult to raise; plants with one or two ascidians, even when they don't flower, are as expensive as the most brilliant orchids. ÆSCHYNANTES. - The tendency of æschynanths to let their long, grey stems hang down in all directions, ending in brilliantly colored flowers in bunches or umbels, makes them one of the most graceful adornments of the warm greenhouse; they are all the more appropriate among orchids in that their cultivation is on a par with that of aérides and dendrobiums. All orchids are known to flower capriciously, and it is always more or less difficult to multiply them; æschynanths, on the other hand, take cuttings as easily as achimenes, and brighten up the orchid greenhouse with their inexhaustible flowering. CHAPTER LV Hot greenhouse plants (continued). Palm trees. - Genus admitted to the greenhouse. - Coconut palms. - germination conditions for coconuts. - Difficulty of raising young coconut palms. - Sowing date seeds. - Date forest in Egypt. - Cutting date suckers. - Latanier. - Rare flowering in Europe. - Areca. - Caryota. - Bromeliads. - Singularity of their colors. - Genus admitted to the greenhouse. - Banana trees. - Cultivation in hot greenhouses. - Food value of their fruits. - Gloxinias. - How to grow them in a greenhouse. - Amhertia nobilis. - The most beautiful plant on earth. - Conclusion. PALM TREES. - The father of botany, Linnaeus, called palms the princes of plants (principes vegetantium); gardeners rightly call them the plants of princes. It takes a princely fortune to properly house a collection of palms in a warm greenhouse no less than 4 to 5 meters high, to heat such a high greenhouse to a temperature of 15 to 25 degrees, and to stock it with plants, the smallest of which, in fine specimens, cost fabulous sums. The palms of the old and new continents are very numerous, all with a rare elegance of form, almost all commendable in their native climate for the sum of useful products they provide for food or industry. The palms most commonly found in European greenhouses belong to the genera cocos, phænix, latania, areca and caryota. All these palms rarely flower and never ripen their fruit in our greenhouses; they are only cultivated there because of the magnificence of their foliage, regarded as the emblem of triumph from the earliest times. COCOTIER. - (cocos-nucifera). This tree is one of the most useful to mankind in the intertropical regions; its half-ripe fruit gives a healthy, nourishing drink known as cocos milk; its flesh is a very healthy food when ripe; The oil of its kernels is suitable for a host of domestic and industrial uses; the fibers of its husk make excellent brooms; the shell of its nut serves as a bowl, and for part of the year, its shade is used as a dwelling. The books of the ancient sages of India all have as their frontispiece a man reading a manuscript, reclining in the shade of a coconut tree laden with fruit, an emblem which signifies that with the coconut tree able to provide for most of his needs, man has the leisure to indulge in speculative studies. In European greenhouses, the coconut palm, despite the most attentive care, never lives for many years, and consequently cannot grow very large. It is propagated by sowing; the coconuts from which we wish to obtain young plants, are placed in heather soil kept constantly moist. To get them to germinate, expose them to very high temperatures, close to the thermosiphon pipes in the orchid greenhouse, and only bring them back to the warm palm greenhouse when their first leaves are well developed. Young coconut palms always need plenty of heat and frequent watering; they are grown in spacious crates filled with a mixture of heather soil and well-consumed potting compost. Those that can be kept long enough to form a nice clump of leaves sell for very high prices. DATTIER - (phœnix dactylifera). The products of the date palm hold a very important place in the diet of the peoples of Africa; the ease with which dates can be preserved, and their very nourishing properties in a small volume, make them particularly suitable for supplying travelers crossing the deserts of this part of the world in caravans. In Darfur and the other states of East Africa, where currency is virtually unknown, boxes of dates of a specific weight, with a value accepted by everyone, are used as a means of exchange for sales and purchases of some importance. In European greenhouses, the date palm doesn't bear fruit, but it flowers quite often, lives a long time and grows to very large proportions as it ages. The date palm is propagated mainly by sowing date seeds, whose envelope is as hard as horn, but which nevertheless sprout easily, even under apparently unfavorable conditions. It was by taking advantage of this peculiarity that the Viceroy of Egypt, Mehemet Ali, succeeded in stopping the shifting sands of the deserts on the limits of cultivable land in his country, by planting several rows of date palms. The fruits of these date palms form a considerable source of income; the seeds of those that fall to the ground, i.e. onto the sand, have sprouted and formed a forest that fulfils its purpose all the better as it expands every year, encroaching on the desert. Date palms are grown in the same mixture as coconut palms. When they reach a certain age, they produce buds at their base, which can be detached and used for propagation, treating them like cuttings. LATANIER. - (latania rubra). The latanier differs from other palms in the shape of its leaves, which form a vast fan, giving it a most picturesque appearance. It is propagated by sowing seeds, mainly from its native island of Bourbon, and is grown in the same way as the coconut palm. It grows almost as tall there as in the tropics, but flowers very rarely. A very large latanier, after half a century's existence in the hot greenhouse of the Muséum d'Histoire Naturelle, flowered there for the first time in 1857; there are few other examples of latanier flowering in Europe. ARECA. - Palms of the areca genus do not have the same importance in their country of origin as their predecessors, and do not provide the same amount of useful products. However, for the decoration of hot greenhouses, preference is given to them, justified by the beauty of their forms and the elegance of their very beautiful green foliage, and even more so by their ease of cultivation. At a pinch, palms of this beautiful type would be content with the temperate greenhouse; but they grow faster and better in the hot greenhouse, where they must be treated like coconuts. CARVOTA. - Palms of the Caryota genus are propagated by sowing seeds from the Moluccan islands, their country of origin; only one species produces rooted suckers which are used for propagation, earning them the nickname sobolifera. The cultivation of all these palms, remarkable for the prickles on the ribs of their leaves, is similar to that of the coconut palm. BROMELIACEES. - The ornamental plants of the Bromeliaceae family all bear a strong resemblance to the pineapple, the type of this family, in the way their fleshy, thick, sharp leaves are grouped like those of the pineapple. They thrive in peaty heather soil, either coarsely crumbled or divided into fragments mixed with damp moss. However, as they don't require the same degree of humidity as orchids, they are reserved to decorate the hot-house dedicated either to palms, or to plants with hot-house facilities other than orchids, grown, as we saw earlier, in a separate greenhouse. The bromeliads most commonly grown in the greenhouse are aechmea, bilbergia, tillandsia and gusmannia. The ornamental effect of these plants is due to a particular feature of their vegetation. The brightly coloured flowers are borne on an equally coloured stem, accompanied by golden bracts of the brightest colors, like the stem and flowers. In bromeliads, neither the stem, bracts nor flowers are of the same color, resulting in a striking contrast of shades. Ornamental bromeliads are propagated by separating the eyelets they often produce at their base, like pineapples. Some also produce fertile seeds, and can be propagated from seed. They need plenty of water, especially at flowering time, and should never be taken out of the warm greenhouse. BANANIERS. (Musa sapientium). - Banana trees are as useful to people in tropical regions as coconut palms and date palms; bananas are the staple diet of entire populations, and only cost them the trouble of picking them. The most productive and easiest to grow of all banana trees is the Chinese dwarf banana, which bears fruit abundantly in the hot greenhouses of Europe. Some years ago, an English missionary wishing to save the inhabitants of one of the archipelagos of Oceania from periodic famines, brought them banana plants, the cultivation of which he had studied in the greenhouses of the late Duke of Devonshire at Chastworth; the introduction of this single plant, which soon spread to all the islands in the vicinity, saved the poor islanders forever from the recurrence of a famine unknown to them since that time. In the hot greenhouse, the banana tree is both an ornamental plant, with its unrivalled, velvety-green foliage, and its hanging bunches of bizarre flowers called "régimes", followed by slightly bland but very nutritious fruit, which can be eaten raw or cooked on the grill. The banana tree cannot be called a tree, as it has no stem in the true sense of the term; what constitutes a trunk in the banana tree is the union of the leaf stalks, which are nested and welded together. The true stem is a simple spike that rises above the tuft of leaves and bends towards the ground before flowering; thus the largest banana trees are, in the final analysis, nothing more than herbaceous plants of extraordinary proportions. Because of the sheer size of its foliage and the activity of its vegetation, the banana tree is a very voracious plant, so it should be planted in a tarpaulin or pit set up in the hot greenhouse and filled with very substantial garden soil, good loam from broken layers and heather soil, in equal parts. This mixture can only be used 'once; when it has nourished a single generation of banana plants and that generation has borne fruit, before renewing the plantation, the compost from which the exhausted banana plants are uprooted must be replaced with entirely new compost. In hot greenhouses, one compartment of which is devoted to banana cultivation, the plant is placed two meters apart in a tarpaulin one meter deep and one meter 50 wide. Banana plants produce offshoots which are used to propagate them; they require abundant and frequent watering throughout their growth. Cultivated solely as ornamental plants and combined with palm trees in the hot greenhouse, banana trees produce the most picturesque effect. The series of ornamental plants for the greenhouse is extremely large, and includes flowering plants of all sizes, which provide flowers all year round in the greenhouse. One of the most precious of the small, highly floriferous plants is the gloxinia genus, so rich in beautiful species and varieties that it has almost become a collector's item. Gloxinias are grown in heather soil in the best heated parts of the greenhouse; they need very frequent watering. The best varieties of gloxinias often produce fertile seeds which, when sown, frequently give rise to new sub-varieties. Older varieties can be preserved by separating the young tubers, or propagated by leaf cuttings, which root quite easily under a cloche, in a smothered environment. We can do no better than end our description of the most beautiful greenhouse plants and their cultivation methods, by paying tribute to the plant to which botanists and horticulturists have unanimously awarded the prize for beauty among all the most brilliant productions of the plant kingdom; it is dedicated to Lord Amherst, former British ambassador to China; it has been named amherstia nobilis in his honor. To get an idea, imagine a tree with a graceful head, from the leguminous family, whose foliage offers the elegant, regular arrangement of that of the locust tree, with the fullness of that of the most beautiful palms. From the base of the branches spring clusters of flowers no less than a meter to a meter and a half long; each of these flowers in particular is adorned with dazzlingly vivid colors. Introduced only a few years ago to the greenhouses of Europe, amherstia nobilis has already bloomed several times in England, so the pleasure of watching it grow and bloom can only be reserved for a very small number of opulent amateurs. It is usually planted in the open ground in the tarpaulin of a warm greenhouse, where it thrives better than in even a sufficiently spacious crate. We have now come to the end of this work, in which we have assembled all the data to justify its title; We can promise that, depending on local conditions and the type of cultivation he adopts, he will have the most beautiful vegetables, the best fruit and the most beautiful flowers in the flowerbed and in the greenhouse. He will enjoy the most innocuous of satisfactions, that of success in work that is both pleasant and useful, and we are happy to think that many people who are strangers to the art of gardening will owe this satisfaction to THE GARDENER OF THE WHOLE WORLD. ON GARDENING WITHOUT A GARDEN. 505 In the course of this work, we have not neglected to give, whenever the opportunity has arisen, a few instructions on the care that certain ornamental plants require to grow and flower in an inhabited room, or on a window sill. Nevertheless, there are so many people in cities whose business keeps them there all year round, and for whom it is a cruel privation to do without a garden, that the Jarbinier de tour le Monde believes it owes them more complete and detailed advice: such is the purpose of this note. In principle, you shouldn't think of brightening up an apartment by growing a few ornamental plants, if you haven't resolved in advance to give them assiduous care. The most necessary of these are watering, always with water at the temperature of the apartment, never with ice-cold water such as that coming from a well or fountain. From time to time, you should also wipe the plant's leaves with a fine cloth or a very slightly damp piece of sponge; In the case of plants like heathers, which have needles instead of leaves that you don't need to think about cleaning one by one, you should take every opportunity to expose them to a good shower of soft, soothing rain, or water them at an angle, so that only their foliage is drenched, without over-wetting the soil in the pots where their roots grow. In the middle of winter, it's a great pleasure for sedentary people to see beautiful bulbous ornamental plants vegetate, some in potting soil kept 29 constantly moist, others in pure water. Tholl's crocuses and tulips, with their red flowers edged in yellow, only flower well in potting soil; on the other hand, hyacinths, daffodils and even the admirable St. James's lily (amaryllis), one of the most beautiful of all ornamental bulbous plants, can be made to flower in pure water. A very important care for these last plants is to keep the vases containing them constantly filled with water, so that the crown, i.e. the bead that surrounds the tray, is in continuous contact with the water, and the rest of the onion is uncovered. If, through carelessness, the tops of the roots are left in contact with the air for only a day or two, forgetting to refill the decanters in good time, vegetation suffers a setback, which is all it takes to cause flowering to fail. Dwarf cacti are perfectly at home on an elegant shelf hung between two windows, or on the wall of a living room or bedroom. The prettiest live and flower in the company of a wide variety of other dwarf succulents, in pots no larger than the diameter of an egg cup. These pots, made of red earth, are placed on shelf dressers, each in a very small saucer of the same earth; in winter, they should be watered just enough to prevent them from drying out completely, and given a spoonful of water per pot every morning, only when they are ready to flower. If the space available allows for the addition of an apartment planter, this elegant piece of furniture, containing a zinc capsule filled with soil, will be the main winter decoration for camellias, heathers, pimelias and other long-blooming, odorless flowering plants, which are followed without interruption by the most beautiful ornamental plants of each season. For those who have neither a garden nor a greenhouse at their disposal, and who don't want to be obliged to visit flower markets all the time, the best way to maintain an apartment window box is to take out a subscription with a horticulturist who, for a moderate monthly fee, will take back plants that have run out of flowers and replace them with others that are ready to bloom. It's enough for a flower lover without a garden to have to look after bulbous plants on his mantelpiece, dwarf succulents on his bookshelf, and an assortment of various plants in his apartment window box. Horticultural pleasures are even more varied if you can afford the luxury of a portable greenhouse, either cold or heated by a spirit-devin lamp. Under this shelter, placed on the pedestal table in the center of the living room, he will be able, in pots similar to those for dwarf succulents filled with heather soil, to multiply by sowing, cutting, marcotting and grafting, almost every series of ornamental, orangery, cold greenhouse and temperate greenhouse plants. The sphere of gardening, practicable for the amateur without leaving his or her home, widens more and more when the apartment he or she occupies is adorned with balconies facing south, east or west; that facing north admits of no other decoration than a frame of ivy, which is not lacking in charm by the perpetuity of its greenery; but no flowering plant can thrive there. If the exposure of the windows is favorable and they open onto a sufficiently spacious street, where air and light are not lacking, they can first be surrounded by an arcade of trellis in the center of which hangs a terracotta vase lined with drooping plants: the trellis is hidden under volubilis, cobéas, runner beans and other annual or perennial climbers. The window sill, like the window box in an apartment, is home to a whole assortment of flowering plants that can be planted from spring to autumn: this is, strictly speaking, the real garden in miniature for city dwellers, the window garden. All the full-ground ornamental plants indicated in this book as suitable for the parterre are available to the window-garden amateur; he can, understandably, adopt only a very limited number, but at least he has the satisfaction of seeing them grow from beginning to end, of seeing them go through all the phases of their vegetation. If, in the corners of balconies, he places Persian lilacs, high-stemmed grafted roses or a few other flowering shrubs in boxes, chosen from among those that can withstand the winters of the Paris climate well in the open air, he can keep them there for an indefinite number of years, especially if he takes the precaution of renewing the soil in the boxes from time to time, and turning them over at least once a week, so that all parts of the shrub heads are equally exposed to air and light. The periodic return of lilacs, roses and jasmine, on shrubs that you've grown and that have kept you faithful company for many years, is one of the most precious compensations for the city dweller that can make him regret less the deprivation of a real garden. This deprivation disappears, so to speak, when, instead of one or more narrow balconies, the city dweller can have at his disposal a terrace on the same level as his apartment, especially when this terrace is not dominated by other constructions that exclude air and light: it's then a real parterre in miniature. The trellis that can be used to cover a terrace in these conditions can accommodate honeysuckle, clematis, sarmentose roses, Virginia jasmine and Chinese wisteria, with the help of a few large enough boxes filled with good soil. Finally, the amateur can choose from all the plants in this series that we have already indicated, and which, in the Paris climate, belong neither to the cold greenhouse nor the temperate greenhouse. In order to indulge his preferred taste, the horticultural enthusiast must take every precaution to ensure that his window and terrace gardening does not contravene any regulations or inconvenience his neighbors or passers-by. This recommendation applies especially to watering: it happens all too often that a clumsy city gardener, in giving water to thirsty plants on his balcony, waters passers-by who don't need it, and even exposes himself to more or less damaging the outside of the house he lives in. Each individual pot should be placed in an earthen bowl designed to catch any superfluous water used for watering; the boxes on the terrace, which serve as flowerbeds, should be lined with zinc, as should the inside of the apartment planter, so as never to be damaged by the dripping of water from watering and basking. The gastronomic side of horticulture is not completely excluded from terrace gardening; a few vines grown in pots, and dwarf cherry trees prepared as indicated for those that have to be forced, can live for a long time on a well-exposed terrace and provide its annual quota of grapes and cherries. Even if the amateur who tends them, in conjunction with the ornamental plants, only obtains one dessert a year, this dessert would certainly be sufficient reward for his care; it would be, in his opinion, the best dessert of the whole year. Such, in short, is the very considerable sum of varied pleasures that gardening in the apartment, on the window and on the terrace can bring to those who have a passion for horticulture and yet are forced to do without a real garden.
